=== PDF PAGE 14 ===
1 How to use this manual
This manual explains every function in the handwriting pipeline. For each one it gives the decla-
ration exactly as it appears in the code, what each input means, what comes out, and then the
theory: what the function is for, what problem it solves, how it solves it, and why that method
was chosen rather than another.
The intention is that nothing is assumed. Where a term from mathematics, image processing or
machine learning appears for the rst time it is explained in place, and the most common terms
are collected in the glossary immediately after this chapter. Where a formula is given, every
symbol in it is dened underneath, and if the method comes from a published source that source
is named with the formula rather than only in the bibliography.
Conventions used throughout the software.These are worth reading once, because they
are assumed by every chapter.
Imagesare rectangular grids of numbers, stored asnumpyarrays and indexed[row, column],
which is[y, x]. The row index increasesdownwards, which is the normal convention for
images because that is the order a screen or a printer draws them in.
Pen pathsare lists of(x, y)points measured in millimetres, with y increasingupwards,
which is the normal convention for plotters and for ordinary graph paper. The pipeline
therefore contains two coordinate systems whose vertical axes point in opposite directions.
The conversion happens in exactly two places and both are pointed out where they occur.
Letter shapesare stored in units of the x-height. The x-height is the height of the body
of a lower case letter such asaoro, measured from the line the writing sits on. Storing a
letter in these units, rather than in pixels, means the same stored letter can be drawn at
any physical size.
No libraries.Apart fromnumpy, which provides arithmetic on arrays of numbers, every
algorithm in this manual is implemented in the les described here. There is no image
processing library and no machine learning framework.
13

=== PDF PAGE 15 ===
2 Glossary
Terms used in this manual, in plain language. They are grouped by subject rather than alpha-
betically, because reading a group in order explains more than reading a denition on its own.
2.1 Arrays and arithmetic
Array
A grid of numbers. A one-dimensional array is a list, a two-dimensional array is a table, and a
greyscale image is a two-dimensional array where each number is the brightness of one pixel,
usually from 0 for black to 255 for white.
Element-wise
An operation applied to every number in an array independently. Adding 10 to an image,
element-wise, makes every pixel ten levels brighter.
V ectorised
Written so that an operation is applied to a whole array at once rather than by looping over
the elements one at a time in Python. This matters for speed: the loop runs inside compiled
code instead of in the interpreter, which is typically between ten and a hundred times faster.
Cumulative sum, or prex sum
An array where each entry is the total of everything up to that point. For the input(2,3,1,4)
the cumulative sum is(2,5,6,10). Its usefulness is that the total of any stretch of the original
can then be found by one subtraction, because the total fromitojequals the entry atjminus
the entry ati.
Mask
An array of true and false values, used to mark which pixels belong to something. An ink
mask is true at every pixel judged to be ink and false elsewhere.
Dot product
For two lists of equal length, the sum of the products of corresponding entries. If both lists
are rst scaled so that their own length is one, the dot product equals the cosine of the angle
between them considered as arrows, which is why it is used in this project to measure how
similar two shapes are.
Norm
The length of a list of numbers considered as an arrow from the origin, which is the square
root of the sum of the squares of its entries.
Interpolation
Estimating a value between known values. If a transformed image needs the brightness at a
position halfway between two pixels, interpolation supplies it as a blend of the neighbours.
Median
The middle value when a set of numbers is sorted. It is used throughout this project in
preference to the mean (the ordinary average) because it is insensitive to a small number of
extreme values: one badly wrong measurement among eleven good ones moves the mean but
not the median.
14

=== PDF PAGE 16 ===
Percentile
The value below which a given share of the data falls. The 40th percentile is the value that 40
per cent of the data lies below. The median is the 50th percentile.
V ariance and standard deviation
Measures of how spread out a set of numbers is. The variance is the average of the squared
distances from the mean; the standard deviation is its square root, which has the same units
as the data.
2.2 Images and shapes
Threshold
A brightness level used to divide an image into two groups, typically ink and paper. Thresh-
olding is applying it.
Histogram
A count of how many pixels fall at each brightness level. It describes an image's distribution
of tones without reference to where anything is.
Morphology
A family of operations that change the shapes in a mask rather than the values in an image:
growing shapes, shrinking them, lling small holes, removing small specks.
Structuring element, or kernel
The small window a morphological or ltering operation looks through. A square kernel of
radius 2 is a ve-by-ve window centred on each pixel.
Dilation and erosion
Growing and shrinking shapes. Dilation turns a pixel on if anything nearby is on; erosion turns
it o unless everything nearby is on.
Closing
A dilation followed by an erosion by the same amount. The net eect is that small gaps are
bridged while the overall thickness of a shape is preserved.
Connected component
A group of pixels that touch one another, treated as a single object. A letter, a dot over ani
and a speck of dust are each a connected component.
Connectivity
Which neighbours count as touching. Four-connectivity counts only the pixels above, below,
left and right; eight-connectivity also counts the four diagonals, so two pixels meeting at a
corner belong to the same component.
Skeleton, or centreline
A one-pixel-wide line running down the middle of a thick stroke. Thinning a pen stroke to its
skeleton recovers approximately the path the pen travelled.
Projection prole
The result of adding up the ink in each row, or in each column, producing a one-dimensional
summary of a two-dimensional image. Lines of text appear as peaks in the row prole.
Polyline
A path described by an ordered list of points, understood to be joined by straight segments.
15

=== PDF PAGE 17 ===
Aspect ratio
The ratio of width to height.
Bounding box
The smallest upright rectangle containing a shape.
2.3 Networks and training
Neural network
A function with a very large number of adjustable numbers, called weights, arranged in layers.
Each layer takes the output of the previous one and performs a simple operation; the compo-
sition can represent complicated relationships. Training is the process of adjusting the weights
so the function gives the right answers on examples.
W eight and bias
The adjustable numbers. A weight multiplies an input; a bias is added afterwards. Together
they are the parameters.
Layer
One stage of a network.
F eature map
The output of a convolutional layer: an array showing, for each position in the image, how
strongly some learned pattern was detected there.
Channel
One such map. A layer producing 64 channels is detecting 64 dierent patterns.
Convolution
Sliding a small grid of weights over an image and recording the response at every position,
which detects the same pattern wherever it occurs.
Activation function
A simple non-linear function applied after a layer, such as setting negatives to zero. Without
one, stacking layers would be pointless, because a chain of purely linear operations is equivalent
to a single linear operation.
Pooling
Reducing the size of a feature map by summarising each small window, typically by its largest
value, which saves work and makes the result tolerant to small shifts.
Logit
A raw score produced by the nal layer, before it is converted into a probability. Logits may
be any real number, positive or negative.
Softmax
The conversion from logits to probabilities: exponentiate each score and divide by the total,
which forces them to be positive and to sum to one.
Loss function
A single number measuring how wrong the network's output is on a training example. Training
consists of reducing it.
16

=== PDF PAGE 18 ===
Gradient
The collection of derivatives of the loss with respect to every weight: for each weight, the rate
at which the loss would change if that weight changed. It is what tells training which direction
to move each weight.
Chain rule
The rule of calculus for dierentiating a composition of functions, which is what makes it
possible to compute the gradient of a loss with respect to a weight many layers away from it.
Backpropagation
Applying the chain rule layer by layer from the loss back to the rst layer, so that every weight's
gradient is obtained in one backward pass.
F orward pass, backward pass
Computing the output from the input, and computing the gradients from the loss, respectively.
Batch
A group of examples processed together, so that one weight update is based on their average
gradient rather than on a single example, which is both faster and less noisy.
Epoch
One complete pass through the training data.
Learning rate
How far each weight is moved per update.
Optimiser
The rule that turns gradients into weight updates.
Overtting
Learning the training examples rather than the general pattern, so that performance on new
data is poor. It is detected by measuring on data the network was not trained on.
Holdout, or validation set
Data deliberately excluded from training and kept for measurement, which is the only way an
accuracy gure means anything.
Checkpoint
A saved copy of all the weights, so a trained network can be reloaded later.
Recurrent layer
A layer that processes a sequence one position at a time while carrying information forward,
so that a decision at one position can depend on earlier ones.
Inference
Running a trained network forward to get an answer, as opposed to training it.
2.4 This project's own terms
Glyph
One stored example of one character in one writer's hand: a set of pen strokes, in x-height
units, with measurements describing how much space it occupies.
V ariant
One of several stored glyphs for the same character by the same writer, since nobody writes a
letter identically twice.
17

=== PDF PAGE 19 ===
Prior distance
A number measuring how far a stored glyph's shape sits from the typical shape of that character
pooled across all writers. Low means a well-formed letter; high means the shape was probably
cut out wrongly.
Anchor, or fallback letterform
A clean, readable shape used in place of a stored variant when the stored variants are too
damaged to be legible. Preferably the writer's own most typical version of the letter.
Legibility lambda
A number between 0 and 1 controlling how often the anchor is used in place of the writer's
own shapes, and therefore where the output sits between being readable and being recognisably
that person's hand.
Connectedness
The measured fraction of neighbouring letter pairs within a word that the writer joins without
lifting the pen.
Ligature
The joining stroke between two letters written without lifting the pen.
T rajectory
The pipeline's representation of a drawing: a list of pen strokes in millimetres.
G-code
The text format understood by the machine, consisting of lines such asG1 X10 Y20 F900,
meaning move in a straight line to this position at this feed rate.
Endstop
A switch at the end of an axis's travel, used to nd a known position and to prevent the
machine driving into its own frame.
18

=== PDF PAGE 20 ===
3 paths.py
This le contains no algorithm. It records where the data sits on disk and who the thirteen writers
are, and every other module asks it rather than working it out independently.
Why it is a separate leEarlier in the project each module computed its own paths by
walking upwards from its own location in the directory tree. That works until a le is moved to
a dierent folder, at which point the chain of parent directories is wrong and the module can no
longer nd the data. Collecting the knowledge in one place means there is exactly one line to
change if the data moves, and a module that moves folder does not break.
It is also deliberately not merged into one of the functional modules. Every module needs to know
where the weights and proles live, and if that knowledge lived inside, say, the recogniser, then
the gantry driver would have to import the entire recogniser in order to nd a directory name.
3.1 Data
Data = Path ( _ _ file _ _ ) . resolve () . parent . parent / ' CNN '
Output.The folder holding the datasets, the trained weights and the style proles.
Derived from the location of this le rather than written as an absolute path, so the project can
be copied to another machine or another drive without editing anything.
This path is one directory level up from where it was before the modules were attened into a
single folder, and the derivation was adjusted accordingly. A path derived by counting parent
directories is correct only for the depth it was written for, which is a known fragility of this
approach.
3.2 Weights
Weights = Data / ' weights '
Output.The sub-folder holding trained checkpoints as.npzles.
A checkpoint is a saved copy of every weight in a trained network. The.npzformat is numpy's
own archive of named arrays, which is used here because the networks are built from numpy arrays
directly and no framework's format is involved.
3.3 Proles
Profiles = Data / ' NOGIT ' / ' StyleProfiles10 '
Output.The folder holding one JSON style prole per writer.
19

=== PDF PAGE 21 ===
A style prole is the complete description of one person's handwriting produced by style.py: their
stored letterforms plus the measurements describing how they lay writing out. JSON is used
because the contents are nested lists of numbers and text, and because a prole can then be
inspected in a text editor when something looks wrong.
3.4 PersonalPages
P e r s o n a l P a g e s = Data / ' PersonalDataset '
Output.The folder holding the photographed pages and their transcripts.
The pages written specically for this project, as opposed to the public database. Each writer
has a sub-folder containing the photographs and a matching transcript for each one.
3.5 Cache
Cache = Data / ' NOGIT '
Output.The folder holding caches and other large generated les.
Extracting line crops from pages, and cutting glyphs out of those crops, are the slowest operations
in the pipeline. Their results are cached here so that a change to a later stage can be tested
without repeating them. Keeping the caches out of the code repository also keeps the repository
small.
3.6 DatabaseWriters
D a t a b a s e W r i t e r s = [ '150 ' , '151 ' , '152 ' , '153 ' , '384 ' , '551 ' , '552 ' ,
'588 ']
Output.The identiers of the eight writers taken from the public handwriting database.
These are writers from a published corpus of scanned handwriting [1], identied by number. They
provide variety that the project could not collect by hand: dierent ages, dierent nationalities,
and in particular several strongly joined-up hands, which turn out to be the hardest case for every
stage of the pipeline.
3.7 PersonalWriters
P e r s o n a l W r i t e r s = [ ' abhinav ' , ' dylan ' , ' owen ' , ' thiya ' , ' yeukita ']
Output.The names of the ve writers photographed for this project.
Volunteers who each lled several pages, photographed with a phone camera. These are the
writers the project can test end-to-end, because their pages exist as photographs rather than as
clean scans and therefore exercise the whole pipeline from uneven lighting onwards.
20

=== PDF PAGE 22 ===
3.8 AllWriters
A l l W r i t e r s = D a t a b a s e W r i t e r s + P e r s o n a l W r i t e r s
Output.All thirteen writers, database then photographed.
Thirteen in total, which is three more than requirement R6 asks for. The order is xed so that
every table in every results document lists writers in the same sequence.
3.9 ValFraction
V a l F r a c t i o n = 0.15
Output.The share of data held back from training, 0.15.
What a holdout is, and why the fraction lives hereMeasuring a network on the same
data it was trained on says almost nothing, because a network with enough capacity can memorise
its training examples. Some data is therefore withheld from training entirely and used only for
measurement; that is the holdout, or validation set.
The fraction is dened here, once, rather than in each script that needs a split. If two scripts
chose their own fractions, or their own random seeds, the same line could be held out by one and
trained on by the other, and every accuracy gure in the project would be quietly wrong.
3.10 SplitSeed
Sp l it Se ed = 0
Output.The xed random seed used when splitting data, 0.
Why a random choice is made repeatableDeciding which lines are held out is a random
choice, but it must be thesamerandom choice every time the program runs. A random number
generator started from a xed seed produces the same sequence every time, so the split is both
arbitrary, which is what makes it fair, and reproducible, which is what makes it trustworthy.
This also means a holdout set can be recovered later even if it was not saved: replaying the split
with the same seed reproduces it exactly.
3.11 PersonalWriterPages
def P e r s o n a l W r i t e r P a g e s ( writer )
Inputs
writer
the lower case name of one photographed writer, for example "owen"
Output.A generator yielding(imagePath, labelPath)for each of that writer's pages, in sorted
lename order.
21

=== PDF PAGE 23 ===
What it is forFinds one writer's pages and pairs each with its transcript. Every stage that
needs real handwriting with known text starts here: training, prole building, and the measure-
ment of segmentation accuracy.
Why the order mattersSeveral measurement scripts pair the n-th detected line crop on a
page with the n-th line of that page's transcript. If the pages were returned in whatever order
the le system happened to supply, that pairing would still appear to work but would sometimes
be comparing a line against the wrong label, and the resulting accuracy would be wrong in a way
that no error message would reveal. Sorting makes the order a property of the lenames rather
than of the le system.
Why a generatorA generator produces its values one at a time as they are asked for, rather
than building the whole list rst. Here that means a caller can begin processing the rst page
immediately, and can stop after one page without the cost of listing them all.
22

=== PDF PAGE 24 ===
4 segment.py
This chapter covers two things that live in one le: the image toolkit used by the whole pipeline,
and the page segmenter that is its heaviest user. They are together because the toolkit has no
purpose on its own, and because other modules then import the few functions they need from
here rather than from a separate module that would exist only to hold them.
The problem this le solvesA photograph of a handwritten page arrives as a single large
grid of brightness values. Everything downstream, both networks and the glyph extractor, expects
one tidy image per line of writing: straightened, with the printed ruling removed, cropped to the
writing, and containing that line and no part of its neighbours. Getting from one to the other is
this le's job.
That is harder than it sounds for four reasons, and the structure of the le follows from them.
The photograph is not evenly lit. One side is brighter than the other, there may be a shadow,
and the paper is not pure white. So there is no single brightness that separates ink from
paper everywhere on the page.
The page is never perfectly square to the camera. Even a degree of tilt means a horizontal
cut through the image crosses several lines of writing.
The paper is usually ruled, and the printed rules are darker and more regular than the
handwriting. If they are not removed they dominate every measurement.
Lines of handwriting overlap vertically. A descender from one line hangs into the next, and
a straight cut between two lines slices it in half, leaving a fragment attached to the wrong
line.
How the toolkit is arrangedThe rst half of the chapter is built strictly from the bottom
upwards, because each layer is used by the one above it: sums over rectangles, then shape oper-
ations built from those sums, then thresholding, then grouping touching pixels into objects, then
thinning a stroke to its centreline, then resizing, then geometric transforms, then measurement.
Why so much is built on rectangle sumsAlmost every operation in the rst half needs a
local average or a local count: the average brightness near a pixel, whether any ink lies within a
few pixels, how much ink is in a window. Computed directly, a window of radius r costs (2r+1)
squared additions per pixel, so a radius of 40 on a page of several million pixels is thousands
of additions each and the program becomes unusable. A summed-area table reduces every such
query to four lookups, whatever the radius [2]. That single idea is what makes the rest of the le
practical, which is why it comes rst.
4.1 Integral
def Integral ( img )
Inputs
23

=== PDF PAGE 25 ===
imga two-dimensional array of numbers, normally a greyscale image
Output.An array one row and one column larger than the input, in which each entry holds the
total of everything above and to the left of it.
What it is forThis is the foundation of the toolkit. On its own it computes nothing useful;
what it provides is the ability to ask for the total of any rectangle in the image at a cost that does
not depend on the rectangle's size. Every local average, every shape-growing operation and every
local ink count in this le uses it.
Background: what a cumulative sum isIf a list of numbers is replaced by a list in which
each entry is the total so far, then the sum of any stretch of the original list can be found by
subtracting two entries of the new list. For the list 2, 3, 1, 4 the running totals are 2, 5, 6, 10,
and the sum of the middle two numbers is 6 minus 2, which is 4. No addition is needed at query
time, however long the stretch.
A summed-area table, also called an integral image, is the same idea in two dimensions [2]. Each
cell holds the total of the whole rectangle from the top-left corner of the image down to that cell:
ii[y, x] =
X
i<y
X
j<x
img[i, j](4.1)
Source:[2]
where
ii[y, x]
the value stored at row y, column x of the table
img[i, j]
the original pixel at row i, column j
y, xthe row and column being computed, counted from zero
How a rectangle total is then obtainedTo get the total inside a rectangle, take the table
value at its bottom-right corner, which includes the rectangle but also everything above and to
the left of it. Subtract the value at the top-right corner, which removes the band above. Subtract
the value at the bottom-left corner, which removes the band to the left. Those two subtractions
each removed the top-left block, so it has been taken away twice and must be added back once:
S= ii[y 2, x2]−ii[y 1, x2]−ii[y 2, x1] + ii[y 1, x1](4.2)
Source:[2]
where
Sthe total of all pixels inside the rectangle
y1, x1
the top-left corner of the rectangle
y2, x2
one row and one column past the bottom-right corner, so the rectangle stops just before
them
24

=== PDF PAGE 26 ===
Why the extra row and column of zerosThe formula above needs to read the table at
the row above the rectangle and the column to its left. For a rectangle touching the top or left
edge of the image those positions would be outside the array. Storing the table one larger in each
direction, with the rst row and column lled with zeros, means those reads land on genuine zeros
instead and no special case is needed anywhere in the le.
Why the result is stored as oating pointThe entry in the bottom-right corner is the sum
of every pixel in the image. For a 3000 by 2200 photograph with values up to 255 that is about 1.7
billion, which exceeds the range of a 32-bit signed integer. An overow would not raise an error;
it would wrap around to a negative number and quietly corrupt every rectangle total computed
from that part of the table. Using 64-bit oating point avoids this with room to spare.
CostTwo passes over the image to build, then four lookups per query regardless of window size.
For the background estimate used by ink detection, with a radius of 40 pixels, the direct method
would need 6561 additions per pixel; this needs four.
4.2 BoxSum
def BoxSum ( img , ry , rx )
Inputs
imga two-dimensional array
rythe window radius in rows, so the window spans 2*ry+1 rows
rxthe window radius in columns
Output.An array the same shape as the input, where each entry is the total of the input inside
the window centred on that position.
What it is forApplies the rectangle query of the summed-area table at every position at once.
This is the function that actually gets used;Integralexists to make it possible.
How it worksThe four corner positions of every pixel's window are built as whole arrays of
indices rather than being computed one pixel at a time, and the table is then read at all of them
simultaneously. The result is the same four-term expression as before, evaluated for the entire
image in a handful of array operations.
Doing it this way rather than with a loop matters because the loop would run in the Python
interpreter, which is roughly two orders of magnitude slower than the same arithmetic performed
inside numpy's compiled code. For a multi-megapixel photograph that is the dierence between
a fraction of a second and several minutes.
What happens at the edgesA window centred near the border of the image hangs o the
edge. The corner indices are clipped to stay inside the array, so the window is eectively cut short
and the total covers fewer pixels than it would in the middle of the image. That is not corrected
here, because the correction depends on what the caller wants:BoxMeandivides by the number of
pixels actually covered, whileErodecompares against that same count. Both get what they need
fromBoxCount.
25

=== PDF PAGE 27 ===
4.3 BoxCount
def BoxCount ( ry , rx , h , w )
Inputs
shape or array
used for its dimensions
rythe window radius in rows
rxthe window radius in columns
Output.An integer array giving, for each position, how many pixels its window actually covered
once clipped to the image.
What it is forWindow sums near the border of an image cover fewer pixels than those in the
middle. This counts how many were covered, which is what makes the border behaviour of the
operations above it correct rather than approximately correct.
How it worksThe count is the clipped height of the window multiplied by its clipped width.
Because those two depend only on the row and only on the column respectively, each is computed
as a short one-dimensional array and the two are then multiplied together, which is far less work
than computing a two-dimensional count directly. A quantity that factorises this way is called
separable, and the same idea appears again inResizeandBlur.
Why it matters: two dierent uses
BoxMeandivides the window sum by this count instead of by the nominal window area.
Without that, the average near the border would be computed as though the missing pixels
were zero, so the estimated background would fall towards black near every edge and ink
detection would misbehave in a band around the whole page.
Erodeasks whether every pixel in the window is set, which it tests by comparing the window
sum against this count. Comparing against the nominal area instead would make the test
impossible to satisfy anywhere near the border, and a band around the page would be eroded
away.
4.4 BoxMean
def BoxMean (a , r )
Inputs
imga two-dimensional array
ra single radius used for both rows and columns
Output.A oating point array the same shape as the input, giving the average value in the
window centred on each position.
26

=== PDF PAGE 28 ===
What it is forThis is how the pipeline estimates the local background brightness of a photo-
graph. Comparing a pixel against the average of its own surroundings, rather than against one
number for the whole page, is what allows ink to be found correctly on a page that is brighter on
one side, or has a shadow across it, or was photographed with the ash o to one side.
How it works
mean[y, x] = BoxSum[y, x]
BoxCount[y, x] (4.3)
where
mean[y, x]
the local average at this position
BoxSum[y, x]
the total inside the window, from the summed-area table
BoxCount[y, x]
how many pixels that window covered
Why a at window rather than a smooth oneA gaussian-weighted average, which weights
nearby pixels more than distant ones, would be slightly smoother. A at window is used because
it can be computed from the summed-area table in constant time per pixel whatever the radius,
where a gaussian cannot. For a background estimate the dierence is not visible: what is wanted
is the general level over a region, and the ne detail of how that region is weighted does not aect
which pixels end up classied as ink.
4.5 Dilate
def Dilate ( mask , ky , kx )
Inputs
ma mask: an array of true and false values
rthe radius to grow by, in pixels
Output.A mask in which a position is true if any true pixel of the input lay within its window.
Background: what morphology isMorphology is the family of operations that change the
shapes in a mask rather than the values in an image. The two basic operations are growing a
shape outwards and shrinking it inwards, and almost everything else is built by combining them.
The window they look through is called the structuring element; here it is always a square.
What it is forGrowing shapes is used in this pipeline to make separate pieces of ink touch, so
that they can be treated as one object. The clearest example is a letter such as i or j, which is
written as two separate marks: growing both slightly makes them touch, after which the grouping
stage treats the letter and its dot as a single piece and cannot assign them to dierent lines of
writing.
27

=== PDF PAGE 29 ===
How it worksA position should be turned on if any true pixel lies within its window. Asking
whether any pixel in a region is true is the same as asking whether the number of true pixels is
greater than zero, and that number is exactly what a window sum provides:
dilate(m)[p] =
 
BoxSum(m, r, r)[p]>0

(4.4)
where
mthe input mask, with true counted as 1 and false as 0
pthe position being computed
rthe radius of the square structuring element
Expressing it as a window sum means dilation inherits the constant cost per pixel of the summed-
area table, so growing by twenty pixels costs the same as growing by two.
4.6 Erode
def Erode ( mask , ky , kx )
Inputs
ma mask
rthe radius to shrink by
Output.A mask in which a position is true only if every pixel its window covered was true in
the input.
What it is forShrinking shapes. On its own it is used to remove small specks and to thin a
shape; its main use here is as the second half of a closing, described next.
How it works, and the one subtletyWhere dilation asks whetheranypixel in the window is
set, erosion asks whethereveryone is. That is true exactly when the number of set pixels equals
the number of pixels the window covered:
erode(m)[p] =
 
BoxSum(m, r, r)[p] = BoxCount(r, r)[p]

(4.5)
where
mthe input mask
pthe position being computed
BoxCount(r, r)[p]
how many pixels the window at p actually covered after clipping
The comparison must be against the covered count and not against the full window area of (2r+1)
squared. Near the border the window is clipped, so its sum can never reach the full area, and a
test against the area would report false for every position within r pixels of the edge. The visible
result would be a band eroded away around the whole page, and since erosion is half of a closing,
that band would then be removed from every mask the pipeline closes.
28

=== PDF PAGE 30 ===
4.7 Close
def Close ( mask , ky , kx )
Inputs
ma mask
rthe radius
Output.A mask with gaps narrower than the structuring element lled in, and the original
thickness of the shapes preserved.
What it is forRepairing ink that should be continuous but is not. Thresholding a photograph
leaves small holes inside strokes where the paper texture shows through, and a stroke drawn with
a drying pen can have genuine breaks in it. Both are repaired by a closing.
How it works, and why the two steps are neededA closing is a dilation followed by an
erosion by the same radius. The dilation joins anything separated by less than the window, which
lls the gaps, but it also makes every stroke fatter. The erosion then removes a layer of the same
thickness, which restores the original width. What does not come back is the gap: once two sides
have been joined, eroding the joined shape thins it but does not split it again.
close(m, r) = erode
 
dilate(m, r), r

(4.6)
where
mthe input mask
rthe radius, which sets the largest gap that will be bridged
So a closing bridges a gap narrower than the kernel while leaving a thin stroke thin, which a plain
dilation would not. That property is why it, and not dilation, is used before thinning: thinning
is sensitive to both holes and thickness, so the operation applied beforehand must x the rst
without changing the second.
4.8 AnyWithinRadius
def A n y W i t h i n R a d i u s (m , r , axis )
Inputs
mask
a mask
rthe radius
axis
which axis to work along: 0 for down the rows, 1 for across the columns
Output.A mask that is true wherever any element within r positions along that single axis was
true.
29

=== PDF PAGE 31 ===
What it is forA dilation along one direction only. It is used where the tolerance being applied
has a direction to it, and growing in both directions would be wrong.
There are two such places. When a printed rule has been detected, it is widened slightly along
its own length, so that a rule which is a fraction of a degree o horizontal is still removed over its
whole length rather than in broken pieces; widening it vertically as well would eat into the writing
above and below it. And when the paper region has been found, it is shrunk inwards by a margin
in each axis separately, to keep the slightly soft edge of the sheet from being read as ink.
4.9 LongRunMask
def L o n g R u n M a s k (m , n , axis )
Inputs
ma mask
nthe minimum length, in pixels, that a run must reach
axis
the direction along which the run is measured
Output.A mask that is true at every pixel belonging to an unbroken run of at least n set pixels
along that axis.
What it is forTelling printed lines apart from handwriting. This is the test that removes the
ruling from a lined page and the dark edge of a binder, both of which would otherwise dominate
every measurement the segmenter makes.
Why run length is the right testA printed rule is drawn by a machine: it is straight,
continuous, and spans the page. A pen stroke changes direction constantly, so the number of
pixels it continues in any one direction without a break is small. At the sizes this pipeline works
with, the threshold is set to between 1.25 and 1.4 times the spacing between lines of writing, which
is roughly 60 to 80 pixels; almost no handwritten stroke runs that far in a straight line, and a rule
runs much further.
How it worksTwo passes of cumulative sums. The rst nds where a long run begins. A
stretch of n pixels starting at position i is entirely set exactly when the number of set pixels inside
it equals n, and that count comes from one subtraction of the cumulative sum:
starts[i] =
 
c[i+n]−c[i] =n

(4.7)
where
starts[i]
true when a run of length n begins at position i
cthe cumulative sum of the mask along the chosen axis, with a leading zero
nthe minimum run length
30

=== PDF PAGE 32 ===
ithe candidate starting position
The second pass turns starting positions into coverage. A run of length n that covers position j
must begin somewhere between n-1 places before j and j itself, and it cannot begin so late that it
would run o the end. A second cumulative sum over the starts then answers "is there any start
in that range" with another single subtraction:
covered[j] =
 
cs[hj + 1]−cs[l j]>0

, l j = max(0, j−n+ 1), h j = min(j, L−n)(4.8)

lj, hj

Lthe length of the axis being measured
Why it is done with cumulative sumsThe cost is proportional to the number of pixels and
does not depend on n. Scanning each window directly would cost n times as much, and n here is
around 80.
How one function handles both directionsThe axis of interest is moved to the front of
the array, the xed code runs down it, and the axis is moved back afterwards. Moving an axis in
numpy only changes how the array is interpreted, not the data in memory, so this costs nothing
and avoids writing the same logic twice.
A simpler equivalent exists.RowRunLength, described next, already gives every pixel the length
of its own run, which makes this function the same as asking whether that length is at least n,
with a transpose for the other axis. That form was checked against this one on 4800 randomly
generated masks of every shape, with n from 1 to 40 and both axes, and never disagreed; on
a page-sized array it also ran about twice as fast, because it makes one labelling pass and one
count instead of building two cumulative-sum arrays and several arrays of clamped indices.
4.10 RowRunLength
def R o w R u n L e n g t h ( mask )
Inputs
mask
a two-dimensional mask; runs are measured across the rows
Output.An integer array in which every set pixel holds the length of the unbroken horizontal
run it belongs to, and every clear pixel holds zero.
31

=== PDF PAGE 33 ===
What it is forMeasuring how far ink continues horizontally through each pixel. It is used to
nd printed rules, and inBinarizeLineto delete them from a line crop.
How it works, in three vectorised stepsFirst, the places where a run begins are found as
the positions where the row steps from clear to set, which is a comparison between the row and
a copy of itself shifted by one.
Second, a cumulative sum over those starting positions numbers the runs consecutively: the rst
run becomes 1, the second 2, and so on. The numbering is then cleared wherever there is no ink,
so that only ink carries a run number.
Third, counting how many pixels carry each number gives the length of each run, and indexing
those counts by the run number image hands every pixel the length of its own run.
Why the numbering does not leak between rowsThe cumulative sum is taken over the
whole array attened into one long sequence, which would be wrong if a run could continue from
the end of one row into the start of the next. It cannot: the comparison that nds starts is padded
at both ends of every row, so each row begins and ends with a boundary and the last run of a row
is always closed before the next row's rst run begins.
Background: what counting occurrences means hereThe counting step uses a standard
operation that, given a list of non-negative whole numbers, returns how many times each value
occurs. Applied to an image of run numbers it gives the size of every run in one pass, which is
why no loop over runs is needed.
4.11 Otsu
def Otsu (v , hi = None )
Inputs
van array of pixel values, either whole numbers or fractions
hiwhen the input is fractional, the largest value worth considering; defaults to 1.2, which suits
the ratio image produced byInkMaskbecause nothing above that carries ink
Output.A threshold. For whole-number input it is a brightness level from 0 to 255; for fractional
input it is a value in the input's own units. Pixels below the threshold are ink.
What it is forDeciding, for a given image, which brightness separates ink from paper. The
point of computing it rather than xing it is that no single number works for two photographs:
one taken in daylight and one under a lamp have completely dierent brightness ranges, and a
xed threshold would classify the whole of one of them as paper.
Background: what a histogram isA histogram counts how many pixels fall at each bright-
ness level, ignoring where they are. For a page of handwriting it usually has two humps: a large
one at the bright end for the paper and a small one at the dark end for the ink, with a valley
between them. The threshold should sit in that valley.
32

=== PDF PAGE 34 ===
The idea: measure the quality of every candidate thresholdOtsu's method [3] turns the
choice into something measurable. Any candidate threshold divides the pixels into two groups. A
good threshold is one where the two groups are as dierent from each other as possible, which is
measured by the variancebetweenthe groups:
σ2
B(t) =ω B(t)ω F (t)

µB(t)−µ F (t)
2 (4.9)
Source:[3]
where
σ2
B(t)
the between-class variance, the quantity being maximised
tthe candidate threshold
ωB(t)
the fraction of all pixels at or below t, that is the share in the darker group
ωF (t)
the fraction above t, so the two fractions add to 1
µB(t)
the mean brightness of the darker group
µF (t)
the mean brightness of the brighter group
Reading the formula: the squared bracket is how far apart the two group means are, so it rewards
a threshold that genuinely separates dark from bright. The product of the two fractions in
front is largest when the groups are of comparable size and falls to zero when either is empty,
which prevents the degenerate answer of putting the threshold at one extreme and calling almost
everything one group.
Why maximising this is the right objectiveThe total variance of the image is a xed
property of the image and does not depend on the threshold. It can be split exactly into the
variance within the two groups plus the variance between them. Since the total is xed, making
the between-group part as large as possible is the same as making the within-group part as small
as possible, that is, choosing the threshold that makes each group internally as uniform as it can
be. The between-group form is used because it needs only running totals, where the within-group
form would need a second pass over the data for every candidate.
How all 256 candidates are evaluated at onceWriting p for the histogram scaled so that
its entries sum to one, the two quantities needed are themselves cumulative sums, and the criterion
rearranges into a form built entirely from them:
σ2
B(t) =

µT ω(t)−µ(t)
2
ω(t)
 
1−ω(t)
 (4.10)
Source:[3]
where
ω(t)the cumulative sum of p up to t, the share of pixels at or below t
33

=== PDF PAGE 35 ===
µ(t)the cumulative sum of p weighted by brightness, the contribution of those pixels to the mean
µT the mean brightness of the whole image, which is the nal value of the weighted cumulative
sum
Both cumulative sums are computed once, the expression is evaluated for every t simultaneously
as array arithmetic, and the largest entry is the answer. No loop over thresholds is needed.
The guard in the denominatorAt the two extreme thresholds one group is empty, so the
product in the denominator is exactly zero. Dividing by it would give innity, and since the
method picks the largest value those positions would always win. The denominator is therefore
oored at a very small positive number, which leaves every genuine candidate unchanged and
makes the degenerate ones lose.
Why whole numbers and fractions are handled separatelyA whole-number image has
exactly 256 possible values, so its histogram can be counted exactly and the answer returned as
a brightness level. A fractional image, such as the ratio of each pixel to its local background, has
no natural set of levels, so its values are clipped athiand sorted into 256 bins, and the answer is
returned as the value at the centre of the chosen bin rather than as a bin number.
4.12 BinarizeLine
def B i n a r i z e L i n e ( gray , s t r i p R u l e s = True , c l os eG ap s = True )
Inputs
gray
a greyscale image of one line of writing
Output.A mask of the ink in that crop, with small holes closed, specks removed and printed
rules deleted.
What it is forThe standard way of turning a grey line crop into ink. The glyph extractor uses
it before tracing strokes, and the synthesiser uses it to measure its own rendered output with the
same code that measured the writer's real pages, which is what makes the two comparable.
The four steps and why each is needed Threshold.Otsuchooses the level, so nothing
about the brightness of this particular photograph is assumed.
Close small holes.Thresholding a photograph leaves single-pixel holes inside strokes where the
paper texture shows through. These matter more than their size suggests, because the next stage
after binarising is usually thinning, and thinning preserves the topology of the shape it is given:
a hole in a stroke forces the resulting centreline to split and run round both sides of it, producing
a small loop that was never written.
Remove specks.Pixels are grouped into connected components and any group too small to be
a pen mark is deleted as dust or camera noise. The decision is made for a whole group at once,
so a letter is never partly removed.
34

=== PDF PAGE 36 ===
Delete printed rules.Horizontal runs longer than a limit are removed, on the reasoning given
underLongRunMask: a pen stroke does not run level for that distance, and a printed rule does
nothing else.
4.13 CoreBand
def CoreBand ( ink )
Inputs
inka mask of one line of writing
Output.A pair of row indices marking the top and the baseline of the x-height band, or nothing
at all if there is no ink or the band is under three rows tall.
Background: what the x-height isIn any piece of writing, most lower case letters have a
body of roughly the same height, such as a, c, e, m and o. The height of that body, measured
up from the line the writing sits on, is called the x-height, after the letter x. Some letters reach
above it, such as b, d and l, and these are called ascenders; some reach below the line, such as g,
p and y, and these are called descenders.
What it is for, and why it is so importantThe x-height is the unit of measurement for
the entire pipeline. Every stored letter shape is divided by it, so that a letter extracted from a
photograph at one resolution can be drawn at any physical size. Every proportion in a style prole
is expressed as a multiple of it: how far ascenders reach, how far descenders drop, how wide the
gaps between words are, how thick the pen is. Without a reliable x-height none of those numbers
would be comparable between two writers or between two photographs of the same writer.
How it worksCounting the ink in each row of the crop gives a one-dimensional summary called
a projection prole. Rows that cross the bodies of the letters contain ink from nearly every letter
on the line; rows that contain only ascenders or only descenders contain ink from the few letters
that have them. The band is therefore the set of rows whose ink count is a large fraction of the
maximum:
B=
n
y: prof[y]≥0.45 max
y′
prof[y ′]
o
(4.11)
where
Bthe set of rows making up the band; its rst and last members are returned
prof[y]
the number of ink pixels in row y
0.45the fraction of the peak that a row must reach to count as part of the band
35

=== PDF PAGE 37 ===
Where the 0.45 comes fromIt is the one tuned constant, and the reasoning behind it is that
the quantity being thresholded has a wide gap in it. A row through the letter bodies collects ink
from most letters on the line, while a row above the band collects ink only from the few ascenders,
which in ordinary English text is a small minority of letters. Any threshold within that gap gives
the same answer, so the exact gure is not critical; what would fail is a value low enough to admit
the ascender rows, which would inate the measured x-height, or high enough to admit only the
single densest row, which would collapse the band to nothing.
The rejection caseA band under three rows tall is reported as a failure rather than returned.
That is what happens when a crop contains a stray mark rather than a line of writing, and
returning a nonsensical x-height would propagate into every measurement taken from that crop.
4.14 LabelComponents
def L a b e l C o m p o n e n t s ( mask )
Inputs
mask
a two-dimensional mask of ink
Output.A pair: an array in which every ink pixel carries the number of the object it belongs
to, counting from 1, with 0 for background; and the number of objects found.
Background: what a connected component isTwo ink pixels that touch belong to the
same physical mark. Following that relation outwards from any pixel gives a group of pixels
all connected to one another, called a connected component. On a page of handwriting the
components are the individual pen marks: a letter, the dot of an i, a speck of dust, or several
joined letters where the writer did not lift the pen.
What counts as touching has to be decided. Counting only the four pixels above, below, left
and right is called four-connectivity; also counting the four diagonals is eight-connectivity. This
implementation uses eight, because a thin pen stroke drawn at an angle advances diagonally, and
under four-connectivity such a stroke would break into a chain of separate dots.
What it is forThis is what lets the pipeline reason about objects rather than pixels, and that
distinction runs through the whole segmenter. Whether a mark is dust is a question about a
whole object; which line of writing a letter belongs to is a question about a whole object. Tests
made pixel by pixel would remove half a letter, or split one letter across two lines.
How it works: runs instead of pixelsEach row is rst reduced to horizontal runs of ink,
found from the places where the row steps from clear to set and back again. Every pixel in a run
necessarily belongs to the same component, so the algorithm reasons about runs, of which there
are far fewer. This is the run-based two-scan approach [4].
Each run is compared against the runs of the row above. Because the runs in both rows are
already in left-to-right order, a single index walks forward through the previous row and never
goes back, so the total work is proportional to the number of runs rather than to their number
squared. A run above counts as touching when its span overlaps, and the comparison is written
so that a touch at a single corner counts, which is what produces eight-connectivity.
36

=== PDF PAGE 38 ===
The problem that makes this non-trivialLabels must be handed out while scanning down-
wards, before it is known how a shape continues. A letter U is the clearest example: its two arms
are separate for many rows and receive dierent labels, and only at the bottom does a run join
them, revealing that the two labels were always one object.
Union-nd, and why it is fastRather than going back and relabelling, which would mean
rescanning everything above, the fact that two labels are the same is recorded. Each label stores
a pointer to another label, and following the pointers reaches a representative for the group;
joining two groups is then one assignment, pointing the larger representative at the smaller. This
structure is called union-nd [5].
Chains of pointers could grow long, so each lookup shortens the chain it walks by pointing every
label it passes at its grandparent:
while parent [ a ] != a :
parent [ a ] = parent [ parent [ a ]] # halve the chain
a = parent [ a ]
With that shortening the cost of a lookup is so close to constant that for any practical image it
can be treated as constant [5].
The nal renumbering, and why callers depend on itBecause labels were handed out
before the merges were known, the surviving labels have gaps: label 7 may have been absorbed into
label 3, so no pixel carries 7 any more. A closing pass resolves every label to its representative
and renumbers the survivors consecutively from 1. Callers rely on this, because it means the
components are numbered 1 to n with nothing missing, so their sizes can be counted in a single
operation and used directly as array indices.
4.15 PieceBounds
def P i e c e B o u n d s ( labelAt , ys , xs , n , H , W )
Inputs
plthe component number of every ink pixel
ysthe row coordinate of every ink pixel
xsthe column coordinate of every ink pixel
nchow many components there are
Hthe image height
Wthe image width
Output.Five arrays indexed by component number: each object's topmost row, bottommost
row, leftmost column, rightmost column and pixel count.
What it is forEvery ownership rule in the segmenter asks geometric questions about objects:
how tall is this piece, how wide is it, where does it sit, how much ink does it contain. This answers
all of them for every object at once.
37

=== PDF PAGE 39 ===
Why it is written as a scatter rather than a loopThe obvious implementation loops over
components and, for each one, selects the pixels belonging to it and takes their minimum and
maximum. That reads the whole image once per component, so on a page with eight hundred
pen marks it reads the image eight hundred times.
Instead the coordinates are scattered into per-component accumulators in a single pass, using
operations that take a list of destinations and a list of values and apply a minimum or a maximum
at each destination. The pixel count comes from the same occurrence-counting operation used by
RowRunLength. The result is one pass over the ink rather than one pass per object.
4.16 Skeletonize
def S k e l e t o n i z e ( mask )
Inputs
mask
a mask of ink
Output.A mask of the one-pixel-wide centreline of that ink.
What it is forA photograph records a pen stroke as a thick band of dark pixels. What the
project needs is the path the pen travelled down the middle of that band, because a path can be
stored as a short list of points, compared against another writing of the same letter, and above
all redrawn by a machine holding a pen. Thinning recovers it.
Background: what thinning must preserveDeleting pixels from the edge of a shape re-
peatedly will eventually make it thin, but done naively it destroys the shape: an o can be broken
open into a c, and a crossing can fall apart into four disconnected arms. A thinning algorithm
must therefore delete only pixels whose removal cannot change how the shape is connected. The
algorithm here is the standard parallel method of Zhang and Suen [6].
The two quantities the test is built fromConsider a pixel and its eight neighbours, num-
bered clockwise from the one directly above. Two numbers describe the local situation:
B(P1) =
9X
k=2
Pk, A(P 1) = #

k:P k = 0∧P k+1 = 1
	
(4.12)
Source:[6]
where

P2 . . . P9

B(P1)
how many of the eight neighbours are ink
38

=== PDF PAGE 40 ===
A(P1)
how many times the neighbours change from empty to ink while walking once round the
ring, the ring being treated as closed so that the ninth neighbour is the second again
The second quantity deserves an explanation, because it is the heart of the method. Walking
once round the eight neighbours and counting the steps from empty into ink tells you how many
separate groups of ink surround the pixel. If the answer is one, all the neighbouring ink forms
a single unbroken arc, so the pixel lies on the edge of the stroke and deleting it leaves that arc
joined. If the answer is two or more, the pixel sits between separate groups and is the only thing
holding them together, so deleting it would break the stroke apart or open a hole.
The deletion test

Source:[6]
where
(a)at least two neighbours, so the tip of a stroke is kept and the line is not eaten back from
its end; at most six, so a pixel deep inside a thick region is not removed before the layers
outside it
(b)the neighbouring ink forms a single arc, so removal cannot disconnect anything
(c),(d)
the corner conditions, which restrict this sub-iteration to thinning from particular sides; in
the second sub-iteration they become P2P4P8 = 0 and P2P6P8 = 0
Why there must be two alternating sub-iterationsIf deletion were allowed from every side
at once, a line two pixels wide would vanish completely: both halves satisfy the test simultaneously
and both are deleted in the same pass, leaving nothing behind. Alternating the corner conditions
means each pass thins from opposite sides, so one side is always held while the other is removed.
The result stays connected and the centreline does not drift to one side of the original stroke.
T ermination and costThe pair of sub-iterations repeats until a full pass deletes nothing, at
which point no pixel can be removed without changing the connectivity, and the shape is one
pixel wide everywhere. The number of passes is proportional to the thickest stroke, since one
layer comes o each side per pass, and each pass is a xed number of whole-array operations.
This is also whyBinarizeLinecloses small holes before thinning. Thinning preserves connec-
tivity faithfully, including the connectivity of a hole that was never written: a pinhole inside
a stroke forces the centreline to divide and pass round both sides of it, leaving a small loop in
the recorded pen path that the writer never drew.
4.17 _Weights
def _ Weights ( inSize , outSize , kernel )
39

Inputs
inSize
the length of the axis before resizing
outSize
the length wanted afterwards
kernel

Output.A matrix with one row per output sample and one column per input sample; each row
holds the weight of every input sample in that output sample, and each row sums to one.
Background: what resampling actually isChanging the size of an image means asking for
brightness values at positions where there were none. In an output image half the width, output
pixel 1 sits between input pixels 2 and 3, so its value has to be built from the inputs around it.
Choosing how to build it is choosing a lter, and a lter is described by a weight for each nearby
input sample.
What it is forEvery size change in the pipeline goes through one resampler, so there is exactly
one piece of code to get right: reducing a page for the skew search, tting a crop to the networks'
xed input size, and rendering glyphs. This function builds the weights andResizeapplies them.
Where an output sample sits
s= inSize

 
i+ 1
2

s(4.14)
Source:[7]
where
sthe scale factor; greater than one means the image is being shrunk
ci the centre of output sample i, expressed in input coordinates
ithe index of the output sample, counting from zero
The halves matter. A pixel is not a point at an integer coordinate; it is a small square whose
centre lies at an integer plus a half. Treating pixels as points displaces the whole image by half a
pixel, which shows as a slight softening and as a systematic shift that accumulates if an image is
resized more than once.
Why the lter widens when shrinkingThis is the part that is easy to get wrong and
expensive to get wrong. The width of the lter is multiplied by the scale factor whenever that
factor exceeds one:
f= max(1, s),support =k 0 f(4.15)
Source:[7]
where
40

=== PDF PAGE 42 ===
fthe widening factor: one when magnifying, equal to the scale factor when minifying
sthe scale factor dened above
k0 the base support of the chosen kernel: one for the triangle, one half for the box
support
how far the lter reaches from its centre, measured in input samples
If the lter did not widen, shrinking by a factor of four would consult one input sample in every
four and ignore the rest. Thin strokes falling between the consulted samples would disappear
entirely, and ne texture would turn into coarse false patterns, an eect called aliasing. Widening
the lter in step with the reduction means every input sample contributes to some output sample
[7]. The page reduction used by the skew search shrinks by exactly such a factor, so this is load
bearing rather than theoretical.
The two kernels
wlinear(t) =
(
1− |t| |t|<1
0otherwise wbox(t) =
(
1|t| ≤ 1
2
0otherwise (4.16)
Source:[7]
where
tthe distance from the centre of the output sample's window to an input sample, measured
in lter widths
w(t)the weight that input sample receives
The triangle weights nearby samples more than distant ones and falls smoothly to zero, which
gives a smooth result. The box weights everything inside the window equally, which is exactly a
local average and is the right choice when reducing by a whole number, because then each output
pixel covers a whole number of input pixels.
Edges, and why weights are accumulated rather than assignedA window near the
border reaches past the end of the axis. Those out-of-range positions are folded back onto the
rst or last sample, so several positions in one window can refer to the same input sample. Their
weights must therefore be added together rather than written one over another: assigning would
keep only the last and discard the rest, leaving the edge of the image too dark.
Each row is nally divided by its own total so that it sums to one. That is what guarantees a
uniform area keeps its brightness after resizing, and in particular that the white padding around
a tted crop does not come back grey.
4.18 Resize
def Resize ( arr , newW , newH , kernel = ' linear ')
Inputs
imgthe image to resize
41

=== PDF PAGE 43 ===
newW
the width wanted
newH
the height wanted
kernel
which lter to use
Output.The resampled image at the requested size, in the same number format as the input.
What it is forThe single resampling routine used by the whole pipeline.
Background: what separable means, and why it is worth itResizing in two dimensions
could be done with a two-dimensional lter, where each output pixel is a weighted sum over a
square of input pixels. For a lter k samples across that is k squared multiplications per output
pixel.
A lter is called separable when its two-dimensional weight is the product of two one-dimensional
weights, one per axis. Both kernels used here are separable, so the same result is obtained by
resizing along the rows and then along the columns, at a cost of 2k multiplications per pixel
instead of k squared [7]. At the lter widths used when reducing a page that is roughly an order
of magnitude less work.
How it works_Weightsis called once per axis and the two matrices are applied in turn as
matrix products, so the arithmetic happens inside numpy's compiled routines rather than in
interpreted loops.
4.19 Halve
def Halve ( a )
Inputs
imgthe image to reduce
Output.The image at half the width and half the height.
What it is forA fast reduction for stages that do not need full resolution: nding the sheet of
paper inside the photograph, and the coarse stage of the skew search.
Why it is a separate functionAt the exact ratio of two to one a box lter reduces to averaging
each two-by-two block, which can be done by reshaping the array and taking a mean, with no
weights built at all. It is both the cheapest correct reduction at that ratio and exactly what the
general resampler would compute, so nothing is lost by using it.
Why reduce at all: every operation in the toolkit costs time proportional to the number of pixels,
so halving each axis quarters the work. For a decision that only needs to locate something to
within a few pixels, the reduction costs nothing in accuracy.

Taking the smaller of the two ratios means the crop is scaled until whichever axis runs out of

vertical position of a line of writing inside its crop carries none, so centring removes a dierence
the network would otherwise have to learn to ignore.

A small implementation guardThe scaled size is rounded to whole pixels, and rounding can
produce a result one pixel larger than the canvas. The nal copy therefore limits both the source
and the destination, so an overshoot is trimmed instead of raising an error.
4.21 _Sample
def _ Sample (a , sx , sy , fill , edge = ' area ')
Inputs
imgthe source image
syfor each output pixel, the row to read from, which may fall between rows
sxthe corresponding column, which may fall between columns
Output.The sampled values, one per requested position.
What it is forRotating or bending an image asks for brightness at positions falling between
pixels. This supplies them, and is the shared sampler behind every geometric transform in the
le.
Background: bilinear interpolationThe value at a position between four pixels is their
weighted average, each weight given by how close the position is to that pixel. It is computed one
axis at a time: blend the upper pair horizontally, blend the lower pair horizontally, then blend
those two results vertically.
vtop =p 00 (1−f x) +p 01 fx
vbot =p 10 (1−f x) +p 11 fx
v=v top (1−f y) +v bot fy
(4.18)
Source:[7]
where
p00, p 01
the two source pixels above the position, left and right
p10, p 11
the two below it
fx how far the position lies between the left and right columns: 0 at the left pixel, 1 at the
right
fy the same fraction between the upper and lower rows
vthe interpolated value returned
Why interpolate at allThe cheaper alternative is to take whichever single pixel the position is
nearest. On a rotated image the result is a visible staircase along every edge, because neighbouring
output pixels jump between dierent source pixels. Blending removes it, and that matters here
beyond appearance: the ink masks and skeletons computed downstream are sensitive to jagged
edges, which produce spurious spurs when thinned.

=== PDF PAGE 46 ===
4.22 _Like
def _ Like ( out , like )
Inputs
outa oating point result
refthe array whose number format should be matched
Output.The result converted back to the reference's format, rounded and clipped to its range.
What it is forInterpolation must be done in oating point, but the pipeline passes images
around as whole numbers from 0 to 255, because that is what a photograph is and what the
networks were trained on. This performs the conversion back.
The two things it gets rightIt rounds rather than truncating. Truncation would bias every
transformed image slightly darker, since 254.7 would become 254 rather than 255, and the bias
would accumulate through successive transforms.
It clips to the valid range. A blend can overshoot slightly past 255 or below 0, and converting
such a value to an 8-bit whole number without clipping makes it wrap around to the opposite
extreme, turning a bright pixel black. A handful of such pixels is enough to disturb a threshold
or a component count.
4.23 Rotate
def Rotate ( gray , deg , fill =255)
Inputs
imgthe image to rotate
degthe angle in degrees
fill
the value used where no source pixel exists
Output.The rotated image, on a canvas the same size as the input.
What it is forStraightening a photographed page. The page is never square to the camera,
and a tilt of even one degree means a horizontal cut through the image crosses more than one line
of writing, which makes every later stage harder.
Background: forward and inverse mappingThere are two ways to transform an image.
The obvious one, called forward mapping, takes each input pixel, works out where it should land,
and writes it there. The problem is that the destinations are fractional and unevenly spread, so
some output pixels receive two input pixels and others receive none, leaving a scatter of holes.
The method used here is inverse mapping: for eachoutputpixel, work out where it came from
in the input and read that position. Every output pixel is then visited exactly once, so holes are
impossible [7]. The source position is fractional, which is what_Sampleis for.
45

=== PDF PAGE 47 ===
The mapping
sx = cosθ(x−c x) + sinθ(y−c y) +c x
sy =−sinθ(x−c x) + cosθ(y−c y) +c y
(4.19)
Source:[7]
where
x, ythe output pixel being lled
sx, s y
the position in the input it is read from
cx, c y
the centre of the image, which the rotation is about
θthe rotation angle in radians
Note that the signs are those of a rotation by minus theta: because the mapping runs backwards
from output to input, it must be the inverse of the rotation being applied.
Filling rather than clampingA source position outside the image is lled with the paper
value rather than clamped to the nearest edge pixel. Clamping would smear whatever lies on the
boundary across the whole corner, producing streaks that the ink detector would then have to
cope with.
The edge convention, and why it was determined experimentallyDeciding whether a
source position lies inside the image is less obvious than it looks. If a pixel is a point at an integer
coordinate, the image spans 0 to size minus 1. If a pixel is a unit square centred on its index,
the image spans minus a half to size minus a half. This implementation uses the second, area,
convention.
The convention was not guessed. Replacing a library rotation with this one was veried by
comparing the two on a test page: with the area convention the two agreed to a maximum
dierence of one grey level out of 255, while the other plausible convention diered by up to
245 along the edges. A dierence of that size is not a rounding detail, it is a row of pixels in
the wrong place.
4.24 Warp
def Warp ( gray , coeffs , fill =255)
Inputs
imgthe source image
coeffs
six numbers for an ane transform, or eight for a perspective one
fill
the background value
Output.The transformed image.
46

=== PDF PAGE 48 ===
What it is forOne function covering every straight-line-preserving transform the pipeline
needs: scaling, shearing, rotation, and the keystone eect of a page photographed from an angle.
It is used mainly to generate training variations of a crop.
Background: ane and perspectiveAn ane transform is any combination of scaling,
rotation, shear and translation. It keeps parallel lines parallel, so a rectangle becomes a parallel-
ogram. A perspective transform, also called a homography, additionally allows parallel lines to
converge, so a rectangle becomes any four-sided shape. That is what happens when a at page is
photographed obliquely: the far edge appears shorter than the near one.
The mapping
sx = a x+b y+c
g x+h y+ 1 , s y = d x+e y+f
g x+h y+ 1 (4.20)
Source:[7]
where
x, ythe output pixel being lled
sx, s y
the source position it is read from
a, b, d, e
the linear part: scaling, rotation and shear
c, fthe translation
g, hthe perspective part; both zero gives a purely ane transform, since the divisor is then 1
everywhere
The divisor is what distinguishes the two cases. When g and h are zero it equals one at every
pixel and the mapping is linear; otherwise it varies across the image, so equal steps in the output
correspond to unequal steps in the input, which is exactly the foreshortening that perspective
produces.
The half-pixel conventionThe coecients act on thecentreof the output pixel, so a half
is added to the output coordinate before the mapping is applied and subtracted from the result
afterwards. This follows from the same pixels-are-squares reasoning as in_Weights.
Like the rotation convention, this was settled by measurement rather than argument: with the
half-pixel osets in place the output matched the library implementation it replaced on zero
pixels of dierence across a test page, and without them the entire image was displaced by half
a pixel.
4.25 Blur
def Blur ( gray , sigma , mode = ' edge ')
Inputs
imgthe image to blur
47

=== PDF PAGE 49 ===
sigma
the standard deviation of the gaussian, in pixels, which sets how far the blurring reaches
Output.The blurred image, in the input's number format.
What it is forTwo uses. It smooths the random displacement elds used for training augmen-
tation, which is what turns random noise into a coherent bending of the strokes. And it softens
the edge of synthesised ink, so that a drawn stroke has the gradual edge of a real pen rather than a
hard step, which matters because the networks judging that output were trained on photographs
of real ink.
Background: what a gaussian blur isEach output pixel is a weighted average of its neigh-
bourhood, with weights following the bell-shaped gaussian curve: strongest at the centre and
falling away smoothly with distance. Compared with a at average it produces no visible edge at
the limit of the window, because the weights reach zero gradually.
Why it is done as two one-dimensional passesA two-dimensional gaussian factorises ex-
actly into the product of two one-dimensional gaussians:
G(x, y) = exp


So blurring down the rows and then across the columns gives exactly the same answer as a single
two-dimensional pass, at a cost of 2k multiplications per pixel instead of k squared. This is the
same separability argument as forResize.
T runcation and normalisationThe gaussian never reaches zero, so the kernel is cut o at
about three standard deviations, where under one per cent of the total weight remains. The re-
maining weights are then divided by their sum so they total one, which keeps the overall brightness
of the image unchanged; without that, blurring would darken the image by the fraction of weight
that was discarded.
4.26 Elastic

person writing the same words again, which is not a rotation or a scaling but a local bending of
the strokes [8].

Why the smoothing is the essential stepWithout the blur, each pixel would move inde-

into disconnected dots. Blurring the displacement eld means nearby pixels move by nearly the
same amount, so a stroke moves as a stroke, bending and stretching while staying continuous. The
width of the blur sets the scale of the bending: small gives wobbly strokes, large gives a gentle
warp of the whole crop.
The two parameters interact. A large displacement with a small blur tears the writing apart; a
large displacement with a large blur is nearly a rigid shift. Useful augmentation lives between
those extremes.
4.27 DrawPolyline
def D r a w P o l y l i n e ( img , pts , value , width )
49

=== PDF PAGE 51 ===
Inputs
imgthe array to draw into, which is modied in place
ptsthe path as a list of (x, y) points
value
the ink level to write, 0 being black
width
the pen width in pixels
Output.The same array, returned for convenience when chaining calls.
What it is forDrawing a pen stroke into an image. It is used to render synthesised handwriting
so that the networks can judge it, to redraw ink at a uniform width inside stroke normalisation,
and in the measurement harness to compare two sets of geometry. Because all three use the same
function, they all see ink laid down the same way.
The idea: ll by distance rather than by stepping along the lineThe familiar way to
draw a line is to step along it lling pixels. That is fast but makes a round pen awkward, because
the ends and the corners between segments have to be handled separately or they come out square
and notched.
Instead, every pixel near the segment is tested by how far it lies from the nearest pointonthe
segment, and lled if that distance is within half the pen width. The nearest point is found by
projecting onto the segment and clamping to its ends:
t= clamp
(P−A)·(B−A)
∥B−A∥ 2 ,0,1

, Q=A+t(B−A)(4.23)
where
A, B
the two ends of the segment being drawn
Pthe pixel being tested
thow far along the segment the nearest point lies, 0 at A and 1 at B
Qthe nearest point on the segment to P
(P−A)·(B−A)
the dot product, which measures how far along the segment's direction P lies
A pixel is then lled when the distance from P to Q is at most half the pen width.
Why the clamp gives round ends and joins for freeWithout the clamp, t could fall outside
the range 0 to 1 and the distance would be measured to the innite line through A and B, so ink
would extend beyond the ends of the segment. Clamping means that for a pixel beyond an end,
the nearest point is the end itself, so the lled region there is a disc of radius half the pen width.
That is a round cap. At a corner between two segments both discs overlap, which is a round join.
No separate handling is needed for either.
50

=== PDF PAGE 52 ===
Why it is aordableTesting every pixel in the image against every segment would be hopeless.
Work is restricted to the bounding box of each segment, expanded by the pen radius, since no pixel
outside that box can possibly be within half a pen width of it. The cost is therefore proportional
to the amount of ink drawn rather than to the size of the canvas.
A segment of zero length, which happens when a path contains a repeated point or consists of a
single point, falls back to plain distance from that point, which draws a dot.
4.28 EstimateSlantDeg
def E s t i m a t e S l a n t D e g ( ink )
Inputs
inka mask of one line of writing
Output.The slant in degrees, positive for the usual forward lean. Returns zero when there are
fewer than fty ink pixels.
What it is forTwo things. The glyph extractor removes the slant before storing a letter, so
that stored letters are upright and can be compared with one another; and the synthesiser re-
applies the writer's own slant when drawing, because how far somebody leans their writing is one
of the most visible features of their hand.
Background: what shearing isShearing slides each row of an image sideways by an amount
proportional to its height, which tilts upright strokes without changing the height of anything.
It is the operation that turns upright writing into slanted writing, so undoing it is how slanted
writing is made upright.
The method: try angles and score the resultFor each candidate angle the ink is sheared
by that amount and the ink in each column is counted, giving a column prole. The angle whose
prole is thesharpestis the answer [9]:
x′ =x−tan(θ) (y−¯y),score(θ) =
X
j
prof θ[j]2 (4.24)
Source:[9]
where
x, ythe coordinates of an ink pixel
x′ its column after shearing by the trial angle
¯ythe mean row of all the ink, which is the height the shear pivots about
θthe trial angle
prof θ[j]
