51

=== PDF PAGE 53 ===
Why squaring the prole measures sharpnessThis is the key step and it is worth being
precise about. The total amount of ink does not change when the image is sheared, so the sum
of the prole is the same for every angle; only itsdistributionchanges. For a xed total, the sum
of squares is largest when the quantity is concentrated in a few entries and smallest when it is
spread evenly. Ten pixels in one column score a hundred; one pixel in each of ten columns scores
ten.
So maximising the sum of squares nds the angle at which the ink stacks into the fewest, tallest
columns, which is the angle at which the vertical strokes of the writing are truly vertical. The
same reasoning underliesSkewScore, applied to rows instead of columns.
Why coordinates are sheared rather than the imageThe score needs only the column
each ink pixel lands in, not an image. Shearing the coordinates and counting them is therefore
enough, and it avoids interpolating a whole image once per candidate angle. With sixty candidate
angles that is the dierence between a measurement that is free and one that dominates the
runtime.
Resolution and the signThe search runs from minus 45 to plus 45 degrees in steps of 1.5
degrees, which bounds how precisely the answer can be reported. The result is negated before
being returned so that a positive number means the ordinary forward lean, which makes the
deslanting step read naturally as subtracting the tangent of the slant times the height.
Fewer than fty ink pixels is treated as too little to measure and zero is returned, since on that
much ink the prole is dominated by where the individual pixels happen to fall.

def P o l y l i n e L e n g t h ( p )
Inputs

Output.The total distance walked along the path.
What it is forA size measure for pen strokes. Its main use is rejecting fragments: a piece
cut out of a line of writing that contains far less pen path than a real example of that letter is a
fragment rather than a letter, whatever its bounding box suggests.
How it works
L=

Each term is the straight-line distance between two consecutive points, by Pythagoras, and the
total is their sum. It is computed as whole-array arithmetic: the dierences between consecutive
points, then their lengths, then one sum.
Why path length and not bounding boxA fragment can have a deceptively large bounding
box. Half of a letter o spans nearly the full width and height of the whole letter while containing
only half the ink, so a box-based test would accept it. Path length measures how much pen
movement the shape actually represents, which is what distinguishes half a letter from a whole
one.
4.30 InkMask
def InkMask ( gray )
Inputs
gray
the page as a 2-D array of grey values, 0 for black and 255 for white, held as oating point
numbers so the division below does not round
Output.a pair. The rst item is a boolean array the same shape as the page, true wherever
the pixel is judged to be ink. The second is theratio image, the page divided by its own local
background, which later stages reuse to measure how dark each piece of ink is.
What it is forThis is the rst real decision in the whole pipeline: for every pixel of the
photograph, is this pen or is this paper? Everything downstream, from nding the lines to cutting
out letters, works on the answer. Getting it wrong in one direction loses strokes; getting it wrong
in the other lls the page with shadows and paper texture that then get mistaken for writing.
Background: why a single threshold is not enoughThe obvious method is a global
threshold: pick one grey value, call everything darker than it ink. That works for a scanner,
where the lamp lights the page evenly. It fails badly for a photograph taken with a phone,
because the illumination is not even. One corner of the page sits under a lamp and the opposite
corner sits in shadow, and the page is often slightly curved so the middle is brighter than the
edges. The result is that paper in the bright corner can be lighter than 220 while paper in the
shadowed corner is darker than 150, and real pen ink in the bright corner is around 90. There is
no single number that separates ink from paper everywhere at once: any threshold low enough
to reject the shadowed paper also rejects the pale pen strokes, and any threshold high enough to
catch the pale strokes also catches the shadow.
The idea: divide out the illuminationThe x is to stop asking "is this pixel dark?" and
ask instead "is this pixel darker than the paper immediately around it?". The illumination varies
smoothly across the page, so over a small neighbourhood it is almost constant. If the brightness
of a pixel is modelled as the true reectance of the page at that point multiplied by the local
illumination, then dividing by an estimate of that local illumination cancels it and leaves the
reectance, which is the thing that actually distinguishes pen from paper.
53

=== PDF PAGE 55 ===
ratio[y, x] = gray[y, x]
max
 
BoxMean(gray, r)[y, x],1
 (4.26)
where
ratio[y, x]
the illumination-corrected value at this pixel; near 1 for paper, well below 1 for ink
gray[y, x]
the measured grey value at this pixel
BoxMean(gray, r)
the average grey value over a square window of radius r centred on each pixel, which serves
as the estimate of the local illumination; see the entry for BoxMean
rthe radius of that window, in pixels
max(·,1)
a guard so that a window which happens to average to zero cannot cause a division by zero
A ratio of 1 means the pixel is exactly as bright as its surroundings, which is what paper looks
like. A ratio of 0.4 means the pixel is well under half as bright as the paper around it, which
is what a pen stroke looks like. The key property is that this is now true in the bright corner
and the shadowed corner alike, because the shadow divides out of both the numerator and the
denominator.
Choosing the window size, and the trap in itThe radius has to satisfy two opposing
requirements. It must be large enough that the window is mostly paper even when it is centred
on a stroke, because the denominator is supposed to estimate the paper brightness, not the ink.
If the radius were the width of a pen stroke, then a window centred inside a thick stroke would be
entirely ink, the estimated background would be as dark as the ink itself, the ratio would come
out near 1, and the stroke would be declared paper. That failure has a name in this le: it is
the "solid ink" case handled below. The radius must also be small enough that the illumination
really is roughly constant across the window, which rules out making it enormous.

Scaling the radius with the image height rather than xing it is what lets the same code handle
a 1000-pixel-tall photograph and a 4000-pixel-tall one. A stroke in the larger photograph is four
times as many pixels wide, so the window that must exceed it has to grow in step.
54

=== PDF PAGE 56 ===
T urning the ratio into a yes or noThe ratio image still has continuous values and a decision
is needed. Otsu's method supplies the threshold, and it is unusually well suited here. Otsu's
method assumes the values fall into two groups and picks the cut that separates them best in
a precise statistical sense; see the entry for Otsu for the derivation. After the division the ratio
image genuinely does have two groups, because the illumination that was smearing them together
has been removed: a tall narrow spike just below 1 for the paper, and a much smaller spread of
low values for the ink. A pixel is ink when its ratio is below the threshold Otsu returns.
mask[y, x] =


(4.28)

the threshold Otsu's method returns for the ratio image, that is, the value that best splits
its two groups
[·]square brackets around a condition mean 1 or true when the condition holds and 0 or false
when it does not
Note that the threshold is computed from the ratio image, not from the original grey image.
This is the whole point of the two-step design: a global method like Otsu's, which cannot adapt
to position, is made to work on a photograph by rst removing the position-dependent part of
the signal.
The solid-ink correctionOne class of object defeats the division: a region of darkness much
larger than the background window. The spine of a ring binder, a deep shadow along a fold, or
the black edge of the desk behind the page are all far wider than 40 pixels. Inside such a region
the window used for the denominator is entirely dark, so the estimated background is dark too,
the ratio comes back near 1, and the interior is correctly rejected. The problem is the boundary,
where the window straddles light and dark and the ratio dips, marking a band of false ink around
every large dark object.
These regions are found by their dening property: they are completely lled. Writing is not. A
pen stroke is thin, so any small window placed on the page contains some paper even when it
contains a stroke; a window inside a binder shadow contains nothing but shadow. A veraging the
mask itself measures exactly this ll fraction.

4the window radius, giving a 9 by 9 window: small enough that no ordinary pen stroke can

55

=== PDF PAGE 57 ===
0.999
eectively a demand that every pixel in the window be ink; it is not written as 1.0 only to
be safe against oating-point rounding in the average
The agged cores are then grown slightly, with a second averaging test, and subtracted from the
mask. Growing them is necessary because the false-ink band lies just outside the solid core, not
inside it, so removing only the core would leave the band behind, which is the artefact being
targeted.
Why the ratio image is returned as wellLater stages need to ask how dark a piece of ink
is, in order to tell a real pen stroke from a faint printed rule or a crease. The original grey values
cannot answer that, because they still contain the illumination. The ratio image can, and it is
already computed, so it is passed out rather than recomputed. DropArtifactPieces uses it for
precisely this test.
4.31 PaperRegionMask
def P a p e r R e g i o n M a s k (g , P )
Inputs
gthe page as a 2-D grey array in oating point
Pthe line pitch in pixels, that is, the typical distance between consecutive lines of writing; see
LinePitch
Output.a boolean array the size of the page, true inside the sheet of paper and false outside it,
shrunk slightly inward from the true paper edge.
What it is forA photograph of a page is rarely just the page. Around it there is desk, keyboard,
hand, shadow and the dark gap at the edge of the sheet. None of that is writing, but much of it
is darker than the paper and therefore survives into the ink mask. This function nds the outline
of the sheet so that everything outside it can be discarded in one move, before any eort is spent
trying to interpret it.
The assumption being madeThe method rests on one observation: of everything in the
frame, the sheet of paper is both bright and large. The desk may be bright too, but it surrounds
the page rather than forming one solid region with it, because a visible dark rim separates them,
which is the shadow the sheet casts and the contrast at its cut edge. So the paper is the largest
connectedbright region, where connected means one can walk from any pixel of it to any other
without leaving it.
Step one: work smallThe search is done on a copy of the page reduced by a factor of four in
each direction, by averaging each 4 by 4 block of pixels into one. Two reasons. Speed: a sixteenth
of the pixels means a sixteenth of the work, and the paper outline does not need pixel accuracy to
be useful. Robustness: averaging suppresses noise and small features, so the result is dominated
by the large-scale structure, which is what is being looked for. The reduction uses a block average
rather than taking every fourth pixel, because taking every fourth pixel can land entirely in a gap
and miss a thin dark rim completely, whereas an average over the block always registers it.
56

=== PDF PAGE 58 ===
Step two: nd the dark surround
dark[y, x] =

c[y, x]<0.45·median(c)

(4.30)
where
dark[y, x]
true when this block of the shrunken page is part of the dark surround
cthe shrunken, locally averaged copy of the page
median(c)
the middle value of all the block brightnesses when sorted; it stands in for the brightness of
the paper
0.45how much darker than the paper a block must be before it counts as surround rather than
page
The median is used rather than the mean because it isrobust, meaning that a minority of
extreme values barely moves it. In a photograph where a third of the frame is black desk, the
mean brightness is dragged a long way down by the desk and no longer describes the paper at all,
while the median still sits in the paper, because the paper is the single largest group of similar
values. The threshold is a fraction of that median rather than an absolute grey level, which is
again what makes it work under dierent lighting: in a dim photograph everything scales down
together and the fraction is unchanged.
The no-surround caseIf less than 0.2 percent of the frame comes out dark, the conclusion is
that there is no surround in the picture, because the page lls the whole frame or the image is a
scan rather than a photograph. In that case the entire image is returned as paper. This matters
because the "largest bright region" logic has nothing to nd when there is no dark rim to separate
regions in the rst place, and forcing it to choose would pick an arbitrary piece of the page.
Step three: the largest bright componentConnected-component labelling, described in
the entry for LabelComponents, is run on the inverse of the dark mask, that is, on the bright
blocks. It returns an array in which every bright block carries an integer label, with all the blocks
of one connected region sharing a label. Counting how many blocks carry each label and taking
the largest count identies the sheet.
ℓ∗ = arg max
ℓ≥1
{(y, x) : lab[y, x] =ℓ}
 (4.31)
where
ℓ∗ the label belonging to the sheet of paper
lab[y, x]

57

=== PDF PAGE 59 ===
Step four: back to full sizeThe chosen region is a mask over blocks, and what is needed is
a mask over pixels. Each block stands for a 4 by 4 square of the original, so the block mask is
expanded by replacing every entry with a 4 by 4 square of copies of itself. This is aKronecker
productwith a 4 by 4 block of ones, which is the standard name for exactly that operation,
and in practice it is nearest-neighbour upsampling: a hard-edged enlargement with no smoothing,
which is the correct choice because the value being enlarged is a yes-or-no ag and a smoothed
version of it would be meaningless.
Step ve: shrink inwardFinally the mask is eroded, that is, every pixel within a radius of
the paper boundary is removed from it, using a radius of 0.4 line pitches. The reason is that the
boundary itself is the single least trustworthy part of the estimate. It was found on a coarse grid,
the sheet's edge is darkened by shadow over a band several pixels wide, and the page may curl.
Pulling the accepted region inward by a fraction of a line's height costs nothing, because people
do not write right up to the cut edge of a sheet, and it reliably excludes the edge shadow that
would otherwise be interpreted as a long horizontal stroke.
Expressing the shrink in line pitches rather than pixels is a habit worth noticing, and it recurs
throughout the segmenter. The pitch is the natural unit of the problem: it is set by the size
of the writing, so a quantity measured in pitches means the same thing on a photograph taken
close up as on one taken from further away.
4.32 LinePitch
def Li ne Pi tc h ( mask )
Inputs
mask
the ink mask of the page, a boolean 2-D array
Output.a pair. The rst item is the line pitch, an integer number of pixels giving the typical
vertical distance from one line of writing to the next. The second is the smoothed row prole, an
array with one value per image row, which LineCentres reuses to locate the lines.
What it is for, and why it comes rstAlmost every decision later in the segmenter is a
judgement of size: is this blob too tall to be a letter, is this gap wide enough to be a word break,
is this dot close enough to that stem to belong to it. Expressing such rules in pixels would tie
them to one camera at one distance. Expressing them as fractions of the line pitch makes them
scale-free, because the pitch grows and shrinks with the writing. So the pitch is measured once,
early, and becomes the ruler for everything else. The symbol P is used for it throughout the le.
Background: what a projection prole isAprojection proleis what results from
collapsing a 2-D image onto one axis by summing along the other. The horizontal projection
prole used here has one number per image row: how many ink pixels that row contains.
prof[y] =
X
x
mask[y, x](4.32)
where
58

=== PDF PAGE 60 ===
prof[y]
the prole value for row y, that is, the count of ink pixels in that row
mask[y, x]
1 where the pixel is ink, 0 where it is not
P
x a sum over every column x of that row
The prole of a page of handwriting has a characteristic shape. Rows passing through the bodies
of letters cross nearly every letter on the line and so collect a great deal of ink. Rows in the white
space between two lines cross almost nothing and collect very little. The prole is therefore a run
of peaks separated by troughs, one peak per line of writing, and the distance between consecutive
peaks is the pitch. Using row projections to nd text lines is a long-standing technique in document
analysis [10].
Columns are trimmed before summing: the leftmost 5 percent and everything past 97 percent of
the width are ignored. The margins are where page edges, binder holes and the shadow at the
spine live, and a vertical artefact there contributes to every row equally, which raises the whole
prole and attens the contrast between peak and trough that the method depends on.
Smoothing, and why it is necessaryThe raw prole is jagged. Within one line of writing
the count jumps about from row to row according to which letters happen to have a horizontal
stroke at that exact height, so a single line of text produces not one clean peak but a cluster of
small ones. Counting those as separate lines would be wrong. Smoothing replaces each value with
a weighted average of its neighbours, which merges the cluster into one broad peak while leaving
the large gaps between lines intact.

This operation is aconvolution: slide a small set of weights along a signal, and at each position
output the weighted sum of the values under it. The particular weights here form a Gaussian,
or bell curve, which is the usual choice for smoothing because it is smooth itself and so does
not introduce ripples of its own. A width of 4 rows is chosen to be comparable to the thickness
of a pen stroke, which is the scale of the jaggedness being removed, and much smaller than the
distance between lines, which is the structure being kept.
Method one: the gaps between peaksA row is alocal maximum, or peak, when it is at
least as large as the row above and strictly larger than the row below, and in addition exceeds 6
percent of the largest value anywhere in the prole. The last condition rejects the small bumps
that noise produces in the white space between lines. The asymmetry, at least as large on one
side and strictly larger on the other, is a detail that matters: if both tests were "at least as large",
a at-topped peak several rows wide would report every one of those rows as a separate peak.
With two or more peaks found, the pitch is themedianof the dierences between consecutive
peak positions.

mhow many peaks were found
median

The median rather than the mean, again for robustness. If the page contains a paragraph break,
one of the gaps is two or three times the others. The mean would be pulled upward by that one
value and would overstate the pitch for the whole page; the median ignores it entirely, since it
only cares which value sits in the middle.
Method two: autocorrelationWhen fewer than two peaks are found there are no gaps to
measure, and a dierent line of attack is needed.Autocorrelationasks how much a signal
resembles a shifted copy of itself. For a repeating signal the resemblance is high when the shift
equals the period of the repetition, because peaks then land on peaks.

smthe mean of the smoothed prole
Subtracting the mean is essential. Without it every product in the sum is positive, the autocor-
relation is dominated by the constant part of the signal, and it decreases steadily with lag simply
because fewer terms overlap, so no peak corresponding to the period is visible. After subtraction
the values straddle zero, and the sum is large only when peaks genuinely align with peaks and
troughs with troughs.
The rst clear peak at a lag of at least 15 rows, reaching at least 70 percent of the maximum in
the searched range, is taken as the pitch. The lower bound of 15 skips the trivial peak at zero
lag, where any signal matches itself perfectly, along with the shoulder beside it caused by the
smoothing. The search is also limited to a third of the signal length, because at large lags very
few rows overlap and the estimate becomes noise.
Method three: letter heightIf even that fails, as it does for a crop containing a single line,
the pitch is inferred from the size of the letters themselves. Connected components are labelled,
each one's height is measured, pieces smaller than 8 pixels in area or under 3 pixels tall are
discarded as noise, and the median height of what remains is multiplied by 2.2.
P= max
 
15,round (2.2·median(h))

(4.37)
where
Pthe line pitch in pixels
hthe heights of the accepted connected components
2.2the ratio of line pitch to typical component height observed for ordinary handwriting
15a oor, below which the estimate is treated as unusable
The factor 2.2 comes from the structure of writing. A typical connected piece of ink is roughly one
x-height tall, the x-height being the height of a letter like x or o with no ascender or descender.
A line of writing occupies the x-height plus the ascender zone above it plus the descender zone
below, and lines are separated by a further gap, which together come to a little over twice the
x-height. The estimate is coarse, but it is only reached when the two better methods have already
failed.
The nal fallbackWith no usable ink at all, 48 pixels is returned. This is an arbitrary number
and is not expected to be right. Its purpose is to keep the caller running on a blank page instead
of failing on a division by zero, and a blank page produces no crops regardless of what pitch is
assumed, so the value cannot do any harm.
The cascade of three methods, each a fallback for the last, is worth reading as a design pattern
rather than as clutter. The rst method is the most accurate but needs at least two lines; the
second needs only a repeating pattern; the third needs only some ink. Each is tried in order of
preference, so the accurate method is used whenever it applies and the crude ones only when
nothing better is possible.

4.33 LeastCostSeam
def L e a s t C o s t S e a m ( cost , step =3)
Inputs
cost
a 2-D array in which every entry is the price of passing through that pixel; here, large where
there is ink and small where there is not
step
the largest number of rows the path may move up or down when advancing one column,
default 3
Output.an array with one entry per column, giving the row the path occupies in that column:
the cheapest left-to-right route across the array.
What it is forTwo lines of handwriting have to be separated from one another. A straight
horizontal cut will not do it, because the descender of a g on the upper line hangs below the
ascender of an l on the lower one, and any single row that clears the g also slices the l. What
is needed is a wavy cut that dodges around the ink: it should dip below a descender and rise
above an ascender, as long as it keeps moving left to right. That wavy cut is called aseam, and
nding the cheapest one is what this function does. The technique is seam carving, introduced
for resizing images [11] and adapted to the separation of handwritten text lines [12].
The problem stated preciselyA seam is a choice of one row for each column, with the
restriction that consecutive choices dier by no more thansteprows, which is what makes the
result a connected curve rather than a scatter of disconnected points. Its cost is the sum of the
cost array along it. The task is to nd the seam of least total cost.
Cost(y1, . . . , yW ) =
WX
x=1
cost[yx, x],|y x+1 −y x| ≤step(4.38)
where
yx the row the seam occupies in column x
Wthe number of columns
cost[y, x]
the price of the pixel at row y, column x
stepthe maximum vertical move allowed between neighbouring columns
Why brute force is impossibleCounting the candidates shows why a direct search is out
of the question. With h rows to choose from in the rst column and up to 2·step+1 choices at
each of the remaining W−1 columns, the number of seams is about h(2·step+1)^(W−1). For a
modest page half a size down, with 1200 columns and step 7, that is 15 raised to the power 1199,
a number with over a thousand digits. No computer will enumerate them.
62

=== PDF PAGE 64 ===
The idea that makes it easy: dynamic programming Dynamic programmingis a
method for problems that can be solved by building up the answer one step at a time, where the
best way to nish does not depend on how the start was reached [13]. That condition holds here.
Suppose the cheapest way to reach row y of column x has been computed, for every row y. The
cheapest way to reach a particular row of column x+1 must arrive from one of the rows of column
x that is withinstepof it, and once it is known how cheaply each of those rows can be reached,
the best arrival is just the smallest of those costs plus the price of the new pixel. Nothing about
the earlier part of the route matters beyond its total cost. So the whole column can be solved
from the previous column alone.
A[y, x] = cost[y, x] + min
|d|≤step

A[y−d, x−1] +λ|d|

(4.39)
where
A[y, x]
the accumulated cost: the price of the cheapest route that starts at the left edge and ends
at row y of column x
cost[y, x]
the price of the current pixel
dthe vertical move made to arrive here, so the route came from row y−d of the previous
column
stepthe limit on that move
λa small penalty per row of vertical movement, 0.02 in this implementation
minthe smallest value over all the choices of d
The rst column needs no recursion, since a route that ends there has visited only that one pixel,
so A in column 1 is just the cost in column 1. Every later column follows from its predecessor
by the formula above. The work is one pass over the array, with 2·step+1 comparisons per pixel,
instead of the astronomical enumeration. That is the entire gain of dynamic programming: it
replaces an exponential count of paths with a linear count of pixels.
The movement penaltyThe termλ|d|charges a small amount for each row of vertical move-
ment. Without it, any two routes through equally empty space would cost the same, and the seam
would wander about aimlessly in white space, picking an arbitrary one of the many equally cheap
options. The penalty breaks those ties in favour of going straight, so that the seam moves only
when moving buys it something, namely avoiding ink. At 0.02 per row it is far smaller than the
cost of crossing a stroke, so it never persuades the seam to cut through a letter in order to stay
level; it only decides between routes that are otherwise equivalent. This is aregularisationterm,
which is the general name for a small extra cost added to express a preference among solutions
that the main objective cannot distinguish.
Recovering the pathThe accumulated cost gives the price of the best seam but not its route,
so as each cell is lled the winning move is recorded in a second array. When the pass is complete,
the lowest value in the nal column is the end of the best seam, and following the stored moves
backwards from there reconstructs the whole path, column by column, right to left. This is
backtracking, and it is the standard companion to dynamic programming: the forward pass
computes the value of the best solution, the backward pass reads o what that solution actually
was.
63

=== PDF PAGE 65 ===
How it is made fast in practiceWritten as a loop over rows inside a loop over columns this
would be slow in Python. Instead, for each column, the candidate costs from all 2·step+1 possible
moves are formed as whole shifted copies of the previous column, stacked into a small array, and
the minimum is taken down the stack in one operation. Shifting a column by d rows and adding
the penalty handles every row's d-th candidate at once. Only the loop over columns remains in
Python, because columns are genuinely sequential: column x cannot be computed before column
x−1.
The shifted copies are lled with innity where the shift runs o the end of the array. Innity
is the natural value for an impossible move, because taking a minimum automatically never
selects it, with no need for a special case in the code.
4.34 SkewScore
def Sk ew Sc or e ( mask , angleDeg )
Inputs
mask
the ink mask of the page
angleDeg
the trial rotation angle in degrees
Output.a single number: how sharply the ink piles into rows when the page is considered at
this angle. Larger is better aligned.
What it is forA photographed page is never perfectly square to the camera, so the lines of
writing run slightly uphill or downhill. Every later stage assumes they are horizontal: the row
prole is used to nd lines, horizontal seams are used to separate them, and a crop is a horizontal
strip. A tilt of even two degrees smears the prole enough to merge neighbouring lines. This
function provides the measurement that lets the tilt be found: given a candidate angle, it scores
how level the page would look at that angle.
The principle: concentrationConsider the row prole again, the count of ink pixels per row.
When the page is level, every letter on a line contributes to the same few rows, so those rows
have very large counts and the rows between lines have almost none. When the page is tilted,
the left end of a line sits higher than the right end, so the same ink is spread over many more
rows, each with a smaller count. The total amount of ink is identical either way, since rotating
the page does not create or destroy pixels. What changes is howconcentratedthe prole is. So
the correct angle is the one that concentrates the prole most, and all that is needed is a number
that measures concentration.
Why the sum of squares measures concentrationSquaring, then summing, does it. Squar-
ing rewards large values disproportionately: doubling a count quadruples its contribution. So for
a xed total, the sum of squares is largest when the total is piled into as few entries as possible
and smallest when it is spread evenly.
S(θ) =
X
k
prof θ[k]2 (4.40)
64

=== PDF PAGE 66 ===
where
S(θ)

prof θ[k]
the number of ink pixels that fall into row index k once the page is considered rotated by
theta
kthe row index in the rotated frame
A numerical illustration makes the behaviour concrete. Suppose 600 ink pixels belong to one line.
Level, they might fall into 4 rows of 150, giving a score of 4·150 2 = 90{,}000. Tilted, the same
600 might spread over 20 rows of 30, giving 20·30 2 = 18{,}000. The totals match at 600 but the
scores dier vefold, and the larger score correctly identies the level case. The sum of squares is
used in the same spirit elsewhere in the project, where EstimateSlantDeg uses it to nd the slant
of letters rather than the skew of lines.

angle would mean interpolating every pixel, seventeen times over for the seventeen angles tried.

and that index follows from one line of trigonometry. The standard rotation of a point about a
centre gives the rotated row as

(4.41)
where
kthe row the pixel lands in after rotation
y, xthe pixel's original row and column
cy, c x
the centre of rotation, taken as the centre of the image
θthe rotation angle in radians, converted from the degrees supplied
cos,sin
the cosine and sine functions, which express how much of each original axis contributes to
the new vertical position
round
rounding to the nearest whole number, since rows are counted in whole pixels
Only the row is computed; the column is never needed, because the prole sums over columns
anyway. Counting how many pixels land in each row is then a single tally of the k values, so the
cost of scoring one angle is proportional to the number of ink pixels rather than to the number
of pixels in the image. Since ink is a small fraction of a page, that is a large saving on top of
avoiding the interpolation.
A subtle trap: never clip the row indexRotation moves some pixels outside the original
image rectangle, so k can come out negative or beyond the image height. The obvious response,
forcing k into the valid range, is badly wrong, and the code carries a comment saying so. Clipping
65

=== PDF PAGE 67 ===
piles every out-of-range pixel into the rst or last row. That creates an enormous articial count in
one row, and because the score squares the counts, this fake spike scores higher than any genuine
line of text. The eect grows with the angle, since larger angles push more pixels out of range,
so the search would reliably select the most extreme angle oered. The code instead shifts the
indices so the smallest becomes zero, which keeps every pixel in its own distinct row and lets the
prole grow as tall as it needs to be.
This is a good example of why a plausible-looking defensive measure can be worse than none.
Clipping an index is usually the safe thing to do; here it silently converts a correct measurement
into one that is guaranteed to give the wrong answer.
4.35 Deskew
def Deskew ( page )
Inputs
page
the photograph, as a 2-D grey array or a 3-D colour array of 8-bit values
Output.ve items: the straightened full-resolution page, the straightened half-resolution copy,
the ink mask of that copy, the ratio image, and the angle in degrees that was applied.
What it is forThis is the rst stage of the segmenter, and its job is to hand the rest of the
pipeline a page whose lines of writing run horizontally, together with the derived images every
later stage needs. It combines three things: reducing the page to grey, searching for the tilt angle,
and applying the rotation.
Step one: colour to greyA colour photograph arrives as three values per pixel, for red, green
and blue. The whole segmenter works on a single brightness value, so the three channels are
averaged. A simple mean is used rather than one of the perceptual weightings that weight green
more heavily, because the distinction being drawn is dark pen against light paper, which shows
up in all three channels, and a plain mean has the advantage of treating blue and black ink alike.
Step two: work at half sizeEvery decision in the segmenter is taken on a copy of the page
reduced by a factor of two in each direction, which is a quarter of the pixels, so everything runs
about four times faster. The decisions involved concern whole lines and whole letters, which are
many pixels across, so halving the resolution does not blur the evidence they rest on. The full
resolution page is kept because the nal crops are cut from it, so the recogniser still sees every
pixel the camera captured.
The reduction averages each 2 by 2 block rather than taking every second pixel. Point sampling
would risk a thin pale stroke falling between the samples and disappearing entirely, whereas any ink
inside a block darkens that block's average and survives. This is the same argument that applies
to the widening of a resampling lter when minifying, discussed in the entry for _Weights.
66

=== PDF PAGE 68 ===
Step three: search for the angleThe angle is found by agrid search, which means simply
trying every candidate in a xed list and keeping the best. The candidates run from−4 to +4
degrees in steps of 0.5, which is seventeen angles, each scored by SkewScore, with the highest
score winning.
θ∗ = arg max
θ∈ {−4,−3.5, ...,4}
S(θ)(4.42)
where
θ∗ the chosen rotation angle in degrees
S(θ)
the concentration score at that angle, from SkewScore
arg max
the angle at which the score is largest
Two choices in that range deserve justication. The limit of 4 degrees reects how a page is
actually photographed: someone aiming a phone at a sheet on a desk is square to it within a few
degrees, and a search that allowed 45 degrees would mostly oer opportunities to pick a wrong
answer. The step of 0.5 degrees is chosen against the consequence of residual error. Over a page
1500 pixels wide, half a degree of tilt lifts one end relative to the other by 1500·tan(0.5 ◦)≈13
pixels, which is well under the height of a line of writing, so the remaining tilt cannot merge
neighbouring lines. A ner step would cost more scoring for no benet the later stages could use.
The search runs on a quarter-size mask, halved again from the working copy, giving sixteen times
fewer pixels to tally per angle. The angle of a whole page is a very coarse property, determined
by the global arrangement of its lines, so it survives aggressive reduction intact.
Step four: apply the rotation, and the ll valuesThe chosen angle is applied to both
the full-size and half-size pages with Rotate. Rotating a rectangle leaves wedges at the corners
that correspond to no original pixel, and these have to be lled with something. The ll is 255,
white, because what lies just outside the frame of a photograph of a page is more page, and
declaring it white means the later stages see paper rather than a black triangle that would read
as a huge ink blob. Where a mask rather than a grey image is rotated the ll is 0 instead, for the
same underlying reason: in a mask, 0 means no ink, so lling with 0 adds nothing that could be
mistaken for writing.
Step ve: derive the maskFinally InkMask is run on the straightened half-size page to pro-
duce the ink mask and the ratio image. Note the ordering: the mask is computed after rotation,
not before. Rotating a mask would require interpolating yes-or-no values, which produces frac-
tional ink along every stroke edge and forces an arbitrary second threshold to clean up. Rotating
the grey image and then thresholding avoids that entirely, since interpolating grey values is exactly
what interpolation is for.
The ink mask is computed twice in this function, once before the rotation to drive the angle
search and once after it for the output. The rst is thrown away. That is deliberate: the search
needs a mask to score, and the output must describe the rotated page, and no single mask can
do both.
67

=== PDF PAGE 69 ===
4.36 RemovePrintedStructure
def R e m o v e P r i n t e d S t r u c t u r e ( mask , g )
Inputs
mask
the ink mask of the page
gthe grey page at the same scale, used to locate the sheet
Output.ve items: the cleaned mask, the paper-region mask, the line pitch, how many ink pixels
lay outside the paper, and how many were removed as printed structure.
What it is forA sheet of lined or gridded paper carries printing that was never written by
hand: the ruled lines, the red margin line, the holes and the darkened spine where the page meets
the binding. All of it is ink as far as the mask is concerned, and all of it is poison for the stages
that follow. A ruled line runs the full width of the page, so it joins every letter on a line into one
connected blob, which destroys the per-letter analysis that the cutter depends on, and it adds a
huge horizontal stroke to every row prole. This function removes that printing while leaving the
writing intact.
The distinguishing property: lengthThe property that separates printing from writing is
not darkness, since a ruled line can be as dark as a pencil stroke, and not thickness, since both
are a pen's width. It islength. A ruled line crosses the whole page in one unbroken straight run.
Handwriting contains straight pieces too, but short ones: the longest horizontal stroke in ordinary
writing is the crossbar of a t or the join between two letters, neither more than a fraction of a
line's height. So a threshold on run length, expressed in line pitches, separates the two reliably.
flat = LongRunMask
 
mask,⌊1.25P⌋,horizontal

(4.43)
where
flatthe mask of pixels belonging to a long horizontal run
LongRunMask
marks every pixel that lies in an unbroken run of set pixels at least the given length; see its
own entry
Pthe line pitch in pixels
1.25the length threshold as a multiple of the pitch
⌊·⌋rounding down to a whole number of pixels
The threshold of 1.25 pitches sits in a wide gap. A ruled line is tens of pitches long, and the
longest horizontal stroke in handwriting is well under one pitch, since a letter is about half a pitch
wide. Anything between about 1 and several pitches would give the same result, so the exact
gure is not delicate. The vertical direction gets 1.4 pitches, slightly more, because a tall letter
like a capital L or an f with a long descender genuinely can run more than a pitch vertically, and
the extra margin keeps such letters out of the deletion.
68

=== PDF PAGE 70 ===
T olerating a tiltA ruled line on a page that is still a fraction of a degree o level is not a single
row of pixels. It drifts downward as it crosses the page, so any one row contains only the piece of
it that happens to lie in that row, and a run-length test applied row by row sees a series of short
runs rather than one long one, and deletes nothing. The x is to dilate the mask by one pixel
vertically before the test, so that each row contains the line's pixels from the rows immediately
above and below as well and the run becomes unbroken again.
But dilating also thickens the writing, and a test applied to a thickened mask would mark pixels
that were never ink. So the result is dilated back and then intersected with the original mask,
which restricts the deletion to pixels that really were ink to begin with. In morphological terms
the sequence dilate, test, dilate, intersect is a closing used as a detector rather than as a lter:
the dilation makes the feature visible to the test, and the intersection connes the consequences
to the original shape.
The vertical pass uses a radius of 3 rather than 1 because the structures it targets, the shadow at
the spine of a notebook and the edge of the sheet, are not straight. They wobble by several pixels
across the height of the page, so a tolerance of one pixel is not enough to make them read as one
continuous run.
Masking o the surround rstBefore any of this, PaperRegionMask locates the sheet and
everything outside it is deleted from the mask in one intersection. This is done rst because it
is the cheapest and most decisive lter available: the desk, the hand and the shadow beyond the
page account for a large share of the false ink on a photograph, and removing them wholesale
means the subtler tests that follow are not distracted by them.
What is returned, and why the countsBesides the cleaned mask, the function returns the
pitch and the paper mask, both already computed here and needed later, and two counts: how
many ink pixels lay outside the paper and how many were deleted as printed structure. The counts
feed the diagnostics dictionary that SegmentLines returns. They exist because a failure of this
stage is otherwise invisible: if the paper mask is wrong, the ink-outside-paper count is enormous,
and if the run-length thresholds are wrong for a given page, the removed-pixel count is far larger
or smaller than expected. Reporting them makes a silent failure a visible one.
4.37 DropArtifactPieces
def D r o p A r t i f a c t P i e c e s ( mask , ratio , P , H , W )
Inputs
mask
the ink mask after the printed structure has been removed
ratio
the ratio image from InkMask, used to judge how dark each piece is
Pthe line pitch in pixels
Hthe mask height
Wthe mask width
Output.ve items: the mask with the artefacts deleted, the component label array, the number
of components, the area of each, and how many pieces were dropped.
69

=== PDF PAGE 71 ===
What it is forAfter the printing has been removed, what remains is mostly handwriting but
not entirely. Creases cast faint shadows. The fold at the centre of a spread leaves a dark line.
The cut edge of the sheet leaves a sliver. Fragments of the ruling survive where a letter crossed
it and broke the run. This function examines the leftovers one piece at a time and deletes those
that are not writing.
Why judge whole pieces, not pixelsThis is the central design decision and it is worth
stating plainly. Every test in this function is applied to a connected component as a unit, and a
component is either kept whole or deleted whole. The alternative, testing each pixel on its own
merits, produces half-erased letters, and a half-erased letter is worse than either outcome: it is
still there, so it still has to be interpreted, but it no longer looks like the letter it is. Deciding per
piece means the worst case is a missing letter, which the recogniser handles as a gap, rather than
a corrupted one, which it condently misreads.
Closing before labellingThe components are found not on the mask itself but on a closed
copy, dilated by about a tenth of a pitch and then eroded by the same amount. The purpose is to
make a letter that the threshold broke into two pieces, where the pen lifted or the ink ran thin,
count as one piece for the purposes of these tests. Otherwise the two halves are judged separately
and each half, being smaller and thinner than a letter, is more likely to be mistaken for an artefact.
The tests then read their statistics from the original mask but group pixels by the labels from the
closed one, so the closing inuences the grouping without inating the measurements.
The geometric testsSeveral tests follow from the simple fact that a letter has a characteristic
size, and the pitch gives the scale for it.
T oo tall:a piece more than 2.2 pitches high cannot be a letter, because that is taller than
a whole line of writing including its ascender and descender zones.
T oo thin:a piece half a pitch wide or less, combined with another suspicious property, is
a sliver rather than a letter.
A vertical bar:a piece under 0.12 pitches wide and over 1.2 pitches tall is deleted outright.
No letter has that aspect ratio; a printed margin rule does, and so does the fold at the centre
of a page.
T ouching an edge:a piece reaching within half a pitch of the paper boundary is suspect,
because writing does not start at the cut edge of a sheet. Touching the left edge is enough on
its own, since the binding is there; touching the right or the top or bottom is only grounds
for deletion in combination with being thin.
F ar left of the text:the fth percentile of the left edges of all the letter-sized pieces
estimates where the writing begins, and a piece lying more than 1.2 pitches to the left of
that is in the margin, not in the text.
The fth percentile, rather than the minimum, estimates the left margin of the text. Apercentile
is the value below which a given fraction of the data falls, so the fth percentile is the value
exceeded by 95 percent of the left edges. Using the minimum would let a single surviving artefact
dene the margin and so protect every other artefact to its right; using a percentile means one
stray piece cannot shift the estimate.
70

=== PDF PAGE 72 ===
The darkness testGeometry alone cannot separate a faint printed rule from a pen stroke of
the same shape, so the ratio image is brought in. The mean ratio over a piece measures how dark
it is relative to the paper around it, and the comparison is made against the same Otsu threshold
that decided what counted as ink in the rst place.

The reasoning is that a real pen stroke is not marginal. It is far darker than the threshold, not
just past it, so its mean ratio sits well below the threshold value. A piece whose average only just
scrapes under is made of pixels that barely qualied, which is the signature of a crease, a shadow
or a printed rule. Being faint is not grounds for deletion by itself, since a pencil stroke is faint too;
it is combined with a shape test, so that a faint thin vertical thing goes but a faint letter-shaped
thing stays.
The per-piece mean is computed in a single pass by a weighted tally. Tallying the labels with
the ratio values as weights sums the ratio over each piece, and dividing by the pixel count gives
the mean, with no loop over pieces. The same trick appears throughout the segmenter, and the
general pattern is worth recognising: a per-group sum is a weighted count indexed by group.
The edge-shadow testOne composite test targets a specic and common artefact: the shadow
of a fold or the curl at the top or bottom of the sheet. It is identied by ve properties at once:
it touches the top or bottom edge, it is no taller than 1.2 pitches, it is wider than a pitch, it is
faint, and itsdensityis below 0.15.
densityℓ = Aℓ

Density distinguishes a sparse scatter from a compact shape. A word lls a reasonable share of its
bounding box; a broken shadow stretching across the page lls almost none of it, because its box
is wide and tall while its ink is a thin broken line. Requiring all ve properties together is what
makes the test safe: each one individually also describes some piece of real writing, but nothing
in handwriting is simultaneously edge-touching, wide, short, faint and sparse.
The background label is explicitly protected from deletion. Label 0 means "not part of any
piece", and deleting it would erase everything that was not assigned to a component, which is
a one-line guard against a catastrophic error.
4.38 LineCentres
def L i n e C e n t r e s ( mask , H )
Inputs
mask
the cleaned ink mask
Hthe mask height in pixels
Output.a pair: the line pitch, and a sorted list of the row positions of the lines of writing.
What it is forThe pitch says how far apart the lines are; this function says where they actually
are. Each entry of the returned list is the row at the heart of one line of writing, and the rest of
the segmenter uses them as anchors: cuts are placed between consecutive centres, and a piece of
ink is judged by how far above or below a centre it sits.
Finding candidatesThe smoothed row prole is reused from LinePitch, and the candidates
are its local maxima, found by the same test: at least as large as the row above, strictly larger
than the row below, and above 6 percent of the prole's peak. The rows where the ink piles up
are the rows through the bodies of the letters, which is the right place to call the centre of a line.
The real problem: suppressing duplicatesSmoothing does not remove every spurious peak.
A line containing several tall ascenders can produce a secondary maximum a few rows above its
main one, and a line of writing that drifts slightly can produce two. Accepting both would create
two lines where there is one, and the consequence is severe: a cut would be placed between them,
straight through the middle of the writing, and the line would be delivered to the recogniser as
two half-height crops. So the candidates must be thinned.
72

=== PDF PAGE 74 ===
Greedy non-maximum suppressionThe thinning is done by a method known asnon-
maximum suppression: sort the candidates by strength, take the strongest, discard anything
too close to it, and repeat with what is left. It is calledgreedybecause it commits to the best
available option at each step and never revisits that choice.
C ← C ∪ {c}if|c−o| ≥0.6Pfor everyo∈ C(4.46)
where
Cthe set of centres accepted so far, considered in order of decreasing prole value
cthe candidate peak currently being considered
oa centre already accepted
Pthe line pitch in pixels
0.6the exclusion radius as a fraction of the pitch
Processing the candidates strongest rst is what makes the greedy rule correct rather than arbi-
trary. The strongest peak in a cluster is the one through the letter bodies, so it is accepted and
the weaker peaks of the same line are then excluded by it. Had the candidates been processed
top to bottom instead, a weak secondary peak slightly above a line would be accepted rst and
would then exclude the true centre below it, placing the line in the wrong row.
Why 0.6 pitchesThe exclusion radius has to exceed the width of a spurious cluster and fall
short of the true spacing between lines. By denition the true spacing is one pitch, and the
duplicate peaks of a single line lie within a fraction of a pitch of each other, since they are all
within one line's x-height. A radius of 0.6 pitches is comfortably inside that gap: it cannot
suppress a genuine neighbour, which is a full pitch away, and it does suppress a duplicate, which
is much closer. Choosing slightly more than half a pitch, rather than exactly half, leaves margin
for the pitch estimate itself being a little low.
Accepted centres are collected in order of strength and only sorted into row order at the end.
The sort matters because every later stage assumes the centres run down the page in sequence,
pairing each with the next to place a cut between them.
4.39 SeamCuts
def SeamCuts ( mask , centres , P , H , W )
Inputs
mask
the cleaned ink mask
centres
the row positions of the lines, from LineCentres
Pthe line pitch in pixels
Hthe mask height
Wthe mask width
Output.three items: the cuts as a list of one row-per-column array each, the same cuts stacked
into a single 2-D array, and L, the number of bands the cuts divide the page into.
73

=== PDF PAGE 75 ===
What it is forWith the lines located, boundaries have to be drawn between them. This
function produces one boundary curve per gap, each given as the row it occupies in every column,
so that the page is divided into L horizontal bands with one line of writing in each. The curves are
seams rather than straight rows, for the reason given in the entry for LeastCostSeam: a straight
cut between two lines of handwriting cannot avoid slicing through the descenders of the upper
line or the ascenders of the lower one, because at some column they overlap vertically.
The cost landscapeA seam needs a cost array, and the art of this function is in building one
whose cheapest path is the cut that a person would draw. It has two terms.
cost[y, x] = 4.0·m[y, x] + 0.012
 y−y mid
g
2
(4.47)
where
cost[y, x]
the price of routing the cut through this pixel
m[y, x]
1 where there is ink in the closed mask, 0 where there is not
4.0the price of crossing a single ink pixel
ymid the row midway between the two line centres this cut separates
ghalf the distance between those two centres, used to express the displacement in units of the
gap rather than in pixels
0.012
the strength of the pull back toward the midline
The rst term is the one that does the work: ink is expensive, so the cheapest route goes around
letters. The second is a weakquadratic penaltyon straying from the middle of the gap,
quadratic meaning it grows with the square of the displacement, so small excursions are nearly
free while large ones become progressively more costly. Its purpose is to settle what happens in
genuinely empty space. Between two words there is no ink at all and every route is equally cheap
on the rst term alone, so without the second term the cut would wander to an arbitrary height
and arrive at the next word from a silly position. The pull keeps it near the middle of the gap,
ready to deect in either direction when the next letter arrives.
The ratio between the two coecients is what matters, not either value alone. At 4.0 against
0.012, crossing one ink pixel costs as much as sitting about eighteen normalised units o the
midline, which is far outside the gap. So the cut will always prefer a long detour through white
space to cutting a stroke, and the midline term only ever acts as a tie-breaker. Expressing the
displacement in units of the half-gap rather than in pixels keeps that balance the same for widely
and narrowly spaced writing.
Closing the mask rstThe mask is closed, dilated then eroded by about 0.07 pitches, before
being used as cost. The reason is a specic failure mode: handwriting has narrow gaps inside it,
between the two strokes of an n or where a pen lifted, and a seam is happy to thread through
such a gap, because threading it is free while going round costs ink. The cut then passes through
the middle of a letter. Closing lls gaps narrower than the structuring element while leaving the
outer shape of the stroke nearly unchanged, so the threading routes disappear but the cut still
traces the true outline of the letter.
74

=== PDF PAGE 76 ===
Closing rather than plain dilation is the point. Dilation alone would also close the gaps, but it
would leave every stroke thickened by the full radius, and the cut would then trace a bloated blob
standing well clear of the real ink, wasting vertical space in both crops. The erosion that follows
the dilation undoes the thickening in the places where it was not needed, which is exactly the
property that distinguishes a closing from a dilation.
The step limitThe seam here is allowed 7 rows of vertical movement per column, against the
default of 3. A tall narrow descender, such as the tail of a y, demands a sharp deection: the cut
must drop below the tail and climb back within the few columns the tail occupies. With a small
step limit the cut simply cannot turn fast enough and is forced through the tail. The cost of the
larger limit is a little more work per pixel and a little more freedom to wander, the latter being
what the midline penalty is there to restrain.
The outer boundariesCuts exist only between consecutive centres, so the rst line has no
cut above it and the last none below. Two straight boundaries are added at 0.75 pitches beyond
the outermost centres, clipped to the page. They are straight because there is no second line on
the far side to avoid, so there is nothing for a seam to dodge, and 0.75 pitches is enough to clear
the ascenders of the rst line and the descenders of the last. The number of bands is then one
less than the number of boundaries, which is why L is computed that way.

What it is forThe cuts divide the page into bands, but a band is not yet an answer. The
question that must be settled for every ink pixel is which line of writing it belongs to, and the
band it falls in is only the rst guess at that. This function makes that guess and then applies
several corrections to it, producing the ownership map that the crops are cut from.
Computing the band index without a loopEach cut is a curve, so asking which band a
pixel is in means asking how many of the curves lie above it. That count is the band index directly,
and it can be formed by adding up one comparison per cut over the whole image at once.
region[y, x] =
L−1X

The expression is read as: a pixel is below cut n, so it lies past that boundary, and summing those
indicators over all the interior cuts counts the boundaries it has passed. The outer two boundaries
are excluded from the sum, since nothing lies beyond them that needs numbering; instead they
are used separately to discard ink above the rst line and below the last.
Why the band is only a guessIf every piece of ink lay wholly inside one band, the band
index would be the nal answer. Two things prevent that. First, a connected piece of ink can
genuinely cross a cut: the descender of a g belongs to the upper line but hangs into the lower
band, and the cut goes around the ink, so the piece ends up with pixels in both. Taking the band
pixel by pixel would split that g in half, delivering its bowl to one crop and its tail to another,
and neither fragment looks like a g. Second, small marks like the dot of an i are separate pieces
that oat well above their own line's body and can easily fall on the wrong side of a cut.
The piece-by-band tableBoth problems need the same information: how much of each piece
lies in each band. That is a two-dimensional table, pieces against bands, and it is built with one
tally by packing the two indices into a single number.

that each pair maps to its own integer. A single tally over the packed keys then produces the
whole table, and reshaping the result into an array of pieces by bands unpacks it. The alternative,
looping over pieces and counting each one's pixels, would be far slower in Python for no gain.

where
major(ℓ)
the band piece l is assigned to
cnt[ℓ, b]
the pixels of piece l in band b
P
b′ cnt[ℓ, b′]
the piece's total ink
0.15the share of its own ink a band must hold to count as substantial
minthe smallest such band index, that is, the highest on the page
The 15 percent test exists to tell a genuine crossing from a trivial one. A piece whose ink is
95 percent in one band and 5 percent in the next merely touches the boundary, and treating it
as spanning two lines would be wrong. A piece split 60 to 40 really does belong to both, and
something must be decided.
Choosing the topmost, rather than the band with the most ink, is a deliberate asymmetry. When
a piece spans two lines it is usually because a descender from the upper line reaches down, or
because two lines of writing touch. In either case the upper line is the one that is incomplete
without the piece, since the shape originates there, while the lower line is merely intruded upon.
Giving it to the upper line keeps the crop that would otherwise be damaged intact. If no band
reaches 15 percent, which happens when a piece is spread thinly over three or more bands, the
fallback is the band with the most ink.
Small pieces: three graded rulesSmall pieces are handled separately, because for them the
band is unreliable and their size tells us what kind of thing they are. The thresholds are areas
measured in P 2 , the square of the pitch, which is the natural unit of area here: a letter occupies
a region roughly a third of a pitch by a half, so letter-sized is a few hundredths of P 2 .
Under 0.06 P 2 , a stray mark.It is compared with the letter-sized pieces assigned to its
own band. If none sits within six pitches horizontally, there is no writing anywhere near it
and it is discarded. Otherwise the median vertical centre of the nearby writing says where
its line really is, and if the mark sits more than half a pitch below that it is moved down
77

=== PDF PAGE 79 ===
a band, or more than 0.7 pitches above it, up a band. A speck under 0.01 P 2 is discarded
in that situation rather than moved, on the grounds that something that small and that far
out of place is noise.
Between 0.06 and 0.15 P 2 , a small but real piece.Its own band and the two neighbours
are considered, and it goes to whichever has writing whose median vertical centre is closest
to its own. This is a nearest-centre rule, and it is used here rather than the band because a
piece this size can easily fall on the wrong side of a cut while clearly belonging, by position,
to one line.
A minor part of a larger piece.A band holding a small, narrow part of a piece, under
0.12 P 2 and under 35 percent of the piece, no wider than 0.55 pitches, in a band adjacent
to the piece's assigned one, is treated as a cut-o tail and follows the rest of the piece. The
width condition is what distinguishes a tail from a whole word: a tail is narrow, and a wide
part of a piece in another band is that band's own writing.
The median is used for the position of nearby writing rather than the mean, for the usual reason:
one misplaced piece among the neighbours would drag a mean, while the median reports where
the bulk of the line actually is.
The dot ruleThe last and most specic correction handles the dots of i and j. A dot oats
clear of its own letter, often nearer to the line above than to its own line's body, so neither the
band nor a nearest-centre test places it reliably. What does place it is the letter underneath it.
A piece is a dot candidate when it is small, under 0.1 P 2 , compact, no more than 0.45 pitches
in either direction, and roughly round, its longer side no more than 2.2 times its shorter. The
roundness test is what excludes a comma or an accent from being treated as a dot. The rows below
the candidate are then scanned, up to 0.4 pitches down, for the rst substantial piece appearing
beneath it, and the dot is given to that piece's line, provided the geometry agrees: the line's
writing must sit between 0.25 and 0.7 pitches below the dot, which is where a dot oats above
its own line's body. That nal check is what stops the rule ring when the piece found below
happens to belong to a line further away.
W riting the resultAll of this produces, for each piece, either a band index or a marker meaning
discard. The ownership array is then lled in one operation: a pixel takes its piece's assigned band
if the piece was one of those the rules relocated, and its own band index otherwise. A copy is
kept before the later corrections run, because those corrections need to know which pixels were
already moved here, in order not to undo that work.
4.41 _DotsJoinedAbove
def _ D o t s J o i n e d A b o v e ( own , mask , lab , area , region , centres , L , P , H , W ,
ys , xs )
Inputs
ownthe ownership array, modied in place
mask
the cleaned ink mask
labthe closed-mask component labels
78

=== PDF PAGE 80 ===
area
the area of each closed-mask component
region
the band index of every pixel
centres
the line centres
Lthe number of bands
Pthe line pitch
Hthe height
Wthe width
ysthe row coordinates of the ink pixels
xstheir column coordinates
Output.four items describing the components of the unclosed mask: the label array, the count,
the per-pixel labels and the areas. The ownership array is corrected in place.
The problem it solvesThe closing applied before labelling in DropArtifactPieces is useful but
it has a side eect. If the dot of an i on one line happens to sit just below a descender from
the line above, the closing can bridge the small gap between them and fuse the two into a single
component. The dot then inherits whatever the fused piece was assigned, which is the upper line,
and the lower line's crop loses its dot while the upper line's crop gains a stray mark.
Why a second labelling is neededThe x requires seeing the dot as its own object, which
the closed labelling can no longer do. So the mask is labelled again, this time without closing. In
the unclosed labelling the dot is separate from the descender, because the gap between them is
real ink-free space; it was only the closing that joined them. The function therefore works with
two labellings at once, the unclosed one to identify the dot and the closed one to recognise when
a piece is in fact part of a larger structure.
Identifying the dotThe same three-part test as before: small area, between 0.015 and 0.1 P 2 ,
compact, no more than half a pitch across, and roughly round. The lower area bound is new and
matters here, since it excludes single-pixel noise that would otherwise pass every other condition.
Finding the stem and its lineThe rows immediately below the candidate are scanned for
the rst other substantial piece, and the band of the pixel where the two meet is read from the
region array. That gives the line the stem belongs to, which is the line the dot should belong to.
The dot's own current assignment is read as the most common ownership value over its pixels.
The three conditions for acting
The stem must be exactly one band below.A fusion of this kind can only displace
a mark by one line, since the closing bridges a gap of a few pixels. A discrepancy of two
bands means something else is wrong, and acting on it would make things worse.
79

=== PDF PAGE 81 ===
The fused piece must not be a whole word.If the closed component containing the
dot has an area of 0.2 P 2 or more, it is a word, not a dot welded to a descender. A genuine
oating dot stays a small component even after closing, so a large closed area is evidence
that the candidate is really part of a letter, such as the bowl of an a that the threshold
happened to detach.
The dot must sit well below the upper line's centre.At least 0.45 pitches below
it. A mark that close to the upper line's body really is part of the upper line, and the rule
declines to move it.
Only the pixels currently assigned to the upper line are moved, not every pixel of the unclosed
piece. This matters when a letter has broken apart in the unclosed mask, so that the candidate
shares its unclosed component with ink that was already correctly assigned: moving the whole
component would drag that correct ink along with it.
The pattern of conditions here is characteristic of the whole ownership stage. Each correction
rule is narrow on purpose, requiring several independent pieces of evidence to agree before it
res. A broad rule would x more cases and break more, and a crop that is wrong is more
costly than a crop that is merely imperfect, because the recogniser will read it condently either
way.
4.42 _RingsToStem
def _ R i n g s T o S t e m ( own , raw , nr , pr , Ar , lab , area , region , centres , P , H ,
W , ys , xs )
Inputs
ownthe ownership array, modied in place
rawthe unclosed component labels
nrthe number of unclosed components
prthe unclosed label of each ink pixel
Arthe area of each unclosed component
labthe closed labels
area
the closed areas
region
the band index of every pixel
centres
the line centres
Pthe line pitch
Hthe height
Wthe width
ysthe row coordinates of the ink pixels
xstheir column coordinates
Output.nothing is returned; the ownership array is corrected in place.
80

=== PDF PAGE 82 ===
The problem it solvesThis is the mirror image of the previous correction. There, a dot on
the lower line was welded to a descender from above and wrongly given to the upper line. Here
the welding runs the other way: a small ring-shaped mark belonging to the lower line, such as the
dot of an i written as a small circle, or the loop at the top of a letter, is fused to the stem it sits
on, and the stem's line is the one it should follow. The two corrections exist separately because
the evidence needed diers: nding a ring requires detecting a hole, which the dot rule does not
do.
Finding a candidate: walking up from the bottomThe candidate is not a whole compo-
nent here but a part of one, so it cannot be found by labelling. It is found geometrically. For each
component, the lowest ink pixel in each column is located, and those bottom points are examined
in order from the lowest upward, since the ring sits at the top of a stem and the stem's foot is the
lowest point of the structure.
From each bottom point the algorithm walks upward, measuring at every row how wide the
component is within a window around the column, and tracking the widest it has been. The walk
stops when the width drops below 45 percent of that maximum. That point is the junction between
the blob and the stem: a ring is wide, the stem below it is narrow, so the width collapses exactly
where one ends and the other begins. This is a one-dimensional version of a shape-decomposition
idea that recurs in image analysis: a narrow neck is where an object naturally divides.
V alidating the shapeThree conditions must hold before the candidate is accepted as a ring.
Its width is between 0.2 and 0.45 pitches.Narrower is a stroke, wider is a letter body.
It is roughly as tall as it is wide,with a height-to-width ratio between 0.6 and 1.6. A
ring is round; a stroke is not.
It encloses a hole.This is the decisive test, and it is carried out by labelling the compo-
nents of the inverse of the blob after padding it with a border. If the blob encloses a hole
then the background splits into two components, the outside and the hole, so a count of
fewer than two components means there is no hole and the candidate is a hook or a curl
rather than a ring.
The padding before that test is not optional. Without a border of background all round, a blob
touching the edge of the extracted window would let the outside connect to the hole along that
edge, merging them into one component and hiding the hole. Padding guarantees the outside is
a single region that surrounds the blob completely. Counting holes by labelling the background
is a standard technique, and the number it yields is related to theEuler numberof the shape,
the classical topological measure of components minus holes.
The decisionAs before, the rst other piece appearing below the ring identies the line to
move to, the two bands must be adjacent, the closed component must not be large enough to
be a whole word, and the junction must lie no more than 0.45 pitches above the target line's
centre. Only the ring's own pixels are reassigned, conned to the rows between the junction and
the bottom point and to the columns the blob occupies, and only where they currently belong to
the band being moved from.
Why the examination is limitedAt most 60 bottom points are examined per component,
and accepting one point suppresses the columns within 0.35 pitches of it. Both are cost controls. A
81

=== PDF PAGE 83 ===
long word has hundreds of columns and the upward walk at each is not free, while the suppression
prevents the same ring being found again from every column it spans. The cap is a deliberate
compromise: a word with more than 60 distinct bottom points may have a ring missed, which
costs one misplaced dot, whereas examining every column would slow the segmenter measurably
on every page.
4.43 _FragmentsToNeighbour
def _ F r a g m e n t s T o N e i g h b o u r ( own , ownBeforeEF , mask , centres , L , P , H , W )
Inputs
ownthe ownership array, modied in place
ownBeforeEF
