Output.the two directions' outputs joined along the feature axis, so twice as wide as one
direction.
Why one direction is not enoughAn LSTM reading left to right has, at each column,
seen everything to the left and nothing to the right. For handwriting that is half the available
evidence and often the less useful half. Consider the stroke that begins both an a and a d: what
distinguishes them is the ascender that comes afterwards. A left-to-right reader at the start of
that letter cannot know. It will often get the answer right in the end, because the output at the
later column can be correct, but its representation of the earlier columns is impoverished, and the
earlier columns are where CTC may want to place the character.
The constructionThe remedy is to run a second, completely separate LSTM over the sequence
reversed, then put its outputs back into forward order and join the two side by side. Each
position then carries a summary of everything to its left, from the forward pass, and a summary
of everything to its right, from the backward pass. The two have independent weights throughout;
nothing is shared.
yt =
 − →h t ; ← −h t

(5.18)
where
yt the combined output at position t, of width twice the hidden size
− →h t the forward LSTM's hidden state at position t, summarising positions 1 to t
← −h t the backward LSTM's hidden state at the same position, summarising positions t to the end
[·;·]
concatenation: the two vectors laid end to end, not added or combined
Concatenation rather than addition is the right choice because the two summaries answer dierent
questions and the layer above should be free to weigh them dierently. Adding them would force
every feature to mean the same thing in both directions and would discard which direction it came
from.
Why this is legitimate here and not alwaysReading the future is only possible because the
whole line is available before any output is needed. A system transcribing speech as it is spoken
could not do this, since the future has not happened yet. Handwriting recognition on a captured
image has no such constraint: the entire line was photographed at once. Bidirectional recurrence
is standard in handwriting recognition for exactly this reason, and the architecture this network
follows is built on it [17].
Reversing a numpy array along its rst axis is a view, not a copy, so running the backward
pass costs nothing extra in memory. The output of the backward pass is reversed again so that
position t of both halves refers to the same column; forgetting that second reversal would align
each position with its mirror image and produce uent-looking nonsense.
115

=== PDF PAGE 117 ===
5.27 MultilayerBidirectionalLstm
def M u l t i l a y e r B i d i r e c t i o n a l L s t m (x , layerParams , h i d d e n S i z e )
Inputs
xthe input sequence
layerParams
a list of parameter sets, one per layer, each holding a forward and a backward set
hiddenSize
the width of each direction in each layer
Output.the sequence after passing through every layer in turn.
What stacking buysEach layer's output sequence becomes the next layer's input sequence.
The reader uses two such layers. The gain is the same as from depth anywhere in a network:
the rst layer works on the convolutional features directly and can form short-range relationships
between neighbouring columns, such as grouping the strokes that make up one letter; the second
works on the rst's output, where each position already summarises a neighbourhood, and can
form relationships between those summaries, such as relating a letter to the word around it.
Why the second layer's input is twice as wideBecause the rst layer concatenates two
directions, its output has twice the hidden size, and the second layer's input weights are shaped
to accept that. This is handled implicitly here: the weights come from the checkpoint and already
have the right shape, so the function needs no knowledge of the widths and the same loop would
work for any number of layers.
Two layers is the depth the architecture species [17]. More would add parameters and se-
quential work for a task where the rst layer already covers a letter and the second a word,
which is the range the evidence actually spans.
5.28 ResizeLineImageFixed
def R e s i z e L i n e I m a g e F i x e d ( img , height = INPUT _ HEIGHT , width = INPUT _ WIDTH ,
padValue =255)
Inputs
imgthe line crop as a grey image
height
the target height, default 64
width
the target width, default 640
padValue
the value to ll unused canvas with, default 255 for white
Output.the crop tted onto a canvas of exactly the trained size.
116

=== PDF PAGE 118 ===
What it is forThe networks accept exactly one input size, for the reasons given under IN-
PUT_HEIGHT. A crop cut from a page is whatever size that line of writing happened to be.
This function reconciles the two, by delegating to FitFixedCanvas in segment.py.
Why the aspect ratio is preservedThe important property, explained in full in the entry
for FitFixedCanvas, is that a single scale factor is applied to both axes, chosen so the crop ts
inside the canvas, and the remainder is padded with white rather than stretched. Stretching each
axis independently to ll the canvas would change the proportions of the letters, and proportion is
precisely what distinguishes some letters from one another and one writer's hand from another's.
A tall narrow l stretched to ll a wide canvas becomes something no letter looks like, and the
lters trained on correctly proportioned letters would not nd their features in it.
White, not black, is the pad value, because at this point the image is still in its natural form
of dark ink on light paper. The inversion happens in the next function, and padding after
inversion would require black instead. Getting this the wrong way round surrounds every crop
with a solid block of apparent ink.
5.29 TensorFromResized
def T e n s o r F r o m R e s i z e d ( img )
Inputs
imgthe tted grey crop
Output.a oating-point array shaped (1, 1, height, width), scaled and inverted into the range
the networks were trained on.
What it is forAn image is 8-bit integers from 0 to 255 with white high. A network expects
oating-point numbers of a particular scale and sign convention. This function performs that
conversion, in three steps that must match exactly what was done during training.
x=
 

where
xthe value handed to the network, running from−1 for paper to +1 for black ink

117

=== PDF PAGE 119 ===
Why invertAfter inversion, ink is a large number and blank paper is zero before standardising.
This matters because of padding: every convolution pads with zeros, and zero must therefore mean
nothing rather than something. With the original convention zero means black, so the padding
round the edge of every crop would read as a border of solid ink and the rst lters would spend
their response on an artefact of the padding.
Why standardise to a range centred on zeroNetworks train better when their inputs are
centred near zero and of a magnitude near one, for the same reasons batch normalisation exists.
Inputs in the hundreds would make the rst layer's outputs correspondingly large and would
demand very small initial weights to compensate. Mapping to−1 to +1 puts the input on the
scale the initialisation assumed.
Why the two extra axesThe array is given the shape (1, 1, height, width) to match the
four-axis convention described in the chapter introduction: one image in the batch, one channel in
that image. The axes are necessary even though both lengths are 1, because every layer indexes
by position and would otherwise misinterpret which axis is which.
These three steps are not a design decision that can be revisited independently; they are a
reproduction of what the training code did. A network is only correct on inputs prepared the
way its training inputs were, and a mismatch here, such as a forgotten inversion, produces a
network that loads without error and returns condent nonsense.
5.30 PenWidthXh
P e n W i d t h X h = 0.14
Output.0.14, the pen width that stroke normalisation redraws at, expressed as a fraction of the
x-height.
Why the width is given in x-heights and not in pixelsA width in pixels would mean
something dierent on every crop, since crops dier in resolution and writers dier in size. Ex-
pressed as a fraction of the x-height it means the same thing everywhere: a stroke whose thickness
is about a seventh of the height of a letter body. The actual pixel width is computed per crop
from its measured x-height, which is what makes the normalisation resolution-free.
Why this valueIt has to be thick enough that the redrawn writing looks like writing rather
than a wire diagram, since the network judging it was trained on strokes of a realistic thickness,
and thin enough that the counters, the enclosed white spaces inside letters like a, e and o, survive.
At about a seventh of the x-height a counter remains open, while a noticeably thicker pen would
ll the loop of an e and make it indistinguishable from a c with a closed top. That failure is not
hypothetical for this project; distinguishing those two shapes has been a real source of error in
the glyph library.
5.31 StrokeNormalize
def S t r o k e N o r m a l i z e ( gray , p e n W i d t h X h = P e n W i d t h X h )
118

=== PDF PAGE 120 ===
Inputs
gray
a line crop as a grey image
penWidthXh
the pen width to redraw at, in x-heights, defaulting to the constant above
Output.the writing redrawn at one constant pen width as ink on white, or nothing if the crop
has no usable ink.
The problem it solvesA network asked to identify a writer from a raw crop will use whatever
is most reliably distinctive, and that is not necessarily the handwriting. Pen width and ink
darkness are extremely easy to measure and happen to be consistent within each writer's pages,
because each writer used one pen. A network can reach high accuracy on such a dataset while
having learned nothing about letterforms at all. This is aconfound: a variable correlated with
the answer that is not the thing being studied.
The confound becomes a real failure precisely where the project needs the namer most. The robot
draws with one pen, so every writer's reproduced output has identical stroke width. A namer
relying on width therefore has nothing to go on and is being asked a question it was never really
trained for. Removing pen width from the input forces the network to use shape, which is the
property that does survive reproduction.
The method: thin, then re-inkThe writing is reduced to its centre line and then redrawn
at a chosen width. Whatever the original thickness, the output thickness is the same, so the
information is genuinely destroyed rather than merely de-emphasised.
Binarise.The crop is thresholded into ink and paper by BinarizeLine.
Measure the x-height.CoreBand nds the band of rows holding the letter bodies, and
its height is the x-height, which sets the scale for the pen width.
Thin.Skeletonize reduces the ink to a one-pixel-wide centre line by the Zhang-Suen method
[6], which removes boundary pixels repeatedly while preserving connectivity, so the skeleton
has the same topology as the original writing.
Re-ink.The skeleton is dilated by a square of the chosen width, which draws a stroke of
that width centred on the centre line, and the result is inverted back to dark ink on white
paper.
Why the pen width is forced to be odd

where

clip(·,2,9)

⌊·⌋rounding down
A dilation by an even-sized square has no centre cell, so the redrawn stroke is oset by half a
pixel from the skeleton it was drawn around. Over a whole line that shifts the writing slightly
and, worse, shifts it in a direction that depends on the rounding, so two crops of the same writing
could be oset dierently. Forcing the size odd puts the pen exactly on the centre line.
The x-height fallbackIf no usable band is found, or the band is under four pixels tall, the
x-height is taken as a third of the crop height instead, with a oor of eight pixels. A crop is built
around one line of writing whose body occupies roughly the middle third, so that is a reasonable
estimate when measurement fails, and it keeps the function returning something usable rather
than propagating a nonsensical width.
Two cases return nothing at all: a crop with no ink, and one whose skeleton is empty. Re-
turning nothing rather than a blank image is deliberate, because the caller must be able to tell
"normalisation failed" from "this line is blank" and fall back to the raw crop. ClassifyImage
does exactly that.
5.32 DecodeCtc
def De co de Ct c ( logProbs )
Inputs
logProbs
the reader's output, shaped (time, batch, classes), holding a log probability per character
per column
Output.a list of strings, one per item in the batch.
The problem CTC exists to solveThe reader produces one set of character scores per
column, around 160 of them, while the line contains perhaps 40 characters. Nothing says which
columns correspond to which character, and the correspondence diers for every line and every
writer: a wide m covers many columns, a narrow i covers few. Training a network to produce
a short sequence from a long one would ordinarily require knowing that correspondence, which
would mean marking by hand where every letter begins and ends on every training line. For tens
of thousands of lines that is not feasible.
Connectionist temporal classication[18] removes the requirement. It lets the network be
trained on the text alone, with no indication of where the characters sit, by dening a rule that
maps any per-column output onto a shorter string, and then training the network so that the
total probability of all column-outputs mapping to the correct string is high. The alignment is
never specied; it is whatever the network nds convenient.
120

=== PDF PAGE 122 ===
The collapsing ruleThe rule has two steps, applied in this order: merge runs of the same
character into one, then delete the blanks.
B
 
π

:merge repeats, then remove blanks (5.21)
Source:[18]
where
Bthe collapsing map, which takes a sequence of one label per column and returns a string
πapath: one label chosen for each column, including possibly the blank
blank
the extra label, slot 0, which stands for "emitting nothing here"
Why the blank is necessaryThe blank is the part that makes the scheme work, and its
purpose is easiest to see from what goes wrong without it. If the rule were only "merge repeats",
the wordlettercould never be produced, because its two consecutive t's would merge into one.
The blank provides a separator: a path reading t, t, blank, t, t collapses rst to t, blank, t and
then, with the blank removed, to tt. So a blank between two runs of the same character means a
genuine doubled letter, while its absence means one letter held across several columns.
a, a, a, b, bcollapses toab, one a and one b.
a, blank, acollapses toaa, a doubled letter.
a, a, blank, bcollapses toab, the blank being harmless between dierent letters.
The blank also gives the network somewhere to put the columns between letters, the gaps and
joins where no character is being written. In practice a trained CTC network emits blank for the
large majority of columns and spikes briey on a character where it is condent, which is why its
output looks sparse.
Greedy decoding, and what it approximatesWhat is wanted is the single most probable
string, which requires summing over every path that collapses to it, since many paths give the
same string. That is expensive. This function instead takes the most probablepath, by picking
the best-scoring character independently in each column, and collapses that. This isgreedyor
best-pathdecoding.
π∗

Bthe collapsing map dened above
The approximation is not exact: the most probable path need not collapse to the most probable
string, because a string whose probability is spread over many moderately good paths can beat
one concentrated in a single excellent path. In practice the dierence is small for a well-trained
network, whose output is condent enough that one path dominates, and the measured accuracy
of 96 to 99 percent on real crops is obtained with this decoder. A beam search would recover the
dierence at several times the cost.
Because the comparison within a column is only between scores in that same column, the
logarithm makes no dierence to which character wins. The log probabilities could be replaced
by raw logits here and the output would be identical. They are log probabilities because the
same array is used by the alignment, which does need them.
5.33 CtcForcedAlign
def C t c F o r c e d A l i g n ( logProbs , text )
Inputs
logProbs
the reader's output for one line, shaped (time, classes)
text
the known text of that line
Output.a list pairing each character with the span of columns it occupies, together with a
condence; or nothing if the text cannot be aligned.
What it is for, and why the project needs itDecoding answers "what does this line say".
This answers a dierent question: given that the line says this,whereis each character. That is
what the glyph extractor needs. To build a library of a writer's letterforms, the pipeline must cut
individual letters out of their line crops, and to do that it must know which columns of the crop
hold which letter. No separate letter-nding step is needed, because the reader already contains
that information implicitly in its per-column scores; forced alignment extracts it.
Why it is called forcedThe text is not being recognised; it is given. The algorithm isforced
to produce an alignment consistent with that text, and among all such alignments it nds the
most probable. So it cannot return the wrong letters, only a wrong idea of where they are. This
is a much easier problem than recognition and correspondingly more reliable, which is why the
project uses known transcripts for glyph extraction rather than the reader's own output.
The extended label sequenceThe rst step is to write out the target as a sequence that
allows blanks everywhere they are permitted: a blank, then each character followed by a blank.
ext =
 
∅, ℓ 1,∅, ℓ 2,∅, . . . , ℓ n,∅

, S= 2n+ 1(5.23)
Source:[18]
where
122

=== PDF PAGE 124 ===
extthe extended sequence of states the alignment moves through
∅the blank label, slot 0
ℓi the slot number of the i-th character of the text
nthe number of characters
Sthe length of the extended sequence, hence the number of states
Every valid path through the columns can now be described as a non-decreasing walk through
these states: at each column the path is in some state, and as columns advance the state index
only ever stays put or increases. Characters sit at odd positions and the optional blanks at even
ones. A path that skips a blank writes two characters back to back; a path that lingers in a blank
puts a gap between them.
The allowed movesFrom state s at the previous column, three moves are considered: stay
at s, advance to s from s−1, or advance to s from s−2. The third, the skip, is what allows the
optional blanks to be passed over, and it carries one restriction.
Stay .The same label continues across another column: a wide letter, or a long gap.
Advance by one.Move to the next state, either from a character into its following blank
or from a blank into the next character.
Skip by two.Jump over a blank, placing two characters in consecutive columns. Permitted
only when the target state is a character and that character diers from the one two states
back.
The restriction on the skip is the whole reason the blanks were inserted. If the two characters
either side of a blank are the same letter, skipping the blank would make a doubled letter collapse
back into one, by the merge rule. So for a doubled letter the blank between them is compulsory,
and the skip is forbidden. A skip onto a blank is likewise meaningless, since it would be jumping
over a character. Both cases are enforced by setting the candidate score to a very large negative
number, which the maximisation then never selects, which is the same device as the innities in
LeastCostSeam.
The recurrenceThis is dynamic programming again, with the same structure as the seam,
but maximising a log probability instead of minimising a cost. Working in log space means the
multiplication of probabilities along a path becomes an addition, as established under LogSoftmax.

maxthe best of those three arrivals; the third is excluded when the skip is not allowed
The initial column may only be in state 0, the leading blank, or state 1, the rst character, since
a path cannot have progressed further than one state in one column. At the nal column the
path must have reached either the last state, the trailing blank, or the state before it, the last
character; whichever of those two scores higher is the end of the best path.
Why maximum and not sumTraining CTC sums over all paths, because the quantity being
maximised is the total probability of the target string. Alignment takes the maximum instead,
because the quantity wanted is one specic path: the single most probable way the text could be
laid out across the columns. Taking the maximum of a sequence of choices like this is theViterbi
algorithm [19], long used for exactly this purpose of recovering the most likely state sequence
behind an observed signal.
F rom the path to column spansBacktracking the recorded moves gives the state occupied
at every column. The i-th character of the text lives in state 1+2i, so the columns whose state
equals that number are the columns that character occupies, and the rst and last of them are its
span. A character whose state is never occupied gets no span, which happens when the aligner
had to pass through it in a single column shared with its neighbours; the caller handles that by
skipping the glyph rather than cutting a zero-width one.

Dividing by the number of columns is what makes the gure comparable between lines. A total

column average, with zero meaning perfect certainty and more negative meaning less. The glyph
extractor uses it to reject lines whose alignment is not trustworthy enough to cut letters from,
which matters because a condently wrong alignment would poison the library with mislabelled
glyphs.
Two guards return nothing rather than a bad answer: a text with no recognisable characters,
and a line with fewer columns than characters. The second is a genuine impossibility rather
than a diculty, since every character needs at least one column of its own, so no alignment
exists at all.

Why the result is clampedA column index can map slightly beyond the crop, because the

real content into the padding. Clamping keeps the returned position usable as an index without the
caller checking. The value is returned as a oating-point number rather than an integer because
the caller may want to interpolate between pixels, and rounding here would discard precision that
was correctly computed.
5.35 PaperCRNNNumpy
class P a p e r C R N N N u m p y
Inputs
checkpointPath
which checkpoint to load, defaulting to the one chosen above
hiddenSize
the LSTM width, default 256
Output.an object holding the loaded weights, whose forward method maps a crop to log prob-
abilities per character per column.
What a CRNN isAconvolutional recurrent neural network[17] is the standard archi-
tecture for reading a line of text, and it is a direct expression of the structure of the problem.
Reading a line requires two dierent kinds of reasoning: recognising a shape, which is local and
spatial, and assembling shapes into words, which is sequential and long-range. Convolutions do
the rst, recurrence does the second, and CTC joins them to text without requiring the letters to
be located rst. Each of the three parts is doing the job it is suited to.
The layer sequence, and what happens to the data
stage1, two convolution groups,on a (1, 64, 640) input, nding edges and stroke direc-
tions.
Pool by two,halving to 32 by 320.
stage2, four groups,nding corners, junctions and curves.
Pool by two,halving again to 16 by 160.
stage3, six groups,nding letter-scale structure, ending with 128 channels.
Collapse the height,taking the maximum down each column, giving 128 features per
column.
T ranspose to (160, 1, 128),making width the sequence axis.
T wo bidirectional LSTM layers,each position gaining a summary of the whole line to
its left and right.
A linear layer,turning each position's features into 75 character scores.
Log softmax,making those scores log probabilities.
126

=== PDF PAGE 128 ===
The transpose is the key structural stepMoving the width axis to the front is what converts
an image into a sequence. Before it, the array is an image with a width; after it, the array is a
sequence of 160 feature vectors, and the word width no longer appears. Everything downstream,
the recurrence and CTC alike, is dened on sequences and would be meaningless on an image. The
160 columns become CTC's time axis, and the fact that they derive from horizontal position is
what makes CTC's monotonic left-to-right assumption correct for this problem: writing proceeds
left to right, so the alignment between columns and characters never needs to go backwards.
Why the weights are unpacked in the constructorThe LSTM parameters are gathered
into a structured list once, when the object is built, rather than looked up by name on every call.
Reading a page means running the forward pass once per line, so repeating a few dozen dictionary
lookups per line would be harmless in cost, but gathering them once means the key names appear
in exactly one place. A checkpoint missing a parameter then fails at construction, naming the
key, rather than part way through reading a page.
The reverse-direction parameters are stored under keys ending in_reverse, which is the
naming convention of the framework the weights were trained with. The forward direction 's
keys are built with a helper while the reverse ones are written out, which is a small asymmetry
of style in otherwise identical work.
5.36 ReadText
def ReadText ( image , model )
Inputs
image
a line crop as a grey image
model
a loaded reader
Output.the text read from that crop, as a string.
What it is forThe three steps of reading in one call: t the crop to the canvas, convert it to
the network's input convention, run the forward pass, and decode. It exists so that no caller has
to remember the order, and so that there is a single place where the preparation and the decoding
are paired with each other.
Why that mattersThe preparation steps are not independently meaningful. Fitting without
inverting, or inverting without standardising, produces an array the network will accept and
misread. Keeping them together in one function makes the complete sequence the only thing a
caller can invoke, which removes a class of error that would otherwise be silent.
Only the rst element of the decoded batch is returned, since the batch axis is always 1 here.
The batch machinery is retained throughout the layers because the training code uses it, and
because removing it would mean two versions of every layer.
127

=== PDF PAGE 129 ===
5.37 Levenshtein
def L e v e n s h t e i n (a , b )
Inputs
aone string
bthe other string
Output.the smallest number of single-character insertions, deletions and substitutions that turn
one string into the other.
What it is forScoring a reader requires a way to say how wrong an answer is. Counting
matching positions does not work, because a single missing letter shifts everything after it and
would be scored as if every later letter were also wrong. What is needed is a measure that
understands insertion and deletion, and that is theedit distance, orLevenshtein distance
[20]: the least number of single-character edits needed to turn one string into the other.
Insertion:add a character.cattocartis one edit.
Deletion:remove a character.carttocatis one edit.
Substitution:replace a character.cattocotis one edit.
The recurrenceThis is dynamic programming once more. Let the problem be solved for every
pair of prexes: how many edits turn the rst i characters of one string into the rst j of the
other. The answer for a larger pair follows from three smaller ones, because the last edit must be
one of the three kinds.

=== PDF PAGE 130 ===
T wo implementation economiesOnly the previous row is kept, not the whole table. Each
row depends only on the one before it, so storing all of them would use memory proportional to
the product of the lengths for no purpose; this version uses memory proportional to the shorter
string alone. That is possible because the distance itself is wanted and not the sequence of edits,
which would require the full table to backtrack through, exactly as the seam does.
The function also swaps its arguments so the shorter string controls the row length. The distance
is symmetric, since every edit has an inverse of the same cost, so swapping cannot change the
answer, and it makes the retained row as short as possible.

How it is denedThe edit distance counts errors; accuracy expresses them as a proportion of
the length of the correct text.

where

the oor at zero, explained below
Why it is divided by the truth lengthThe denominator is the length of the correct text,
not of the prediction and not the longer of the two. That choice makes the gure answer "what
fraction of what should have been written came out right", which is the question of interest, and it
makes the measure comparable across lines of dierent lengths. Dividing by the prediction length
instead would let a system improve its score by predicting less.
129

=== PDF PAGE 131 ===
Why the oor at zeroThe edit distance can exceed the length of the correct text, when the
prediction is much longer than the truth: turning a two-character truth into a fty-character pre-
diction takes around fty edits, giving a raw score around−24. Negative accuracy is meaningless
and, more practically, averaging it across a page would let one runaway line dominate the mean
and produce a negative overall accuracy. The oor caps the damage at zero, which is the honest
statement that nothing on that line was right.
The empty-truth case is handled separately: an empty prediction scores 1, anything else scores
0. Without it the division would be by zero. This is the right convention, since correctly
reading a blank line as blank is a success and reading text o a blank line is a complete failure.
5.39 WordAcc
def WordAcc ( pred , truth )
Inputs
pred
the predicted text
truth
the correct text
Output.the fraction of the correct text's words that appear, in order, in the prediction.
Why a second measureCharacter accuracy can be high while the text is still hard to read,
because one wrong character per word leaves every word wrong. Word accuracy is the stricter
measure and is closer to whether a person could use the output: a word counts only if every
character in it is right. The gap between the two gures is informative, since a large gap means
errors are spread thinly across many words rather than concentrated.
Longest common subsequenceThe implementation counts how many of the correct words
appear in the prediction in the same order, which is thelongest common subsequenceof the
two word lists. Asubsequencetakes items in order but allows gaps, so it tolerates both missing
and spurious words without penalising the words around them.

Why case is ignoredWords are compared with case folded, soThematchesthe. That is a
deliberate relaxation: a capitalisation error is a real error and is counted in full by the character
measure, but counting it again as a whole wrong word would double-count one mistake in the
measure meant to indicate readability. A reader is not impeded by a wrong capital.
Words are split on whitespace, so punctuation stays attached to the word it touches and a word
followed by a comma must have that comma right to count. That is strict, and it is part of
why the measured word accuracies sit well below the character accuracies.
5.40 AuthorClassierCNNNumpy
class A u t h o r C l a s s i f i e r C N N N u m p y
Inputs
checkpointPath
which checkpoint to load, defaulting to the namer chosen above
Output.an object holding the weights and the writer name mapping, whose forward method
maps a crop to one raw score per writer.
What it is forThe namer answers aclassicationquestion: given this crop, which of the
enrolled writers produced it. Unlike the reader it produces one answer for the whole crop, not a
sequence, and so it needs no recurrence and no CTC.
What it shares with the reader, and whyThe convolutional backbone is identical: the
same three stages with the same number of groups, the same two poolings, the same collapse of
the height. That is not laziness. The features useful for recognising a letter and the features useful
for recognising a hand are largely the same features: stroke direction, curvature, how strokes join,
the proportions of a letter. The two tasks diverge only in what is done with those features, and
that is exactly where the two networks dier.
131

=== PDF PAGE 133 ===
Where it diers
No recurrence.Writer identity is not a sequential property. There is no analogue of using
a later letter to disambiguate an earlier one, because the answer is a single global label.
A verage pooling over position.The remaining sequence axis is averaged away, turning
the line into one feature vector. A veraging rather than maximising, for the reason given in
that entry: the question is how prevalent a trait is, not whether it occurred.
A two-layer head.A linear layer, a ReLU, then a second linear layer producing one score
per writer. The non-linearity between them lets the decision depend on combinations of
pooled features rather than on each independently, which a single layer could not express.
Raw scores out.The forward pass returns logits, leaving the softmax to the caller, so
that the same output can be used for a decision, which needs only the ranking, or for a
condence, which needs the normalisation.
The dropout layer that is not thereThe head's parameters are numbered 0 and 3, with 1
and 2 missing. Position 1 is the ReLU, which has no parameters, and position 2 isdropout, a
training-time technique that randomly sets a fraction of the values to zero at each step [21]. Its
purpose is to prevent the network relying on any one feature, since that feature may be absent,
which forces redundancy and reduces overtting. At inference time it is disabled entirely, so the
forward pass steps from 0 to 3 and the gap in the numbering is the trace of a layer that exists
only during training.
The writer name mapping is read from the checkpoint alongside the weights and kept on
the object. This is what lets an output slot be reported as a person 's name, and keeping
the two together is what prevents a checkpoint trained on a dierent writer list from silently
misattributing every page.
5.41 ClassifyImage
def C l a s s i f y I m a g e ( image , model , a u t h o r M a p p i n g = None , s t r o k e N o r m a l i s e = True )
Inputs
image
a line crop as a grey image
model
a loaded namer
authorMapping
an optional name-to-slot mapping, unused by this function
strokeNormalise
whether to redraw at a constant pen width rst, default true
Output.a pair: the index of the most likely writer, and the full array of probabilities across all
writers.
What it is forThe namer's equivalent of ReadText: the whole identication in one call, from
a crop to a writer and a set of condences.
132

=== PDF PAGE 134 ===
The normalisation switchThe one real decision in this function is whether to stroke-normalise
rst, and it must match the checkpoint being used. A network trained on normalised crops must
be given normalised crops, and one trained on raw crops must be given raw crops. Passing the
wrong kind does not fail; it produces condent answers from a network looking at a distribution
of inputs it has never seen. The default is to normalise, which suits the shape-based checkpoint
used for judging drawn output, and the ink-based checkpoint is used with the switch turned o.
If normalisation returns nothing, because the crop has no usable ink or no skeleton, the raw crop
is used instead. That is a compromise: the network is then seeing the wrong kind of input. It is
preferred to failing outright because the alternative on a marginal crop is no answer at all, and
the caller can weigh a low-condence answer. The condition is rare, since a crop that produces
no skeleton has essentially no writing in it.
F rom scores to an answerThe logits are turned into probabilities by softmax and the largest
is taken as the prediction. Both are returned, because they answer dierent questions: the index
says who, and the full array says how sure and who the runners-up were. The second is what the
synthesis search uses, since it needs a continuous score to optimise rather than a discrete decision.
As noted under Softmax, these probabilities are normalised across the enrolled writers and so
cannot express that the crop belongs to none of them. A page from an unenrolled writer still
receives a condent-looking answer. TheauthorMappingargument is accepted and not used,
the mapping being carried on the model itself; it remains in the signature for callers that pass
it positionally.
133

=== PDF PAGE 135 ===
6 style.py
This le answers one question: given some pages a person wrote, what is their handwriting?
The answer it produces is astyle prole, which holds a library of that writer's own letterforms
together with measurements of how they lay letters out. synthesise.py then uses that prole to
write new text in the same hand.
The four steps
T race.The ink is thinned to a one-pixel centre line and then followed, turning a picture of
a stroke into an ordered list of points: a path the pen could have taken.
Cut.Each line is divided into individual characters, using the reader from recognise.py to
say where each character sits, so no separate letter-nding method is needed.
Filter.Most cuts are imperfect, so a series of gates rejects anything that is not a plausible
letter. A gate only ever rejects; nothing is invented or repaired.
Measure.Slant, x-height, how far ascenders reach, the gap left between words, and how
joined-up the hand is.
The one convention that makes the rest possibleEvery stored coordinate is measured in
x-heights, not pixels. The x-height is the height of a letter body such as an o or an x, with no
ascender or descender. A glyph is stored with its origin at the left edge of its cell and the baseline
at y = 0, with y increasing upward, and both axes divided by the x-height.
Three consequences follow, and they are why the convention is worth the trouble. The same stored
glyph can be drawn at any size, because scaling is a single multiplication. Glyphs from pages
photographed at dierent distances become directly comparable, so they can be averaged and
compared. And the measurements of the hand describe the writing rather than the photograph,
so a prole built from a close-up page and one built from a distant page agree.
Stored y increases upward, which is the opposite of image coordinates, where row 0 is the top.
The conversion happens once, inside ExtractLineGlyphs, and everything downstream works in
the upward convention because that is how handwriting is naturally described: an ascender has
a positive height and a descender a negative one.
6.1 DATA_DIR
DATA _ DIR = paths . Data . parents [1] / ' Data ' / ' Datasets ' / ' IAMpages10 '
Output.the folder holding the public handwriting database pages.
What it is forNames where the dataset pages live. The path is built from the central paths
module rather than written out, so the project has one denition of where data sits. The dataset
itself is the IAM database of handwritten English text [1], a standard corpus of scanned pages
with transcripts, which is what makes it usable here: the transcripts are what the forced alignment
needs in order to place characters.
134

=== PDF PAGE 136 ===
6.2 CACHE_DIR
CACHE _ DIR = paths . Cache / ' line _ cache _ authors10 '
Output.the folder holding cached per-line extractions.
Why anything is cachedCutting a page into letters is the slow part of building a prole,
because every line has to be read by the network, thinned, traced and divided. The ltering and
measuring that follow are fast. Caching the result of the slow part means that adjusting a lter
threshold, which is the thing most often adjusted, costs seconds instead of re-running the whole
extraction.
This is the ordinary reason for a cache, but the division of labour here is worth noticing as a
design choice: the expensive stage was deliberately made to produce an unltered result, so that
the cheap stage can be re-run against it without invalidating it. A cache that stored ltered
glyphs would have to be thrown away whenever a lter changed, which is exactly when its value
is wanted.
6.3 NOGIT_DIR
NOGIT _ DIR = paths . Cache
Output.the folder for large generated les that are kept out of version control.
Why it existsExtraction caches and glyph libraries run to hundreds of megabytes. Version
control stores a full copy of every revision of every le it tracks, so a large le that changes
often makes the repository grow without bound, and nothing is gained, because the les can be
regenerated from the code and the data. Keeping them under one named directory means a single
ignore rule covers all of them, rather than a rule per le pattern that someone must remember to
extend.
6.4 TEXT_WEIGHTS
TEXT _ WEIGHTS = paths . Weights / ' paper _ cnn _ bilstm _ ctc _ best . npz '
Output.the path of the reader checkpoint used to place character boundaries.
What it is forGlyph extraction needs a reader, because the character positions come from
forced alignment of that reader's output. This constant names which checkpoint to use for that
purpose.
Why the choice of checkpoint matters hereThe quality of the alignment sets the quality
of every glyph cut from it. A reader that is unsure where a character sits produces boundaries
in the wrong place, and a boundary in the wrong place cuts a letter in half or sweeps part of its
neighbour into it. Those are precisely the two defects the lters later spend most of their eort
rejecting. So a stronger reader does not merely improve recognition; it reduces the amount of
work the lters have to do and raises the proportion of cuts that survive them.
135

=== PDF PAGE 137 ===
The lename this constant points at is not present in the weights directory of this project as
it stands. Code that depends on it therefore has to pass an explicit checkpoint path, which the
callers do. It is recorded here because a constant naming a le that does not exist will mislead
anyone reading the module to believe it is the checkpoint actually in use.
6.5 PERSONAL_WEIGHTS
PERSONAL _ WEIGHTS = paths . Weights / ' paper _ cnn _ bilstm _ ctc _ personal _ best .
npz '
Output.the path of the reader checkpoint adapted to the photographed writers.
What makes it dierentA reader trained on the public database has seen thousands of
writers but none of the ve photographed for this project, and it has seen scans rather than
phone photographs. A checkpoint further trained on this project's own pages is better matched
to them on both counts. For alignment, where the text is already known and only its position is
in question, that closer match is directly useful.
The trade-o being madeAdapting a network to a small set of writers risks making it worse
on everyone else, a phenomenon calledcatastrophic forgetting: training on new data overwrites
what was learned from the old. That is acceptable here precisely because this checkpoint has one
narrow job. It is used to align pages from the writers it was adapted to, and the database writers
are aligned with a checkpoint that has seen them.
6.6 RA W_DIR
RAW _ DIR = paths . Cache / ' GlyphCache10 '
Output.the folder holding the uncut, unltered extraction per writer.
What it is forWhere the output of ExtractAuthorRaw is kept, one entry per writer. The
word raw is the important part: what is stored is every glyph the cutter produced, including the
fragments and the over-wide cuts, before any gate has rejected anything. That is what allows a
lter to be changed and the eect measured without re-extracting, and it is also what allowed the
rejection rates quoted in this project's results to be computed at all, since a ltered cache could
not report what it had discarded.
6.7 MAX_V ARIANTS_PER_CHAR
MAX _ VARIANTS _ PER _ CHAR = 12
Output.12, the number of dierent shapes of one letter kept per writer.
Why more than one shape is keptA person does not write a letter identically twice. Storing
one shape per letter would make the output visibly mechanical, because every a on the page would
be the same a, which no handwriting ever is. Keeping several genuine variants lets synthesis choose
among them and gives the output the irregularity real writing has.
136

=== PDF PAGE 138 ===
Why not keep all of themA writer's pages yield far more than twelve examples of a common
letter, and the extras are not free. They enlarge the prole, they slow the search that synthesis
runs over candidate glyphs, and, most importantly, the ones beyond the rst dozen are the least
typical: the variants are sorted by how close they sit to the writer's own usual size and shape
before this limit is applied, so the ones discarded are the outliers. Keeping a bad variant is worse
than keeping no extra variant, because synthesis may select it.
This cap interacts with a separate limit applied at synthesis time, which considers only a few
of the stored variants per character when choosing. The consequence is that a substantial share
of stored variants can never actually be drawn. That is a property of the two limits together
rather than of either alone, and it is worth knowing before the cap here is raised in the hope
of more variety.
6.8 ALIGN_CONF_PCT
ALIGN _ CONF _ PCT = 25
Output.25, the percentile of alignment condence below which a line is not used.
What it is forForced alignment returns a condence, the mean log probability per column
along the chosen path, as described in the entry for CtcForcedAlign. A low value means the reader
could not comfortably t the known text to what it saw, so the character positions it produced
are not to be trusted. Lines in the bottom quarter by this measure are dropped before any glyph
from them is used.
Why a percentile rather than a xed thresholdThis is the important design point. A
xed condence cut would mean something dierent for every writer, because condence depends
on how closely a hand resembles what the reader was trained on. A clearly written upright hand
produces high condences throughout, and a cut placed to suit it would discard almost every line
of a heavily cursive writer, leaving them with no prole at all. Conversely a cut placed low enough
to admit the cursive writer would admit everything from the clear one, including its genuinely
bad lines.
A percentile isrelative: it asks which lines are the worst quarterfor this writer, whatever the
absolute numbers. Every writer therefore contributes their best three quarters, which is the
intended behaviour. The cost is that a writer whose every line aligned badly still contributes their
best three quarters, since the measure cannot express that all of them are poor in absolute terms.
Why a quarterThe gure trades the number of glyphs against their quality. Discarding
nothing admits the lines where alignment failed outright; discarding half would roughly halve the
glyph count, and since a letter needs several examples before a prototype can be formed from it,
that is a real loss for the rarer letters. A quarter removes the clear failures while leaving enough
examples of an uncommon letter such as a q or a z to be usable.
The percentile is only applied when at least eight lines are available. Below that the ranking
is meaningless, since a quarter of ve lines is one line chosen on very little evidence, so the
cut is disabled and every line is kept.
137

=== PDF PAGE 139 ===
6.9 FRAGMENT_PEN_FRAC
FRAGMENT _ PEN _ FRAC = 0.5
Output.0.5, the fraction of a letter's usual pen path that a cut must have to count as a whole
letter.
The defect it targetsThe commonest bad cut is afragment: a boundary placed inside a
letter, so that what is stored is half an a or the rst stroke of an n. A fragment is dangerous in
a way that other bad cuts are not, because it looks like a legitimate small glyph. Its bounding
box is plausible, its stroke count is plausible, and it will be drawn without complaint, producing
a letter that is simply missing half of itself.
Why pen path length is the right measureThe bounding box cannot detect a fragment.
Half of an o spans nearly the full width and the full height of the whole letter while containing half
the ink, so any test on extent accepts it.Pen path length, the total distance the pen travels,
measures how much writing the shape actually represents. Half an o has half the path of a whole
one, and no amount of clever positioning changes that.
Why the threshold is relative to the letter itselfThe comparison is against the median pen
length of that writer's own examples of that same character, not a universal gure. This matters
because letters dier enormously in how much pen path they contain: an i is a short stroke and a
dot, while an m is three arches, perhaps four times the path. A single absolute threshold would
either accept half an m or reject every whole i. Taking each character's own median gives each
letter its own oor, derived from how that writer actually writes it.
penFloor(c) = max

Why one halfThe threshold has to sit between the shortest legitimate variant of a letter and
the longest fragment. Legitimate variation in how much pen path one writer puts into one letter is
modest, perhaps 20 or 30 percent, because they are writing the same shape each time. A fragment,
by denition, is a part of a letter, so it has well under all of it. One half sits in the gap: generous
enough that an unusually compact but complete letter passes, strict enough that a genuine half
rarely does.
138

=== PDF PAGE 140 ===
The median, not the mean, for the reason it is used throughout this project: the pool being
summarised still contains the fragments at the point the oor is computed, and a mean would
be dragged down by them, lowering the very threshold meant to exclude them. The median is
unmoved as long as fragments are a minority.
6.10 MIN_PEN_ABS
MIN _ PEN _ ABS = 0.3
Output.0.30, the least pen path any glyph may have, in x-heights.
Why a relative threshold needs an absolute oor beneath itThe relative test above
compares a glyph with the median of its own character's instances, which fails in one specic
situation: when most of the instances are themselves bad. If two thirds of the cuts of some rare
letter are fragments, the median is a fragment's length, half of that is a quarter of a fragment, and
almost anything passes. The relative test has no way to detect this, because it has no external
reference for what the letter should be.
An absolute oor supplies that reference. Whatever the median says, a glyph with less than
0.3 x-heights of pen path is rejected. Three tenths of an x-height is shorter than the shortest
meaningful mark in handwriting: the stem of an i alone is a full x-height, and a comma or an
apostrophe is a short stroke but still around a third. So the oor cannot reject a real letter while
it does catch the degenerate case.
The pairing of a relative test with an absolute oor appears repeatedly in this le, and the
reason is always the same. A relative test adapts to the writer, which is what is wanted, but
it assumes the population it measures is mostly sound. The oor is what holds when that
assumption fails.
6.11 MIN_LONGEST_STROKE
MIN _ LONGEST _ STROKE = 0.55
Output.0.55, the least length the longest single stroke of a glyph may have, in x-heights.
The defect it targetsTotal pen path can be reached by accumulation: a glyph made of eight
short disconnected scraps has the same total path as one made of a single proper stroke. The
total test therefore accepts a scatter of debris, which is what a cut through a crowded cursive
region produces. This test adds the requirement that at least one of the strokes be substantial on
its own.
Why the longest stroke is the right thing to measureEvery letter contains one dominant
stroke. An l is a single long stroke, the body of an a is one continuous curve, an n has two arches
of which either is substantial. The dominant stroke is the letter's skeleton in the ordinary sense of
the word, and if it is absent then whatever was cut is not a letter however much ink it contains.
Asking for the longest stroke rather than the average also means a legitimate letter with a small
extra mark, such as the dot of an i or the bar of a t, is not penalised for having one short stroke
among its strokes.
139

=== PDF PAGE 141 ===
Why 0.55A little over half an x-height. The shortest dominant stroke in ordinary writing
belongs to a letter like a c or an r, which spans roughly the x-height as a curve and so has a
path at least comparable to it. Setting the bar at just over half gives that case clear room while
excluding the scraps, which are typically a fraction of an x-height each.
6.12 DOT_MIN_AREA
DOT _ MIN _ AREA = 0.004
Output.0.004, the least area a component may have to be treated as a dot rather than dust, in
squared x-heights.
The problem it solvesA dot, such as the one over an i, is nearly round and only a few pixels
across. Thinning reduces it to a single pixel, and a single pixel is not a path, so the polyline
tracer produces nothing for it and the dot is silently lost. An i stored without its dot is a wrong
letterform that will be drawn as a bare stem.
The recovery is to nd connected components of ink that the tracer produced no path for, and
add each as a short horizontal dash across its own width. This threshold separates the dots worth
recovering from the specks that are not.
Why the units are squared x-heights
minDotPx = max
