δa candidate shift, one of nine values from−0.22 to +0.22 x-heights
Nthe number of points per stroke, 44
The alternation of aligning to an estimate and then re-forming the estimate is the general shape
of aniterative renement, the same pattern as the classical algorithms for registering point
sets. It is not guaranteed to nd the best possible joint alignment, but each step cannot make
the agreement worse, and two rounds are enough here because the misalignment being corrected
is small and the rst round removes most of it.
Step four: reject the worst fth, then take the medianAfter alignment, each variant's
mean deviation from the median is computed, and the worst 20 percent are dropped before the
nal median is taken. This is a second line of defence: a variant that survived every earlier
gate but is still wrong will disagree with the aligned consensus, and here it is identied by that
disagreement alone, with no reference to what kind of defect it has.
The median, not the mean, at every stage. Its robustness is exactly what is wanted: a prototype
must not be bent toward a single bad variant, and with the median one outlier among several has
no inuence at all on the result rather than a proportional one.
Returning nothing is common and is not a failure. A character with few examples, or whose
examples disagree about stroke count, has no reliable prototype, and inventing one from two
variants would produce a shape less trustworthy than either. The caller falls back on the
writer's actual variants, which is the right behaviour: a real if imperfect letterform beats a
condently wrong average.
6.39 SmoothStroke
def S m o o t h S t r o k e ( pts , step =0.055 , passes =2)
Inputs
ptsa stroke
step
the spacing to resample to, in x-heights, default 0.055
166

=== PDF PAGE 168 ===
passes
how many smoothing passes, default 2
Output.the stroke resampled evenly and smoothed, as rounded coordinates.
What it is forA traced stroke still carries the pixel staircase: it was assembled from whole-
pixel positions, so it zigzags at the scale of one pixel even where the writer's stroke was smooth.
Drawn by a plotter that staircase is visible, and it also misrepresents the writing, since the writer
drew a curve. This function removes it.
Step one: resample evenlyThe stroke is rst resampled to points about 0.055 x-heights
apart. Even spacing is required before smoothing, because the smoothing averages each point
with its neighbours by index, and if the points are unevenly spaced then that averaging reaches
dierent distances in dierent places, smoothing the sparse parts far more than the dense ones.
Step two: the smoothing pass
q′
k = 0.25q k−1 + 0.5q k + 0.25q k+1 (6.17)
where
q′
k the new position of the k-th point
qk its current position
qk−1, q k+1
its neighbours along the stroke
0.25,0.5,0.25
the weights, which sum to 1 so the stroke is not scaled or shifted
This is a convolution along the stroke with a three-point kernel, the same operation as the prole
smoothing in LinePitch but applied to a path rather than a signal. Weights summing to 1 are
what guarantee the stroke keeps its position and size; weights that summed to less would shrink
it toward the origin a little with every pass.
Two passes are applied. Applying the same small kernel repeatedly is equivalent to one wider
kernel, and it is the cheaper way to get a gentle wide smoothing, since each pass costs three
multiplications per point. Two passes of this kernel remove the one-pixel zigzag while leaving the
curvature of a letter, which acts over many points, essentially untouched.
Why the endpoints are held xedOnly the interior points are smoothed; the rst and last
are kept exactly. The endpoints carry meaning that the interior does not: they are where the pen
entered and left, they are what a join is drawn to, and they dene the glyph's extent. Smoothing
them would pull them inward, shortening every stroke slightly with each pass and, worse, moving
the attachment points that synthesis relies on.
Coordinates are rounded to three decimal places on output. At a scale where 1 is the x-height,
three decimals is a thousandth of a letter's height, far ner than any plotter can draw or any
descriptor can distinguish. The rounding roughly halves the size of the stored prole, which
runs to thousands of strokes, at no cost in delity.
167

=== PDF PAGE 169 ===
6.40 RenderRefStats
def R e n d e r R e f S t a t s ( gray )
Inputs
gray
one real line crop from this writer
Output.a dictionary of measurements describing how that line looks, both to the networks and
geometrically.
What it is for, and the idea behind itReproducing a hand is not only a matter of letter
shapes. The output must alsolooklike a photograph of handwriting to the networks that judge
it, and that involves properties no glyph library captures: how much of the image is ink, how dark
the ink is, how bright the paper is, the proportions of the line. This function measures those on
a real line, so that synthesis has a target to match rather than having to guess.
Background: domain shift Domain shiftis the name for the situation where a model is
used on data drawn from a dierent distribution than it was trained on. A network trained on
photographs of handwriting has learned the statistics of photographs: paper that is not pure
white, ink that is not pure black, and soft grey edges where the two meet. Rendering a synthetic
line as pure black on pure white produces an image that is correct as geometry but statistically
unlike anything in training, and the network's response to it is unreliable, which would corrupt
the judgement of the output.
This is why the paper and ink levels are measured as percentiles of the real crop rather than
assumed: the median of the bright pixels is what this writer's paper actually is, and the median
of the dark pixels is what their ink actually is. The fraction of intermediate greys is measured
too, since that describes the softness of the stroke edges.
Why the word gap is measured from the ink, not the recogniserThe width of the space
character as the aligner reports it is not a reliable measure of how widely this writer spaces their
words. CTC concentrates its output and tends to assign a space a narrow territory, well inside
the actual gap. So the gap is measured directly instead: the columns with no ink are found, the
runs between inked columns are collected, those under half an x-height are discarded as being
gaps within a word, and the median of the rest is taken.
The half-x-height threshold is what separates the two kinds of gap. Within a word the pen may
lift, leaving a small gap, but a word boundary is a deliberate space and is substantially wider.
The median of the wide gaps therefore describes word spacing, and the median again rather than
the mean, so that one unusually wide gap at a paragraph end does not inate it.
Why slant, ascender and descender are measured here tooThese are also measured
in the prole from the glyphs themselves, so measuring them again on the rendered line looks
redundant. It is not, and the comment in the code gives the reason: these are measuredthe
same waythey will be measured on the synthesised output. A quantity measured by two dierent
methods is not comparable with itself, so to check whether the synthesis reproduced the writer's
slant, both sides must be measured by the same procedure. These values exist to be the reference
half of that comparison.
168

=== PDF PAGE 170 ===
Fields that cannot be measured are lled with a not-a-number value rather than a plausible
default. That is deliberate: the caller takes a median across lines with those values excluded,
so a line that could not be measured contributes nothing, whereas a default would contribute a
fabricated measurement that is indistinguishable from a real one.
6.41 _MedianWordGap
def _ M e d i a n W o r d G a p ( refs , s pa ce Ad vs )
Inputs
refs
the per-line reference statistics
spaceAdvs
the widths the aligner gave to space characters
Output.the typical gap this writer leaves between words, in x-heights.
The preference orderThree sources are tried in order. The measured ink gaps from Ren-
derRefStats are preferred, because they measure the real thing. If none is available, the aligner's
space widths are used, which are biased narrow for the reason given above but are at least derived
from this writer. If there are no spaces at all, 1.2 x-heights is returned as a generic value.
Why the fallback order is this way roundIt descends from the most direct evidence to
the least. The same cascade pattern appears in LinePitch and in the checkpoint selection: try
the best-informed method, fall back only when it cannot apply. The consequence worth noting is
that the fallback is known to be biased, so a prole built from a writer with no measurable gaps
will have word spacing that is too narrow, and that is a better failure than having none at all.
Not-a-number values are dropped by testing whether each value equals itself, which is false only
for not-a-number. That is the standard idiom and it works without importing anything, which
is why it is used in a le that avoids dependencies.
6.42 ExtractAuthorRaw
def E x t r a c t A u t h o r R a w ( authorId , lineItems , model )
Inputs
authorId
which writer these lines belong to
lineItems
pairs of a line crop and its known text
model
a loaded reader for alignment
Output.a list of per-line results, each a pair of the glyphs cut from that line and its statistics.
169

=== PDF PAGE 171 ===
What it is forRuns the cutter over every line of one writer and collects the results. It is the
expensive half of prole building, and the half that gets cached.
Why the output is deliberately unlteredNothing is rejected here. Every glyph the cutter
produced is kept, including the fragments and the over-wide cuts. This is the design decision
that makes the lters tunable: the ltering happens in BuildAuthorProle, which runs on this
output, so a threshold can be changed and the eect measured in seconds against a cache that
is still valid. Had ltering happened here, every change to a lter would require re-reading and
re-cutting every page.
It is also what made the project's rejection statistics possible. Reporting what fraction of cuts
were discarded, and why, requires having kept the discarded ones; a pipeline that ltered as it
extracted could report only what survived.
A line whose extraction returned nothing is skipped rather than recorded as empty. Those are
the lines where alignment failed outright or no x-height band could be found, which carry no
glyphs and no usable statistics, so an entry for them would only have to be ltered out again
by every consumer.
6.43 BuildAuthorProle
def B u i l d A u t h o r P r o f i l e ( authorId , parsed , refs = None , prior = None , verbose =
True )
Inputs
authorId
which writer this prole is for
parsed
the raw per-line extraction from ExtractAuthorRaw
refs
the per-line reference statistics, taken from the extraction if not supplied
prior
the cross-writer reference descriptors
verbose
whether to print a one-line summary, default true
Output.the nished style prole as a dictionary, or nothing if the writer has too little usable
data.
What it is forThe second half of prole building, and the cheap one. It takes everything the
cutter produced, discards what is not a letter, keeps the most typical examples of what remains,
builds fallback letterforms, and measures the hand. Its output is the prole that synthesise.py
consumes.
170

=== PDF PAGE 172 ===
Stage one: discard the badly aligned linesLines in the worst quarter by alignment con-
dence are dropped before anything else, for the reasons set out under ALIGN_CONF_PCT.
This is rst because it is the only lter that acts on whole lines: a line whose alignment failed
has every boundary wrong, so its glyphs are unreliable as a set, and judging them individually
afterwards would be more work for a worse result.
Stage two: the gates, cheapest rstEvery surviving glyph passes through a series of tests,
each of which can only reject. The order is by cost, so that the expensive shape comparisons run
on the fewest glyphs.
Extent.Width within bounds, which depends on the character; height over 0.25 x-heights;
no more than six strokes. Three comparisons on numbers already stored.
Height class.Does the ink reach where this letter should reach, from _ClassOk.
Cell overow.The ink must not be much wider than the cell it was cut from: lead plus
width at most 1.45 times the advance plus 0.25. Ink spilling well outside its own cell means
the cut took part of a neighbour, which is a dierent and more sensitive test than the
absolute width limit.
Pen path.Total path above the per-character oor, and longest single stroke above 0.55
x-heights.
Size consistency .Width between 0.6 and 1.5 times the 40th percentile of the surviving
widths, and height between 0.55 and 1.7 times the median height. Applied only if at least
three variants survive it.
Cross-writer prior,theninternal consensus.The two descriptor-based lters, in that
order, for the reason given under _ConsensusFilter.
Why the 40th percentile and not the medianThe reference width for the consistency test
is taken at the 40th percentile rather than the middle. The reason is specic: a doubled letter,
where the cut took two copies, is wide, and if such cuts are common for a character they push the
median up and the test then accepts them while rejecting the correct narrow ones. Anchoring a
little below the middle biases the reference toward the narrower, more likely correct, population.
Stage three: the two fallback letterformsTwo single letterforms are built per character,
and they are dierent things built for dierent purposes.
idealMedoid:the one real variant most typical of this writer's own set, found as the medoid
of the descriptors. It is genuine writing, with all the irregularity that implies.
idealGlyphs:the denoised prototype, the per-point median of several aligned variants. It
is cleaner than any real variant but is an average, so it is not something the writer actually
wrote.
Keeping both lets the choice be made according to what is needed: the medoid when authenticity
matters, the prototype when legibility does. That trade-o between resembling the writer and
being readable is the central tension of the whole project, and having both forms available is what
lets synthesis navigate it.
171

=== PDF PAGE 173 ===
When the prototype is promoted to a variant
promote⇐ ⇒D proto < Draw −0.02andD proto <0.32(6.18)
where
Dproto
the prior distance of the denoised prototype
Draw
the median prior distance of this character's kept variants
0.02the margin by which the prototype must beat them, so a tie does not count
0.32an absolute ceiling, so a prototype that is merely better than a bad set is still not accepted
⇐ ⇒if and only if: the promotion happens exactly when both conditions hold
The prototype is placed at the front of the variant list, so that it is drawn in preference, only
when it is measurably closer to the cross-writer idea of the letter than the writer's own variants
are. Both conditions matter. The margin prevents promotion on a dierence within noise. The
absolute ceiling prevents the comparison being won by default when every variant is poor, which
is exactly the situation where an averaged shape is least trustworthy, since averaging several bad
variants gives a clean rendering of a bad shape.
Stage four: measuring the hand
slantDeg,the median per-line slant: how far the writing leans.
xHeightPx,the median x-height in pixels, which records the scale the prole was built at.
strokeWidthXh,ink area divided by skeleton length, which is the average stroke thickness,
expressed in x-heights.
ascender and descender,how far above and below the band this writer's letters actually
reach, measured only from glyphs that clearly do reach, so that a mis-cut at l cannot pull
the ascender gure down.
wordSpaceXh,the gap left between words.
connectedness,the fraction of adjacent in-word letter pairs where ink genuinely crossed
the boundary.
letterAdvance,the median advance per character, which preserves this writer's own letter
spacing per letter rather than one global gure.
Why connectedness is measured this way
connectedness =
{adjacent in-word pairs with ink crossing the cut}

{adjacent in-word pairs}
 (6.19)
where
connectedness
a number from 0 for fully separated printing to 1 for fully joined cursive
172

=== PDF PAGE 174 ===
adjacent in-word pairs
pairs of neighbouring characters where both were cut successfully and neither is a space
The numerator counts the pairs where a traced centre line actually passed through the boundary,
which means the pen was never lifted there. That is the honest denition, as opposed to inferring
joining from the gap between letters, which confuses tight spacing with genuine connection. Spaces
are excluded because letters either side of a space are not expected to join and including them
would make the measure depend on how many words the sample happened to contain.
This single number turns out to be the most useful descriptor of a hand in the whole prole.
Synthesis uses it to decide how to join letters, and it is the gure this project's results use to
separate print-like hands from cursive ones, since reproduction accuracy falls as it rises.
The two ways the function declines to produce a proleNothing is returned when there
are no parsed lines at all, and again when no line survived the condence cut with a measurable
x-height. Returning nothing rather than a prole with empty elds is the right behaviour, because
every consumer of a prole assumes its measurements are real, and a prole full of defaults would
be used condently and produce output in nobody's hand.
The verbose summary printed at the end reports the line count, character count, variant count,
slant, connectedness and reach. Those six numbers are enough to recognise a failed build at a
glance: a writer with far fewer variants than usual has had most of their cuts rejected, and a
connectedness of 0 or 1 exactly is almost always a sign that the crossing detection has gone
wrong rather than a real hand.
173

=== PDF PAGE 175 ===
7 synthesise.py
This le does the reverse of style.py. Given a style prole and some text, it produces a pen path:
an ordered list of strokes in millimetres that a plotter can draw, written in the hand the prole
describes.
The central tension of the whole projectTwo goals pull against each other here, and most
of the design is a response to that. The output shouldlook like the writer, which argues for
using their own stored letterforms exactly as extracted. And the output shouldbe readable,
which argues for clean, well-formed letters. These conict because the stored letterforms are not
clean: they were cut from joined-up writing at boundaries that often do not exist, so a proportion
of them are fragments or carry part of a neighbour. Drawing those faithfully reproduces the
writer's hand and some of the extraction's mistakes along with it.
The le resolves this with a single number, called thelegibility lambdaand written as lam,
running from 0 to 1. At 0 the writer's own shapes are always used. At 1 they are abandoned
entirely in favour of a fallback letterform. In between, each glyph is swapped with a probability
that depends both on lam and on how far that particular shape sits from what the letter should
look like. The value is derived automatically per writer, so a hand whose library extracted cleanly
keeps its own shapes and one whose library is mostly fragments does not.
The three sources a letter can come from
own:a variant cut from this writer's own pages. The most faithful and the least reliable.
core, the anchor:a single clean letterform. Preferably the writer's own most typical
variant of that letter, falling back to a built-in alphabet when even that is too rough.
fallback:the built-in alphabet directly, used when the writer has no example of a character
at all.
Every rendered glyph records which source it came from, and the counts are returned in the
trajectory's metadata. That is what makes the trade-o measurable rather than a matter of
impression: a page drawn mostly from the anchor is readable but is not really the writer's hand,
and the counts say which happened.
UnitsGlyphs arrive in x-heights, as stored. Layout converts them to millimetres using the
requested x-height size, and the rasteriser converts millimetres to pixels. So three coordinate
systems appear in this le, in that order, and the conversions happen at exactly two points.
7.1 SCRIPT_DIR
SCRIPT _ DIR = Path ( _ _ file _ _ ) . resolve () . parent
Output.the directory this le sits in.
174

=== PDF PAGE 176 ===
What it is forRecords the module's own location, from which a path to data beside the code
could be built. The project's paths are otherwise dened centrally in paths.py, which is the better
practice, since one denition serves the whole project.
This constant is assigned and never read anywhere in the module. It is a remnant of an earlier
layout, before the paths module existed. It is harmless, but a reader should not take its presence
as evidence that the le locates its data this way.
7.2 CNN_DATA_DIR
CNN _ DATA _ DIR = Path ( _ _ file _ _ ) . resolve () . parent . parent / ' CNN '
Output.a path to a sibling directory of network data.
What it is forPoints at a folder beside the pipeline holding network-related data, built by
stepping up from this le's own location.
Like the constant above, this is assigned and never read. Both are left in place rather than
removed because removing a name is a change that has to be checked against every caller, and
neither does any harm; but neither describes how the module actually nds anything.
7.3 _FB
_ FB = { 'a ': [[(0.75 ,0.75) ,(0.45 ,0.95) ,(0.15 ,0.75) ,(0.1 ,0.4) ,(0.35 ,0.05)
,(0.65 ,0.1) ,
(0.78 ,0.35) ,(0.78 ,0.0) ] ,[(0.78 ,0.3) ,(0.95 ,0.0) ]] , ' b ': [[(0.1 ,1.8)
,(0.1 ,
0.0) ] ,[(0.1 ,0.55) ,(0.35 ,0.85) ,(0.7 ,0.7) ,(0.75 ,0.3) ,(0.5 ,0.0)
,(0.15 ,0.1) ]] ,
'c ': [[(0.8 ,0.75) ,(0.5 ,0.95) ,...}
Output.a dictionary mapping a character to a built-in printed letterform, as a list of strokes in
x-heights.
What it is and why it existsA hand-written alphabet of 47 characters: the 26 lowercase
letters, the ten digits, and eleven punctuation marks, each given as one or more strokes of points
in the same x-height convention the extracted glyphs use. These are the letterforms of last resort.
Three situations require them. A writer may have no example of a character at all, which is
common: a page of ordinary English contains no q, z or semicolon. A writer's examples of some
character may all have been rejected by the lters. And the legibility mechanism may decide a
stored shape is too far from a letter to be drawn, in which case something must take its place.
Why they are written out by handThey could not be obtained any other way. A font is
not usable: a font describes letters as lled outlines, and what is needed here is the path a pen
travels along the centre of a stroke, which is a dierent object. Deriving centre lines from a font
by thinning would reintroduce every artefact that the extraction pipeline exists to deal with, for
letters that are supposed to be the clean reference. So the coordinates were laid out directly, in
the same coordinate system as the stored glyphs, so that a fallback letter can be substituted for
an extracted one with no conversion at all.
175

=== PDF PAGE 177 ===
The coordinate conventionA point of (0.4, 0.7) means four tenths of an x-height to the right
of the glyph's origin and seven tenths of an x-height above the baseline. A y above 1.0 is in the
ascender zone and a negative y is below the baseline, in the descender zone. Being in the same
convention is what allows _BaselineGlyph to stretch these shapes to a particular writer's reach
by rescaling only the parts outside the band.
There are no uppercase letters in this dictionary. A capital is handled instead by taking the
lowercase form and scaling it up to the writer's ascender height, which is what _GlyphSource
does under the labelcase. That is correct for letters whose capital is a larger version of the
lowercase, such as C, O, S and W, and is a visible approximation for the ones where it is not,
such as A, E and R.
7.4 _CURSIVE
_ CURSIVE = { 'a ': [[(0.0 ,0.3) ,(0.2 ,0.62) ,(0.46 ,0.72) ,(0.62 ,0.52)
,(0.6 ,0.18) ,(0.42 ,
0.02) ,(0.2 ,0.1) ,(0.16 ,0.34) ,(0.3 ,0.52) ,(0.52 ,0.46) ,(0.62 ,0.2)
,(0.64 ,0.02) ,
(0.86 ,0.3) ]] , ' b ': [[(0.0 ,0.3) ,(0.14 ,1.1) ,(0.24 ,1.66) ,(0.3 ,1.2)
,(0.26 ,0.44) ,
(0.34 ,0.1) ,(0.56 ,0.04) ,(0.7 ,...}
Output.a dictionary mapping each lowercase letter to a joined-up letterform.
Why a second alphabet is neededThe printed alphabet above is wrong for a cursive hand,
and not merely in appearance. A printed letterform begins and ends wherever is convenient, often
at the top, and has no provision for a stroke arriving from the previous letter or leaving toward
the next. Substituting one into joined writing produces a letter that cannot be connected: the
join from the preceding letter has nowhere to attach, so either it is omitted, leaving a visible break
in the middle of a word, or it is drawn to a point on the letter that no writer would join to.
These forms are drawn to enter near the baseline on the left and leave near the baseline on the
right, which is what a cursive letter does, so a ligature can be drawn to and from them naturally.
Which alphabet is chosenThe choice is made per writer, from their measured connectedness:
a hand at or above 0.5 gets the cursive forms and one below gets the printed ones. Using a single
number measured from their own writing, rather than a setting, means the decision follows the
evidence; see CORE_CURSIVE_CONN.
This alphabet holds only the 26 lowercase letters, where the printed one also holds digits and
punctuation. That is sucient because joining only ever applies between two adjacent alpha-
betic characters, so a digit or a comma never needs a joinable form. Where a cursive writer
needs one of those, the lookup falls through to the printed alphabet.
7.5 _FB_ADV
_ FB _ ADV = 1.0
Output.1.0, the advance after a fallback letter, in x-heights.
176

=== PDF PAGE 178 ===
What an advance isTheadvanceis how far the pen origin moves to the right after drawing
a character, before the next one is placed. It is not the same as the character's width: it includes
the space that belongs between this letter and the next, so advancing by the width alone would
butt every letter against its neighbour.
For extracted glyphs the advance is measured from the cell the character was cut from, which is
what preserves the writer's own spacing. A built-in letterform has no such measurement, so this
constant supplies one: one x-height, which is approximately the advance of a lowercase letter in
ordinary handwriting.
It is used only when the writer's prole has no recorded advance for the character either.
Where the prole does have one, even though the shape is a fallback, the writer's own gure
is preferred, so the spacing stays theirs even when the letterform is not.
7.6 _SIMILAR
_ SIMILAR = { 'I ': 'l ' , 'O ': '0 ' , 'l ': 'I ' , '0 ': 'O ' , 'o ': '0 ' , '; ': ': ' , '" ':
" '" , '! ': 'l '}
Output.a mapping from a character to another whose letterform can stand in for it.
What it is forSome characters are near-identical in handwriting, so one writer's example of
the one can serve for the other. A capital I and a lowercase l are the same vertical stroke in most
hands. A capital O and a zero dier by convention rather than by shape. A semicolon is a colon
with a tail, an opening quote is a pair of apostrophes, and an exclamation mark is a vertical stroke
with a dot.
Where it sits in the order of preferenceIt is consulted after the writer's own examples of
the character itself and after the opposite case of it, and before resorting to the anchor or the
built-in alphabet. The ordering reects how much of the writer's own hand each option preserves:
their own l really is their own stroke, so borrowing it for an I is closer to their writing than
substituting a designed letterform would be.
The mapping is deliberately not symmetric in every case. An exclamation mark may borrow
an l, but an l may not borrow an exclamation mark, because the dot would come with it.
Substitution is only valid in the direction where nothing spurious is inherited.

The x-height band itself, from 0 to 1, is left untouched, and that is the point of the piecewise
form. The band is the reference against which everything is measured, so it is 1 by denition and
rescaling it would be meaningless. What varies between writers is how far they reachbeyondthe
band, and those are exactly the two zones the formula adjusts. A letter like an o, entirely inside
the band, is therefore unchanged, while an l is stretched or compressed only above y = 1, leaving
its body the same size as its neighbours'.
Why there is a oor of 0.35A writer whose measured ascender is close to 1.0, which happens
when the measurement failed or when most of their ascender letters were rejected, would otherwise
give a ratio near zero and atten every ascender to the height of the band. An l indistinguishable
from an e is worse than a slightly wrong l, so the oor keeps at least a third of the designed reach
whatever the measurement says.
How the advance is derivedThe advance is the shape's own horizontal extent plus 0.12
x-heights of spacing, computed from the points rather than stored. Deriving it means the two
alphabets need no advance table to be maintained alongside them, and the margin is added so
that consecutive fallback letters do not touch.
178

=== PDF PAGE 180 ===
7.8 UNIFORM_INK
UNIFORM _ INK = True
Output.true, meaning every writer is drawn at the same pen width.
Why the ink is uniform, and why that is not a limitationThe prole records the writer's
own stroke width, so the renderer could vary the pen width per writer. By default it does not,
and the reason is that the machine cannot. The plotter has one pen tted in one holder; every
stroke it draws is the same width. Rendering a writer with a thick pen when the machine will
draw them with the standard one would produce an image unlike the thing being built.
That matters because the rendered image is not only for display: it is what the two judging
networks score during the best-of-N search. A search that optimised against images the machine
cannot produce would be optimising the wrong quantity. So the renderer is made to match the
hardware, and the search then measures what will actually be drawn.
The non-uniform path is retained and is reachable by passing the ag, because it is the right
choice when rendering a comparison against a real photographed page, where the real page does
have the writer's own pen width. The default matches the machine; the option matches the
camera.
7.9 UNIFORM_PEN_WIDTH_XH
UNIFORM _ PEN _ WIDTH _ XH = 0.085
Output.0.085, the uniform pen width as a fraction of the x-height.
Why the width is relative to the x-heightA width in pixels or millimetres would be right
at one writing size only. Expressed as a fraction of the x-height it scales with the writing, so text
drawn large and small both look like the same pen, which is what a real pen does.
Why it is thinThe code comment records that this value was reduced from 0.11, and the
reason is specic: a heavy stroke lls small loops. The enclosed white space inside an a, e or o is
only a fraction of an x-height across, and a pen drawn at 0.11 closes it, turning an e into a c with
a lled top and an a into a blob. Those are precisely the confusions that cost character accuracy,
so the thinner pen is chosen to keep the counters open.
The trade-o is that a thinner stroke carries less ink, so a rendered line is lighter than a pho-
tographed one. That is addressed separately, by the soft edge below and by the ink density
matching, rather than by thickening the pen.
7.10 UNIFORM_SOFT_FRAC
UNIFORM _ SOFT _ FRAC = 0.3
Output.0.30, the blur radius as a fraction of the pen width.
179

=== PDF PAGE 181 ===
Why a drawn stroke is blurredA rasterised stroke has a hard edge: a pixel is ink or paper
with nothing in between. A photographed pen stroke does not. Ink spreads slightly into the
paper, the camera's optics are not perfect, and the sensor averages over each pixel's area, so a
real stroke edge is a gradient a pixel or two wide.
This is the domain-shift problem described under RenderRefStats. The networks judging the
output were trained on photographs, so they have learned what a real stroke edge looks like.
A hard-edged synthetic stroke is unlike anything in their training data, and their response to it
is unreliable, which would corrupt the very scores the search is optimising. Blurring makes the
rendered stroke statistically closer to a photographed one.
Why the radius is a fraction of the pen widthBecause the physical cause scales that way.
The spread of ink and the softness of a stroke edge are roughly proportional to how much ink
was laid down, so a thick stroke has a wider soft edge than a thin one. A xed blur radius would
over-soften a thin stroke, which at this pen width would close the small loops the thin pen was
chosen to keep open, and under-soften a thick one.
7.11 JOIN_PROB_CAP
JOIN _ PROB _ CAP = 1.0
Output.1.0, the greatest probability with which two letters may be joined.
What it would doJoining is decided per pair of letters by a random draw against the writer's
connectedness, so a hand measured at 0.8 joins about four pairs in ve. This constant caps that
probability, so a value below 1 would force even a fully joined hand to leave some pairs unjoined.
Why it is set to 1, which disables itAt 1.0 the cap never binds and the writer's measured
connectedness is used as it stands. It is kept as a named constant rather than removed because it is
the natural place to intervene if fully joined output proves unreadable, and because the alternative
of editing the join condition itself is a change in behaviour hidden inside a long function. A constant
at its neutral value documents an available adjustment.
Deciding each pair by a random draw, rather than by comparing connectedness against a
threshold, is the substantive choice here. A threshold would make a hand either fully joined
or fully separated, with nothing in between, whereas real writing of intermediate connectedness
joins some pairs and not others. The draw reproduces that.
7.12 CORE_SLANT_CAP_DEG
CORE _ SLANT _ CAP _ DEG = 22.0
Output.22.0, the greatest lean in degrees applied to a printed anchor letterform.
What it is forEvery glyph is sheared to the writer's measured slant, so the page leans as they
do. An anchor letterform is sheared too, because a perfectly upright substitute in a slanted hand
is conspicuous. But it is sheared less, and this constant is the limit.
180

=== PDF PAGE 182 ===
Why the anchor is treated dierentlyThe anchor exists to be readable; that is the whole
reason it was substituted for the writer's own shape. Shearing degrades legibility, because a
letterform drawn upright and then slanted is not the same as a letterform designed at that slant:
the curves distort, loops narrow, and at a steep angle an o becomes a thin diagonal ellipse.
Applying a 42-degree slant to the anchor would undo the substitution's purpose.
So the anchor keeps the writer's lean up to a point and then stops. Twenty-two degrees is a visible
lean, enough that the substituted letter does not stand upright among slanted neighbours, while
being well short of the angle at which the shear starts closing loops. A cursive anchor is allowed
30 degrees, since those forms were drawn to be joined and already lean.
This is one of several places where the anchor is deliberately held back from the writer's full
style: it also receives less jitter and only a fraction of the baseline drift. The pattern is
consistent, and the reasoning is always the same: style is applied to the anchor only as far as
it does not cost the legibility that justied using it.
7.13 CORE_JITTER_FRAC
CORE _ JITTER _ FRAC = 0.15
Output.0.15, the fraction of the usual random variation applied to an anchor letterform.
What it is forEach glyph is nudged, rotated and scaled slightly at random so that repeated
letters are not identical; see _Jitter. An anchor receives only 15 percent of that, so it barely
wobbles.
WhyThe same reasoning as the slant cap. Jitter exists to make the output look handwritten
rather than printed, which is a cosmetic goal, and it costs a little legibility, which is why the
amount is small to begin with. An anchor was substituted precisely because legibility was at risk
for that character, so it is the glyph that can least aord the cost. Keeping a small fraction rather
than none at all means an anchor among its neighbours is not conspicuously rigid.
7.14 COLLISION_MIN_GAP_XH
CO L LI SI ON _ MIN _ GAP _ XH = 0.05
Output.0.05, the clear space forced between one glyph's ink and the next, in x-heights.
The problem it solvesPlacing glyphs by their advances assumes each glyph's ink ts inside
the space its advance allots. That assumption fails regularly. A variant may be wider than the
advance recorded for its character; the random scaling may enlarge it; the shear may push the
top of a tall letter well to the right of its base. When it fails, the next glyph is drawn on top of
the previous one, and two overlapping letters are usually unreadable as either.
How it is detected and xedAfter a glyph is placed, its leftmost inked point is compared
with the rightmost inked point of the previous glyph's body. If the gap is less than this constant,
the pen is pushed right by the shortfall and the glyph is placed again at the new position.
181

=== PDF PAGE 183 ===
need =x prev
max + 0.05 xh−x cur
min (7.2)
where
need
how far the current glyph must be pushed right; nothing is done when it is not positive
xprev
max
the rightmost inked point of the previous glyph's body stroke
xcur
min
the leftmost inked point of the glyph just placed
xhthe x-height in millimetres, converting the constant into the working units
Measuring actual ink positions rather than advances is what makes this work: it tests the thing
that matters, where the ink really landed, after every transformation has been applied. A check
based on advances could not see a collision caused by the shear, since the shear does not change
the advance.
Why the correction only ever pushes rightWriting proceeds left to right and the previous
glyph has already been committed, so the only available remedy is to move the current glyph
on. Pulling it left would reintroduce the collision, and moving the previous one would require
reconsidering everything before it. The cost of pushing right is a slightly wider than intended gap,
which is a far smaller defect than an overlap.
7.15 _ELIGIBLE_V ARIANTS
_ ELIGIBLE _ VARIANTS = 5
Output.5, how many of a character's stored variants may be chosen from.
The problem it solvesUp to twelve variants of a letter are stored, and it might seem that
drawing from all of them would give the most natural variety. It does the opposite, and the reason
is worth stating carefully. A person forms a given letter fairly consistently: their a's dier in detail
but share a shape. The stored variants are ranked by how close each sits to what the letter should
look like, so the rst few are that consistent shape and the tail is the unusual ones, including the
mis-cuts that survived ltering.
Sampling evenly over all twelve therefore draws from the tail as often as from the characteristic
shape. The output varies more, but what it varies over is no longer the writer's hand: it is a
mixture of their hand and the extraction's errors. The result reads as scrambled rather than
natural, and the writer identication drops, because the signature being averaged away is exactly
what the namer recognises.
Why veEnough variants that repeated letters in a line are visibly dierent, which one or two
would not achieve, and few enough that all of them are drawn from the well-formed part of the
ranking. The selection among those ve is itself weighted toward the rst, so even within the
eligible set the most typical shape is drawn most often.
182

=== PDF PAGE 184 ===
This cap and the storage cap of twelve interact in a way worth knowing: variants ranked sixth
and below are stored but can never be drawn. For this project's proles that is a substantial
fraction of everything stored. The storage is not wasted, since the whole set is what the medoid
and the prototype are computed from, but raising the storage cap alone would not produce more
variety in the output.
7.16 CORE_CURSIVE_CONN
CORE _ CURSIVE _ CONN = 0.5
Output.0.5, the connectedness at or above which a hand is treated as cursive.
What it decidesWhich of the two built-in alphabets a writer's anchor letters come from, and
whether an anchor may be joined to its neighbours at all. A hand at or above 0.5 gets the cursive
forms and may join them; below, the printed forms, which never join.
Why the midpointConnectedness is the fraction of adjacent in-word letter pairs that were
genuinely joined, so 0.5 means half. That is the natural place to divide, and it is not a tuned
value: a hand that joins more pairs than it separates is cursive in the ordinary sense of the word,
and one that separates more is not.
Why a threshold is acceptable here, unlike for joiningA hard threshold was rejected for
deciding whether to join a given pair, because real writing of intermediate connectedness joins
some pairs and not others, which a threshold cannot express. Here a threshold is right, because
the thing being chosen is which alphabet to draw from, and there are only two alphabets. There is
no meaningful way to be half-way between the printed form of an a and the cursive one; a choice
must be made, and the measured majority behaviour is the sound basis for making it.
7.17 JOIN_LEGIBILITY_COUPLING
JOIN _ L E G I B I L I T Y _ COUPLING = 0.0
Output.0.0, how much the legibility setting reduces joining.
What it would doJoins are a major cause of illegibility in synthetic cursive: a ligature drawn
to the wrong point, or several in succession, turns a word into a tangle. This constant would let
the legibility setting thin the joins out, so that a writer whose library is poor would also be drawn
with fewer joins.
conneff = conn·

conneff

conn

κthis constant, the coupling strength
lamthe legibility setting, from 0 to 1
Why it is zeroAt zero the expression reduces to the measured connectedness and the cou-
pling has no eect, which is the current behaviour. The code comment marks this as deliberate.
The reasoning is that connectedness is the single most recognisable property of a hand, and the
namer relies on it heavily: thinning the joins of a cursive writer makes the output more readable
and markedly less like them. Since that writer's identication is already the weaker of the two
measures, trading it away for text accuracy is the wrong direction.
Like JOIN_PROB_CAP, this is a mechanism left in place at its neutral setting. Both are
the result of trying the adjustment and nding it harmful, and leaving the constant named at
zero records that the possibility was considered, which removing it would not.
7.18 IDEAL_ANCHOR_MAX_PRIORD
IDEAL _ ANCHOR _ MAX _ PRIORD = 0.3
Output.0.30, how far from typical the writer's own best letterform may sit and still serve as the
anchor.
The decision it governsWhen a glyph needs replacing, the rst choice of replacement is the
writer'sownmost typical version of that letter, not a built-in form. That keeps the substitution
inside their hand. But their most typical version is only useful if it is itself a decent letter, and
for a heavily cursive writer it may not be: if most of their a's were cut badly, the most typical
of them is a typical bad cut. This constant is where that judgement is made, against the prior
distance described in style.py.
Why 0.30The prior distance runs from 0 for a shape matching the cross-writer reference exactly
upward. The same scale appears in the legibility thresholds, where 0.12 counts as clean and 0.55
as unusable. At 0.30 the limit sits between those: a shape noticeably unlike the general idea of
the letter is still accepted, because it is the writer's own and the alternative is a generic form,
but one approaching the unusable range is not. It is deliberately permissive, since preferring the
writer's own shape is the whole point of trying them rst.
7.19 IDEAL_SOURCE
IDEAL _ SOURCE = ' medoid '
Output.'medoid', selecting which of the two stored fallback letterforms is used as the anchor.
The two candidatesstyle.py stores two single letterforms per character. Themedoidis the
one real variant most typical of the writer's own set. Theprototype, stored as idealGlyphs, is
the per-point median of several aligned variants, which is cleaner than any real variant but is an
average and so is not something the writer ever wrote.
184

=== PDF PAGE 186 ===
Why the medoid is chosenIt is real writing. An averaged shape has had the irregularity
removed along with the noise, and that irregularity is part of what identies the writer, so a page
of prototypes is smoother and less recognisable. The medoid keeps the writer's own character
while still being the best-formed example they produced.
There is a second reason, visible in this project's measurements. Prototype-based anchors im-
proved text accuracy slightly and cost more in writer identication than they gained, which is the
wrong trade for a system whose purpose is to reproduce an individual hand. The constant is a
string rather than a boolean so that switching between them is a one-word change, which is how
that comparison was made.
7.20 _PRIOR_REJECT
_ PRIOR _ REJECT = 0.25
Output.0.25, a prior distance beyond which a stored variant would not be oered.
What it was forA hard cut-o: a stored shape further than this from the cross-writer reference
would simply not be considered for drawing, however the legibility setting stood.
Why the approach it represents was supersededA hard rejection is the wrong instrument
for this decision, and comparing it with what replaced it is instructive. It admits no middle ground:
a variant at 0.249 is used freely and one at 0.251 is never used, although nothing distinguishes
them. For a writer whose every variant of some letter sits above the line it rejects all of them,
leaving no shape at all. And it cannot take account of how trustworthy the library is as a whole,
so it treats a stray bad variant in a good library the same as a uniformly poor one.
The mechanism that replaced it, the probabilistic swap in _SwapProb, addresses all three. It
is graded rather than binary, so borderline shapes are used sometimes; it always leaves a shape
available, because swapping requires a replacement to exist; and it is driven by a per-writer
legibility value, so the same variant is treated dierently depending on the company it keeps.
This constant is declared and never read. It is recorded here as the earlier design rather than
as live behaviour, because a reader nding it in the settings block would reasonably assume
variants beyond 0.25 are excluded, and they are not.
7.21 Trajectory
class T r a j e c t o r y
Output.an object holding the pen path: a list of pen-down strokes in millimetres, plus metadata
describing how it was produced.
What it representsThe output of synthesis, and the thing the rest of the project consumes.
It is a list ofpen-down paths: each entry is a sequence of points the pen visits with its tip on
the paper, and the gaps between consecutive entries are where the pen lifts. That representation
carries exactly the information a plotter needs and nothing more.
185

=== PDF PAGE 187 ===
Why the pen-up moves are implicitNothing records the lifts explicitly. They are implied
by the boundaries between strokes, because by denition the pen must rise at the end of one and
fall at the start of the next. Storing them would duplicate information already present and create
the possibility of the two disagreeing, which is a class of bug that cannot arise if the state is stored
once.
The coordinate convention, and the one place it changesCoordinates are millimetres
with y increasing upward, which is the natural orientation for describing writing and the convention
the stored glyphs use. Images have y increasing downward, and some machines expect one or the
other. Rather than taking a position on that, the trajectory states its own convention and each
consumer converts: the rasteriser ips y as it draws, and the G-code writer ips it if the machine
needs it. The ip therefore happens at the boundary, once per consumer, rather than being
half-applied inside the trajectory.
What the two methods report
Boundsreturns the smallest rectangle containing every point, which the rasteriser needs to
size its canvas and the G-code writer needs to check the drawing ts the machine's travel.
PenT ravelMmreturns the distance drawn with the pen down and the distance moved with
it up. The rst is how much ink the page needs; the second is wasted motion, and it is the
quantity a stroke-ordering optimisation would try to reduce.
This is one of only two classes in the project, and it earns the form: it bundles data with two
derived measurements of that same data, which is what a class is for. The rest of the pipeline
is plain functions, because elsewhere there is no state that several operations need to share.
7.22 _GLYPH_CLEAN
_ GLYPH _ CLEAN = 0.12
Output.0.12, the prior distance at or below which a stored shape counts as clean.
What it marksThe lower of the two reference points on the prior-distance scale used by the
swap rule. A shape this close to the cross-writer reference for its letter is a well-formed example
of that letter and is kept almost always, whatever the legibility setting says.
Almost always, not always: even a clean shape carries a small swap probability proportional
to lam. That is deliberate, because a library can be poor overall while containing some clean
shapes, and a writer whose lam is high is one whose extraction is not trusted in general.
7.23 _GLYPH_GARBAGE
_ GLYPH _ GARBAGE = 0.55
Output.0.55, the prior distance at or above which a stored shape is always replaced.
186

=== PDF PAGE 188 ===
What it marksThe upper reference point. A shape this far from the reference is not a
recognisable instance of its letter at all: a fragment, or a cut carrying most of a neighbour. It is
replaced unconditionally, with no regard to the legibility setting.
Why this one case is unconditionalEvery other decision in the swap rule is probabilistic,
so this exception needs justifying. The argument is that lam expresses a trade-o between delity
and legibility, and the trade-o only exists where there is something to trade. Drawing a fragment
is not more faithful to the writer; it reproduces the extraction's mistake rather than their hand.
With no delity to be gained, there is nothing to weigh against legibility, and the shape goes.
7.24 _COMMIT_LAM
_ COMMIT _ LAM = 0.85
Output.0.85, the legibility setting at or above which the library is abandoned entirely.
What it doesAbove this setting every stored shape is replaced by the anchor, so the writer's
extracted library contributes nothing and the output is built from anchor letterforms throughout.
Why there is a hard commitment point at allWithout it, a writer at lam 0.95 would still
draw a few percent of glyphs from their library, and those few would be drawn from a library that
the lam value itself declares untrustworthy. The result is the worst of both: a page that is mostly
anchor letterforms, so it does not look much like the writer, with a scattering of fragments in it,
so it is not clean either. Committing fully above 0.85 at least produces a consistent page.
This case genuinely arises. A heavily cursive hand has no honest place to cut between letters, so
most of its variants are fragments, its median prior distance is high, and AutoLegibility returns a
value near 1. For such a writer the output is drawn almost entirely from anchor forms, which is
the correct response to a library that could not be extracted, and it is also the mechanism behind
this project's measured result that heavily cursive hands reproduce less faithfully than clean ones.

as a whole is trusted, it returns the chance that the writer's own shape is set aside in favour of a
clean one.

The order matters because the cases overlap. Garbage is tested rst, so a hopeless shape is replaced
even at lam 0. Full commitment is next, so above 0.85 everything goes regardless of quality. Only

This maps the prior distance onto a 0 to 1 scale between the two reference points, which is what
lets a single expression cover the whole middle range. The swap probability is then lam times a
factor running from 0.55 at the clean end to 1.45 at the garbage end, clipped to 1. So at a given
lam, a worse shape is swapped more often, and at a given quality, a less trusted library is swapped
more often. The two inputs act multiplicatively, which is the behaviour wanted: neither alone
should force a swap.
188

=== PDF PAGE 190 ===
Why a probability and not a decisionReturning a probability rather than a yes or no is
what produces a mixed page, and a mixed page is the point. A threshold rule would draw every
instance of a given character from the same source, so a letter whose shape sits near the line would
be either always the writer's or always the anchor. With a probability, some instances are theirs
and some are not, which both preserves more of the hand and spreads the risk: one bad instance
of a letter in a line is recoverable from context, where every instance being bad is not.
Because the draw is made per glyph from a seeded generator, the same text and seed give the
same page. That is what makes the best-of-N search meaningful, since each attempt diers
only by its seed, and what makes a result reproducible rather than something that happened
once.
7.26 _AUTO_LAM_GOOD
_ AUTO _ LAM _ GOOD = 0.24
Output.0.24, the median prior distance at or below which a library is trusted completely.
What it marksThe lower end of the scale AutoLegibility maps onto. A writer whose variants
have a median prior distance at or below this gets lam 0, meaning their own shapes are used
throughout.
Why the median across the whole libraryThe quantity being judged is the trustworthiness
of the extraction as a whole, not of any one glyph, which the swap rule already handles per glyph.
The median over every stored variant of every character answers the right question: is this writer's
library, in general, made of letters? Using the median rather than the mean keeps the answer
from being set by the worst few.
7.27 _AUTO_LAM_BAD
_ AUTO _ LAM _ BAD = 0.32
Output.0.32, the median prior distance at or above which a library is not trusted at all.
What it marksThe upper end of the same scale. At or above this, lam is 1, the library is
abandoned, and the output is drawn from anchor letterforms.
Why the window is so narrowThe two thresholds are only 0.08 apart, which makes the
mapping steep: a small change in median prior distance moves lam a long way. That is deliberate,
and it reects what the measurements show. Across this project's writers the median prior distance
clusters tightly, around 0.18 to 0.24 for the hands that extracted cleanly and around 0.31 for the
two most cursive. The thresholds are placed on either side of that gap, so the mapping separates
the populations that actually exist rather than spreading a gentle gradient over a range where no
writer falls.
The consequence is that most writers receive a lam of exactly 0 or exactly 1, with few in
between. That is appropriate for a measurement with two clear groups, but it does mean the
189

=== PDF PAGE 191 ===
intermediate behaviour of the swap rule is exercised mainly by the repair mechanism, which
raises lam for individual characters rather than for the writer.
7.28 AutoLegibility
def A u t o L e g i b i l i t y ( profile )

What it is forDerives the legibility setting from the prole instead of requiring it to be chosen.
The question it answers is: how far can this writer's extracted library be trusted?

Why this works, and what it is really measuringThe chain of reasoning behind it is the
substantive part. The prior distance measures how far a stored shape sits from what its letter
should look like. A library whose shapes sit far from that is a library of bad cuts. Bad cuts come
from boundaries placed where no honest boundary exists. And that happens exactly when the
writing is joined up, because in cursive the pen does not lift between letters, so there is no place
to cut that does not damage something.
So the median prior distance is, indirectly, a measure of how cursive the hand is, arrived at by
measuring the consequence rather than the cause. That is why it works as a trust signal: it
responds to the thing that actually makes an extraction unreliable. And it is why heavily cursive
writers end up drawn from anchor forms, which is the mechanism behind this project's nding
that such hands reproduce less faithfully.
190

=== PDF PAGE 192 ===
Why an empty library returns 1With no variants there is nothing to measure and nothing
to draw from, so the anchor is the only available source and lam 1 states that. Returning 0 instead
would claim a library worth trusting, and the caller would then try to draw from one that does
not exist.
A prole may carry an explicit legibilityLambda, which callers prefer over this function. That
is how a value found by experiment for a particular writer can override the derived one, and
it is why the stored lam column appears in this project's prole tables even though it is usually
empty.
7.29 _CoreGlyph
def _ Co re Gl yp h ( ch , profile )
Inputs
chthe character wanted
profile
the writer's style prole
Output.the anchor letterform as strokes, advance, lead and whether it is a cursive form; or
nothing if no anchor exists.
What the anchor isThe clean letterform that a stored variant is swapped for. The function
implements a preference order with two steps, and the order is the whole content of it.
First choice: the writer's own most typical letterformThe medoid stored in the prole
is tried rst, accepted if its prior distance is within IDEAL_ANCHOR_MAX_PRIORD. This
matters more than it may appear. An anchor is by denition drawn when the writer's chosen
variant was not trusted, so anchors account for a large share of a cursive writer's page. If the
anchor were always a built-in form, such a page would be generic handwriting with the writer's
spacing, and the namer would not identify it. Preferring their own medoid keeps the substitution
inside their hand: it is still a letter they actually wrote, merely the best-formed one rather than
a random one.
This project's experiments bear that out directly. Extending the anchor-selection improvements
to more writers raised writer identication substantially for the most cursive hand, and the gain
came from better anchor letterforms rather than from any change to how variants were chosen.
Second choice: the built-in alphabetIf the medoid is absent or too rough, _BaselineGlyph
supplies a built-in form, cursive or printed according to the writer's connectedness, scaled to their
reach. This is a real loss of delity and is accepted only because the alternative is drawing a shape
that is not a letter.
Why the advance is taken as a maximum
adv = max
 
advstored,lead + width + 0.04

(7.7)
where
191

=== PDF PAGE 193 ===
advthe advance actually used
advstored
the advance recorded with the letterform
leadthe gap from the cell's left edge to where the ink starts
width
the horizontal extent of the ink
0.04a small margin so two letters do not touch
The stored advance came from the cell the character was originally cut from, and that cell can
be narrower than the ink it contains, since a cut can leave ink spilling past its own boundary.
Advancing by the stored gure alone would then place the next letter on top of this one. Taking
whichever of the two is larger guarantees the advance clears the ink while preserving the writer's
own spacing wherever it is sucient.
7.30 _GlyphSource
def _ G l y p h S o u r c e ( profile , ch , rng , pr ev Ex it Y = None , lam =0.0 , f or ce Co r e = False ,
f o r c e P r i n t = False )

What it is forGiven one character, decide what to draw. This is where the legibility trade-o,

outcome auditable.
The order of preference
forcePrint, when set, returns the printed alphabet immediately. It is the last resort invoked
by the repair loop for a character the reader still cannot read.
192

=== PDF PAGE 194 ===
The writer's own variantsof this exact character, ranked and sampled as described
below, then possibly swapped for the anchor. Labelledown, orcoreif swapped.
The opposite caseof the character. A capital drawn from the writer's lowercase is scaled
up to their ascender height. Labelledcase.
A similar characterfrom the _SIMILAR table, such as an l for an I. Labelledsimilar.
The anchor, when the writer has nothing for this character at all. Labelledcore.
The built-in alphabetdirectly, labelledfallback.
Nothing, labelledblank: no strokes, but the advance is still applied, so an unavailable
character leaves a gap rather than closing up the line.
The order descends by how much of the writer's own hand each option retains, which is the right
criterion for a system whose purpose is to reproduce an individual. Note that the swap check is
applied in the rst three branches, not only the rst: a borrowed or case-converted shape is still
a stored cut and can still be a bad one.
How a variant is chosen: weighted samplingThe candidates are ranked by prior distance,
cut to the eligible few, and one is drawn with probability proportional to a weight.
wi = 1
1 + 1.4i ·exp

 ∆yi
0.45
2!
(7.8)
where
wi the weight given to the i-th ranked candidate

1.4how steeply the weight falls with rank
∆yi the dierence between this variant's entry height and the height the pen left the previous

0.45the tolerance on that dierence, in x-heights
exp(−(·)2)
a Gaussian fallo, applied only when joining; otherwise this factor is absent
The rst factor implements the preference for typical shapes: the top-ranked variant is weighted
1, the second about 0.42, the fth about 0.15. So the characteristic shape is drawn most often
while the others still appear, which is what gives repeated letters their variation. A reciprocal
fallo is gentler than an exponential one, which would make the top variant dominate so heavily
that the others might as well not be eligible.
Why the entry height matters when joiningThe second factor is the more interesting one.
When this letter will be joined to the previous one, a ligature must be drawn from where the pen
left that letter to where this one begins. If the two heights dier a great deal, the ligature has to
climb or dive, which is what produces the tangled joins that make synthetic cursive unreadable.
Since several variants are available and they dier in where they begin, the one whose entry height
is closest to the pen's current height can be preferred, and the join is then short and natural.
This is a small instance of a general principle worth naming: where a choice exists anyway, it
costs nothing to make it with regard to context. The variant selection had to pick one of ve;
biasing that pick toward the one that joins well improves the output without any extra drawing
or searching.
193

=== PDF PAGE 195 ===
Why the variant's own advance is usedEach variant carries its own advance, and that is
used rather than the character's median. The code comment gives the reason: a wide variant
advanced by a narrow median would collide with the next letter. The median describes the
character across all its instances, but what is being drawn is one particular instance, and the
spacing must suit the ink actually placed.
The label returned is counted in the trajectory's metadata, giving a breakdown of how many
glyphs came from each source. That breakdown is how the delity-legibility trade-o is mea-
sured in this project: a page reported as mostlycoreis readable but is largely not the writer's
own letterforms, and the counts say so plainly where looking at the output would not.
7.31 _Jitter
def _ Jitter ( strokes , rng , amp , rotAmp , scaleAmp )
Inputs
strokes
one glyph's strokes
rngthe seeded random generator
ampthe position shift amplitude, in x-heights
rotAmp
the rotation amplitude, in degrees
scaleAmp
the scale variation amplitude, as a fraction
Output.the strokes shifted, rotated and scaled by small random amounts.
Why randomness is added deliberatelyWithout it, every instance of a letter on the page is
identical to the pixel, because the same stored variant is drawn the same way. Identical repetition
is the visual signature of print, and a page of it reads as a font however handwritten the individual
letterforms are. Real writing never repeats a letter exactly, so a small random variation per glyph
is not a aw introduced into the output; it is a property of handwriting that must be put back.
The three transformations
x′
y′

=
cosθ−sinθ
sinθcosθ
 sxx
syy

+
δx
δy

(7.9)
where
x, ythe glyph's original coordinates, in x-heights
x′, y ′
the jittered coordinates
θa small random rotation, drawn from a normal distribution
sx, s y
independent random scale factors, each near 1
194

=== PDF PAGE 196 ===
δx, δ y
a random translationcosθ−sinθ
sinθcosθ

the standard 2-D rotation matrix, which turns a point anticlockwise about the origin by
theta
The order is scale, then rotate, then translate, and it is not interchangeable: rotating before
scaling would make the independent x and y scalings act along the rotated axes, shearing the
glyph rather than stretching it. Scaling x and y independently is itself deliberate, since a writer's
letters vary slightly in proportion as well as in size.
Background: why a normal distributionEach amount is drawn from anormal distribu-
tion, the bell-shaped curve, rather than uniformly from a range. The dierence is that a normal
distribution makes small deviations much commoner than large ones, with no hard limit, whereas
a uniform draw makes a large deviation exactly as likely as a tiny one. Real variation behaves
the rst way: most letters are close to the writer's usual form and a few are noticeably o. A
uniform draw produces output that wobbles unconvincingly, because every letter is about equally
displaced.
Why the vertical shift is smallerThe vertical amplitude is 0.6 of the horizontal. Vertical
position is read more sharply than horizontal: the eye uses the baseline as a reference, so a letter
sitting high stands out in a way that one sitting slightly right does not. The recogniser is similarly
sensitive, since its features were learned relative to the baseline. Reducing the vertical component
keeps the variation from reading as a fault.
The amplitudes reaching this function are already scaled by the caller's jitter setting and, for
an anchor glyph, by CORE_JITTER_FRAC. So the same function serves for a fully varied
extracted glyph and a nearly rigid anchor, with the policy decision kept in the caller rather
than here.
7.32 _Resample
def _ Resample ( poly , step )
Inputs
poly
a path
step
the desired spacing between points
Output.the path with points evenly spaced at about that interval.
What it is forRespaces a path so its points sit at roughly equal distances along it, by the
arc-length method described under _ResampleN in style.py: cumulative distance along the path
is computed and new points are read o at even distances by interpolation.
195

=== PDF PAGE 197 ===
Why layout needs itPaths reaching this point come from several sources with very dierent
point densities. A stored glyph was simplied, so a straight run is two points. A ligature is
generated from three control points. A built-in letterform was laid out by hand at whatever
spacing was convenient. Concatenating them gives a path whose density varies several-fold along
its length, and two later stages are sensitive to that.
The rasteriserdraws by stepping along the path, so a sparse region is drawn from fewer
samples; with a long enough gap the round pen's footprints no longer overlap and the stroke
appears as a row of dots.
The G-code writeremits a move per point, so the machine's motion is smoother where
points are dense. Even spacing gives even pen speed, which is visible in the drawn line.
Why 0.35 millimetresThe spacing used throughout layout. It is ne enough that successive
pen positions overlap at any plotted size, and coarse enough that a line of text does not become
tens of thousands of points, which would slow both the rasteriser and the machine's motion
planning for no visible gain.
This duplicates _ResampleN in style.py, diering in that it takes a spacing rather than a count
and returns tuples rather than an array. Both forms are needed: averaging requires a xed
count so that points correspond between strokes, while drawing requires a xed spacing so that
density is uniform. Neither can be expressed as the other.
7.33 USE_STYLE_CALIBRATION
USE _ STYLE _ C A L I B R A T I O N = True
Output.true, enabling the measured correction of the output's style back toward the writer's
real pages.
What it enablesA feedback step: render a sample of synthetic text, measure its slant and
reach the same way they were measured on the writer's real lines, compare, and correct. Turning
it o uses the prole's measurements directly.
Why any correction is neededThe prole records the writer's slant and reach as measured
from their pages, and synthesis applies exactly those numbers. One might expect the output to
measure the same, and it does not. The discrepancy accumulates through the pipeline: glyphs
were de-slanted during extraction and are re-sheared during layout, but not every stroke shears
the same way; the jitter rotates glyphs slightly; the anchor glyphs are capped at a shallower
slant than the writer's own; and the baseline drift shifts letters vertically. Each is small and the
measured result is systematically o.
Rather than trying to account for each eect analytically, the calibration measures the total
discrepancy and corrects for it. That is the sound approach when the individual contributions are
numerous, small, and interact, which is the case here.
7.34 _STYLE_CAL
196

=== PDF PAGE 198 ===
_ STYLE _ CAL = {}
Output.a cache of the computed correction, keyed by writer and render scale.
Why it is cachedComputing the correction requires rendering a long sample sentence three
times and measuring each, which is far more work than drawing the text actually requested. Since
the correction depends only on the prole and the render scale, it is the same every time for a
given writer, so computing it once per program run is sucient.
Why the key includes the scaleThe key is the writer together with the x-height in millimetres
and the pixels per millimetre. Both aect the measurement: at a small rendered size the stroke
width is a larger fraction of the x-height, which changes what the binarisation and the band
detection nd, so a correction computed at one scale does not apply at another. Keying on the
scale as well as the writer prevents a cached value being reused where it does not hold.
This is a module-level cache that is never cleared, so a prole edited within a single run
would keep its old correction. That is acceptable because proles are loaded from disk and not
modied in place, but it is the kind of assumption worth knowing when a cache is keyed by
identity rather than content.
7.35 StyleCalibration
def S t y l e C a l i b r a t i o n ( profile , mmPerXh =4.0 , pxPerMm =18.0)
Inputs
profile
the writer's style prole
mmPerXh
the x-height in millimetres, default 4.0
pxPerMm
the render resolution, default 18.0
Output.a dictionary of three corrections: a shear adjustment and two zone scale factors.
The method: measure the output, compare, correct, repeatA xed sample sentence is
rendered with the current correction applied, the rendered image is measured exactly as Render-
RefStats measured the writer's real lines, and the correction is updated from the dierence. That
is repeated three times.
Why the comparison is validThe crucial property is that both sides are measured by the
same procedure. The reference values stored in the prole as slantMeasRef, ascMeasRef and
descMeasRef were produced by running the same binarisation, band detection and slant estimation
on the writer's real crops, for exactly this purpose. Comparing a quantity measured one way with
the same quantity measured another way would confound the measurement methods with the
thing being measured; comparing like with like isolates the real discrepancy.
197

=== PDF PAGE 199 ===
The three corrections
∆shear += tan(slantref)−tan(slant got)
kasc ∗= ascref −1
ascgot −1
kdesc ∗= descref
descgot
(7.10)
where
∆shear
the additive shear correction, applied on top of the prole's slant
kasc the multiplicative correction to the ascender zone
kdesc
the same for the descender zone
refthe value measured on the writer's real lines
gotthe value measured on this render
+=,∗=
the correction is accumulated across rounds rather than replaced
−1subtracted from the ascender gures because the ascender zone is measured from the top of
the x-height band, not from the baseline
The shear correction is additive and is computed on the tangent rather than on the angle. That is
because shear is applied as a tangent: a point is displaced horizontally in proportion to its height,
with the tangent as the constant. Dierences of tangents therefore compose by addition, while
dierences of angles do not, so correcting in degrees would be wrong by an amount growing with
the angle.
The zone corrections are multiplicative because they scale a zone's extent, and the subtraction of
1 in the ascender case matters: the ascender is measured as a height above the baseline, of which
the rst 1.0 is the x-height band itself. The part that can be corrected is the reach beyond the
band, so the ratio must be taken of the excess rather than of the total. Taking the ratio of the
totals would under-correct, since the xed band would dilute the dierence.
Why it iterates, and only three timesOne round is not exact, because the corrections
interact: stretching the ascender zone changes which pixels the band detection nds, which changes
the measured slant. So each round measures the state the previous round produced and corrects
the remaining error. Three rounds are used because the residual after the rst is already small
and the process is convergent, so further rounds would change little at considerable cost, each
requiring a full render and measurement.
Why the corrections are clippedEach zone factor is held between 0.5 and 1.6. The ratios
are quotients, and a quotient whose denominator is near zero is unbounded: a render in which the
measured ascender came out barely above the band gives a denominator near zero and a correction
of hundreds, which would stretch the next render's ascenders o the page. Clipping bounds the
damage from a bad measurement, and the bounds are wide enough not to interfere with a genuine
correction, which is a few tens of percent.
198

=== PDF PAGE 200 ===
The cases where no correction is madeIf any reference value is missing or not a number,
or if the render cannot be measured, the neutral correction is returned and cached. Those are the
cases where there is nothing to compare against, and applying a correction derived from a failed
measurement would be worse than applying none.
The sample sentence is a pangram-like text chosen to contain ascenders, descenders and a
wide spread of letters, rendered with jitter switched o and on a line wide enough that no
wrapping occurs. All three are necessary: without jitter the measurement is repeatable, and
without wrapping the measured band belongs to one line rather than several stacked.

The early exitWhen both factors are within a thousandth of 1, the strokes are returned
