the source label before calling.
7.37 _Ligature
def _ Ligature ( p0 , p1 , xh , rng )
Inputs
p0where the pen left the previous letter, in millimetres
p1where it enters the next
xhthe x-height in millimetres
rngthe seeded random generator
Output.a path joining the two points, as a resampled curve.
What a ligature is hereThe connecting stroke drawn between two joined letters. Because the
stored glyphs had their ligature tails trimmed o during extraction, as described under _Trim-
LigatureTails, synthesis must supply its own, drawn between whatever pair of letters it actually
placed. That is the advantage of trimming: the join suits the pair at hand rather than the pair
the glyph was cut beside.
The shape: a dip whose depth depends on the distance
sag = min
 
0.11 xh,0.16|∆x|

+ε, ε∼ N(0,0.015 xh)(7.12)
where
saghow far below the lower endpoint the curve's midpoint is placed
∆xthe horizontal distance between the two endpoints
xhthe x-height in millimetres
0.11the ceiling on the dip, as a fraction of the x-height
0.16the dip as a fraction of the distance travelled
εa small random variation
N(0, σ)
a normal distribution with mean 0 and standard deviation sigma
200

=== PDF PAGE 202 ===
The code comment names the defect this avoids: a xed dip turns a short join into a loop, and
tangled loops are what makes synthetic joined writing unreadable. Where two letters are close
together, a dip of a xed depth has to descend and return within a very short horizontal distance,
which draws a narrow vertical loop below the baseline rather than a connecting stroke. Making
the dip proportional to the distance travelled means a short join is nearly straight and only a long
one sags.
The ceiling then caps the other end: over a long gap, proportionality alone would produce a dip
deeper than a descender, which would collide with the line below. Taking the smaller of the two
expressions gives a dip that grows with distance up to a limit, which is how a real pen behaves.
Why a single midpoint and interpolation, not a formal curveThe curve is constructed
by placing one point below the midpoint of the two endpoints and resampling the three-point
path, which the even resampling turns into a smooth arc. A formal spline would give a more
precisely controlled curve, and it is not needed: a ligature spans a fraction of an x-height and is
drawn with a pen whose width is a tenth of that, so the dierence between a resampled three-point
path and an exact curve is well below what the pen can express.
The degenerate caseWhen the horizontal distance is under 0.06 x-heights, a straight segment
between the two points is returned. At that distance the two letters are eectively touching and
any dip would be the vertical loop the whole design avoids. Returning the two points still produces
a connection, which is what joining means, without drawing anything misleading.
7.38 _SplitWords
def _ S p l i t W o r d s ( text )
Inputs
text
the text to be written
Output.a list of words with newline markers between the lines.
What it is forBreaks the text into the units layout works with. Words are the unit because
wrapping happens at word boundaries: a word that will not t on the current line moves to the
next one whole, since handwriting is not hyphenated.
Why newlines survive as markersThe text is split on newlines rst and a marker is inserted
between the resulting chunks, so an explicit line break in the input is preserved rather than being
treated as a space. The distinction matters because an explicit break is the writer's instruction
and must be obeyed wherever the line happens to be, while a space is merely a place where a
break is permitted. Keeping the two distinct lets layout handle them as what they are.
Empty strings between consecutive spaces are discarded, so repeated spaces collapse to one gap,
and a trailing marker is removed so text ending in a newline does not produce an empty nal
line. Both are small conveniences that keep the layout loop from needing to test for them.
201

=== PDF PAGE 203 ===
7.39 _WordWidth
def _ Wo rd Wi dt h ( word , profile )
Inputs
word
one word
profile
the writer's style prole
Output.approximately how wide the word will be, in x-heights.
What it is forAn estimate used only to decide whether a word ts on the current line. It sums
the writer's median advance for each character, falling back to 0.9 x-heights for a character with
no recorded advance.
Why an estimate is acceptable hereThe true width is not available in advance, because
it depends on which variant of each letter is chosen, how the jitter scales them, and whether the
collision guard pushes anything right. All of that happens while the word is being drawn, which is
after the decision to put it on this line has been made. Determining the exact width would mean
laying the word out, measuring it, and discarding the result if it did not t, which is several times
the work for a decision whose only consequence is where a line breaks.
The estimate is slightly low on average, since it ignores the collision guard, which only ever widens.
So a word occasionally overruns the requested line width by a small margin. That is the right
direction for the error to fall: a line a few percent too long is unremarkable, where a conservative
estimate that broke lines early would leave visible ragged gaps.
7.40 SynthesizeText
def S y n t h e s i z e T e x t ( text , profile , mmPerXh =4.0 , seed = None , l i n e W i d t h M m =180.0 ,

a precomputed calibration, to avoid recomputing it
Output.a Trajectory: the pen path for that text in that hand.
What it is forThe main layout function. It walks the text character by character and builds
the pen path, handling glyph selection, calibration, variation, placement, collision, joining and
line breaking. Everything above exists to be called from here.
The per-character sequence
Choose a shapewith _GlyphSource, passing the height the pen left the previous letter at
so that a joinable variant can be preferred.
Calibrateit, if it is an extracted glyph, so its zones match the writer's measured reach.
Jitterit, at full strength for an extracted glyph and a fraction of that for an anchor.
Placeit at the pen position, applying the shear for slant and the current baseline drift.
Check for collisionagainst the previous glyph's body and push right if needed.
Identify the body strokeand decide whether to join to the previous letter.
Advancethe pen position.
Why the slant cap depends on legibility
capauthor = 45−19·clip(lam,0,1)(7.13)
where
capauthor
the greatest lean in degrees applied to the writer's own glyphs
lamthe legibility setting
45the cap at lam 0, which is permissive
19how much the cap tightens as lam rises, giving 26 degrees at lam 1
Steep slant and poor legibility tend to occur together, because a steeply slanted hand is usually
a joined one and a joined one extracts badly. So a high lam is evidence that the writing is
hard to read, and shearing a glyph steeply makes it harder still, for the reasons given under
CORE_SLANT_CAP_DEG. Linking the cap to lam reduces the lean exactly for the writers
whose output most needs the help, while leaving a clean upright-ish hand free to keep its full
measured slant. The anchor glyphs are capped harder again, at 22 or 30 degrees.
203

=== PDF PAGE 205 ===
The baseline drift
vt = 0.85v t−1 +η t,drift t = clip
 
driftt−1 +v t,−0.25,0.25

(7.14)
where
vt the drift velocity after this character
0.85how much of the previous velocity is retained, which is what makes the wander slow
ηt a small random impulse drawn from a normal distribution
driftt
the vertical oset applied to this character, in x-heights
clip(·,−0.25,0.25)
the oset held within a quarter of an x-height either way
A real hand does not write along a perfectly straight line; the baseline wanders slowly, especially
on unruled paper. Adding an independent random oset per character would not reproduce that,
because it would produce letters jumping up and down independently, which looks like a fault
rather than a wander. What distinguishes a wander is that it iscorrelated: if this letter is high,
the next is probably also high.
The construction achieves that by making the random impulse act on a velocity rather than
on the position, and by retaining 85 percent of the velocity each step. The oset is then the
accumulated velocity, which changes direction only gradually. This is a discreteautoregressive
process, and it is the standard way to generate a slowly varying random signal. The clip prevents
the accumulation drifting arbitrarily far over a long line, which an unbounded random walk would.
Why the body stroke is identiedA glyph may consist of several pen-down strokes: the
body, plus a dot, a crossbar or an accent. Only one of them carries the connection to the next
letter. The body is identied as the stroke with the greatest horizontal extent, and the join is
made from and to it.
Getting this wrong has a specic and visible consequence. If the exit point were taken from the
last stroke drawn rather than the body, then for an i the exit would be the dot, and the ligature
to the next letter would be drawn from the dot, sloping down from above the x-height. The width
test identies the body reliably because a dot or an accent is by nature narrow while the body
spans the letter.
The conditions for joining
Not the rst letter of the word,and a previous exit point exists.
Both characters are alphabetic.Writing does not join across a digit or a comma.
Both glyphs come from compatible sources:either both are the writer's own shapes,
or both are cursive anchor forms. A printed anchor never joins, because it has no entry or
exit designed for one.
A random drawagainst the eective connectedness succeeds.
The source-compatibility condition is the one that needs explaining. A join between an extracted
glyph and a printed anchor would be drawn to a point on the anchor that was never meant to
204

=== PDF PAGE 206 ===
receive one, typically partway up its side, producing a stroke across the middle of the letter.
Requiring both ends of a join to be of a joinable kind means an anchor substituted into cursive
writing creates a clean break in the word rather than a malformed connection, which is the less
damaging of the two outcomes.
How strokes accumulateA running chain holds the current pen-down path. When a glyph
joins, its ligature and body are appended to the chain, so the pen stays down across the join and
several letters become one continuous stroke, which is what cursive writing is. When it does not
join, the chain is ushed as a completed stroke and a new one begins. Non-body strokes such as
dots are always ushed separately, since the pen must lift to draw them.
The metadata returned records the glyph source counts, the writer, the slant, the connectedness
and the line count. The source counts in particular are what make the delity-legibility trade-
o measurable after the fact, and they are the reason _GlyphSource returns a label at all.
7.41 _Draw
def _ Draw ( traj , pxPerMm , padMm , strokePx , paper =255 , ink =0 , softPx =0.0)
Inputs
traj
the trajectory to draw
pxPerMm
the resolution in pixels per millimetre
padMm
the margin in millimetres
strokePx
the pen width in pixels
paper
the background value, default 255
inkthe stroke value, default 0
softPx
the blur radius in pixels, 0 for none
Output.a grey image of the trajectory, ink on paper.
Why the pen path is turned into an image at allThe plotter draws from the trajectory
and never needs an image. The image exists because the two judging networks score pictures,
not paths. Every evaluation in this project, and the best-of-N search itself, works by rendering a
candidate and asking the networks what they see. So the rasteriser is not a display convenience; it
is part of the optimisation loop, and how faithfully it imitates a photograph determines whether
the scores mean anything.
205

=== PDF PAGE 207 ===
The coordinate ip
xpx = (x−x 0 + pad)·ppm, y px = (y 1 −y+ pad)·ppm(7.15)
where
xpx, y px
the pixel position
x, ythe trajectory position in millimetres, y upward
x0 the leftmost extent of the trajectory
y1 the topmost extent
padthe margin in millimetres
ppm
pixels per millimetre
The x expression merely shifts the origin and scales. The y expression also reverses direction, by
subtracting from the top extent rather than adding to the bottom one, because the trajectory has
y increasing upward and an image has row 0 at the top. This is the single place that conversion
happens on the rendering path, which is the discipline described under Trajectory: each consumer
converts at its own boundary.
Why the round pen needs no end capsEach stroke is drawn by DrawPolyline, which sets a
pixel according to its distance from the path rather than by stepping along and stamping a shape.
A consequence worth drawing out is that round ends and round joins come out automatically:
the set of points within a xed distance of a line segment is a rectangle with a semicircle at
each end, and the union of two such regions is already correctly rounded where they meet. A
stamping rasteriser would need explicit end caps and join handling, and would leave notches at
sharp corners.
Why the blur comes lastAll the strokes are drawn and then the whole image is blurred once.
Blurring each stroke as it was drawn would be both slower and wrong: where two strokes overlap,
each having been individually softened, the overlap would be darker than a single stroke's core,
producing a visible dark patch at every crossing. Blurring the nished image treats a crossing as
the single region of ink that it is.
The canvas is sized from the trajectory's own bounds plus the margin, so it is exactly as large
as the writing requires. A xed canvas size would either clip long text or surround short text
with blank paper, and the second matters as much as the rst, because the proportion of the
crop that is ink is one of the statistics the networks respond to.
7.42 RenderTrajectory
def R e n d e r T r a j e c t o r y ( traj , pxPerMm =8.0 , padMm = None , strokePx = None , profile =
None ,
size = None , matchInk = True , u n i f o r m I n k = None )
Inputs
206

=== PDF PAGE 208 ===
traj
the trajectory to render
pxPerMm
the resolution, default 8.0
padMm
the margin, derived from the writing size if not given
strokePx
the pen width in pixels, derived if not given
profile
the writer's prole, for the non-uniform path
size
a nal size to resample to, or nothing
matchInk
whether to search for the pen width matching the writer's ink density
uniformInk
whether to use one pen width for every writer
Output.a grey image of the trajectory, prepared to resemble the crops the networks were trained
on.
What it is forThe policy layer above _Draw. It decides the pen width, the margin, the ink
and paper levels and the softness, so that the rendered image resembles a photographed crop
closely enough for the networks' scores to be meaningful.
The two modes
Uniform,the default: one pen width for every writer, black on white, matching what the
plotter physically draws. This is the mode the best-of-N search uses, because the search
must optimise the thing that will actually be produced.
Per-writer:the pen width, ink level, paper level and softness taken from the writer's
prole, optionally with a search for the width that matches their measured ink density. This
is for comparison against their real photographed pages, where the real page does carry their
own pen.
Why the margin is proportional to the writingThe margin defaults to 0.18 of the x-height
rather than a xed number of millimetres. The code comment gives the reason: a xed pad would
inate the crop height and skew the line's aspect ratio. A line of writing is long and thin, so a xed
margin adds a large fraction to its height and a small one to its width, changing the proportions.
Aspect ratio is one of the statistics recorded in the prole as a reference, and the networks were
trained on crops of a characteristic shape, so distorting it is a domain shift of exactly the kind
the rendering is designed to avoid.
Why the ink core is painted darker than the prole's ink levelThe prole's inkLevel is
the median of the already-dark pixels of a real crop, which includes the soft edges of every stroke
as well as their cores. It is therefore an average over the whole stroke, not the colour of its centre.
Painting the core at that value and then blurring would darken nothing and lighten the edges
207

=== PDF PAGE 209 ===
further, giving a stroke lighter overall than the real one. So the core is painted dark and the blur
produces the intermediate values, which is the order the physical process works in.
Where a nal size is requested, the image is resampled with the project's own resizer rather
than being drawn at that size directly. Drawing at the nal size would be better in principle,
since resampling softens the image slightly, but the pen width is quantised to whole pixels, so
at small sizes drawing directly would force a one-pixel pen and lose control of the stroke weight
entirely.
7.43 _MatchInkDensity
def _ M a t c h I n k D e n s i t y ( traj , pxPerMm , padMm , startPx , target , mm , paper =255 , ink
=0 , soft =0.0)
Inputs
traj
the trajectory
pxPerMm
the resolution
padMm
the margin
startPx
the pen width to start from
target
the ink fraction to match
mmthe x-height in millimetres
paper
the paper level
inkthe ink level
soft
the blur radius
Output.a pair of the rendered image and the pen width that produced it.
What it is forFinds the pen width whose rendered image has the same fraction of dark pixels
as the writer's real pages. Ink fraction is one of the statistics a network responds to most directly,
since it is visible at every scale, so matching it removes one axis of domain shift between synthetic
and photographed crops.
Why a search rather than a formulaThe relationship between pen width and ink fraction
cannot be written down. Doubling the width does not double the ink, because strokes overlap:
where two strokes cross, the ink is counted once, and the amount of overlap depends on the
particular text and letterforms. The blur and the threshold used to count dark pixels add further
non-linearity. So the quantity is evaluated rather than predicted.
208

=== PDF PAGE 210 ===
The search: hill climbing in one dimensionThe current width is probed, the direction that
should help is determined from whether there is too much ink or too little, and steps of one pixel
are taken in that direction for as long as the error decreases. It stops at the rst step that does
not improve.
err(w) =
 f(w)−f ∗, f(w) =fraction of pixels below 128 at pen widthw(7.16)
where
err(w)
the error at pen width w, which the search reduces
f(w)
the ink fraction produced by that width
f ∗ the target ink fraction from the writer's real pages
| · |the absolute value, since overshooting and undershooting are equally wrong
This ishill climbing: move in whichever direction improves the objective and stop when no step
does. It is only guaranteed to nd a local best, but here that is enough, because ink fraction
increases with pen width throughout, so the error has a single minimum and any local best is the
global one.
Why the pen width is a whole number of pixelsThe width is quantised to whole pixels
because that is what the rasteriser can express, which is what makes a one-pixel step the natural
increment and makes the search nite. The consequence is that the target can rarely be hit
exactly; the search returns the closest achievable, and at small rendered sizes the available widths
are coarse enough that the residual error is noticeable.
Why results are cachedEach probe requires a full render and a resize, which is the expensive
part. The search revisits a width when it steps past the minimum and compares back, so caching by
width removes those repeats. The upper bound on the width, a little over half an x-height, exists
so that a target the geometry cannot reach makes the search terminate rather than thickening the
pen until the text is a solid block.
7.44 _LineLogProbs
def _ L i n e L o g P r o b s ( reader , img )
Inputs
reader
a loaded reader
imga rendered line image
Output.the reader's log probabilities, one row per column and one entry per character.
209

=== PDF PAGE 211 ===
What it is forRuns a rendered image through the reader and returns the raw per-column
scores rather than decoded text. Those scores are what the alignment needs, and the alignment
is what identies which characters are weak.
Why the raw scores and not the textDecoded text says what the reader concluded; the
scores say how condently, and where. The repair loop needs the second, because its question is
not whether the line reads correctly overall but which particular characters are responsible for it
not doing so. That information exists only in the per-column scores, and decoding discards it.
The batch axis is indexed away immediately, since rendering and scoring are done one line at
a time. The reader retains the axis because the training code uses it.

The repair loop uses the answer to redraw only those characters, instead of redrawing the whole
line and hoping for a better outcome.
Why forced alignment rather than decodingThe text is known, so there is no recognition
to be done. What is wanted is per-character evidence, and forced alignment provides it: it nds
where each known character sits in the rendered line, after which the reader's own score for that
character over exactly those columns is a direct measure of how legible that character came out.

A veraging rather than summing is the point of that factor. A summed score would rank an m

The unaligned caseA character the alignment could not place at all is given a very large
negative score, so it sorts to the front. That is the correct ranking: a character with no columns
of its own was so poorly drawn that the aligner had to pass through it in a single step shared
with its neighbours, which is a more complete failure than a character that was placed but scored
badly.
Whitespace is skipped, since a space is not a drawn shape and cannot be repaired by substituting
a letterform. Positions are returned as indices into the original text, not into the ltered
character list, because that is what the per-character override table is keyed by.
7.46 REPAIR_ROUNDS
REPAIR _ ROUNDS = 3
Output.3, how many targeted redraw passes follow the search.
What a repair round isAfter the best-of-N search has chosen its best attempt, the weakest
characters in it are identied and pinned to the anchor letterform, and the line is drawn again. If
the result scores better it is kept. That is one round, and three are performed.
Why repair is more eective than more attemptsThis is the substantive point. Drawing
the whole line again with a dierent seed changes every character at once, so a line failing because
of one bad letter is as likely to acquire a new bad letter elsewhere as to x the original. Repair
changes only the characters identied as weak and leaves the rest exactly as they were, so it is a
directed improvement rather than another sample. It is the dierence between searching randomly
and using the gradient.
Why threeEach round costs a full render and two network evaluations, and the returns fall
away quickly: the rst round addresses the worst characters, which are where most of the loss is,
and by the third the remaining weak characters are usually ones the anchor cannot help, because
the diculty lies in the neighbouring letters or the join rather than in the letterform. Three
rounds reach that point without spending on further ones.
7.47 REPAIR_WORST_K
REPAIR _ WORST _ K = 3
Output.3, how many characters are pinned to the anchor per round.
211

=== PDF PAGE 213 ===
Why not just the worst onePinning one character per round would need many more rounds
to x a line with several bad letters, and each round costs a render and two network evaluations.
Pinning three advances faster at the same cost per round.
Why not all of themBecause the eects interact and the acceptance test is all-or-nothing. A
round's result is kept only if the overall score improves, so a round that xes four characters and
breaks a fth may be rejected entirely, losing the four improvements with it. Changing a few at
a time keeps each round's eect small enough to be judged usefully. Pinning everything at once
would simply be the lam-1 case, which abandons the writer's hand altogether.
Characters already pinned are not reconsidered in later rounds, whether or not the round
that pinned them was accepted. That prevents the loop cycling on the same characters and
guarantees it terminates, at the cost of occasionally passing over a character that a dierent
combination would have xed.
7.48 DEF AULT_LEGIBILITY
DEFAULT _ L E G I B I L I T Y = 0.65
Output.0.65, the legibility setting used when none is supplied.
Where it appliesOnly in SynthesizeLegible, the earlier search that optimises text accuracy
alone. The joint search derives its setting from AutoLegibility instead, which is the better approach
because it responds to the writer's own library rather than applying one gure to everyone.
Why a middling value0.65 leans toward legibility without abandoning the writer's shapes,
which is the reasonable default for a search whose only objective is text accuracy: with nothing
measuring delity, a setting that preserved more of the hand would not be rewarded for it. The
value is a compromise standing in for a measurement that the function does not make, which is
precisely what AutoLegibility supplies and why the joint search prefers it.
7.49 SynthesizeLegible
def S y n t h e s i z e L e g i b l e ( text , profile , nTries =6 , mmPerXh =4.0 , l i n e W i d t h M m
=180.0 ,
jitter =0.5 , seed =0 , reader = None , pxPerMm =18.0 , l e g i b i l i t y = None , repair
= True )
Inputs
text
the text to write
profile
the writer's style prole
nTries
how many attempts, default 6
212

=== PDF PAGE 214 ===
mmPerXh
the x-height in millimetres
lineWidthMm
where to wrap
jitter
the variation amount, default 0.5
seed
the base random seed
reader
a loaded reader, constructed if not given
pxPerMm

repair

Output.the best trajectory found, with its scores recorded in the metadata.
What it doesDraws the text several times with dierent seeds, renders and reads each, keeps
the attempt the reader reads most accurately, and then runs repair rounds on it. It optimises text
accuracy alone.
Why a search is needed at allSynthesis is random: which variant is chosen for each character,
whether each is swapped for the anchor, the jitter and the drift all depend on the seed. So the
same text in the same hand has many possible renderings of diering quality, and nothing in the
layout process knows which it has produced. Drawing several and measuring them is a direct way
to nd a good one, and it requires no model of what makes a rendering good: the reader supplies
that.
The escalation ladder in the repair loop
A fresh weak characteris pinned to the anchor, which is preferably the writer's own most
typical letterform.
A character still weak after being pinnedescalates to the printed alphabet, which is
the last resort and abandons the writer's hand for that character entirely. Only the single
worst such character escalates per round.
The ladder exists because the anchor sometimes does not help. If the writer's own best form of
a letter is itself the problem, pinning to it changes nothing, and the only remaining option is a
designed letterform. Escalating one character per round, and only one that has already failed at
the lower rung, keeps the loss of delity to the minimum that produces a readable line.
The function falls back to plain layout if a reader cannot be constructed, returning text drawn
without any search. That keeps synthesis usable without the network weights present, which
matters for testing the layout alone.

=== PDF PAGE 215 ===

=== PDF PAGE 216 ===
What it is forThe search this project's reported results were produced with. It diers from
the one above in judging each attempt by both networks at once: the reader, for whether the
output says the right words, and the namer, for whether it still looks like this writer.
Why both judges are neededOptimising text accuracy alone has a predictable failure. The
most readable rendering of any text is the one drawn entirely from clean anchor letterforms, so a
search rewarded only for readability will drive the output toward generic handwriting. It would
score well and would have abandoned the purpose of the project. Adding the namer puts a cost
on that, so the search cannot buy readability with delity.
The harmonic mean, and why not an average
S= 2a w
a+w (7.18)
where
Sthe combined score the search maximises
athe text accuracy: the fraction of characters the reader got right
wthe namer's condence that this is the right writer
2aw/(a+w)
the harmonic mean of the two, which is zero if either is zero
Theharmonic meanis used rather than the arithmetic mean, and the dierence is the whole
point of the combinator. An arithmetic mean lets one measure compensate for the other: an
attempt scoring 0.95 on text and 0.15 on writer averages 0.55, the same as a balanced 0.55 and
0.55, so the search would be indierent between a perfectly readable page in nobody's hand and
a moderate page in the right one. The harmonic mean of the rst pair is 0.26 and of the second
0.55, so the balanced candidate wins clearly.
The mechanism is that the harmonic mean is dominated by the smaller value: as either term
approaches zero the product in the numerator does too, while the denominator does not. So it
rewards being adequate on both axes rather than excellent on one, which is exactly the requirement
here. It is the same quantity as the F-measure used to combine precision and recall, and for the
same reason.
Why the repair loop is more cautious hereTwo characters are pinned per round rather
than three, and an accepted round's pins are retained while a rejected round's are discarded. The
caution follows from what is being optimised. Pinning a character to the anchor reliably helps
text accuracy and reliably costs writer identication, so under the joint score a repair is no longer
close to free, and taking smaller steps keeps each one's net eect measurable.
Why the search stops early at 0.99Both loops break once the combined score reaches 0.99,
since further attempts cannot improve meaningfully and each costs a render and two network
evaluations. With a search of six to fourteen attempts per line and a page of many lines, that
saving is substantial on the writers whose rst attempt is already good.
The writer mapping defaults to an attribute read from the namer under the nameauthor_mapping,
while the namer stores it asauthorMapping. The default therefore raises an attribute error if
reached, and every working caller passes the mapping explicitly. It is recorded here because a
reader would otherwise assume the argument is optional.
215

=== PDF PAGE 217 ===
8 reproduce.py
This le draws. It takes a list of strokes in millimetres and turns them into motion, and nothing in
it knows anything about handwriting: the strokes may have come from synthesis or from tracing
a real page, and the le behaves the same either way. That separation is deliberate, and it is what
allows the drawing half to be tested without any of the recognition half being involved.
The two halves, and why they are kept apart
The top halfconverts a pen path into a G-code le. It is pure arithmetic, runs on any
computer, and is the half this project's measured geometry results were taken on.
The bottom halfdrives the real gantry: stepper motors, limit switches, homing and the
pen lift. It needs the single-board computer and the wiring, so its imports are guarded and
the top half still works without them.
The guard matters more than it appears. Without it, importing this module on a laptop would
fail at the hardware import, and since every test and every evaluation script imports it, none of
them could run anywhere but on the machine itself.
Background: what G-code is G-codeis the standard language for telling a machine tool
where to move. A line is a command letter and number, such as G0 or G1, followed by parame-
ters:G1 X12.5 Y40.0means move in a straight line to that position. It dates from numerically
controlled milling and is what nearly every plotter, 3-D printer and mill accepts, which is why it
is used here rather than a private format.
G0is a rapid move, used with the pen up, where only the destination matters.
G1is a controlled move at a feed rate, used with the pen down, where the path matters.
G2 and G3are circular arcs, clockwise and anticlockwise.
G4is a dwell: wait for a stated time.
M3 and M5are auxiliary commands, here mapped to the pen actuator.
G21 and G90declare millimetres and absolute coordinates.
UnitsThe top half works in millimetres throughout. The bottom half works in motor steps,
converting at the boundary using a steps-per-millimetre gure that calibration measures rather
than assumes.
8.1 GantryCong
class G a n t r y C o n f i g
Output.a record of every physical number describing the machine, with derived step scaling
available as properties.
216

=== PDF PAGE 218 ===
What it is forCollects everything physical about the machine into one place: the gearing, the
work area, the feed rates and the pen actuator's timing. A physical change to the machine, a
dierent pulley or a larger bed, is then one edit here rather than a search for the number wherever
it was used.
Why steps per millimetre is derived and not typed inThis is the one piece of real
reasoning in the record. Steps per millimetre is the number that converts a distance into motor
steps, and it could simply be stated. It is instead computed from the parts the machine is actually
built from.
stepsPerMm = fullStepsPerRev×microstepping
mmPerRev (8.1)
where
stepsPerMm
how many motor steps move the axis one millimetre
fullStepsPerRev
the motor's own step count per revolution, 200 for the NEMA 17 used
microstepping
how many electrical sub-steps the driver divides each full step into, 16 here
mmPerRev
how far the axis travels per motor revolution, set by the belt and pulley
With the values given this is 200 times 16 divided by 40, or 80 steps per millimetre. The reason
for deriving it is that each of the three inputs is a fact someone can check against the hardware:
the motor's label says 1.8 degrees per step, the driver's jumpers say 1/16, and the belt is 2 mm
pitch with a 20-tooth pulley, giving 40 mm per revolution. A single stated 80 could not be checked
that way, and if a pulley were changed it would be silently wrong, with the only symptom being
a drawing of the wrong size.
Background: what microstepping isA stepper motor moves in xed increments, 1.8 degrees
for a 200-step motor.Microsteppingis the driver's ability to hold the rotor between two full
steps by driving the coils at intermediate currents, dividing each step into smaller ones. At 1/16
the resolution becomes 0.1125 degrees, which for this gearing is 12.5 micrometres of travel. Finer
resolution is not the main benet: full stepping produces an abrupt jump between positions
that makes the motor vibrate audibly and visibly, and microstepping smooths that into nearly
continuous motion, which matters when the thing being drawn is a curve a few millimetres across.
Why the pen actuator has timing in the congThe pen is raised and lowered by a geared
motor that turns 90 degrees per pulse, so the time one pulse takes is a physical property that the
G-code must wait for. The pulse and settling times are recorded here and used to emit a dwell
of the right length. Getting that wrong does not fail cleanly: too short a dwell means drawing
begins while the pen is still descending, which produces a stroke that starts in the air.
The acceleration limit is declared in this record and is never read: no acceleration limiting is
implemented. Motion steps at a constant rate computed from the feed, so a move begins at
full speed. For the light gantry and modest feeds used that is tolerable, but a reader should not
take the eld's presence as evidence that a trapezoidal prole is applied.
217

=== PDF PAGE 219 ===
8.2 ToMachine
def To Ma ch in e ( traj , cfg , f i t T o B o u n d s = True , o p t i m i z e O r d e r = True )

What it is forA trajectory is positioned wherever synthesis happened to put it, with its origin

Why it never enlarges
k= min

(8.3)
where
218

=== PDF PAGE 220 ===
kthe scale factor actually applied
availW
the bed width available to the right of the origin
availH
the height available below it
w, h
the drawing's own width and height
1the cap, which is what prevents enlargement
The 1 in the minimum means the drawing is shrunk when it would not t and is otherwise left
at the size asked for. That is the right behaviour because the size was chosen deliberately: the
caller specied an x-height in millimetres, so the writing is meant to be that size. Scaling up to ll
the bed would override that choice, and a request for small writing would produce large writing.
Scaling down is dierent in kind: it is not overriding the request but avoiding an impossibility,
since a drawing larger than the bed cannot be drawn at all.
Why both axes get the same factorA single factor is taken as the smaller of the two ratios,
rather than each axis being scaled to t independently. Independent scaling would stretch the
writing, changing the proportions of every letter, which is both visibly wrong and destroys the
property the whole pipeline maintains, that the stored shapes are reproduced faithfully.
8.3 _Decimate
def _ Decimate ( poly , minSeg )
Inputs
poly
a path in millimetres
minSeg
the shortest segment to keep, in millimetres
Output.the path with points closer together than that merged away.
What it is forA path may carry far more points than the machine can act on. A traced stroke
has a point per pixel, and resampling during layout placed points every 0.35 mm. This removes
the points that are closer together than the machine can resolve.
The method, and why it is not Douglas-PeuckerEach point is kept only if it is at least the
minimum distance from the last point kept. This is a dierent rule from the path simplication in
style.py, which keeps points according to how far they stray from a straight line, and the dierence
is appropriate to the dierent purpose. Here the question is not whether a point carries shape
information but whether the machine can act on it at all: a commanded move shorter than the
minimum segment is below the resolution of the motion, so it costs a line in the le and produces
nothing.
219

=== PDF PAGE 221 ===
Why the comparison is against the last kept pointComparing against the last kept point
rather than the previous input point is what makes the rule accumulate correctly. Against the
previous input point, a long run of points each slightly under the threshold would all be dropped,
however far the path travelled in total. Against the last kept point, the distance accumulates until
the threshold is reached, so a point is emitted every minimum segment along the path regardless
of how nely the input was sampled.
The rst and last points are always kept, whatever the spacing. The endpoints dene where
a stroke begins and ends, so dropping one would shorten the stroke, and the nal point in
particular is where the pen lifts and where the next stroke's travel is measured from.
8.4 OptimizeStrokeOrder
def O p t i m i z e S t r o k e O r d e r ( strokes , rowTolMm = None )
Inputs
strokes
the strokes in bed coordinates
rowTolMm
how far apart two strokes may sit vertically and still count as the same row; derived if not
given
Output.the same strokes in an order that reduces pen-up travel.
What it is forThe strokes may be drawn in any order, since each is independent. The order
determines how far the pen travels raised between them, and that travel is pure waste: it takes
time and draws nothing. For a page of handwriting with hundreds of strokes the dierence between
a good order and a poor one is substantial.
Background: this is a travelling salesman problemFinding the order that minimises total
travel is thetravelling salesman problem: given a set of places, nd the shortest route visiting
all of them. It is one of the classic hard problems in computing, with no known method that nds
the exact best route in reasonable time as the number of places grows. With several hundred
strokes an exact answer is out of reach, so aheuristicis used: a rule that gives a good answer
quickly with no guarantee of being best.
The heuristic: bands, then nearest neighbourTwo ideas are combined. The strokes are
rst grouped into horizontal bands by their vertical centres, and the bands are processed from top
to bottom. Within a band, the nearest unvisited stroke is taken next.
Bandingexploits the structure of the problem. Text is arranged in lines, and almost all
the strokes of one line of writing are near each other, so a route that nishes one line before
starting the next is close to optimal. A pure nearest-neighbour search without banding can
wander between lines, since a stroke on the line below may happen to be nearer than the
next stroke along.
220

=== PDF PAGE 222 ===
Nearest neighbourwithin a band is the standard simple heuristic: from where the pen
is, go to the closest place not yet visited. It is greedy, so it can paint itself into a corner
and leave a long move to the last stroke, but within one line of text the strokes are nearly
collinear and the greedy route is close to the best.
Why the band tolerance is derived from the strokes
rowTol = max
 
1,1.2·median{h s }

(8.4)
where
rowTol
how far apart two strokes' vertical centres may be and still share a band, in millimetres
hs the height of stroke s, from its lowest point to its highest
1.2a margin above the typical stroke height
1a oor in millimetres, for a drawing of very small strokes
A xed tolerance would be wrong at every size but one. Deriving it from the median stroke height
ties it to the writing: a band should be about as tall as a line of text, and a typical stroke is about
the height of a letter. The median again rather than the mean, so that one tall stroke spanning
several lines, which a long descender or an underline can produce, does not widen every band.
Reversing a stroke to enter from the nearer endA stroke can be drawn from either end,
and the result is identical ink. So when the far end is closer to the pen than the near end, the
stroke is reversed. The code comment notes that this is free for a plotter, and it is worth saying
why: unlike a milling cut or an extrusion, a pen stroke has no direction-dependent behaviour, so
nothing is lost. It can nearly halve the travel to a long stroke.
Fewer than three strokes are returned unchanged. With two strokes there is only one mean-
ingful order, and the banding statistics would be computed from a sample too small to describe
anything.
8.5 _Clamp
def _ Clamp ( strokes , cfg )
Inputs
strokes
the strokes in bed coordinates
cfgthe machine conguration
Output.a pair: the strokes with every point forced inside the work area, and how many points
were moved.
What it is forA nal safety net. Every point is forced inside the machine's declared bounds,
and the number of points that had to be moved is counted and reported.
221

=== PDF PAGE 223 ===
Why it exists when T oMachine already ts the drawingThe tting scales the drawing
to the available area, so in principle nothing can lie outside it. This clamp exists for the cases
where that reasoning does not hold: the tting can be switched o by the caller, the origin can be
set such that the available area is computed wrongly, and a stroke can be reversed or reordered
by code that has no knowledge of the bounds. A point outside the work area is not a cosmetic
problem; it commands the machine to drive into its own frame.
Why the count is returnedClamping silently would hide a real fault. A drawing that needed
clamping was mispositioned, and the clamped version is not what was asked for: the aected
strokes are attened against the boundary, which is visible as a straight edge where writing
should be. Returning the count lets the writer emit a warning into the le, so the condition is
recorded where someone will see it rather than inferred from the drawing.
Clamping each coordinate independently is not geometrically principled: a stroke crossing the
boundary diagonally is bent rather than cut, so its shape changes. Properly clipping the path
to the rectangle would preserve the shape of the part inside. That is not done because the
condition is a fault to be reported rather than a case to be handled well, and the simple form
cannot itself fail.
8.6 NewPenState
def N e w P e n S t a t e ( cfg )
Inputs
cfgthe machine conguration
Output.a small mutable record holding whether the pen is up and how many pulses have been
emitted.
Why the pen's state must be tracked at allThis is the central peculiarity of the machine
and it shapes the whole of the G-code writing. The pen is raised and lowered by a geared motor
that turns 90 degrees per pulse. It is aone-way actuator: it accepts "move" and nothing
else. There is no command meaning "be up", and nothing in the G-code can ask where the pen
currently is.
The consequence is that the writer must know the pen's state by remembering it. The state begins
at a known value, assumed at power-on, and every pulse emitted ips it. Emitting a toggle when
the pen is already in the wanted state would move it to the wrong one, and from then on every
pen command in the le would be inverted: the machine would travel with the pen down and
draw with it up.
Why it is a list rather than a plain valueThe record is a two-element list so that the
function updating it can modify it in place and the caller sees the change. A tuple or an integer
would be copied, and the caller would keep the old state. This is the smallest possible piece of
shared mutable state and it exists because the pen genuinely has a state that persists across many
lines of output.
222

=== PDF PAGE 224 ===
The starting state is an assumption, not a measurement. If the machine is powered on with the
pen down, every pen command in the le is inverted. The hardware half resolves this properly,
with a switch that reports the pen 's actual position, but a written le cannot: it has no way to
ask.
8.7 SetPenLines
def S e t P e n L i n e s ( state , cfg , wantUp )
Inputs
state
the pen state record, updated in place
cfgthe machine conguration
wantUp
the state wanted
Output.the G-code lines needed to reach that state, which is nothing at all when it is already
current.
What it doesCompares the wanted state with the tracked one. If they agree, no lines are emit-
ted. If they dier, the state is ipped, the pulse count incremented, and two lines are produced:
the toggle command and a dwell.
Why emitting nothing is the important caseThe empty return is what makes the mech-
anism correct. Because the actuator toggles, a redundant command is not merely wasteful, it is
wrong. The writer calls this function before every travel move and before every stroke, which
means it is called many times with a state that is already current, and every one of those calls
must produce nothing. Placing that check inside the function rather than at each call site is what
guarantees it, since a single forgotten check at one call site would invert the rest of the le.
Why a dwell follows every toggle
dwell = penPulseMs + penSettleMs
1000 (8.5)
where
dwell
the wait emitted after a toggle, in seconds
penPulseMs
how long one 90-degree rotation takes
penSettleMs
extra time for the mechanism to come to rest
1000
the conversion from milliseconds to the seconds a G-code dwell expects
223

=== PDF PAGE 225 ===
G-code is executed line by line, and the machine would begin the next move as soon as the toggle
command was issued, while the pen was still travelling. Drawing would then start before the pen
reached the paper, losing the beginning of the stroke, or a travel move would begin before the pen
was clear, dragging a line across the page. The dwell blocks until the motion is complete. The
settling time is added on top of the rotation time because the mechanism has backlash and the
pen oscillates slightly as it lands.
The pulse count is accumulated and reported by the writer. It is a useful gure because each
pulse is a mechanical operation taking about a third of a second: a le with twice the necessary
pulses takes noticeably longer to draw and wears the actuator twice as fast. It is also how a
fault in the stroke ordering shows up, since a poor order lifts the pen more often than necessary.
8.8 WriteGcode
def W r i t e G c o d e ( traj , cfg , path , title = ' ')
Inputs
traj
the trajectory to write
cfgthe machine conguration
path
where to write the le
title
an optional title for the header comment
Output.a dictionary summarising what was written: line and stroke counts, pen pulses, the
scale applied, and how many points were clamped.
What it is forThe entry point of the top half. It places and orders the strokes, clamps them,
and writes the le.
The pattern for one stroke
Pen up,if it is not already, so the travel does not draw.
G0 to the stroke's rst point,at travel feed. A rapid move, since the path taken does
not matter with the pen raised.
Pen down,with its dwell.
G1 at drawing feed,then one G1 per remaining point.
The two feed rates are set once rather than repeated on every line, because G-code feed is modal:
it persists until changed. Repeating it would lengthen the le by a third for no eect.
224

=== PDF PAGE 226 ===
Why the header carries the machine's own numbersThe header comments record the
writer, the stroke count, the scale applied, the steps per millimetre for both axes, and how the
pen codes are to be interpreted. None of it aects the machine, since comments are ignored. It is
there because a G-code le outlives the program that wrote it, and a le whose scale and gearing
are unknown cannot be checked against the machine it is about to be run on. Recording the steps
per millimetre in particular makes it possible to tell afterwards whether a drawing came out the
wrong size because the le was wrong or because the machine was congured dierently.
The warning commentWhere points had to be clamped, a warning comment is written into
the le. That places the diagnosis where the evidence is: someone examining a drawing with a
attened edge looks at the le, and the explanation is in it. A warning printed to the console at
the time of writing would have scrolled away long before the drawing was made.
Parking at the endThe le ends by raising the pen, moving back to the origin, and issuing the
program-end command. Parking matters practically: it leaves the machine in a known position,
so the next le starts from the same place, and it takes the pen o the drawing, where a pen left
resting bleeds ink into the paper.
8.9 ParseGcode
def P a r s e G c o d e ( path )
Inputs
path
a G-code le to read
Output.the strokes recovered from it, as lists of points in millimetres.
What it is for, and why it is written separatelyReads a G-code le back into strokes,
by following the pen state and the coordinates through the le and collecting the points visited
with the pen down. Its purpose is verication: comparing what comes back with what went in
measures whether the le faithfully represents the intended geometry.
The code comment makes the design decision explicit: it shares no code with the writer, deliber-
ately. That is the whole value of the test. If both used a common routine to format and interpret
coordinates, a fault in that routine would cancel between them and the comparison would pass
while the le was wrong. Two independent implementations can only agree if the le itself is right,
which is the property being tested.
What the test can and cannot detect
It detectsrounding error from writing coordinates to three decimal places, a stroke re-
ordering or reversal that lost a point, a clamp that moved geometry, and any error in the
pen-state tracking, since a mistracked pen makes the reader collect the wrong points entirely.
It cannot detectanything about the machine. The feed rates, the dwells, the acceleration
and every mechanical eect are invisible to it. The measured agreement of better than 0.999
by area overlap is a statement about the le, not about the drawing.
225

=== PDF PAGE 227 ===
Being clear about that boundary is the point of describing the test at all. A round-trip agreement
this close is easy to misread as a statement that the robot draws accurately, and it is not: it says
the arithmetic between a pen path and a G-code le loses essentially nothing, which locates any
remaining error in the mechanics rather than in the software.
How the reader tracks stateIt maintains a current position and a pen ag. Comments
are stripped, the command letter is taken from the rst word, and coordinates are extracted by
pattern. A pen-down command starts a new stroke at the current position; a pen-up command
ends it; a move updates the position and, if the pen is down, appends the point.
Starting the stroke at the current position on pen-down, rather than at the rst move after it, is
what makes the recovered stroke begin where the writer's travel move left the pen. Without it
every stroke would be missing its rst point, and the comparison would show a systematic error
that was the reader's and not the le's.
Strokes of fewer than two points are discarded, which matches the writer, since it skips such
strokes too. A stroke still open at the end of the le is closed and kept, so a le that ends
without a nal pen-up does not lose its last stroke.
8.10 GantryNotConnectedError
class G a n t r y N o t C o n n e c t e d E r r o r
Output.an error type raised when something needs the hardware and the hardware is not
available.
What it is forRaised when the GPIO library is missing or the chip device cannot be opened,
which is the ordinary situation on any computer that is not the machine's own controller.
Why a named error rather than letting the import failAn unguarded import of the
hardware library would make this module unimportable o the machine, and since the test and
evaluation scripts all import it, none of them could run anywhere else. A distinct error type
lets the hardware functions fail cleanly when called, while the arithmetic half of the le remains
usable.
Being a distinct type rather than a generic error is what lets a caller distinguish "there is no
machine here" from "the machine is there and something went wrong". The rst is expected and
should be reported as a plain message; the second is a fault.
8.11 NotCalibratedError
class N o t C a l i b r a t e d E r r o r
Output.an error type raised when an absolute move is attempted before homing.
226

=== PDF PAGE 228 ===
Why calibration is a precondition and not a convenienceA stepper motor has no idea
where it is. It moves a commanded number of steps from wherever it happens to be, and nothing
in the motor or the driver reports a position. So a command to move to a particular place on the
bed is meaningless until the machine has established where that place is relative to its current
position, which is what homing does.
Executing such a command anyway is not a silent error. The machine drives toward a position
computed from an unknown origin, which is as likely as not to be outside the bed, and it will drive
into its own frame until the limit switches catch it. A distinct error type refusing the command
is the appropriate response to a request that cannot be carried out safely.
This is one of the few places in the project where a function refuses rather than falling back.
The usual pattern is to return a degraded result, but there is no degraded version of a physical
move: it either goes to the right place or it drives the machine somewhere arbitrary.
8.12 CHIP_PATH
CHIP _ PATH = '/ dev / gpiochip0 '
Output.the device path of the GPIO controller.
Background: what a GPIO chip is GPIOstands for general-purpose input/output: pins
on a single-board computer that software can drive high or low, or read as high or low. They
are how a program controls hardware that has no other interface, which here means the stepper
drivers, the limit switches and the pen motor.
On Linux the pins are reached through a device le representing the controller. Opening it yields
a handle through which individual lines are requested and then set or read. The path names which
controller, and a board with several exposes several.
This is a Linux path, which is part of why the hardware half must be guarded: on any other
system the le does not exist, and the library that opens it is not installed.
8.13 PEN_SWITCH_PIN
PEN _ SWITCH _ PIN = 70
Output.the GPIO line number of the switch that reports the pen's position.
Why the pen has a sensor when the G-code path only tracks itThe written le has no
choice but to remember the pen's state, because a le cannot ask a question. The machine can.
This switch closes when the pen is raised, so the hardware half knows the pen's actual position
rather than its assumed one.
The dierence is what allows the hardware to be correct where the le can only be consistent.
SetPen compares the wanted state with the measured one and does nothing if they already agree,
so a pen that starts in an unexpected position is handled rather than inverting everything that
follows. It also allows a failure to be detected: if the motor runs and the switch never changes,
something is mechanically wrong, and that can be reported instead of the machine continuing to
draw with a pen that never came down.
227

=== PDF PAGE 229 ===
The switch reports only one of the two states directly: it is closed when up and open otherwise.
That is sucient, since there are only two states, but it means a fault in which the pen sticks
halfway reads as "down". The overrun time below is what guards against the related case of
stopping the motor the instant the switch rst trips.
8.14 PEN_MOTOR_PIN
PEN _ MOTOR _ PIN = 71
Output.the GPIO line number that drives the pen actuator.
How the actuator is drivenSetting this line active runs the geared motor; setting it inactive
stops it. There is no position control and no direction: the motor turns one way only, and a
quarter turn of its cam moves the pen between up and down.
Why the software must decide when to stop itBecause the motor has no limit of its own,
running it too long carries the cam past the position wanted and begins the next quarter turn,
which returns the pen to where it started. So the software runs the motor while watching the
switch and stops it when the wanted state is reached. That is a closed loop, in contrast with the
written le's open-loop timed dwell, and it is why the hardware path is more robust than the le
path for the same operation.
8.15 ENDSTOP_PINS
ENDSTOP _ PINS = { ' X _ MIN ': X _ MIN _ PIN , ' X _ MAX ': X _ MAX _ PIN , ' Y _ MIN ': Y _ MIN _ PIN ,
'Y _ MAX ': Y _ MAX _ PIN }
Output.a mapping from each limit switch's name to its GPIO line number.
What a limit switch is forAnendstopor limit switch is a mechanical switch at the end of an
axis's travel, closed when the carriage reaches it. Four are tted, at the minimum and maximum
of each axis, and they serve two distinct purposes.
Homing.Since the motors cannot report their position, the only way to establish one is to
drive until a switch is reached, which is a known place. Calibration drives to both ends of
each axis, which also measures the travel between them.
Protection.During any motion, a switch closing means the carriage has reached a limit it
was not supposed to reach, which stops the machine. Without that, a mistaken coordinate
drives the carriage into the frame and the motor stalls, skipping steps and losing the position
entirely.
Why they are named rather than numberedThe switches are held in a mapping keyed by
name, so the code refers to X_MIN rather than to a pin number. The names carry the information
the logic needs: which axis a switch belongs to and which direction to retreat when it trips. The
retreat direction table is keyed by the same names, so the two cannot drift out of correspondence,
which a pair of parallel numeric lists could.
228

=== PDF PAGE 230 ===
8.16 INVERT_X
INVERT _ X = True
Output.true, meaning the X axis's direction signal is reversed.
What it correctsA stepper driver has a direction pin: one level turns the motor one way and
the other the opposite. Which physical direction each level produces depends on how the motor's
coils happen to be wired to the driver and on how the motor is mounted relative to the axis.
There is no convention that settles it, and the wiring is a four-conductor connector that can be
tted either way round.
So the mapping between the signal and the physical direction is determined by trying it, and the
result is recorded here. The alternative, rewiring the motor until the default convention happens
to be right, achieves nothing a single ag cannot.
How the ag is appliedAxisDirValue combines the wanted direction with this ag using an
exclusive-or, so a wanted positive direction with inversion set gives the inactive level. Keeping the
correction in one function means every motion path inherits it, and a new motion routine cannot
forget it as long as it asks for a direction by meaning rather than by signal level.
8.17 INVERT_Y
INVERT _ Y = True
Output.true, meaning the Y axis's direction signal is reversed as well.
Why each axis has its own agThe two axes are wired and mounted independently, so the
correction each needs is independent. Both happen to require inversion on this machine, which is
coincidence rather than something that could be assumed: a single shared ag would work here
and would be wrong on a machine where only one axis needed it.
8.18 MICROSTEPS
M I C R O S T E P S = 16
Output.16, the microstepping division set on the drivers.
What it recordsThe driver divides each of the motor's full steps into this many sub-steps,
which is set by jumpers on the driver board. The value here must match those jumpers: the
software cannot read them, so a mismatch produces motion scaled by a factor of two or more with
no error of any kind, only a drawing of the wrong size.
Why sixteenIt is the nest division the A4988 driver used oers, and the reason to take the
nest is smoothness rather than resolution. At 1/16 the motor advances in increments small
229

=== PDF PAGE 231 ===
enough that the motion is eectively continuous, which matters for drawing curves a few millime-
tres across: coarser stepping is audible as a buzz and visible as faceting on a curve. The cost is
that sixteen times as many step pulses must be issued per millimetre, which for a software-timed
stepper sets the maximum speed.
This constant duplicates the microstepping eld in GantryCong, because the two halves of the
le were written to be independent and each carries its own copy of the machine's numbers.
The duplication is a real hazard: the two must be changed together, and nothing enforces it.
8.19 STEPS_PER_REV
STEPS _ PER _ REV = 200 * M I C R O S T E P S
Output.3200, the microsteps per motor revolution.
How it is obtainedThe motor's 200 full steps per revolution multiplied by the microstepping.
Computing it rather than stating 3200 keeps the two factors visible, each of which is a checkable
fact about the hardware, and means a change to the microstepping jumpers is one edit.
200 appears here as a literal rather than as a named constant, which is the one piece of the
derivation not spelled out. It is the motor's step angle of 1.8 degrees divided into 360.
8.20 STEPS_PER_MM
STEPS _ PER _ MM = 80.0
Output.80.0, a provisional steps-per-millimetre gure.
What it is for, and why it is provisionalA placeholder used only before calibration has
run. Once the machine has homed, it hasmeasuredits own steps per millimetre, by counting the
steps between the two limit switches and dividing by the known distance between them, and the
measured gure replaces this one.
Why a measurement beats the calculationThe calculated 80 assumes the gearing is exactly
as specied, and it is not quite. A GT2 belt's pitch varies slightly with tension and temperature,
a pulley's eective radius depends on where the belt sits in its teeth, and both are manufactured
to a tolerance. Measuring the actual travel captures all of that at once, without needing to know
which part contributed what. The measured value is typically within a percent or two of the
calculated one, which over a 200 mm page is a few millimetres.
The calculated value remains in use in one place: RunGcodeFile reads the starting position
using this constant rather than the calibrated gure, so the position it begins from is slightly
o even after calibration. The subsequent moves use the calibrated value, so the error does
not accumulate, but the rst move of a job is aected.
230

=== PDF PAGE 232 ===
8.21 CIRCLE_STEP_DELAY_S
CIRCLE _ STEP _ DELAY _ S = 2e -06
Output.0.000002, the shortest delay between step pulses, in seconds.
What it boundsA step pulse is produced by setting a pin high, waiting, and setting it low.
This is the wait, and it therefore sets the fastest the motor can be driven. Two microseconds
between edges is near the practical oor for a driver's minimum pulse width and well beyond
what the motor can follow.
Why it is used as a oor rather than a speedMotion is normally timed from the requested
feed rate: the delay is computed so that the move takes the time the feed implies. This constant
is the lower bound on that computation, so a feed rate too high to be achieved is silently reduced
to the fastest the machine can step instead of producing a negative or zero delay.
The delay is produced by asking the operating system to sleep, which on a general-purpose
Linux system is accurate to perhaps tens of microseconds rather than to two. So at short
delays the actual rate is set by the operating system's scheduling rather than by this value, and
the real maximum speed is lower and less regular than the constant suggests. Step timing of
this kind properly belongs in dedicated rmware or a hardware timer.
8.22 DEF AULT_CIRCLE_RADIUS_MM
DEFAULT _ CIRCLE _ RADIUS _ MM = 8.0
Output.8.0, the radius of the test circle in millimetres.
What it is forThe machine has a test mode that drives a continuous circle, and this is its
default radius. A circle is a useful test pattern because it exercises both axes continuously and in
varying proportion, so it reveals faults that a straight line does not: a mismatch between the two
axes' steps per millimetre shows as an ellipse, and backlash in a belt shows as a at spot where
an axis reverses.
8.23 X_SWITCH_TRA VEL_MM

Why this number is the key to calibrationCalibration counts the steps between the two
switches, which gives a travel in steps. Converting that into steps per millimetre requires knowing
the travel in millimetres, and that is this constant, measured once from the physical machine with
a rule.
stepsPerMmX = travelStepsX
X_SWITCH_TRAVEL_MM (8.6)

where

travelStepsX

X_SWITCH_TRAVEL_MM
