So the accuracy of every subsequent move rests on this one hand-measured gure. An error of
one percent in it scales the whole drawing by one percent, and nothing in the system can detect
that, because the machine has no other reference for absolute distance. It is the single value in
the project that must be got right by measurement rather than by reasoning.
The distance is between the switches'trip points, not between the switch bodies or the ends of
the rail. A switch trips a little before the carriage reaches it, by however much its lever travels,
so the gure has to be taken from where motion actually stops.
8.24 Y_SWITCH_TRA VEL_MM
Y _ SWITCH _ TRAVEL _ MM = 262.0
Output.262.0, the same distance for the Y axis.
Why the two axes dierThe axes are physically dierent lengths, 204 and 262 millimetres,
which is simply how the machine is built. Each is calibrated against its own gure, giving each
its own steps per millimetre. Keeping them separate also allows the two axes to come out with
slightly dierent scaling, which they will even with identical gearing, because belt tension and
pulley tolerance dier between them. Forcing a single shared value would make circles come out
as ellipses.
8.25 EDGE_TOLERANCE_MM
EDGE _ TO LE RA NC E _ MM = 5.0
Output.5.0, the margin kept clear of every limit switch, in millimetres.
What it is forAfter homing, the usable area is dened as the measured travel less this margin
at each end of each axis, and the coordinate origin is placed this far in from the minimum switches.
So the machine never deliberately drives within ve millimetres of any switch.
Why a margin is necessaryDriving to a position where a switch is about to trip is hazardous
in a specic way. A limit switch tripping during normal motion is treated as an emergency: the
drive stops, the pen lifts and the job aborts. If the usable area reached the switches exactly,
then ordinary rounding, a step lost to a sti belt, or the thermal expansion of the frame would
232

=== PDF PAGE 234 ===
occasionally trip a switch during a legitimate move, and a job would abort partway through a
drawing for no real fault.
Five millimetres is comfortably more than any of those eects and costs ten millimetres of travel
on each axis, under ve percent of the bed. That is a cheap price for the dierence between a
boundary that is occasionally crossed and one that is crossed only when something is genuinely
wrong.
usableWidth = switchTravel−2·EDGE_TOLERANCE_MM(8.7)
where
usableWidth
the width the machine will address
switchTravel
the measured distance between the switches
2the margin applies at both ends
Because the origin is oset by this margin, the conversion between a coordinate and a step count
adds it back: a commanded 0 corresponds to the margin 's worth of steps from the minimum
switch, not to zero steps. Both _GcodeLinearMove and the position readback have to apply
that oset, and a mismatch between them would put every move out by ve millimetres.
8.26 HOMING_STEP_DELAY_S
HOMING _ STEP _ DELAY _ S = 0.0008
Output.0.0008, the per-step delay used while homing, in seconds.
Why homing is slowThis delay is four hundred times the minimum, making homing much
slower than ordinary motion. Three reasons require that.
The switch must be detected before the carriage reaches the frame.At high speed
many steps elapse between consecutive switch readings, and the carriage can travel several
millimetres past the trip point, which on an axis whose switch sits close to the frame means
driving into it.
The stop must not lose steps.Stopping a fast-moving axis abruptly can make the motor
skip, and a skipped step during homing corrupts the measurement that calibration exists to
produce.
The direction is unknown at the start.Homing drives until something stops it, with
no knowledge of how far away that is, so the motion is deliberately cautious.
Since homing happens once per session and takes a few seconds, the cost is negligible, which makes
this an easy trade: all the speed in exchange for reliability on the one measurement everything
else depends on.
233

=== PDF PAGE 235 ===
8.27 HOMING_MAX_STEPS
HOMING _ MAX _ STEPS = 200000
Output.200000, the most steps homing will take before giving up.
What it protects againstHoming drives until a switch trips. If no switch ever trips, because
one is disconnected, miswired or mechanically stuck, the loop would run forever while the motor
drives the carriage into the frame and stalls there. This bound turns an endless fault into a
reported one.
Why this manyAt 80 steps per millimetre, 200000 steps is 2500 millimetres, about ten times
the longest axis. So a correctly functioning machine cannot approach the limit however far from
the switch it starts, while a broken one reaches it in under a minute at the homing delay. The
margin is deliberately generous because the consequence of setting it too low, a false failure on a
machine that was simply far from its switch, is worse than taking longer to report a real one.
Exceeding it raises an error naming the wiring as the likely cause, which is the right message:
by far the commonest reason for a switch never tripping is a connector that has worked loose,
and the person reading the message is standing next to the machine.
8.28 ENDSTOP_DEBOUNCE_S
ENDSTOP _ DEBOUNCE _ S = 0.006
Output.0.006, the gap between conrmation samples of a switch, in seconds.
Background: contact bounce and electrical noiseA mechanical switch does not close
cleanly. Its contacts strike and separate several times over a few milliseconds before settling, so
a single reading taken during that window may catch it either way. This iscontact bounce.
Separately, the wires running to the switches are long and run alongside the motor cables, which
carry switched currents of an amp or more, and that induces voltage spikes on the switch lines.
Either eect can make a switch read as closed when it is not. Since a switch reading closed triggers
an emergency stop, a spurious reading aborts a drawing that was proceeding correctly, which on
a long page is a real cost.
The remedy: sample repeatedly , spaced out in timeA switch is believed only if it reads
closed on several consecutive samples with this delay between them. The spacing is what makes
it work: bounce and induced noise are brief, lasting microseconds to a millisecond or two, so
samples six milliseconds apart are eectively independent of one another and a transient cannot
be present in all of them. Sampling rapidly instead would read the same disturbance several times
and conrm it.
8.29 ENDSTOP_CONFIRM_SAMPLES
ENDSTOP _ CONFIRM _ SAMPLES = 3
234

=== PDF PAGE 236 ===
Output.3, how many consecutive readings are required before a switch is believed.
The trade-oMore samples reject noise more reliably and delay a genuine detection. The delay
matters because the axis keeps moving while the switch is being conrmed: with three samples six
milliseconds apart the conrmation takes twelve milliseconds, during which a carriage travelling
at the drawing feed covers about 0.2 millimetres. Three is enough that a transient surviving all
three is very unlikely, while the overshoot stays well inside the ve-millimetre margin kept clear
of the switches.
Why it exits early on a disagreementThe check stops as soon as any sample reads open,
concluding that the switch is not pressed. This is the asymmetry that makes the cost acceptable:
the expensive path, taking all three samples, is only followed when the rst reading is closed,
which is rare. In the normal case of a switch reading open the function returns after one reading,
so the debounce costs nothing during ordinary motion, and it is called before every single step.
8.30 RETRACT_MM
RETRACT _ MM = 5.0
Output.5.0, how far to back o a tripped switch, in millimetres.
Why a retract is needed after a stopWhen a switch trips, the carriage is resting against
it. Leaving it there is unworkable for two reasons. The switch continues to read closed, so every
subsequent check sees a tripped endstop and refuses to move, including the move needed to get
clear: the machine is stuck. And a switch held closed under pressure can be damaged over time.
So after an emergency stop the axis is stepped away from the switch until it releases, by this
distance. Five millimetres matches the edge margin, which is sensible: it is the distance that puts
the carriage back inside the usable area, so the machine ends up where it is allowed to be rather
than just clear of the switch.
Why the retract watches the other switchesThe retract loop checks for any switch other
than the one being retracted from, and stops if one trips. That case means something is genuinely
wrong, a switch stuck closed or an axis shorter than believed, and continuing to drive would push
against a second limit. Stopping and reporting is the correct response, since the situation cannot
be resolved by moving.
8.31 PEN_UP_OVERRUN_S
PEN _ UP _ OVERRUN _ S = 0.04
Output.0.04, extra running time after the pen switch rst trips, in seconds.
The problem it solvesThe switch trips the moment the pen reaches the up position, and
stopping the motor there leaves the cam exactly at the point where the switch just closed. That
is the worst place to stop. A small amount of backlash, a vibration from the next move, or the
235

=== PDF PAGE 237 ===
pen's own weight can rotate the cam back past the trip point, and the pen drops onto the page
during a travel move, drawing a line across the drawing.
Running on for a further 40 milliseconds carries the cam past the trip point into the middle of its
up region, where neither backlash nor vibration can bring it back. The principle is general: when
a detector marks the boundary of a safe region, stop inside the region rather than on its edge.
Why only on the way upThe overrun is applied when raising the pen and not when lowering
it. Down is the position gravity and the pen's spring favour, so it is stable without help, whereas
up is held against them. Overrunning on the way down would also press the pen harder into the
paper than intended, which broadens the stroke and can damage the nib.
8.32 GCODE_MIN_FEED_MM_S
GCODE _ MIN _ FEED _ MM _ S = 0.1
Output.0.1, the slowest feed rate accepted, in millimetres per second.
What it protects againstThe step delay is computed by dividing the move's duration by its
step count, and the duration is the distance divided by the feed. A feed of zero therefore divides
by zero. A le containingF0, whether through a typing error or a generator's fault, would crash
the job partway through a drawing.
Why a oor rather than a rejectionClamping to a very slow but nite rate means such
a line produces a slow move instead of a failure. That is the better behaviour here because the
alternative, aborting, loses the whole drawing, whereas one unusually slow move is a visible pause
and nothing more. At a tenth of a millimetre per second the move is clearly wrong and clearly
deliberate, so it is noticeable without being destructive.
8.33 _GCODE_WORD_RE
_ GCODE _ WORD _ RE = re . compile ( '([ A - Za - z ]) \\ s *( -?[0 -9]*\\.?[0 -9]+) ')
Output.the compiled pattern that splits a G-code line into letters and numbers.
What a G-code word isA G-code line is a sequence ofwords, each a letter followed by a
number:G1,X12.5,F900. This pattern matches one such word, capturing the letter and the
number separately, and nding every match in a line splits it into its parts.
Why the pattern allows space between themIt permits whitespace between the letter and
the number, soX 12.5parses as well asX12.5. Both forms occur, because dierent generators
format dierently and some les are hand-edited. A reader of a standard interchange format
should accept the variations the standard permits rather than only the form it writes itself.
236

=== PDF PAGE 238 ===
Why the number pattern is as it isIt accepts an optional minus sign, any number of digits,
an optional decimal point and more digits, so it matches5,-5,5.25and.5alike. The last of
those is why the integer part is optional: a coordinate written as.5is valid and would otherwise
be read as a word with no number at all.
The pattern is compiled once at module level rather than on each use. A compiled pattern is
parsed into its matching machinery once instead of on every call, which matters here because
this is applied to every line of a le that may run to tens of thousands.
8.34 _RETREAT_SIGN
_ RETREAT _ SIGN = { ' X _ MIN ': +1 , 'X _ MAX ': -1 , 'Y _ MIN ': +1 , 'Y _ MAX ': -1}
Output.a mapping from each switch's name to the direction to move to get o it.
What it encodesWhich way is away from each switch: positive for the minimum switches and
negative for the maximum ones. A minimum switch sits at the low end of the axis, so escaping it
means moving toward higher coordinates, and the reverse for a maximum.
Why it is a table and not computed from the nameThe sign could be derived by testing
whether the name ends in MIN. A table states it instead, which is better for a value with physical
consequences: driving the wrong way o a switch pushes the carriage harder into its limit. A
table is read directly against the machine, whereas a derivation depends on a naming convention
holding, and a switch named dierently would silently produce the wrong direction rather than a
missing-key error.
8.35 Calibration
C a l i b r a t i o n = { ' done ': False , ' steps _ per _ mm _x ': STEPS _ PER _ MM ,
' steps _ per _ mm _y ': STEPS _ PER _ MM ,
' usable _ width _ mm ': X _ SWITCH _ TRAVEL _ MM - 2 * EDGE _ T OL ER AN CE _ MM ,
' usable _ height _ mm ': Y _ SWITCH _ TRAVEL _ MM - 2 * EDGE _ T OL ER AN CE _ MM }
Output.a dictionary holding the measured machine geometry and whether homing has been
done.
What it holdsThe measured steps per millimetre for each axis, the usable area in millimetres,
and a ag saying whether calibration has run. Everything in it is a measurement rather than a
conguration: the values are produced by driving the machine and counting, not by being stated.
Why the ag is checked rather than the valuesCode requiring calibration tests the ag,
not whether the steps-per-millimetre values look plausible. That is the right test because the
values have defaults that are perfectly plausible, being the calculated 80: there is no value of
them that means "not yet measured". An explicit ag can express that where the data cannot.
This is module-level mutable state, shared by every function in the hardware half, which is the
pattern the whole bottom half uses. It is defensible here in a way it would not be elsewhere:
237

=== PDF PAGE 239 ===
there is exactly one physical machine, so a single shared record of its state is an accurate model
of the situation, and two independent copies would be able to disagree about a machine that
can only be in one state.
8.36 req
req = None
Output.the live GPIO line request once a connection has been made, or nothing before that.
What it holdsThe handle through which every pin is set and read. It is obtained by requesting
a set of lines from the GPIO chip, and until that has happened it is nothing, which is also how
the module knows whether it is connected.
Why requesting lines is exclusiveThe Linux GPIO interface grants a requested line exclu-
sively to the process that asked for it: no other program can drive the same pin while the request
is held. That is essential here, since two programs both driving a stepper's step pin would produce
motion neither intended. It also means the request must be released on shutdown, or the pins
remain claimed by a process that has exited and the next run cannot acquire them.
Using the handle's presence as the connection ag means there is one piece of state rather
than two, so the ag cannot disagree with reality. A separate boolean would be able to say
connected while the handle was absent, which is the kind of inconsistency that produces an
error far from its cause.
8.37 CurrentMode
C u r r e n t M o d e = ' RPM '
Output.which of the manual control modes the machine is in.
What the modes areThe hardware half includes a small interactive console for testing, and
this says what it is currently doing: turning the motors at a set speed, drawing a test circle,
running a G-code le, or homing. The main loop reads it each pass and acts accordingly.
Why a mode variable rather than separate loopsA single loop that consults a mode
can be interrupted and redirected from the keyboard thread at any moment, because the thread
only has to change one variable. Separate loops per mode would each need their own mechanism
for being told to stop, and switching between them would mean leaving one loop and entering
another, during which the motors would be in an undened state.
The whole manual console exists for bringing the machine up and diagnosing it, not for draw-
ing. A drawing job is run by RunGcodeFile, which does not consult the mode at all.
238

=== PDF PAGE 240 ===
8.38 targetRpm
ta r ge tR pm = 0.0
Output.0.0, the wanted motor speed in revolutions per minute.
Why the initial value mattersThe code comment is explicit that this must start at zero,
and the reason is a real hazard rather than tidiness. The main loop begins stepping as soon as it
starts, at whatever speed this says. A non-zero initial value would move the gantry the instant
the program ran, before the operator had asked for anything and quite possibly before homing,
so the machine would drive from an unknown position toward an unknown one.
A device that moves on startup is a genuine safety problem, and the general principle is that a
machine's initial state should be the one in which it does nothing. The same reasoning sets the
step pins inactive and the pen motor o when the pins are rst claimed.
8.39 stepDelayS
s t e p D e l a y S = 0.0
Output.0.0, the pause between step pulses derived from the wanted speed.
What it isThe delay the main loop waits between step edges, computed from the requested
speed by CalculateDelay. A value of zero means stopped, which is consistent with the initial speed
of zero.
A delay and a speed are inversely related, so this variable and targetRpm hold the same infor-
mation in two forms. The derived form is cached because it is needed in the innermost loop,
where recomputing a division per step would be wasteful. The hazard of a cached derivative is
that the two can disagree, which is why the only function that sets the speed also sets this.
8.40 running
running = True
Output.true while the main loop should continue.
How a thread is asked to stopThe main loop tests this each pass and exits when it becomes
false, which the keyboard thread sets on a quit command. This is acooperative shutdown:
the loop is asked to stop and does so at a point of its own choosing, rather than being terminated
from outside.
Why that matters for a machineTerminating a thread part way through a step pulse would
leave a step pin high, so the driver would hold the coil energised indenitely, heating the motor.
Letting the loop nish its current iteration guarantees every pulse completes and the shutdown
path can then set the pins to a safe state and release them. For software a forced stop is usually
recoverable; for a machine holding a physical state it is not.
239

=== PDF PAGE 241 ===
8.41 CurrentXSteps
C u r r e n t X S t e p s = 0
Output.0, the X axis position in steps from the homing reference.
Why the position is tracked in softwareThis is the machine's only knowledge of where it
is. A stepper motor has no position feedback: it moves when pulsed and reports nothing. So the
position is maintained by counting, incremented or decremented on every single step issued by
every motion routine.
What that implies, and why it is acceptedThe count is the position only as long as every
pulse produced a real step. A motor that stalls, from an obstruction or an over-fast acceleration,
skips steps that were counted, and the recorded position silently becomes wrong by that amount
with no way to detect it. This isopen-loopcontrol, and it is universal in low-cost motion systems
because closing the loop requires an encoder on every axis.
What makes it workable is that the error does not accumulate unnoticed indenitely: homing
re-establishes the position from a physical reference, and the limit switches catch the case where
the error is large enough to drive into a limit. Within one drawing, the loads are light and the
feeds modest, so stalling is unlikely.
A step count is an integer, so counting cannot accumulate rounding error the way tracking a
position in millimetres would. That is the reason the count is kept in steps and converted to
millimetres only when a coordinate is needed.
8.42 CurrentYSteps
C u r r e n t Y S t e p s = 0
Output.0, the Y axis position in steps.
Why the axes are counted separatelyEach axis has its own motor, its own switches and its
own calibrated scaling, so each needs its own count. Keeping them separate also means the two
can be converted with dierent steps-per-millimetre gures, which they must be: the calibration
measures each axis independently and the two dier slightly even with identical gearing.
8.43 theta
theta = 0.0
Output.0.0, the current angle of the test circle in radians.
What it is forThe circle test mode advances this angle a little each pass and moves to the
corresponding point on the circle, so the carriage traces a continuous circle. It persists between
loop passes because the position must continue from where it reached, not restart.
240

=== PDF PAGE 242 ===
This is a convenience for mechanical testing rather than part of drawing. A circle run con-
tinuously is the most useful single test pattern for a gantry, since it exercises both axes in
constantly varying proportion and makes axis mismatch and belt backlash visible as a distorted
shape.
8.44 LastCircleTime
L a s t C i r c l e T i m e = time . perf _ counter ()
Output.the time the circle test last advanced.
Why the time is recorded rather than the loop simply sleepingThe circle must advance
at a steady rate, and the main loop does other work whose duration varies. Recording when the
last advance happened and comparing against the current time lets the loop advance when enough
time has elapsed, independently of how long the rest of the pass took. Sleeping for a xed interval
instead would make the real period the interval plus the work, so the circle's speed would depend
on what else the loop was doing.
A high-resolution performance counter is used rather than wall-clock time, because it is monotonic:
it cannot jump backwards if the system clock is adjusted, which would make an elapsed-time
comparison give a negative result.
8.45 CircleIntervalS
C i r c l e I n t e r v a l S = 0.0015
Output.0.0015, how often the test circle advances, in seconds.
What it setsThe interval between successive points of the circle, which together with the
angular step sets how fast the circle is traced. At 1.5 milliseconds the motion appears continuous.
8.46 CircleRadiusMm
C i r c l e R a d i u s M m = DEFAULT _ CIRCLE _ RADIUS _ MM
Output.the current test circle radius in millimetres.
Why it is separate from the defaultIt starts from the default constant and can be changed
from the keyboard during testing, so the current value and the default are distinct: the default is
what the machine starts with and this is what it is doing now. Varying the radius during a test
is useful because the faults a circle reveals show up dierently at dierent sizes, backlash being
most visible on a small circle and axis mismatch on a large one.
8.47 CircleRadiusSteps
241

=== PDF PAGE 243 ===
C i r c l e R a d i u s S t e p s = C i r c l e R a d i u s M m * STEPS _ PER _ MM
Output.the same radius converted into steps.
Why it is precomputedThe circle loop needs the radius in steps on every pass, and converting
once avoids repeating the multiplication inside the motion loop.
The conversion uses the uncalibrated constant rather than the measured scaling, so a circle
drawn after calibration is slightly the wrong size. For a mechanical test pattern that does not
matter, since what is being looked at is the shape rather than the dimensions, but it is the
same duplication hazard noted under STEPS_PER_MM.
8.48 estopped
estopped = False
Output.false, becoming true once an emergency stop has red.
What it enforcesOnce an emergency stop has happened, this stays set until someone clears
it from the console. Every motion path checks it, so nothing moves in the meantime.
Why the stop latches instead of clearing itselfThis is the important property. After
the retract, the switch is no longer pressed, so a test that only looked at the switches would
conclude all was well and allow motion to resume. But the reason the switch tripped has not been
addressed: the coordinate was wrong, or the belt slipped, or the work is not where it was thought
to be. Resuming would drive into the same limit again.
Alatchingfault requires a human to acknowledge it, which is the standard arrangement for a
machine's emergency stop and for the same reason: the stop indicates that the machine's idea of
the world is wrong, and only a person can establish whether it is right again.
8.49 TrippedSwitch
T r i p p e d S w i t c h = None
Output.which switch caused the emergency stop, or nothing.
Why the identity is keptKnowing that a stop occurred is not enough to act on it. Which
switch tripped says which axis and which end, which tells the retract which way to move and tells
the operator where to look. The message printed on a stop names the switch for exactly that
reason.
8.50 Connect
def Connect ()
Output.nothing; it claims the GPIO lines or raises if it cannot.
242

=== PDF PAGE 244 ===
What it doesRequests every pin the machine uses from the GPIO chip in a single call, declaring
each as an input or an output and giving each output its initial level.
Why all the pins are requested at onceOne request for the whole set is better than ten
separate ones. It is atomic: either every pin is obtained or none is, so the module cannot end
up holding half the pins it needs, with some motion possible and some not. And it establishes
every initial level in the same operation, so there is no window during which some outputs are
congured and others are oating.
The initial levels, and why they are what they are
Step pins inactive.A step is a transition, so holding them low means no step is in progress
and none is commanded.
Pen motor inactive.The actuator is o, so the pen stays wherever it is rather than
beginning to turn.
Direction pins active.The level is arbitrary, since every motion sets the direction before
stepping, and a direction pin by itself causes no movement.
Every switch an input.Driving a switch line as an output would ght the switch and
could damage the pin.
The common thread is that the initial state must be one in which nothing moves, which is the
same principle that puts the initial speed at zero.
Why failure is translatedAny failure to open the chip is caught and re-raised as the module's
own not-connected error, with the original attached. The handle is set back to nothing rst, so a
failed connection does not leave the module believing it is connected. Translating the error lets
a caller distinguish an absent machine, which is expected on a laptop, from a fault on a machine
that is present, without having to know what the GPIO library raises.
Calling it twice returns immediately rather than requesting the lines again, which would fail
since the lines are held exclusively by this same process. Making repeat calls harmless lets
every entry point call it without tracking whether someone else already has.
8.51 Disconnect
def D i s c o n n e c t ()
Output.nothing; it sets the outputs safe and releases the lines.
The order of operationsThe step pins and the pen motor are set inactive rst, and only
then are the lines released. The order is necessary: once a line is released, its level is no longer
controlled, and a line released while active may stay active. Releasing a step pin while high would
leave the driver holding a coil energised, which heats the motor with nothing running to notice.
243

=== PDF PAGE 245 ===
Why the release is in a nally clauseThe setting of safe levels is wrapped so that the release
happens whether or not it succeeded. If a pin cannot be set, because the chip has gone away or
the device was unplugged, the lines must still be released, or they stay claimed by a process that
has exited and the next run cannot acquire them. Releasing a resource must not depend on the
success of the tidying done before it.
8.52 IsConnected
def I s C o n n e c t e d ()
Output.whether the GPIO lines are currently claimed.
What it is forTests whether the handle exists. Callers use it to report the machine's availability
without attempting an operation that would raise, which is what a user interface needs: it must
say whether the machine is there, not nd out by trying to move it.
8.53 CalculateDelay
def C a l c u l a t e D e l a y ( rpm )
Inputs
rpmthe wanted motor speed in revolutions per minute
Output.nothing; it sets the module's step delay.
The conversion
tdelay = 1
2 · 60
|rpm| ·stepsPerRev (8.8)
where
tdelay
the pause between step pin edges, in seconds
rpmthe wanted speed in revolutions per minute
stepsPerRev
microsteps per revolution, 3200 here
60seconds per minute, converting the speed into revolutions per second
1
2 the halving, explained below
Read it from the inside out. The speed in revolutions per minute divided by 60 is revolutions per
second; multiplied by the steps per revolution it is steps per second; its reciprocal is seconds per
step. That is the time one whole step must occupy.
244

=== PDF PAGE 246 ===
Why the result is halvedA step is produced by two transitions: the pin goes high, waits,
goes low, waits. So the delay is used twice per step, and each wait must be half the step period
for the step to take the time intended. Omitting the halving would run the motor at half the
requested speed, which is the kind of error that looks like a mechanical problem.
The dead band at low speedsA requested speed below 0.1 revolutions per minute sets the
delay to zero, meaning stopped. Without it, a speed approaching zero gives a delay approaching
innity, and the loop would wait essentially forever between steps with no way to interrupt it.
Treating a very small speed as stopped is both what the operator means and the only behaviour
that remains controllable.
8.54 TriggeredEndstop
def T r i g g e r e d E n d s t o p ( ignore = f ro z en se t () )
Inputs
ignore
switch names to disregard
Output.the name of a switch conrmed to be pressed, or nothing.
What it is forThe safety check, called before essentially every step of every motion. It reads
each switch and returns the name of the rst conrmed to be pressed.
How the debounce is arrangedA switch reading open is dismissed immediately. One reading
closed is then sampled twice more, spaced by the debounce delay, and is believed only if all three
agree. The reasoning behind the spacing and the count is given under ENDSTOP_DEBOUNCE_S
and ENDSTOP_CONFIRM_SAMPLES.
Why the asymmetry is what makes this aordableThis function runs before every step
pulse, so its cost is multiplied by the tens of thousands of steps in a drawing. The early dismissal is
what keeps that cost down: in the normal case every switch reads open and the function performs
four pin reads and returns, with no delays at all. The twelve milliseconds of conrmation are
spent only when a switch actually reads closed, which happens once per job at most.
Why some switches can be ignoredThe ignore set exists because there are legitimate
situations in which a switch is pressed and should not stop the machine. Retracting o a switch
necessarily begins with that switch pressed, and homing one axis may hold a switch while the
other axis is moved. Without the ability to disregard a named switch, the only remedy would be
to omit the safety check entirely during those operations, which would remove it exactly where
the machine is deliberately being driven against its limits.
The check returns at the rst switch it conrms rather than examining all four. For stopping
that is sucient, since one tripped switch is reason enough. The separate _AllTriggeredEnd-
stops exists for the emergency stop, which does need the full list so that it can retract o every
one of them.
245

=== PDF PAGE 247 ===
8.55 _AllTriggeredEndstops
def _ A l l T r i g g e r e d E n d s t o p s ()
Output.a list of every switch currently conrmed as pressed.
Why a complete list is neededAn emergency stop must retract o every pressed switch, not
just the one that was noticed rst. Two can be pressed at once: a carriage driven into a corner
reaches an X and a Y limit together, and retracting o only one leaves the machine still held
against the other, so the next motion check still nds a tripped switch and nothing can move.
How it is built from the single checkEach switch is tested by calling the single check with
everyotherswitch in the ignore set, so the answer concerns that switch alone. It is a roundabout
construction, but it reuses the debounce logic rather than duplicating it, which matters for a safety
check: two implementations of a debounce could diverge, and the one in the rarely exercised path
is the one that would not be noticed.
8.56 AxisDirValue
def A x i s D i r V a l u e ( invert , positive )
Inputs
invert
whether this axis's direction signal is reversed
positive
whether the wanted motion is in the positive direction
Output.the level to set on the direction pin.
What it resolvesTurns a wanted direction into a pin level, accounting for the axis's wiring
inversion. It is the single place the inversion is applied, so every motion routine inherits it by
asking for a direction by meaning rather than by level.
forward = positive⊕invert(8.9)
where
forward
whether the direction pin should be driven active
positive
whether the wanted motion is toward increasing coordinates
invert
this axis's wiring inversion ag
⊕exclusive or: true when exactly one of the two is true
246

=== PDF PAGE 248 ===
Exclusive or is exactly the right operation, and it is worth checking the four cases. Wanting
positive motion on an uninverted axis drives the pin active; wanting negative on the same axis
drives it inactive. On an inverted axis both answers swap. So the output is active when the two
inputs disagree, which is what exclusive or computes. Written as nested conditions it would be
four branches that must each be right; as an exclusive or it is one expression that cannot be half
correct.
8.57 _RetractOSwitch
def _ R e t r a c t O f f S w i t c h ( hit , ignore = f ro z en se t () )
Inputs
hitthe name of the pressed switch
ignore
other switches to disregard while retracting
Output.nothing; it steps the axis clear and updates the position count.
What it doesSteps the aected axis away from the switch by the retract distance, at homing
speed, updating the step count as it goes. The direction comes from the retreat table and the axis
and pins from the switch's name.
Why it watches the other switchesEach step checks for any switch other than the one being
escaped and the ones explicitly ignored, and stops if one trips. The code comment calls that a
real fault, and it is: moving away from one limit should not approach another, so if it does, the
machine's geometry is not what is believed, perhaps because a switch is stuck closed or an axis is
shorter than its recorded travel. Continuing to drive would push against a second limit.
Why the position count is updatedThe retract moves the carriage, so the recorded position
must follow, or every subsequent absolute move is out by the retract distance. The count is
accumulated during the loop and applied to the right axis afterwards, which also means a retract
cut short by another switch updates by the distance actually travelled rather than the distance
intended.
Retracting at homing speed rather than drawing speed is deliberate. The machine is at a limit,
in a state it was not supposed to reach, and the geometry is not trusted. Moving slowly means
the other-switch check has time to act before much further travel occurs.
8.58 _TriggerEmergencyStop
def _ T r i g g e r E m e r g e n c y S t o p ( hit )
Inputs
hitthe switch that caused the stop
Output.nothing; it stops the drive, retracts and latches the fault.
247

=== PDF PAGE 249 ===
The sequence, and why it is in this order
Latch the fault rst,before anything else. Setting the ag and recording the switch
immediately means that any other code checking it from this point on sees the stop, including
the retract that follows.
Set the step pins inactive.This ends any pulse in progress so no coil is left energised
mid-step.
Find every pressed switch,since more than one may be.
Retract o each of them,so the machine is clear of its limits and able to move again
once the fault is cleared.
Report,naming the switches and the retract distance.
Why re-entry is refusedThe function returns immediately if the fault is already latched.
Without that, a second switch tripping during the retract would trigger another stop, which
would retract again, which could trip again, and the recursion would continue until the program
ran out of stack. The guard makes the stop happen once and the retract complete, which is the
behaviour wanted: the rst stop has already established that the machine is to halt, and a second
detection adds nothing.
Why the pen is raisedRaising the pen is part of stopping, not a courtesy. A pen left down
during an abort rests on the paper at the point of failure, where the ink bleeds into a visible blot,
and if the machine is subsequently moved by hand it drags a line across the drawing. The drawing
is already spoiled by the abort; the object is to avoid spoiling it further.
8.59 BresenhamMove
def B r e s e n h a m M o v e ( dx , dy , s t e p D e l a y S = CIRCLE _ STEP _ DELAY _ S )
Inputs
dxthe X displacement in steps
dythe Y displacement in steps
stepDelayS
the pause between step edges
Output.nothing; it steps both motors so the carriage travels in a straight line.
The problemTwo motors must move together so that the carriage travels along a straight line
from one point to another. Each can only take whole steps, so the path is necessarily a staircase;
the task is to arrange the staircase so that it stays as close to the true line as whole steps permit.
A move of 300 steps in X and 100 in Y must take one Y step for every three X steps, spread
evenly, not all 100 at the start.
248

=== PDF PAGE 250 ===
The naive approach, and why it is avoidedThe obvious method computes, for each X step,
the exact Y position on the line and rounds it. That requires a oating-point multiplication and
a rounding per step, and this loop runs for every one of tens of thousands of steps in a drawing.
It also accumulates rounding error, since the exact position is recomputed from a division that is
not exact.
Bresenham's methodThe algorithm [23] keeps a running error term instead, representing how
far the minor axis has fallen behind the ideal line, and uses only integer addition and comparison.
e← ⌊a x/2⌋
for each major step:e←e−a y
ife <0 :step the minor axis,e←e+a x
(8.10)
Source:[23]
where
ethe error term: how far the minor axis is behind
ax the major axis displacement in steps, taken as positive
ay the minor axis displacement, positive
⌊ax/2⌋
the starting value, which centres the error so the rst minor step falls half-way through the
rst run rather than at its start
The reasoning is a scaled comparison. The true slope is the minor displacement over the major, and
testing whether the accumulated fraction has passed a half-step would need division. Multiplying
the whole test through by the major displacement removes the division: subtracting the minor
displacement each major step accumulates the same quantity scaled up, and comparing against
zero is the same test as comparing the unscaled fraction against its threshold. Adding the major
displacement back when the minor axis steps is the scaled equivalent of subtracting one whole
step.
So the loop contains one subtraction, one comparison and occasionally one addition, all on integers.
There is no division, no oating point and no accumulated rounding error: the error term is exact
at every step, so the path is the closest staircase to the true line by construction rather than by
approximation.
Why the major axis is chosen rstThe loop runs over whichever axis has the greater
displacement, with the other as the minor. That is necessary rather than cosmetic: the minor
axis steps at most once per major step, so if the shorter axis were driven as the major the longer
one could not keep up and the line would come out at the wrong angle. The two branches of the
code are mirror images, diering only in which pin is pulsed and which count is updated.
The safety check inside the loopEvery iteration checks the endstops and returns imme-
diately if one has tripped. Returning part way leaves the move incomplete and the carriage
somewhere along the line, which is why the step counts are updated as the loop proceeds rather
than at the end: the recorded position is correct for where the carriage actually stopped, not for
where it was told to go.
249

=== PDF PAGE 251 ===
Bresenham's algorithm was published for drawing lines on raster displays, where the problem
is identical: choose which pixels best approximate a straight line between two points. A stepper
gantry is the same problem with the pixels replaced by step positions, which is why an algorithm
from computer graphics is the standard solution for motion control.
8.60 PenIsUp
def PenIsUp ()
Output.whether the pen switch currently reads as up.
What it is forReads the pen position switch. This is the capability the written G-code le
lacks: the machine can ask where the pen is, so it never has to assume.
The read is not debounced, unlike the endstop check. That is acceptable because of how it is
used: SetPen polls it repeatedly in a loop while the motor runs, so a single spurious reading
advances the loop by one iteration and the next reading corrects it. A spurious endstop reading,
by contrast, aborts the whole job, which is why that one is conrmed and this one is not.
8.61 SetPen
def SetPen ( targetUp , timeoutS =3.0)
Inputs
targetUp
the pen position wanted
timeoutS
how long to wait for conrmation, default 3 seconds
Output.true if the pen reached the wanted position, false otherwise.
The closed loopThe motor is started, the switch is polled until it reports the wanted position or
the timeout expires, and the motor is stopped. This isclosed-loopcontrol: the action continues
until the measurement conrms the result, in contrast with the written le's timed dwell, which
commands a pulse and assumes it worked.
Why it checks the position rstIf the pen already reads as being where it is wanted, the
function returns immediately without running the motor. The code comment is emphatic, and the
reason is the one-way actuator: running it when the pen is already correct turns the cam a further
quarter turn and moves the pen to the wrong position. For a toggle, a redundant command is not
merely wasteful but actively wrong.
Why it refuses while an endstop is trippedA pen move is declined outright if any limit
switch is pressed. The machine is in a fault state, the pen motor draws current from the same
supply as the steppers, and moving the pen does nothing to resolve the fault. Refusing keeps the
machine still until the fault has been dealt with, which is what a latched stop is for.
250

=== PDF PAGE 252 ===
Why a timeout, and what its expiry meansIf the switch never reports the wanted position
within three seconds, the motor is stopped and the function reports failure. A toggle takes about a
third of a second, so three seconds is ample and its expiry means something is mechanically wrong:
the motor is not turning, the switch is disconnected, or the linkage has broken. Without a timeout
the motor would run indenitely, turning the cam round and round and overheating. Reporting
the failure lets the caller abort rather than continuing to draw with a pen in an unknown position.
The overrunOn a successful raise the motor runs a further 40 milliseconds past the switch
trip, for the reason given under PEN_UP_OVERRUN_S: stopping exactly on the trip point
leaves the cam where backlash or vibration can drop the pen onto the page during a travel move.
8.62 _StepAxis
def _ StepAxis ( stepPin , dirPin , dirValue , stopWhen , ignore = fr oz en se t () )
Inputs
stepPin
the step pin for this axis
dirPin
its direction pin
dirValue
the level to drive the direction pin at
stopWhen
a test saying which switch is the expected one
ignore
switches to disregard
Output.the switch that stopped the motion and how many steps were taken.
What it is forDrives one axis in one direction until a switch stops it, counting the steps. It
is the primitive the homing and calibration are built from, and the step count it returns is the
measurement that calibration converts into steps per millimetre.
Why the stopping condition is a test rather than a nameThe caller supplies a function
saying which switch is the expected one, rather than naming it. That is necessary because homing
begins without knowing which end of the axis it will reach: the carriage may be anywhere, so
driving in one direction may bring it to either switch. The rst call accepts either, and the second
accepts only the opposite one.
Why an unexpected switch is an error rather than a stopA switch that trips which is
not the expected one raises an error naming the wiring, rather than being treated as a normal
stop. The distinction matters because the step count is about to be used as a measurement:
stopping on the wrong switch means the count does not measure what it is believed to, and a
calibration built on it would scale every subsequent move wrongly. A measurement known to be
invalid should fail rather than be used.
251

=== PDF PAGE 253 ===
Why the step limit is also an errorExceeding the step bound raises an error too. A correctly
functioning machine reaches a switch in a fraction of that distance, so the bound being reached
means no switch is going to trip, which is the disconnected-wiring case. Again the appropriate
response is to stop and say so, since there is no sensible way to proceed without a reference
position.
8.63 _CalibrateAxis
def _ C a l i b r a t e A x i s ( stepPin , dirPin , minName , maxName , O t h e r A x i s N a m e s =
f ro ze ns et () )
Inputs
stepPin
the step pin
dirPin
the direction pin
minName
the minimum switch's name
maxName
the maximum switch's name
OtherAxisNames
the other axis's switches, to disregard
Output.the rst switch reached, the second, and the steps measured between them.
The measurementThe axis is driven in one direction until either switch trips, then reversed
until the opposite one does, counting the steps of the second move. That count is the full travel of
the axis in steps, which divided by the known travel in millimetres gives the steps per millimetre.
Why the count comes from the second moveThe rst move starts from wherever the
carriage happened to be and ends at a switch, so its length is arbitrary and carries no information.
The second starts at a known position, one switch, and ends at another known position, the other
switch, so its length is the distance between two physical references. Only a move between two
known points measures anything.
Why the rst switch is ignored during the reverseThe reverse move begins with the
rst switch still pressed, since the carriage is resting against it, and it stays pressed for the rst
few steps until the lever releases. The code comment calls this release lag and notes it is not a
fault. Without adding that switch to the ignore set, the reverse move would immediately detect
it, decide it was unexpected, and raise an error on every calibration.
Why the other axis is ignored throughoutThe other axis may be resting against one of
its own limits while this one is being calibrated, which is a perfectly normal state and nothing to
do with this axis's motion. Ignoring its switches lets each axis be calibrated regardless of where
the other happens to be parked.
252

=== PDF PAGE 254 ===
8.64 Calibrate
def Ca li br at e ()
Output.a copy of the calibration record: the measured scaling for both axes and the usable
area.
What it establishesTwo things the machine cannot otherwise know: where it is, and how far
a step moves it. Both come from driving to physical references, since the motors report neither.
The sequence
Refuse if a switch is already pressed.Calibration drives until a switch trips, so starting
against one makes the rst move meaningless.
Measure the X axisbetween its two switches, and set its steps per millimetre from the
count.
Retract o the switchit nished against, so it is clear of its limit.
Measure the Y axisthe same way, ignoring the X switches.
Retractagain, record the usable area, and park at the safe corner.
Where the position count comes fromAfter each axis, the step count is set to the full
travel if the axis nished at its maximum switch, and to zero if at its minimum. That is the whole
point of homing: the carriage is at a switch, the switch is at a known place, so the count can be
set rather than guessed. Every subsequent absolute move is measured from it.
The usable area and the switch-travel allowance
usable = switchTravel−2·edgeTolerance(8.11)
where
usable
the area the machine will address, per axis
switchTravel
the hand-measured distance between the two switches
edgeTolerance
the margin kept clear at each end
The usable area is deliberately smaller than the measured travel, for the reasons given under
EDGE_TOLERANCE_MM. The coordinate origin is placed at the margin's distance in from
the minimum switches, so a commanded zero is a position the machine is allowed to occupy
rather than the switch itself.
Why it parks at the endCalibration nishes with a move to the origin of the usable area.
That leaves the machine at a known, legal position, clear of every switch, which is what a drawing
job expects to start from. Finishing while resting against a limit would mean the rst motion
check of the next job found a tripped switch.
253

=== PDF PAGE 255 ===
8.65 ParseGcodeLine
def P a r s e G c o d e L i n e ( raw )
Inputs
rawone line of a G-code le
Output.the command and its parameters, or nothing for a line with no command.
What it doesStrips comments, splits the line into words, takes the rst G or M word as the
command, and collects the rest as parameters keyed by their letters.
The two comment conventionsG-code has two: a semicolon makes the rest of the line a
comment, and text in parentheses is a comment wherever it appears, including in the middle of a
line. Both are handled, the rst by truncating at the semicolon and the second by removing every
parenthesised span. Handling only the rst would leave comment text to be parsed as words, and
a comment containing a letter followed by a digit would be read as a parameter.
Why the rst G or M word becomes the commandA line has one command and any
number of parameters, and the command is identied by being a G or M word. Taking the rst
such word rather than the rst word generally is what lets a line with a leading line number be
parsed, and the test that the command slot is still empty is what stops a later G word being
mistaken for a second command.
The command's number is converted to an integer while parameters stay as oating point.
That is correct for each: G1 and G01 denote the same command, so an integer normalises
them, while a coordinate of 12.5 must keep its fraction.
8.66 _ClampToUsableArea
def _ C l a m p T o U s a b l e A r e a ( xMm , yMm )
Inputs
xMmthe target X coordinate
yMmthe target Y coordinate
Output.the coordinates forced inside the homed usable area.
Why the machine clamps as well as the writerThe G-code writer already clamps to the
congured bounds. This clamps again, against themeasuredusable area, and the two are not the
same check. The writer's bounds are conguration, stated in advance; this one is what calibration
actually found, which may be smaller, and it applies to any le whatever its origin, including a
hand-written one or one from another program.
The principle is that the machine must protect itself rather than trusting its input. A clamp at
the point of motion is the last line of defence and the only one that cannot be bypassed by using
a dierent generator.
254

=== PDF PAGE 256 ===
Why it prints when it actsA clamp that res means the le asked for something outside the
machine, so the drawing will be wrong: strokes are attened against the boundary. Printing the
original and clamped coordinates makes that visible while the job runs, so the operator can stop
it rather than discovering afterwards that part of the drawing is a straight line along one edge.

8.68 _GcodeArcMove
def _ G c o d e A r c M o v e ( x0Mm , y0Mm , x1Mm , y1Mm , iMm , jMm , clockwise , f ee dM mM i n )
Inputs
x0Mm
the arc's start X
y0Mm
its start Y
x1Mm

y1Mm
its end Y
iMmthe centre's X oset from the start
jMmthe centre's Y oset from the start
clockwise

feedMmMin
the feed rate
Output.nothing; it draws the arc as a sequence of short straight moves.
256

=== PDF PAGE 258 ===
Why arcs must be subdividedThe machine can only move in straight lines, because that
is what the Bresenham stepper does. An arc is therefore approximated by a sequence of short
chords. This is what every machine controller does; the only question is how short.
How G-code species an arcThe command gives the end point and thecentre oset, the
vector from the start point to the centre, conventionally written as I and J. The radius is then the
length of that oset, and the start and end angles follow from the two endpoints relative to the
centre. Specifying the centre by oset rather than absolutely is the convention because it makes
the command independent of where the arc sits.
F orcing the sweep to go the right wayThe two angles alone do not determine the arc:
the same pair of endpoints can be joined by a short arc one way or a long one the other. The
direction ag settles it, and the code enforces it by adjusting the end angle by whole turns until
the sweep runs strictly the right way. For a clockwise arc the end angle is decreased until it is
below the start; for anticlockwise, increased until above. Without that, an arc crossing the point
where the angle measurement wraps would be traced the long way round, sweeping nearly a full
circle instead of a small arc.
How many chords
n= max

4,
 |∆θ| ·r
0.4

(8.14)
where
nthe number of straight segments the arc becomes
|∆θ|the angle swept, in radians
rthe arc's radius in millimetres
0.4the target chord length in millimetres
max(4,·)
a oor, so even a tiny arc is drawn as a curve rather than a straight line
The numerator is the arc's length, since arc length is radius times angle in radians, so the expression
is simply the arc length divided by the wanted chord length. Fixing the chord length rather
than the number of segments is what makes the approximation uniformly good: a large arc gets
proportionally more segments, so the deviation from the true curve is the same whatever the size.
Why 0.4 millimetresThe relevant quantity is thesagitta, how far the chord departs from
the arc at its midpoint, which for a short chord is approximately the chord length squared divided
by eight times the radius. For a 0.4 mm chord on a 5 mm radius that is about 4 micrometres, far
below the pen's line width and well below what the machine can position to. So the approximation
is invisible, and making the chords shorter would add moves without changing the drawing.
Arcs are included for completeness of the G-code reader rather than because this project emits
them: handwriting is written out as straight segments throughout. They matter because the
reader is meant to run any ordinary G-code le, and arcs are among the commonest commands
in les from other sources.
257

=== PDF PAGE 259 ===
8.69 RunGcodeFile
def R u n G c o d e F i l e ( path )
Inputs
path
the G-code le to execute
Output.nothing; it draws the le or aborts.
What it doesReads the le and executes it line by line, maintaining the current position, the
feed rate and whether coordinates are absolute or relative, and carrying out each move.
Why calibration is required rstThe function refuses with a dedicated error if homing
has not run. A le's coordinates are absolute positions on the bed, and an absolute position is
meaningless until the machine knows where it is. Executing one anyway would drive from an
unknown origin toward a position computed as though it were known.
The safety check before every lineEach line begins by checking the endstops and the latched
fault ag, and aborts on either. Checking per line as well as per step gives two dierent guarantees:
the per-step check inside the stepper stops a move in progress, and this one stops the job rather
than merely the move. Without it, a move cut short by a switch would be followed by the next
line of the le being executed from the wrong position.
The abort reports the line number. That is what makes a failure diagnosable: it says how far the
drawing got and which command was being executed, so the le can be examined at that point
rather than searched.
Modal stateG-code ismodal, meaning several settings persist until changed: the feed rate,
absolute or relative coordinates, and the units. The interpreter must therefore carry that state
across lines, which is why the feed and the absolute-mode ag are variables of the loop rather
than parameters of each move. A line consisting only of coordinates inherits the feed set many
lines earlier, which is how G-code les are normally written.
The starting position is read using the uncalibrated steps-per-millimetre constant rather than
the measured value, so the position the job believes it starts from is slightly wrong even after
calibration. Subsequent moves use the calibrated gure, so the error does not accumulate,
but the rst move of a job is displaced by the dierence between the calculated and measured
scaling.
8.70 WriteGcodeFile
def W r i t e G c o d e F i l e ( path )
Inputs
path
where to write

=== PDF PAGE 260 ===
Output.nothing; it writes a small test le.
What it is forEmits a short G-code le for exercising the machine during bring-up, so the exe-

not part of drawing handwriting: a real drawing is written by WriteGcode from a trajectory.
8.71 InputListener
def I n p u t L i s t e n e r ()
Output.nothing; it runs the keyboard control loop until told to stop.
What it is forA console for operating the machine by hand: home it, run a le, set a speed,
draw a test circle, clear a latched fault, quit. It is what the machine is brought up and diagnosed
with.
Why it runs in its own threadReading a line from the keyboard blocks until something is
typed. If that happened in the main loop, the machine would stop stepping while waiting for
input, so motion would proceed only while the operator was typing. Running the console in a
separate thread lets it wait on the keyboard while the main loop continues to step the motors.
How the two threads communicateEntirely through the module-level state: the console
sets the mode, the speed, the fault ag, and the loop reads them. There are no locks. That is
sound here for a specic reason rather than by luck: every shared value is a single machine word,
whose assignment cannot be observed half-complete, and each is written by only one of the two
threads. Nothing requires two values to change together, which is the situation locks exist to
protect.
Why the thread is a daemonAdaemonthread does not keep the program alive: when
the main thread nishes, the process exits whether or not the daemon is still running. That is
wanted here because the console is blocked reading the keyboard most of the time, so if it had to
nish before the program could exit, a quit command issued from anywhere else would hang until
a further key was pressed.
The clear command for a latched fault is deliberately a manual action. It is the acknowledge-
ment described under the latching ag: the machine stopped because its idea of the world was
wrong, and only a person standing at it can establish that it is right again.
259

=== PDF PAGE 261 ===
9 train.py
This is where the networks are trained. recognise.py runs them forward; everything that works
out how the weights should change is here, derived by hand and written in numpy. No automatic
dierentiation is used, which is the main reason this le is the largest in the project.
Background: what training actually isA network starts with random weights and gives
nonsense answers. Training is a loop: run some examples through, measure how wrong the output
is with aloss function, work out how each weight would have to change to make the loss smaller,
and change it a little in that direction. Repeat for days.
The quantity that says how a weight should change is thegradient: the rate at which the loss
changes as that weight changes. If the gradient with respect to some weight is positive, increasing
that weight increases the loss, so the weight should decrease. Moving every weight a small step
against its gradient isgradient descent, and the size of that step is thelearning rate.
w←w−η ∂L
∂w (9.1)
where
wone weight of the network
ηthe learning rate: how big a step to take
Lthe loss, a single number saying how wrong the output was
∂L
∂w thepartial derivativeof the loss with respect to that weight: how much the loss changes
per unit change in the weight, holding every other weight xed
←assignment: the weight is replaced by the value on the right
Background: the chain rule and backpropagationA network has millions of weights and
the loss depends on all of them through many layers, so computing each gradient separately is
out of the question. What makes it feasible is thechain ruleof calculus: if a quantity depends
on another through an intermediate step, the derivatives multiply.
∂L
∂x = ∂L
∂y · ∂y
∂x (9.2)
where
Lthe loss

∂L
∂y the gradient arriving from the layer above: how the loss changes with this layer's output
∂y
∂x the layer's own derivative: how its output changes with its input
∂L

260

=== PDF PAGE 262 ===
So each layer needs to know only two things: the gradient of the loss with respect to its own
output, which the layer above hands it, and how its own output depends on its input and weights,
which is local knowledge. It multiplies them, keeps the weight gradients, and passes the input
gradient down. Starting at the loss and working backwards layer by layer computes every gradient
in the network in one sweep, at about the cost of one forward pass. That isbackpropagation.
Why every function here comes in pairsA layer's backward pass needs values from its
forward pass. The gradient through a ReLU depends on which inputs were positive; the gradient
through a convolution depends on what the input patches were. So each forward function returns
its output together with acacheof exactly what its backward partner will need, and the backward
function takes that cache. The pairing is the whole structure of the rst part of this le.
The naming convention follows from it: a function named Forward here diers from the same-
named function in recognise.py only in returning that cache, and sometimes in behaving dierently
during training, as batch normalisation and dropout do.
A note on notationThroughout, a quantity written with a leading d, such asdoutordx,
means the gradient of the loss with respect to that quantity, not a small change in it. Sodx
has exactly the same shape as x, because there is one gradient per element. This is the usual
convention in hand-written backpropagation code and it is worth xing in mind before reading
any of what follows.
9.1 Conv2dForward
def C o n v 2 d F o r w a r d (x , weight , bias , padding =1)
Inputs
xthe input, shaped (batch, channels, height, width)
weight
the lters
bias
one number per output channel, or nothing
padding
zeros added round the input, default 1
Output.a pair: the ltered result, and the cache the backward pass needs.
What it adds to the inference versionThe arithmetic is identical to Conv2d in recognise.py,
including the im2col rearrangement and the strided view, both explained in that entry. The only
dierence is that this one keeps a cache.
What is cached, and why each item is needed
The input's shape,not the input itself. The backward pass builds a gradient array of
that shape and never needs the values.
261

=== PDF PAGE 263 ===
The weights,because the input gradient is formed by multiplying the output gradient by
them.
The attened columns,which is the signicant one. The weight gradient is the output
gradient multiplied by exactly these patches, so keeping them avoids rebuilding them, which
would mean redoing the strided view and the reshape.
The padding and the lter and output sizes,so the backward pass can fold the column
gradient back into image shape without recomputing the geometry.
The cost of caching, and why it is acceptedThe columns are the memory cost of training.
A 3 by 3 lter over three channels makes each patch 27 numbers, and there is one patch per output
position, so the cached columns are roughly nine times the size of the input feature map. Every
convolution in the network holds its own for the whole forward pass, since none can be released
until the backward pass has used it. That, rather than the weights, is what sets the largest batch
size that ts in memory, and it is why training needs far more memory than inference.
No bias-related value is cached. The bias gradient is just the output gradient summed over
every position, which needs nothing from the forward pass, because adding a constant has a
derivative of one.

gradient. Both the shapes and the transposes follow from requiring the result to have the same
shape as the thing dierentiated.

The sum over the batch is not an approximation or an average applied afterwards; it is what the
derivative is. The same lter was applied to every item in the batch and at every position, so
the loss depends on each weight through all of them, and the chain rule adds the contributions.
This is the general rule for a shared parameter: wherever one value is used in several places, its
gradient is the sum over those places.

That gives a gradient with respect to thecolumns, which is not what is wanted: the layer below
needs a gradient with respect to the image. Converting one to the other is the only part of this
function that is not a matrix product, and it is the subtle part.
The reason it is subtle is that the forward view was overlapping. One input pixel appears in up
to nine patches, so it inuenced up to nine output positions, and by the chain rule its gradient is
the sum of the gradients from all of them. The loop over the nine lter osets does exactly that:
for each oset, the corresponding slice of the column gradient isaddedinto the padded image
gradient at the shifted position. Nine additions over whole arrays accumulate every contribution.
The accumulation is the whole point, and it is why the forward trick cannot be run in reverse.
The forward pass could use a strided view because reading the same memory from several places
is harmless; writing to it from several places is not, since the writes would overwrite rather than
accumulate. So the backward direction must be an explicit addition, and the operation is known
ascol2im, the counterpart of im2col.
Finally , removing the paddingThe gradient was accumulated into an array the size of the
padded input, and the layer below knows nothing of the padding, so the border is discarded.
That is correct rather than merely convenient: the padding was constant zeros, not data, so the
gradient with respect to it has nowhere to go.

One bias is added to every position of its channel, so by the shared-parameter rule its gradient is

of adding a constant is one.

264

=== PDF PAGE 266 ===
Why using the batch is what makes it workStandardising by the batch is not a conve-
nience; it is the mechanism. The purpose of the layer is to x the scale a layer presents to the
next one, and during training that scale is changing constantly because the layers below are being
updated. Measuring it fresh from the current batch tracks those changes immediately, where
stored statistics would always describe an earlier state of the network.
The running statistics, and why they are an exponential average
ˆµ←(1−m) ˆµ+m µ c (9.7)
where
ˆµthe stored mean, kept for inference
mthe momentum, 0.1
µc this batch's mean
This is anexponential moving average: each update keeps 90 percent of the old value and
takes 10 percent from the new observation, so the stored gure is a weighted average over recent
batches with older ones fading away. It is used rather than a plain average over all batches for
two reasons. It needs no count and no accumulator, only the current value. And the fading is
correct here: early batches were produced by a network whose weights were nearly random, so
their statistics do not describe the trained network and should be forgotten.
Why the stored variance is the unbiased estimate
σ2
unbiased =σ 2
biased · N
N−1 (9.8)
where
σ2
biased
the variance computed with the mean measured from the same data
σ2
unbiased
the corrected estimate of the population variance
Nthe number of values
Measuring the variance about a mean estimated from the same sample systematically underesti-
mates it, because the sample mean is by construction the point about which the squared deviations
are smallest.Bessel's correction, dividing by N−1 rather than N, compensates. The biased
form is used to normalise this batch, since that is the quantity that actually standardises these val-
ues, while the corrected form is stored, since that is an estimate of the variance of all data. The
code comment notes this matches the reference framework's behaviour, which matters because
checkpoints must be interchangeable.
What is cachedThe standardised values, the standard deviation, the scale and the element
count. Notably the input itself is not cached: the backward derivation below is written entirely in
terms of the standardised values, which is both less memory and fewer operations than recovering
the input.

9.4 BatchNorm2dBackward
def B a t c h N o r m 2 d B a c k w a r d ( dout , cache )
Inputs

the gradient with respect to this layer's output
cache

Why this one is genuinely hardEvery other layer in this le has a local derivative: the
output at one position depends on the input at one position, or a few. Batch normalisation does
not. The mean and variance are computed fromeveryvalue in the channel, so changing one input
changes the mean, which changes the normalisation of every other value in the channel as well.
Each input therefore aects its own output directly and every other output indirectly, and the
gradient must account for all three routes.
The three paths
Directly ,through its own standardised value.
Through the mean,since the input contributed to the mean, and the mean is subtracted
from every value in the channel.
Through the variance,since the input contributed to the variance, and the variance
divides every value in the channel.
Dierentiating each path and collecting the terms gives an expression that simplies considerably,
which is the form the code uses.

266

=== PDF PAGE 268 ===
The three terms correspond to the three paths, and each has a readable meaning. The rst is the
direct eect. The second subtracts the average gradient over the channel, which is the correction
for the mean: it ensures the gradients sum to zero, so the layer below cannot be pushed in a
direction that would merely shift the whole channel, since such a shift would be normalised away
anyway. The third subtracts the component aligned with the standardised values themselves,
which is the correction for the variance: it removes any push that would merely scale the channel,
for the same reason.
So the layer passes down only the part of the gradient that is not a pure shift or a pure rescaling
of the channel. That is the deeper statement of what batch normalisation does: it makes the
network's behaviour independent of the scale and oset of each channel, and the backward pass
correspondingly refuses to pass down gradients that would only change those.
The scale and shift gradients

These two are straightforward by comparison. The output is the scale times the standardised
value plus the shift, so the derivative with respect to the scale is the standardised value and with
respect to the shift is one, each summed over every position the parameter was used at, by the
shared-parameter rule.
The code comment attributes the simplied form to the standard derivation taught in the
Stanford CS231n course notes, which is where this arrangement of the terms is usually given.
The project's numerical gradient check exists largely to conrm that this function in particular
is right, since it is the one most easily got wrong and an error in it produces slow or unstable
training rather than an obvious failure.
9.5 ReluForward
def R e l u F o r w a r d ( x )
Inputs
xany array
Output.a pair: the rectied array, and a mask of which entries were positive.
267

=== PDF PAGE 269 ===
What is cached and why it is cheapThe forward arithmetic is the same clamping as in
recognise.py. What it adds is a boolean mask recording which inputs were positive, which is all the
backward pass needs. A boolean array is one byte per element against eight for a double, so this
is the cheapest cache of any layer, which matters because ReLU appears after every convolution.
The mask could be derived from the output instead, since the output is positive exactly where the
input was. Caching it explicitly costs a little memory and keeps the backward pass independent
of what the caller did with the output, which would otherwise be a hidden requirement that the
output not be modied in place.
9.6 ReluBackward
def R e l u B a c k w a r d ( dout , cache )

∂L

∂y ·

∂L
∂x the gradient to pass down
∂L
∂y the gradient arriving
xthe original input, consulted only through the cached mask
ReLU is the identity where the input was positive and constant zero where it was not, so its
derivative is one or zero accordingly. The backward pass is therefore the incoming gradient with
the inactive positions zeroed: the gradient passes unchanged through the units that red and
stops dead at the ones that did not.
Why this is the property that makes deep networks trainableA derivative of exactly
1 on the active path is what distinguishes ReLU from the smooth activations it replaced. The
sigmoid's derivative is at most 0.25 and much smaller when saturated, so a gradient crossing twelve
sigmoid layers is multiplied by twelve such factors and arrives at the rst layer eectively zero.
That is the vanishing gradient problem, and it is why networks of this depth were impractical
before ReLU. With a factor of exactly 1 the gradient reaches the rst layer at full strength.
The derivative at exactly zero does not exist, since the function has a corner there. Taking it
as zero is conventional and the choice has no practical consequence: an input of exactly zero
is a measure-zero event in oating point, and either choice gives a valid subgradient.
268

=== PDF PAGE 270 ===
9.7 MaxPool2dForward
def M a x P o o l 2 d F o r w a r d (x , kernel =2 , stride =2)
Inputs
xthe input
kernel
the window size, default 2
stride
the step between windows, default 2
Output.a pair: the pooled output, and the cache recording which element won each window.
What must be cached, and whyThe forward arithmetic is as in recognise.py. What training
requires in addition is thepositionof the winner in each window, not merely its value. The
backward pass must send the gradient to the element that was passed through, and only the
position identies it.
The position is stored as an index into the attened window, a number from 0 to 3 for a 2 by 2
window, rather than as a pair of coordinates. A single index is one array instead of two and can
be used directly by the indexed-write operation the backward pass uses to place the gradient.
This is why the forward pass attens the window axes and takes the maximum by nding the
winning index and then reading it, instead of simply taking a maximum over two axes. The
inference version does the latter, which is simpler; here the index is needed anyway, so the
value is obtained by reading it.
9.8 MaxPool2dBackward

The routing ruleMax pooling passes one element of each window through unchanged, so its
derivative with respect to that element is one and with respect to every other element of the
window is zero. The gradient is therefore routed entirely to the winner and the rest of the window
receives nothing.
∂L
∂x[b, c, i, j] =



=== PDF PAGE 271 ===
where

(p, q)
which window
An input that lost its window has no inuence on the output at all, so changing it slightly changes
nothing and its gradient is genuinely zero. That is a real consequence of pooling rather than an
approximation: only a quarter of the inputs to a 2 by 2 pooling receive any gradient on a given
example, which is part of why the layers below a pooling train more slowly.
Why no accumulation is needed hereThe code comment notes that the stride equals
the kernel, so the windows do not overlap and each input belongs to exactly one window. The
gradient blocks can therefore be written straight into position rather than added. With overlapping
windows an input could win several, and the contributions would have to be accumulated as they
are in the convolution's backward pass.
The transpose before the nal reshape interleaves the window axes back with the output axes, so
that the blocks land in the right places. Getting this wrong produces a gradient that is correct in
magnitude and scrambled in position, which trains to something, slowly and wrongly, without
any error being raised. It is exactly the kind of fault the numerical gradient check exists to
catch.
9.9 AdaptiveMaxPoolHeightForward
def A d a p t i v e M a x P o o l H e i g h t F o r w a r d ( x )

xthe input
