What is cachedWhich row won in each column of each channel. The reasoning is the same
as for ordinary max pooling: the gradient goes entirely to the element that was passed through,
so its position must be known.
This is the layer that converts the image into a sequence, as described in the inference entry,
so it is also the point where the gradient converts back: the sequence gradient coming from the
recurrent layers becomes an image gradient here, placed into the rows that won.
270

=== PDF PAGE 272 ===
9.10 AdaptiveMaxPoolHeightBackward
def A d a p t i v e M a x P o o l H e i g h t B a c k w a r d ( dout , cache )
Inputs
dout
the gradient with respect to the collapsed output
cache
the input shape and the winning rows
Output.the gradient with respect to the full-height input.
What it doesCreates a zero array of the original shape and writes the incoming gradient into
the winning row of each column. Every other row gets nothing.
What that means for trainingOf the sixteen rows the feature map has at this point, exactly
one per column receives any gradient on a given example. So each column of each channel trains
from one row at a time, though which row varies between examples and over the course of training
as the weights change. The eect is that the layer below learns to make its strongest response
correct, rather than learning anything about the rows that lose, which is the behaviour a maximum
should produce: the network is being asked whether a feature appears anywhere in the column,
so only the best evidence matters.
9.11 AdaptiveA vgPoolWidthForward
def A d a p t i v e A v g P o o l W i d t h F o r w a r d ( x )
Inputs

Why the cache is a single numberAn average needs nothing from its input to be dier-
entiated, only how many values it was taken over, which is one integer. That is the cheapest
cache in the le, and the contrast with max pooling is instructive: a maximum depends onwhich
value won and so must remember it, whereas an average treats every value identically and so has
nothing to remember.

Each input contributes one N-th of the average, so each receives one N-th of the gradient. The

The contrast with maximum pooling, and what it means for trainingWhere max

it thinly over all of them. Every position therefore receives gradient on every example, which
is why the writer namer's backbone trains more evenly than the reader's. It is also the right
behaviour for what that layer means: the namer asks how prevalent a feature is across the line,
so every position genuinely contributes to the answer and every position should be told how it
contributed.

Why the leading axes are attenedThe code comment explains the one wrinkle. The input
may carry more than a batch axis before its feature axis: the reader's text head applies this
layer to a sequence, shaped time by batch by features, so there are two leading axes. The weight
gradient must sum over every one of those positions, since the same weights were used at each.
Flattening every leading axis into one reduces any such case to the two-dimensional form, after
which the single matrix product performs the sum automatically. The input gradient needs no
attening, because it has the same leading axes as the output and the product acts only on the
last; it is reshaped back to the input's shape at the end. This is what lets one implementation
serve both the namer's single vector and the reader's sequence.

9.15 DropoutForward
def D r o p o u t F o r w a r d (x , p , training , rng )
Inputs
xthe input
pthe fraction to drop
training
whether this is a training pass
rngthe seeded random generator
Output.a pair: the input with some entries zeroed and the rest scaled up, and the mask; or the
input unchanged outside training.
What dropout is for Overttingis when a network learns the training examples rather than
the pattern in them, so it performs well on what it has seen and poorly on anything new. One way
it happens is that the network comes to rely on a particular combination of features that happens
to be reliable in the training data.Dropout[21] prevents that by randomly removing a fraction
of the values at each training step, so no feature can be depended on: it may not be there. The
network is forced to spread its evidence across several features, which generalises better.
Why the survivors are scaled up
mask = [u≥p]
1−p , u∼Uniform(0,1)(9.15)
where
mask
what the input is multiplied by, elementwise
ua random number drawn uniformly between 0 and 1, one per element
pthe drop probability
[u≥p]
1 when the element survives and 0 when it is dropped
1
1−p the compensating scale applied to the survivors
Dropping a fraction p of the values reduces the sum reaching the next layer by that fraction, so
without compensation the layer below would see a smaller signal during training than at inference,
and everything calibrated for one would be wrong for the other. Dividing the survivors by 1−p
restores the expected total: a fraction 1−p of the values survive and each is multiplied by 1/(1−p),
so the expectation is unchanged.
274

=== PDF PAGE 276 ===
Why the scaling is done here rather than at inferenceThe compensation could instead
be applied by scaling everything down by 1−p at inference, which is how dropout was originally
described. Putting it in the training path instead is calledinverted dropout, and it is now
standard for a practical reason: inference then requires no dropout-related code at all. The layer
simply vanishes outside training, which is exactly what recognise.py does, where the gap in the
parameter numbering is the only trace of it.
Outside training the input is returned unchanged with no mask. The backward pass accepts that
absent mask and passes the gradient through untouched, which keeps the two halves consistent
without either needing to know whether the other is in training mode.
9.16 DropoutBackward
def D r o p o u t B a c k w a r d ( dout , cache )
Inputs
dout
the gradient with respect to the output
cache
the mask, or nothing
Output.the gradient with respect to the input.
The derivativeDropout multiplies elementwise by a mask, and the derivative of multiplying
by a constant is that constant, so the backward pass multiplies by the same mask. The mask
includes the scaling factor, so the gradient is scaled by it too, which is necessary: the forward pass
amplied the survivors, so their inuence on the loss is amplied by the same factor.
A dropped element receives exactly zero gradient, which is correct rather than approximate. It
genuinely had no eect on the output for this example, so the loss does not depend on it at all
this step. It will receive gradient on steps where it survives, which is how a unit that is dropped
half the time still trains, from half the examples.
9.17 ConvBlockForwardTrain
def C o n v B l o c k F o r w a r d T r a i n (x , params , prefix , nLayers )
Inputs
xthe input feature maps
params
the parameter dictionary
prefix
the block's name prex
nLayers
how many groups the block contains
Output.a pair: the output, and a list of per-group caches.
275

=== PDF PAGE 277 ===
What it adds to the inference versionThe same convolution, normalise, activate repetition,
with the batch-statistics version of the normalisation and with every group's three caches collected
into a list.
Why the caches are kept in orderThe list is built front to back as the groups are applied,
and the backward pass walks it in reverse. That correspondence is the whole structure of back-
propagation through a sequence of layers: the gradient must pass through them in the opposite
order to the data, so the last cache created is the rst one needed.
The running statistics are updated in place inside the parameter dictionary rather than re-
turned. They are not parameters in the usual sense, since no gradient is computed for them
and no optimiser touches them, but they must be saved in the checkpoint, so they live alongside
the parameters and are excluded from the trainable set by name.
9.18 ConvBlockBackwardTrain
def C o n v B l o c k B a c k w a r d T r a i n ( dout , caches , prefix , nLayers )
Inputs
dout
the gradient with respect to the block's output
caches
the per-group caches from the forward pass
prefix
the block's name prex
nLayers
how many groups
Output.a pair: the gradient with respect to the block's input, and a dictionary of weight
gradients keyed as the parameters are.
The reversalThe groups are walked in reverse, and within each group the three layers are
walked in reverse too: activation, then normalisation, then convolution. Each step takes the
gradient from the step before it, so the gradient ows back through the block in exactly the
opposite order to the way the data owed forward.
Why the gradients are keyed by parameter nameThe weight gradients are collected into
a dictionary using the same names the parameters themselves use. That is what lets the optimiser
be written without any knowledge of the network's structure: it iterates over the parameters and
looks up each one's gradient by name. The names are therefore a contract between three separate
things, the initialiser, the backward pass and the checkpoint format, and a mismatch in any of
them fails with a missing key rather than silently training the wrong array.
The variable holding the gradient is reassigned at each step rather than being given a new
name per layer. That reads oddly but is exactly right: there is only ever one gradient travelling
backwards at a time, and naming its successive values separately would suggest they coexist.
276

=== PDF PAGE 278 ===
9.19 LstmForwardTrain
def L s t m F o r w a r d T r a i n (x , weightIh , weightHh , biasIh , biasHh , h i d d e n S i z e )
Inputs
xthe input sequence, shaped (time, batch, features)
weightIh
the input weights, all four gates stacked
weightHh
the recurrent weights
biasIh
the input bias
biasHh
the recurrent bias
hiddenSize
the width of the hidden and cell state
Output.a pair: the output at every step, and a cache holding every intermediate value at every
step.
What it adds to the inference versionThe same gate arithmetic as LstmForward in recog-
nise.py, with one addition that dominates its memory use: at every time step it records the input,
the two incoming states, all four gate activations and the squashed cell value.
Why so much must be keptThe backward pass needs each of them, and the reason is that
the LSTM's updates are products of the gates with the states. Dierentiating a product requires
both factors, so the gradient with respect to the forget gate needs the previous cell value and the
gradient with respect to the previous cell needs the forget gate. None of it can be recomputed
cheaply either, because recomputing the state at step t requires replaying every step before it.

277

=== PDF PAGE 279 ===
With two layers and two directions that is eight such caches. At a hidden size of 256 and 160
steps it comes to a few million values per batch item, which is why the text model trains with
small batches. This is the characteristic cost ofbackpropagation through time: the whole
history of the sequence must be held in memory until the backward pass has consumed it.
The step caches are kept in a plain list indexed by time, built forwards and consumed backwards,
which is the same arrangement as the convolution block's cache list and for the same reason.
9.20 LstmBackward
def L s t m B a c k w a r d ( dOutputs , cache )
Inputs
dOutputs
the gradient with respect to the output at every step
cache
the per-step values and the weights
Output.ve gradients: with respect to the input sequence, both weight matrices and both biases.
Backpropagation through timeAn LSTM applies the same weights at every step, so the
loss depends on each weight through all 160 steps. By the shared-parameter rule the weight
gradient is the sum over the steps, and the state gradients must be carried backwards through
them, because the state at step t inuenced the output at every later step. The procedure is
backpropagation through time: walk the steps in reverse, accumulating weight gradients and
carrying state gradients.
The two gradients carried backwardsAt each step two quantities arrive from the step after
it: the gradient with respect to the hidden state and the gradient with respect to the cell state.
The hidden gradient is combined with the gradient arriving directly from the layer above, because
the hidden state at step t is used twice, as that step's output and as the next step's input.
dht = ∂L
∂yt
+ dh carried
t+1 (9.17)
where
dht the total gradient with respect to the hidden state at step t
∂L
∂yt the gradient arriving from the layer above for this step's output
dhcarried
t+1
the gradient the following step computed with respect to its incoming hidden state
Adding the two is the single most important line of the function. Omitting the carried term
computes the gradient as though each step's output were independent, which trains something
that works tolerably on short sequences and never learns any long-range dependency, which is the
entire reason an LSTM was chosen.
278

=== PDF PAGE 280 ===
Dierentiating the two state updatesThe updates are h = o·tanh(c) and c = f·c_prev
+ i·g, and everything else follows from dierentiating them. Each gate's gradient is the gradient
of whatever it multiplied, and then each is passed back through its own squashing function.

where
do,df,di,dg

dct−1
the gradient carried back to the previous cell state
ct, c t−1

⊙elementwise multiplication
1−tanh 2
the derivative of the hyperbolic tangent
Every line is the product rule: the gradient with respect to one factor is the incoming gradient
times the other factor. The cell gradient has two contributions because the cell is used twice,
revealed through the hidden state at this step and carried forward to the next, which is the same
double-use argument as for the hidden state.
The gradient path that makes long memory possibleThe last line is worth isolating.
The gradient carried back to the previous cell state is the current cell gradient multiplied by the
forget gate, and by nothing else: no weight matrix is applied along this path. So if the forget gate
is near 1, the gradient passes backwards through that step essentially unchanged, and it can do
so for many steps in succession without shrinking.
That is the precise sense in which the LSTM solves the vanishing gradient problem. A plain
recurrent layer multiplies the carried gradient by its recurrent weight matrix at every step, so after
a hundred steps it has been multiplied by that matrix a hundred times and is either negligible or
enormous. The LSTM's cell path multiplies only by gate values the network itself controls, and
it can learn to set them near 1 where information must survive.
Through the squashing functionsEach gate gradient is then passed back through its own
activation to reach the pre-activation value, which is what the weights actually produced. For the
three sigmoid gates the derivative is the output times one minus the output, and for the candidate
it is one minus the output squared. Both are written in terms of the output rather than the input,
which is why the cache holds the gate activations and not the pre-activations.
279

=== PDF PAGE 281 ===
The weight gradientsThe four pre-activation gradients are concatenated back into the stacked
layout the weights use, and the weight gradients are then matrix products of that stack with the
step's input and previous hidden state. They are accumulated across steps with addition rather
than assignment, which is the shared-parameter sum. Both biases receive the same gradient, since
both were added to the same quantity, which is the backward counterpart of the inference note
that only their sum has any eect.
The input gradient is written per step into a preallocated array rather than appended, because
the steps are visited in reverse and the result must come back in forward order. Appending
and reversing would also work; writing by index makes the ordering explicit at the point it
matters.
9.21 BidirectionalLstmForwardTrain
def B i d i r e c t i o n a l L s t m F o r w a r d T r a i n (x , paramsFwd , paramsBwd , h i d d e n S i z e )
Inputs
xthe input sequence
paramsFwd
the forward direction's parameters
paramsBwd
the backward direction's parameters
hiddenSize
the width per direction
Output.a pair: the two directions concatenated, and a cache holding both directions' caches.
What it doesRuns both directions as in the inference version and keeps both caches. Each
direction is a complete independent LSTM with its own weights, so each has its own full set of
per-step values, and the memory cost is doubled.
The backward direction is run on a reversed view of the input, which is free, and its output is
reversed again to align positions. The reversals must be undone consistently in the backward
pass too, which is the one real opportunity for error in an otherwise mechanical function.
9.22 BidirectionalLstmBackward
def B i d i r e c t i o n a l L s t m B a c k w a r d ( dout , cache )
Inputs
dout
the gradient with respect to the concatenated output
cache
both directions' caches and the hidden size
Output.the gradient with respect to the input, and both directions' weight gradients.
280

=== PDF PAGE 282 ===
Splitting the gradientThe forward pass concatenated the two directions along the feature
axis, so the incoming gradient is split at the same point: the rst half belongs to the forward
direction and the second to the backward one. Concatenation and splitting are exact inverses, so
no arithmetic is involved.
Why the two input gradients are addedBoth directions read thesameinput sequence, so
the input is a shared quantity and by the shared-parameter rule its gradient is the sum of the
two directions' contributions. This is easy to get wrong by treating them as separate inputs and
returning only one, which halves the gradient reaching the convolutional backbone and makes it
train at half rate with no visible symptom.
The reversal, undoneThe backward direction's gradient must be reversed before being handed
to its LSTM backward pass and reversed again afterwards, mirroring the two reversals of the
forward pass. The invariant to hold on to is that every array crossing the boundary of this
function is in forward time order, and the reversed order exists only inside it.
9.23 MultilayerBidirectionalLstmForwardTrain
def M u l t i l a y e r B i d i r e c t i o n a l L s t m F o r w a r d T r a i n (x , layerParams , h i d d e n S i z e )
Inputs
xthe input sequence
layerParams
a list of per-layer parameters
hiddenSize
the width per direction
Output.a pair: the nal output, and the list of per-layer caches.
What it doesApplies each bidirectional layer in turn, collecting the caches. The same build-
forwards, consume-backwards arrangement as the convolution block, one level up: a list of layer
caches, each of which holds two direction caches, each of which holds a list of step caches.
9.24 MultilayerBidirectionalLstmBackward
def M u l t i l a y e r B i d i r e c t i o n a l L s t m B a c k w a r d ( dout , caches )
Inputs
dout
the gradient with respect to the nal output
caches
the per-layer caches
Output.the gradient with respect to the input, and every layer's weight gradients.
281

=== PDF PAGE 283 ===
What it doesWalks the layers in reverse, passing each layer's input gradient to the layer below
and collecting the weight gradients. There is no arithmetic of its own; it is the sequencing that
makes the chain rule hold across the stack.
This completes the recurrent half. The gradient arriving here came from the text head, and
the gradient leaving goes to the height-collapsing pool, which converts it back from a sequence
gradient into an image gradient. Those two conversions, forward and backward, are what join
the convolutional and recurrent halves of the reader.
9.25 LogSumExp
def Lo gS um Ex p (a , b )
Inputs
aone array of log-space values
banother of the same shape
Output.the logarithm of the sum of their exponentials, computed without overow.
What it is forCTC adds probabilities together, and the probabilities are held as logarithms to
avoid underow, as explained under LogSoftmax. But a logarithm turns aproductinto a sum, not
asuminto anything simple: adding two probabilities held as logarithms requires this function.
log(ea +e b) =m+ log
 
ea−m +e b−m
, m= max(a, b)(9.19)
where
a, bthe two log-space values being combined
mthe larger of the two, factored out

at most 1, since a is at most m, so it cannot overow
The identity follows from factoring the larger exponential out of the sum and taking the logarithm.
Its value is numerical: the exponentials in the rearranged form are at most 1, so nothing can
overow, while a direct computation would exponentiate a log probability of−800 and get zero,
or of a large positive number and get innity.
The innity case, and why it needs handlingA forbidden transition is represented by
negative innity, meaning zero probability. When both arguments are that, the maximum is
negative innity and the rearranged expression computes innity minus innity, which is not a
number. The answer should be negative innity: the sum of two impossibilities is still impossible.
So the result is selected explicitly in that case. The code comment notes that numpy evaluates
both branches of the selection eagerly, so the invalid arithmetic still happens and still warns
even though its result is discarded, and the warning is suppressed for that reason. Suppressing
a warning is usually a bad sign; here it is suppressing a warning about a value that has already
been correctly replaced, and the suppression is scoped narrowly to this one expression.
282

=== PDF PAGE 284 ===
Preserving negative innity exactly, rather than using a large negative number, matters for
correctness: an impossible path must contribute exactly nothing, and a nite stand-in con-
tributes a tiny amount that accumulates over 160 steps and can make a forbidden alignment
the winner.
9.26 CtcLoss

What is being computedThis is the heart of training the reader. The label says what was
written and not where, so the loss cannot compare the output to a target column by column. In-
stead CTC [18] denes the probability of the target text as the total probability ofeveryalignment
of it to the columns, and the loss is the negative logarithm of that.

into a sum, which is both numerically stable and what makes the gradient manageable.
283

=== PDF PAGE 285 ===
Why the sum cannot be computed directlyThe number of alignments is enormous. A
40-character label across 160 columns has on the order of 10 to the power 40 valid alignments,
so enumerating them is as impossible as enumerating the seam paths in segment.py, and for the
same reason. The remedy is also the same: dynamic programming over a quantity that can be
built up column by column.
The forward variable

The recurrence is the same topology as the forced alignment: the same three transitions and the
same restriction that a skip may not land on a blank or repeat the label two states back. The
one crucial dierence is that alignment took the maximum of the three arrivals and this takes the
sum.
Why sum here and maximum thereThis is the distinction worth being clear about. Align-
ment wants one particular layout, the most probable, so it takes the best arrival. Training wants
the total probability of the text however it is laid out, so it adds the arrivals: every alignment
that reaches this state contributes, and their probabilities add because they are mutually exclusive
ways of the same thing happening.
Summing is also what makes CTC trainable at all. If training used the maximum it would be
committing to one alignment and pushing the network toward it, which would be arbitrary early
on when the network has no idea where anything is. Summing lets the network be rewarded for
any layout that spells the right text, and the alignment emerges from training rather than being
imposed.
In log space the sum is performed by LogSumExp, applied twice to combine the three terms,
which is why that function exists.

The forward variable measures the probability of everythingbeforea point and the backward
variable the probability of everythingafterit. Both are needed because the gradient requires
knowing, for each column and each label, the total probability of all the complete alignments
passing through that point, and that is the product of the two.
The total probability , and the lossThe total probability of the label is the forward variable
at the nal column, in either of the two states a valid alignment may end in, the last character or
the trailing blank, summed. The loss is its negative logarithm.

The second term is called theoccupation: among all the alignments that spell the target, the
proportion that have class k at column t. So the gradient is the dierence between what the
network currently predicts for that class and what itshouldpredict given the target. Where the
network already agrees with the occupation the gradient is zero; where it predicts too much of a
class the gradient is positive and that score is pushed down.
That this comes out as a simple dierence is the same simplication that makes the cross-entropy
gradient a dierence, and it arises for the same reason: the softmax and the logarithm in the
loss are inverse operations, so dierentiating their composition cancels the awkward terms. It is
why the gradient is computed with respect to the raw logits rather than with respect to the log
probabilities, even though the loss was dened on the latter: taking the whole softmax and loss
together is far simpler than either separately.
285

=== PDF PAGE 287 ===
Why there is a loop over the batchEach example has its own label and so its own length
of extended sequence, so the dynamic programming tables are dierent shapes and cannot be
computed as one array. The loop over the batch is therefore unavoidable, and inside it the loops
over time and over states remain in Python too, which makes this the slowest function in the le.
The small loop over states checking the skip restriction could be vectorised by precomputing the
mask once per example instead of once per column, which is the obvious remaining optimisation.
Both the loss and the gradient are divided by the batch size, so the loss is a mean over examples
rather than a total. That makes it comparable between batch sizes, which matters because the
learning rate is tuned against the gradient's magnitude: without the division, doubling the
batch size would double the gradient and eectively double the learning rate.

Why the negative logarithm of the correct class Cross-entropyfor a single correct

makes it a good loss: a probability of 1 gives a loss of 0, a probability of 0.5 gives about 0.69,
and as the probability approaches zero the loss grows without bound. So being condently wrong
is punished far more than being uncertain, which is the behaviour wanted from a classier that
reports condences.

The gradient is the predicted distribution minus the target distribution, the latter being 1 on the
correct class and 0 elsewhere. The code expresses it exactly that way: copy the probabilities and
subtract one from the correct class. Every wrong class is pushed down in proportion to how much
it was predicted, and the right one is pushed up in proportion to how much it was missed.
The same cancellation as in CTC produces this. Dierentiating the softmax alone gives a full
matrix of terms, and dierentiating the logarithm gives a reciprocal, and composing them cancels
almost everything. This is the standard reason softmax and cross-entropy are implemented as one
unit rather than two layers.
The log-sum-exp stabilisation is applied here too, the maximum being subtracted before ex-
ponentiating, for the reason given under LogSoftmax. It costs one pass and removes any
possibility of overow on a large logit.
9.28 RMSprop
class RMSprop
Output.an optimiser object holding the parameters and a running average of squared gradients.
The problem plain gradient descent hasA single learning rate must suit every weight, and
it cannot. Dierent weights have gradients of wildly dierent magnitudes, by orders of magnitude
between layers and between a bias and a convolution weight. A rate small enough to be stable for
the largest gradients is far too small for the smallest, so some weights barely move while others
oscillate.
The idea: divide by the typical gradient size RMSprop[24] keeps, for each weight, a
running average of its squared gradient, and divides the step by the square root of that. A weight
whose gradients are habitually large is divided by a large number and takes a small step; one with
habitually small gradients takes a comparatively large one. The eect is that every weight moves
at a comparable rate regardless of its gradient's scale.
v←α v+ (1−α)g 2, w←w−η g√v+ϵ (9.26)
where
287

=== PDF PAGE 289 ===
vthe running average of the squared gradient, per weight
αhow much of the old average to keep, 0.99 here
gthe current gradient for this weight
wthe weight
ηthe learning rate
ϵa small constant, 1e-8, so a weight with no gradient history does not divide by zero
The square root is what makes the division dimensionally sensible: the running average is of
squared gradients, so its square root is in the same units as a gradient, and the ratio is dimen-
sionless. The name is from that root-mean-square. At 0.99 the average covers roughly the last
hundred steps, so it describes the gradient's recent typical size rather than this step's value, which
is what makes the scaling stable.
Weight decay here is added to the gradient, which is the classical formulation. The entry for
AdamW explains why that interacts badly with the per-weight scaling and what the alternative
is.
9.29 AdamW
class AdamW
Output.an optimiser object holding the parameters and two running averages per weight.
What it adds to RMSprop Adam[25] keeps a running average of the gradient itself as well
as of its square, and uses the smoothed gradient for the step direction. The rst ismomentum:
averaging successive gradients cancels the part that uctuates between batches and keeps the part
that is consistent, so the step follows the persistent downhill direction rather than the noise of
whichever examples happened to be in this batch.

288

=== PDF PAGE 290 ===
ˆm,ˆv
the bias-corrected averages

ηthe learning rate
Why the averages must be bias-correctedBoth averages start at zero, which is not a neutral
starting point but a strong claim that the gradient is zero. After one step the rst moment is only
one tenth of the observed gradient, and the second is one thousandth of its square, so early steps
would be far too small and, worse, badly misproportioned between the two, since they are biased
by dierent amounts.
The correction divides each by one minus its decay raised to the step number, which is exactly
the total weight the average has accumulated so far. At step 1 the rst moment is divided by 0.1,
recovering the full gradient; by step 100 the divisor is nearly 1 and the correction fades away. So
the optimiser behaves sensibly from its very rst step, which matters because the rst steps are
taken from random weights where large corrections are most needed.
Why the W, and what it xes W eight decayshrinks every weight slightly at each step,
which discourages large weights and reduces overtting. Classically it is implemented by adding
a multiple of the weight to its gradient, which for plain gradient descent is exactly equivalent.
With Adam it is not equivalent, and that is the point of AdamW [26]. A decay term added to
the gradient passes through the division by the square-root average like any other gradient, so a
weight with large gradients has its decay divided down and is barely regularised, while one with
small gradients is decayed heavily. The strength of the regularisation therefore varies per weight
in a way nobody intended, and it depends on the gradient history rather than on the weight.
The x is to apply the decay to the weight directly, outside the scaled step, which is the separate
term in the update above. Every weight is then shrunk by the same proportion, which is what
weight decay is supposed to mean. This is calleddecoupledweight decay, and the W in the
name stands for it.
Writing the decay inside the same parenthesis as the scaled step means it is multiplied by the
learning rate, so the eective decay changes as the learning rate is annealed. The original
formulation decouples it from the learning rate as well. The dierence matters mainly late in
training, when the rate has fallen and the decay falls with it.
9.30 ReduceLrOnPlateau
class R e d u c e L r O n P l a t e a u
Output.a scheduler object that lowers the learning rate when progress stalls.
Why the learning rate should change during trainingA large rate is right early on, when
the weights are far from any good conguration and large steps make rapid progress. It is wrong
later, when the weights are close and a large step jumps past the minimum and bounces around
it. So the rate should start large and end small, and ascheduleris what changes it.
289

=== PDF PAGE 291 ===
This scheduler's ruleIt watches a metric, usually the validation loss, and remembers the best
value seen. Each epoch that fails to beat the best counts as a bad epoch; once the bad epochs
exceed the patience, the learning rate is multiplied by the factor and the count resets. A new best
resets it too.
Why it reacts rather than following a scheduleThe advantage over a xed schedule is
that it needs no estimate of how long training will take. It lowers the rate exactly when progress
has stopped, which is the evidence that the current rate is now too large, whatever epoch that
happens to be. The disadvantage is that it only reacts after the patience has elapsed, so several
epochs are spent at a rate already known to be too large.
Why the patience is neededValidation loss is noisy, because it is measured on a nite set
and the weights change between epochs. A single epoch failing to improve means little. Requiring
ve consecutive failures distinguishes a genuine plateau from the ordinary uctuation of a metric
that is still improving overall. Too little patience lowers the rate on noise and ends training early
at a rate too small to make progress.
The oor on the learning rate stops the repeated halving from reaching zero, at which point
training would continue indenitely without changing anything. It is a way of bounding wasted
time rather than a property of the method.
9.31 CosineAnnealingLr
class C o s i n e A n n e a l i n g L r
Output.a scheduler object that eases the learning rate down a cosine curve.
The schedule
ηt = η0
2

1 + cos
 πt
T

(9.28)
where
ηt the learning rate at epoch t
η0 the initial learning rate
tthe current epoch
Tthe total number of epochs planned
costhe cosine, which runs from 1 at the start to−1 at the end over half a period
At epoch 0 the cosine is 1 and the rate is the full initial value; at the nal epoch it is−1 and the
rate is zero, with a smooth decrease between.
Why a cosine rather than a straight lineThe shape is what recommends it. The cosine
is at at both ends and steepest in the middle, so training spends a while at nearly the full rate,
then drops briskly, then spends a while at a very small rate. Both plateaux are useful: the rst
makes rapid early progress, and the second lets the weights settle precisely into a minimum rather
than continuing to step past it. A linear decay spends equal time at every rate, including the
middling ones that are neither fast nor precise.
290

=== PDF PAGE 292 ===
The trade-o against the plateau schedulerThis one requires knowing the total number
of epochs in advance, since the schedule is dened against it. In exchange it never wastes epochs
at a rate that has become too large, because it lowers the rate continuously rather than waiting
for evidence. Having both available lets the choice be made per training run according to whether
the length is known.
9.32 ClipGradNorm
def C l i p G r a d N o r m ( grads , maxNorm =5.0)
Inputs
grads
the gradient dictionary, modied in place
maxNorm
the greatest allowed total magnitude, default 5.0
Output.the total gradient magnitude before any clipping.
The problem it addressesRecurrent networks occasionally produce an enormous gradient.
A particular batch, often one with an unusually long or badly labelled line, can give gradients
orders of magnitude larger than usual, and a single step with such a gradient moves the weights
so far that everything learned so far is destroyed. Training then has to recover from near-random
weights, and hours of work are lost to one batch. This is theexploding gradientproblem.
The remedy
∥g∥=
sX
k
X
i
gk[i]2, g←g·min

1, τ
∥g∥+δ

(9.29)
Source:[27]
where
∥g∥the total gradient norm: every parameter's gradient treated as one long vector
gk[i]element i of the gradient for parameter k
τthe maximum norm allowed, 5.0
δa tiny constant preventing a division by zero
min(1,·)
so the scaling only ever shrinks
If the total magnitude exceeds the limit, every gradient is multiplied by the same factor so that
the total comes to exactly the limit. The published remedy for exploding gradients is precisely
this [27].
291

=== PDF PAGE 293 ===
Why the whole network is scaled togetherThis is the part worth understanding. The norm
is computed over every parameter at once, and one factor is applied to all of them, rather than
each being clipped separately. The reason is that the gradient'sdirectionis the useful information:
it says which way the loss decreases. Scaling everything by one factor preserves that direction
exactly and changes only the distance travelled. Clipping each parameter independently would
shorten some components and not others, which rotates the direction into one that is not a descent
direction at all, so the step might increase the loss.
Why the norm is returnedThe function returns the magnitude it measured, before any
clipping, so the training loop can log it. That number is one of the most informative diagnostics
available during training: a norm that is steady and comfortably below the limit means training
is healthy, one that is clipped on most batches means the learning rate or the limit is wrong, and a
sudden spike identies the batch that caused it. Without the return value, clipping would silently
conceal the very instability it is protecting against.
9.33 EncodeCrop
def E n c o d e C r o p ( arr )
Inputs
arra line crop as an array
Output.the crop packed into bytes for storage.
Why crops are cached at allExtracting line crops from a page is slow: the page must be
read, binarised, analysed for line positions and cut. Training visits every line once per epoch
for dozens of epochs, so repeating that work would dominate the run. The crops are therefore
extracted once and stored.
What encoding means hereThe array is attened into a byte sequence with its shape
recorded alongside, so it can be written into a single le and read back by oset. Storing each
crop as a separate image le would mean tens of thousands of small les, which is slow to open
and wasteful of disk, since a lesystem allocates a whole block to a le however small.
9.34 DecodeCrop
def D e c o d e C r o p ( raw )
Inputs
rawthe stored bytes
Output.the crop as an array again.
292

=== PDF PAGE 294 ===
What it doesReverses the encoding: reads the shape and reinterprets the bytes as an array of
that shape. Reinterpreting rather than converting means no arithmetic is performed and no copy
is made beyond the read itself, which matters because this runs for every crop of every batch of
every epoch.
9.35 ReadLabelLines
def R e a d L a b e l L i n e s ( imgPath , l ab el Pa th = None )
Inputs
imgPath
the page image's path
labelPath
the transcript's path, derived from the image's if not given
Output.the transcript as a list of lines.
What it is forReads the text that was written on a page. Supervised training needs the correct
answer for every input, and for a page of handwriting that is the transcript, typed when the page
was collected.
Why the transcript is per line rather than per pageThe reader works on one line at
a time, so each crop needs its own label. Keeping the transcript as one line of text per line of
writing means the correspondence is positional: the n-th transcript line labels the n-th crop. That
is what makes it possible to check the extraction, since a page whose crop count does not match
its transcript line count has been segmented wrongly and can be rejected rather than silently
mislabelled.
Deriving the transcript's path from the image's by convention, rather than being told it, is
what lets a directory of pages be processed by listing the images alone. The cost is that the
convention is implicit: a transcript named dierently is simply not found, and the page is
skipped.
9.36 ClusterRows
def C l u s t e r R o w s ( rows , maxGap =5)
Inputs
rows
row indices
maxGap
the largest gap that still counts as the same cluster, default 5
Output.the indices grouped into runs of nearby rows.
293

=== PDF PAGE 295 ===
What it is forGiven a set of interesting rows, such as those containing a printed rule, groups
the ones that are close together. A single printed line a few pixels thick registers as several adjacent
rows, and what is wanted is one entry per rule rather than one per row.
The ruleThe indices are taken in order and a new cluster is started whenever the gap to the
previous one exceeds the limit. This is single-link clustering in one dimension, and it is the natural
formulation when the data is ordered: two rows belong together if they are close, and closeness
chains, so a run of rows each near the last forms one cluster however long it is.
A gap of ve pixels suits the printed rules on ruled paper, which are a pixel or two thick with
occasional breaks where writing crosses them. The chaining property is what bridges those
breaks, and it is also the method's weakness: a dense enough sequence of separate features
merges into one cluster.
9.37 FindRuleLineClusters
def F i n d R u l e L i n e C l u s t e r s ( binaryImg , r a w G r a y s c a l e A r r a y = None )
Inputs
binaryImg
the binarised page
rawGrayscaleArray
the original grey page, if available
Output.the row clusters that look like printed rules.
What it is forFinds the printed ruling on lined paper, so that it can be removed before the
handwriting is analysed and so that its positions can hint at where the lines of writing sit.
How a printed rule is recognisedBy the same property segment.py uses: a printed rule
crosses the whole page in one straight unbroken run, which handwriting never does. Rows con-
taining an unusually long horizontal run of ink are therefore candidates, and clustering them
groups each rule into one entry.
Why the rules are useful as well as unwantedThey are removed because they corrupt the
analysis, joining every letter on a line into one blob. But their positions are also the best possible
evidence about where lines of writing are, because the writer wrote on them. So they are returned
rather than merely deleted, and the line splitter takes them as hints.
This is a separate implementation from the equivalent in segment.py, because this le processes
database pages and that one processes photographs, which dier enough that one routine serving
both would be a compromise for each. The duplication is real but the inputs genuinely dier:
a database scan has even lighting and no perspective.
294

=== PDF PAGE 296 ===
9.38 RemoveStraightFormLines
def R e m o v e S t r a i g h t F o r m L i n e s ( hwRegion )
Inputs
hwRegion
the region of the page containing handwriting
Output.the region with the printed ruling deleted.
What it doesDeletes the long straight runs identied as printing, leaving the handwriting. The
reasoning is set out at length under RemovePrintedStructure in segment.py: length, not darkness
or thickness, distinguishes printing from writing, and the threshold sits in a wide gap between the
two.
Why the removal must be of whole runsRemoving the rule at the places where writing
crosses it would cut the writing too, since at a crossing the same pixels belong to both. The
remedy used here and in segment.py is to restrict the deletion to pixels that form part of a long
run, which a letter's stroke does not, so a crossing loses the rule and keeps the letter.
9.39 DetectPageBounds
def D e t e c t P a g e B o u n d s ( binaryImg , r a w G r a y s c a l e A r r a y = None )
Inputs
binaryImg
the binarised page
rawGrayscaleArray
the grey page, if available
Output.the rectangle of the page that holds handwriting.
What it is forA database page is a scanned form, with a printed header, a typed prompt and
sometimes a footer, of which only the handwritten region is wanted. This nds that region so the
rest can be ignored.
Why the printed parts must be excluded rather than toleratedPrinted text is text, so
the reader would read it, and it has no corresponding transcript line, so every crop after it would
be labelled with the wrong line. A single extra crop at the top of a page misaligns the whole
page's labels, which is far worse than losing the page: a mislabelled example teaches the network
something false, where a missing one teaches nothing.
This is why the crop count is checked against the transcript line count and the page rejected if
they disagree. The check cannot tell which crop is wrong, only that something is, and rejecting
the page is the right response: the database has thousands of pages and losing a few is cheaper
than training on bad labels.
295

=== PDF PAGE 297 ===
9.40 MergeUndersizedFragments
def M e r g e U n d e r s i z e d F r a g m e n t s ( lines , m i n R e l a t i v e H e i g h t =0.4)
Inputs
lines
the candidate line regions
minRelativeHeight
the least height relative to the typical line, default 0.4
Output.the regions with the too-short ones merged into their neighbours.
The problem it solvesLine splitting can divide one line of writing into two, typically when
a row of low ink inside the line is mistaken for the gap between lines. The symptom is a region
much shorter than the others, since one real line has been halved.
Why the test is relativeThe height is compared with the typical line height on the same
page rather than with a xed gure, because the right height depends on the writing and the scan
resolution. A region under 40 percent of the typical height cannot be a line, since no writer varies
their line height that much within a page, so it must be a fragment of one.
Why merging rather than discardingA fragment contains real writing, which the transcript
expects to nd. Discarding it would lose that text and, worse, would change the crop count and
so misalign every label after it. Merging it back into its neighbour restores both the writing and
the count.
9.41 MergeOrSplitToExpectedLineCount
def M e r g e O r S p l i t T o E x p e c t e d L i n e C o u n t ( lines , rowInkCount , e x p e c t e d L i n e C o u n t
)
Inputs
lines
the candidate line regions
rowInkCount
the ink per row prole
expectedLineCount
how many lines the transcript has
Output.the regions adjusted to match that count.
The idea: use the answer to x the methodThe transcript says how many lines the
page has. That is information the splitter did not use, and it is reliable, because it was typed
by a person reading the page. So if the splitter produced the wrong number, the discrepancy is
known exactly, and the regions can be adjusted until the count agrees: merging the most doubtful
boundary when there are too many, splitting the most promising region when there are too few.
296

=== PDF PAGE 298 ===
Why this is legitimate and where its limit isUsing the label to guide the extraction would
be improper if the label were being predicted, and it is not: the task here is to prepare training
data, and the transcript is given for exactly that purpose. It is the same reasoning that justies
forced alignment in style.py, where the known text is used to locate characters rather than to
recognise them.
The limit is that it cannot verify correctness, only count. A page split in the wrong places but
into the right number of pieces passes, and its labels are then condently wrong. The count is a
necessary condition for a correct extraction, not a sucient one.
9.42 EstimateLinePitch
def E s t i m a t e L i n e P i t c h ( smoothedRowInk , minPitch =40 , maxPitch =400)
Inputs
smoothedRowInk
the smoothed ink per row prole
minPitch
the smallest pitch considered, default 40
maxPitch
the largest, default 400
Output.the estimated spacing between lines of writing, in pixels.
What it is forThe same quantity LinePitch nds in segment.py, estimated from a smoothed
row prole, and used as the scale for the line splitting below. The reasoning for using a projection
prole, and for smoothing it rst, is given in that entry.
Why the search is boundedThe bounds keep the estimate within the range a line of writing
on a scanned page can plausibly occupy. Without a lower bound the method can lock onto the
spacing between the strokes within a word, which is a genuine periodicity in the prole and a
much shorter one. Without an upper bound it can lock onto the spacing between paragraphs.
Both are real patterns; the bounds say which scale is being asked about.
9.43 SplitLinesByPeriodicity
def S p l i t L i n e s B y P e r i o d i c i t y ( binaryRegion , r u l e L i n e H i n t s = None , s m o o t h W i n d o w
=9 ,
m i n L i n e H e i g h t =30)
Inputs
binaryRegion

ruleLineHints

297

=== PDF PAGE 299 ===
smoothWindow
the prole smoothing width, default 9
minLineHeight
the shortest acceptable line, default 30
Output.the row ranges of the individual lines of writing.
The approach, and why periodicity is the right toolLines of writing on a page are close
to evenly spaced, because the writer followed the ruling or kept a steady hand. That regularity is
strong evidence and it is what the splitter uses: rather than looking for each gap independently,
it establishes the pitch and then expects boundaries at roughly that spacing.
The advantage over independent gap-nding is robustness to a single bad gap. A line whose
descenders reach into the next line leaves almost no gap at all, and a method looking only for
gaps misses that boundary entirely, merging two lines. A method that knows the expected spacing
looks for a boundary where one ought to be and places it at the best available row, even where
that row is not a clear gap.
Why the printed rules are used as hintsWhere the ruling was found, it says where the
writer intended each line to sit, which is better evidence than the ink prole: it is unaected
by descenders, by a line of unusual height, or by a blank line. The hints are used to guide the
boundaries rather than to set them, because writers do not follow ruling exactly.
This whole routine is a separate, earlier solution to the problem segment.py solves with seam
carving. The seam approach is better, since it cuts around descenders instead of choosing a
row to cut through. This one remains because it is what the cached training data was extracted
with, and re-extracting it would change the data the reported checkpoints were trained on.
9.44 ExtractLinePatches
def E x t r a c t L i n e P a t c h e s ( page , t a r g e t H e i g h t =32 , maxWidth =1024 ,
e x p e c t e d L i n e C o u n t = None ,

Output.the line crops, with their labels where the count matched.
298

=== PDF PAGE 300 ===
What it is forThe whole page-to-crops pipeline for database pages, in one call: nd the page
bounds, remove the printed ruling, split into lines, merge the fragments, reconcile against the
expected count, and cut and scale each crop.
Why the crops are scaled hereEach crop is scaled to a xed height, preserving its aspect
ratio. Doing it at extraction rather than at training time means the cache holds crops of a known
height, so loading a batch needs no resizing, and the cache is smaller, since a scanned line is
several times taller than the network needs.
Why width is capped rather than scaledAn unusually wide crop is truncated rather than
squeezed. Squeezing would change the aspect ratio and so the letter proportions, for the reasons
given under ResizeLineImageFixed. An over-wide crop is also usually a fault rather than a long
line, two lines having been merged, so truncating loses little that was right.
Where the crop count does not match the transcript, the crops are returned without labels and
the page cannot be used for training the reader, though it remains usable for the writer namer,
which needs only to know who wrote the page. That is why the two networks are trained from
dierent views of the same cache.
9.45 EncodeText
def E n c o d e T e x t ( text )
Inputs
text
a line of text
Output.the sequence of class indices for its characters.
What it doesConverts a label into the numbers CTC works with, by looking each character
up in the charset mapping from recognise.py and dropping anything not in it.
Why unknown characters are dropped rather than substitutedA character outside
the charset has no output slot, so the network cannot be asked to produce it. Substituting
a placeholder would teach it to emit that placeholder whenever something unfamiliar appears,
which is worse than omitting it: the network would learn a response to a situation it can never
handle correctly.
Dropping the character does introduce a small inconsistency, since the image contains a mark the
label does not mention. CTC tolerates that, because it allows the network to emit blank there,
and the alternative would be discarding every line containing any unusual character, which over
a large database is a substantial loss.
9.46 FindPeaks
299

=== PDF PAGE 301 ===
def Fi nd Pe ak s ( sm , fl o or Fr ac =0.0 , minGap =0 , p r o m i n e n c e = None , distance = None
)
Inputs

floorFrac

Output.the indices of the accepted peaks, in order.
What it is forA general peak nder, used by the line splitting. It applies three lters in turn,
each addressing a dierent way a spurious peak arises.
Finding candidatesA candidate is at least as large as its left neighbour and strictly larger
than its right. The asymmetry is deliberate, as the code comment notes: if both comparisons
were inclusive, a at-topped peak several samples wide would report every one of those samples.
With one strict comparison it reports exactly one.
The height oor, and a trap in itA candidate must exceed a fraction of the prole's
maximum. The comment records a real pitfall: the oor must not default to zero, because the
prole can be negative. A prole with its mean removed has negative valleys, and a peak in a
negative region is still a peak. Defaulting the oor to negative innity rather than zero is what
makes the lter optional rather than silently active.
Prominence, which is the interesting lter Prominencemeasures how much a peak stands
out from its surroundings, as opposed to how tall it is. It is dened as the peak's height above
the higher of the two lowest points reached before the prole rises above the peak again on either
side.

The code comment names what this rejects: a ripple riding on a big peak's ank. Such a ripple
can be tall in absolute terms, taller than a genuine small peak elsewhere, so a height lter keeps
it. But it does not descend far on either side before the prole rises again, so its prominence is
small and this lter removes it. Height asks how large a peak is; prominence asks whether it is
a peak at all rather than a bump on something larger. The term is borrowed from topography,
where it distinguishes an independent summit from a shoulder of a bigger mountain.
The separation lterFinally, peaks too close together are thinned by the same greedy strongest-
rst suppression used for the line centres in segment.py: take the tallest, exclude everything within
the minimum separation, repeat. Processing tallest rst is what makes it correct rather than ar-
bitrary, for the reason given there.
The three lters are each optional and disabled by default, so the function is a plain local-
maximum nder unless asked for more. That keeps one implementation usable for both the
careful line-splitting case and the simple ones.
9.47 MyAne
def MyAffine ( img , angleDeg =0.0 , shearDeg =0.0 , f i ll Va lu e =255)
Inputs
imgthe image
angleDeg
the rotation in degrees
shearDeg
the shear in degrees
fillValue
what to ll uncovered pixels with, default 255
Output.the image rotated and sheared about its centre.
Background: what an ane transformation isAnanetransformation is any combi-
nation of rotation, scaling, shearing and translation: it maps straight lines to straight lines and
keeps parallel lines parallel. Every such transformation can be written as a matrix multiplication
once the coordinates are expressed inhomogeneousform, which means appending a 1 to each
point so that a translation becomes part of the matrix rather than a separate addition.
Why the transformations are composed as matrices
M=T +c R S T −c (9.31)
where
301

=== PDF PAGE 303 ===
Mthe combined transformation
T−c a translation moving the image's centre to the origin
Sthe shear
Rthe rotation
T+c a translation moving the centre back
Rotation and shear are both dened about the origin, which for an image is a corner. Applying
them directly would swing the image about that corner and out of frame. Translating the centre
to the origin rst, transforming, and translating back makes both act about the centre instead.
Matrix composition is what allows the four steps to be combined into one, so the image is resampled
once rather than four times, which matters because each resampling blurs it slightly.
The order reads right to left, since the rightmost matrix acts on the point rst. That is the usual
convention for column-vector coordinates and it is the opposite of the order the operations are
listed in prose.
Why the matrix is invertedThe combined matrix is inverted before being used. The reason
is the direction resampling works in: for eachoutputpixel, the warp asks which input position
it came from, which is the inverse map. Working forward instead, pushing each input pixel to
its output position, leaves gaps where no input pixel happens to land and collisions where two
do. Working backwards lls every output pixel exactly once, which is why it is universal in image
warping. The same argument appears under Warp in segment.py.
9.48 AugmentShear
def A u g m e n t S h e a r ( img )
Inputs
imga line crop
Output.the crop with a random shear applied.
Background: what augmentation is for Data augmentationcreates extra training exam-
ples by altering existing ones in ways that do not change their label. A sheared line of handwriting
still says the same words, so it is a valid extra example. The purpose is to teach the network
which variations are irrelevant: having seen the same line at many slants, it learns to read the
letters rather than to recognise one particular slant.
Why shear in particularSlant is the largest single source of variation between hands, and
between one writer's pages. Augmenting it is directly useful because the network must read every
slant, and it is cheap because shearing is exactly the transformation that changes slant and nothing
else.
302

=== PDF PAGE 304 ===
The range, and why it is expressed as a tangentThe shear factor is drawn uniformly
from−0.6 to 0.6 and then converted to an angle. Working in the factor rather than the angle is
deliberate: the factor is what the shear matrix contains, so a uniform draw on it gives a uniform
transformation strength, where a uniform draw on the angle would concentrate the eect near the
extremes. A factor of 0.6 is about 31 degrees, which spans the range of real handwriting, from
upright to the steepest cursive in this project's writers.
9.49 AugmentRotate
def A u g m e n t R o t a t e ( img )
Inputs
imga line crop
Output.the crop rotated by a small random angle.
What it simulatesA line that is not quite level, which happens when a page is photographed
slightly askew or when a writer's line drifts. The range is only 2.5 degrees either way, far smaller
than the shear range.
Why the range is smallBecause the real variation is small. The segmenter deskews every
page before cutting crops, so by the time a crop reaches the network its residual tilt is a fraction
of a degree. Augmenting much further than the real variation teaches the network to handle a
situation that never occurs, at the cost of capacity it could spend on ones that do. A rotation
also changes the measured slant and the apparent x-height, so a large one would conict with the
shear augmentation rather than complementing it.
9.50 FindPerspectiveCoes
def F i n d P e r s p e c t i v e C o e f f s ( startpoints , en dp oi nt s )
Inputs
startpoints
the four source corners
endpoints
where they should land
Output.the eight coecients of the perspective transformation.
Background: what a perspective transformation isAn ane transformation keeps parallel
lines parallel, which a photograph taken at an angle does not: the far edge of a page appears shorter
than the near one, so its sides converge. Aperspectiveorprojectivetransformation is the more
general map that allows that convergence, and it is what actually happens when a at page is
photographed from an angle.
303

=== PDF PAGE 305 ===
x′ = ax+by+c
gx+hy+ 1 , y ′ = dx+ey+f
gx+hy+ 1 (9.32)
where
x, ya point in the source image
x′, y ′
where it lands
a . . . f
the six coecients an ane map would also have
g, hthe two extra coecients that produce the perspective eect
1the xed ninth coecient; the map is unchanged by scaling all nine, so one can be xed
The division by a function of position is what distinguishes it from an ane map and what makes
parallel lines converge: points further away in the implied depth direction are divided by a larger
number and so are compressed toward one another.
How the eight coecients are foundFour corner correspondences are given, each xing two
equations, giving eight equations for the eight unknowns. The equations above are not linear in
the coecients because of the division, but multiplying each through by its denominator makes
them linear, and that rearrangement is what the rows of the matrix in the code are: each row is one
such multiplied-out equation, with the cross terms carrying the negative signs the rearrangement
produces.
The resulting system is then solved by standard linear algebra. The reason four corners are
exactly right is that eight unknowns need eight equations: three corners would leave the sys-
tem underdetermined and ve would overdetermine it, requiring a least-squares t rather than a
solution.
9.51 AugmentPerspective
def A u g m e n t P e r s p e c t i v e ( img )
Inputs
imga line crop
Output.the crop with a random perspective distortion applied.
What it simulates, and why it matters for this projectA page photographed from an an-
gle rather than square on. This augmentation matters more here than it would for a database-only
system, because this project's own pages are phone photographs: a page on a desk photographed
by hand always carries some perspective. A network trained only on at scans would be seeing a
distortion it had never met.
304

=== PDF PAGE 306 ===
Why it is applied to the line rather than the pageStrictly the perspective applies to
the whole page, and a line crop is a narrow strip of it, so the distortion within one strip is a
small part of the page's. Applying a crop-sized perspective is therefore an approximation, and a
slightly stronger one than reality. That is acceptable for augmentation, whose purpose is to cover
the variation rather than to model it exactly, and erring toward stronger distortion is the safer
direction.
9.52 PerspectiveJitterPoints
def P e r s p e c t i v e J i t t e r P o i n t s ( width , height , D i s t o r t i o n S c a l e )
Inputs
width
the crop width
height
its height
DistortionScale
how far the corners may move, as a fraction
Output.a pair of corner lists: the originals and the jittered destinations.
What it doesMoves each of the four corners by a random amount within a fraction of the
crop's size, producing the correspondences the coecient solver needs.
Why the corners are moved independentlyIndependent corner displacements generate
a general perspective distortion rather than a parameterised one. The alternative, choosing a
viewing angle and deriving the corners from it, would be more physically faithful and would
require a camera model, a focal length and an assumed distance, none of which are known.
Moving the corners directly covers the same space of distortions with no model at all, which is
what augmentation needs.
The displacement is a fraction of the crop's dimensions rather than a number of pixels, so the
strength of the distortion is the same for a crop of any size. This is the same scale-relative
reasoning that governs the thresholds throughout the project.
9.53 AugmentElastic
def A u g m e n t E l a s t i c ( img )
Inputs
imga line crop
Output.the crop with a smooth random warp applied.
305

=== PDF PAGE 307 ===
What it simulates Elastic distortion[8] displaces every pixel by a small smoothly varying
random amount, so the image is stretched here and compressed there. For handwriting it imitates
the natural variation of a hand: the same person writing the same word twice produces shapes
that dier in just this way, locally stretched and squeezed rather than globally transformed.
How the smoothness is obtained, and why it is essentialTwo random displacement
elds are generated, one per axis, and each is blurred with a Gaussian before being applied. The
blurring is what makes the method work. An unblurred random displacement moves each pixel
independently, which is simply noise: it destroys the strokes rather than deforming them. After
blurring, nearby pixels move together, so a stroke stays a connected stroke and is merely bent.
The blur width therefore controls the character of the distortion. A wide blur gives a nearly
uniform displacement, which is just a translation; a narrow one approaches noise. The useful
range is a width comparable to the features being deformed, which for handwriting is around
a letter's size. The amplitude scales the displacement after blurring, since blurring reduces the
magnitude as well as smoothing it.
The implementation calls the Elastic function in segment.py, which is the same operation
the glyph pipeline uses. Sharing it means the augmentation applied during training and any
distortion applied elsewhere are identical, which matters if the two are ever compared.
9.54 AugmentLineImage
def A u g m e n t L i n e I m a g e ( pilImg )
Inputs
pilImg
a line crop
Output.the crop with a randomly chosen augmentation applied, or unchanged.
What it doesPicks one augmentation at random from the available list and applies it, or
sometimes leaves the crop alone.
Why one at a time rather than all of themComposing several distortions compounds their
severity, and the result quickly stops resembling handwriting: a crop sheared, rotated, warped in
perspective and elastically distorted is unreadable, and training on unreadable examples teaches
nothing. Applying one at a time keeps every augmented example plausible while still covering
each kind of variation across the training set as a whole.
Why unaugmented crops are kept tooSome proportion of crops pass through untouched,
so the network sees the real distribution as well as the augmented one. If every example were
distorted, the network would be trained exclusively on distorted data and would be slightly mis-
matched to the undistorted crops it meets at inference, which is the domain-shift problem applied
to augmentation itself.
306

=== PDF PAGE 308 ===
9.55 AUGMENTATIONS
A U G M E N T A T I O N S = [ AugmentShear , AugmentRotate , AugmentElastic ,
A u g m e n t P e r s p e c t i v e ]
Output.the list of augmentation functions to choose from.
Why the augmentations are a listCollecting them in a list means the chooser needs no
knowledge of what they are: it picks an entry and calls it. Adding or removing one is an edit to
the list alone, and more importantly, running an experiment on which augmentations help means
editing one line, which is how such questions are answered in practice.
All four take a crop and return a crop, which is what makes them interchangeable. That
uniform signature is the real content of the design: a function needing extra parameters could
not be in the list, so each augmentation draws its own random amounts internally rather than
being given them.
9.56 LineImageCache
class L i n e I m a g e C a c h e
Output.an object holding extracted crops on disk and serving them by index.
What it is forStores every extracted crop in a single le with an index of where each one
starts, so a crop can be read by number without reading the rest. It is what makes the extraction
cost payable once rather than once per epoch.
Why one le with an indexTens of thousands of separate image les would be slow, because
each open is a separate lesystem operation, and wasteful, because a lesystem allocates a whole
block per le. One le read at an oset is a single operation, and the index is small enough to
hold in memory.
Why the whole cache is not simply loaded into memoryThe extracted crops for the
full database run to several gigabytes, more than is comfortable to hold while also holding the
activations a training step needs. Reading each crop as the batch needs it keeps the memory use
to the batch, and the operating system's own le cache keeps the recently used parts in memory
anyway, which recovers most of the speed without the program managing it.
9.57 IAMLineDatasetRaw
class I A M L i n e D a t a s e t R a w
Output.an object presenting the database's pages as a sequence of labelled line crops.
What it is forWraps the cache and the labels so that training sees a simple sequence: ask for
item n and get a crop and its text. Where the pages came from, how they were cut and how they
are stored are all hidden behind that.
307

=== PDF PAGE 309 ===
Why this separation mattersThe training loop should not know whether its data came from
the public database, from this project's own photographs, or from a third source. Presenting every
source through the same interface is what allows TrainTextModel to accept a source argument and
otherwise be unchanged, and what allows the two sources to be combined by AddPersonalSamples
without the loop being aware of it.
The dataset is the public IAM database of handwritten English [1], whose value for this project
is its transcripts: without a known text per line there is nothing to train against and no way
to align characters.
9.58 TrainEvalView
class T r a i n E v a l V i e w
Output.a view of a dataset that applies augmentation or does not.
Why the same data needs two viewsAugmentation must be applied when training and
must not be applied when validating. The reason is what validation is for: it estimates how the
network will perform on real data, so it must use real data. Measuring on augmented crops would
measure performance on distorted handwriting, which nobody is going to present.
Aviewwraps the same underlying dataset and changes how items are served, rather than copying
the data. So the training and validation views share one cache and one set of labels, and there is
no possibility of them drifting apart, which two copies would allow.
This also makes the training and validation split a property of which indices each view holds
rather than of the data itself. A line can therefore appear in only one of them, which is the
whole requirement of a held-out set, and it is enforced by construction rather than by care.
9.59 TextCollateFn
def T e x t C o l l a t e F n ( batch )
Inputs
batch
a list of crop and label pairs
Output.the batch assembled into arrays.
What collation isA dataset serves one item at a time; a network wants a batch as one array.
Collationis the step between: taking a list of items and stacking them, which for images means
padding them to a common size.
Why the labels stay as a listThe crops are stacked into one array, but the labels cannot be:
they are sequences of dierent lengths, and CTC requires each one's true length, since padding a
label would add characters the image does not contain. So the labels are kept as a list of varying-
length sequences, which is exactly what CtcLoss expects and why that function loops over the
batch.
308

=== PDF PAGE 310 ===
9.60 AuthorLabeledView
class A u t h o r L a b e l e d V i e w
Output.a view of the same crops labelled by who wrote them.
What it is forPresents the same cached crops with a dierent label: the writer's identity
instead of the text. The namer is trained from this view and the reader from the text view, over
one shared cache.
Why one cache can serve bothBecause the input is the same object and only the question
diers. Extracting the crops twice would double the slowest part of the pipeline and, worse, would
allow the two extractions to dier, so that a nding about one network might not transfer to the
other.
There is also a practical gain: a page whose crop count did not match its transcript is unusable
for the reader but perfectly usable here, since the writer's identity is a property of the page and
does not depend on the lines being correctly divided. So this view can draw on pages the text
view must reject.
9.61 AuthorCollateFn
def A u t h o r C o l l a t e F n ( batch )
Inputs
batch
a list of crop and writer-index pairs
Output.the batch assembled into arrays.
How it diers from the text versionSimpler, because the labels are single integers rather
than sequences, so they stack into one array like the crops. There is no variable length to preserve
and no padding question for the labels.
That simplicity is a direct consequence of the task: classication has one answer per example,
where transcription has a sequence whose length varies. It is the same distinction that makes
the namer need no CTC and no recurrence.
9.62 AddPersonalSamples
def A d d P e r s o n a l S a m p l e s ( base )
Inputs
base
the database-derived dataset
Output.the dataset with this project's own photographed pages added.
309

=== PDF PAGE 311 ===
What it is forMixes the ve photographed writers' pages into the database training set. The
result is the joint checkpoint, as opposed to the scratch one trained on the database alone.
Why mixing rather than training separatelyTwo things are wanted at once. The database
supplies volume: thousands of writers, which is what teaches the network the general shape
of handwriting. This project's pages supply the specic domain: phone photographs of these
particular hands, which is what the system will actually be given.
Training on the database alone leaves a domain gap, since the network has never seen a photograph.
Training on the personal pages alone gives far too few examples, a few hundred lines against tens
of thousands, and the network would overt them immediately. Mixing gets the volume and the
domain together, which is why the joint checkpoint measures so much better on this project's
crops than the scratch one: 96 to 99 percent character accuracy against 22 to 26.
That dierence is the single largest measured eect in the project, and it is a statement about
which data a network was trained on rather than about any architectural choice. It is also why
the checkpoint actually loaded at inference matters so much, and why the candidate ordering
in recognise.py deserves attention.
9.63 TekliaLineDataset
class T e k l i a L i n e D a t a s e t
Output.a dataset presenting an alternative public corpus of line images.
What it is forA second public source of labelled line images, already segmented into lines,
selectable by the source argument of the training function.
Why a second source is usefulIt arrives already cut into lines, so it exercises the training
path without the page-segmentation path, which separates two possible causes of poor results: a
network that trains well on pre-cut lines and badly on self-cut ones has a segmentation problem
rather than a training one. It is also simply more data, from a dierent collection with dierent
scanning conventions, which broadens what the network has seen.
9.64 MAX_SAFE_LABEL_CHARS
MAX _ SAFE _ LABEL _ CHARS = int ( INPUT _ WIDTH // 4 * 0.75)
Output.the longest label that can safely be trained on, derived from the input width.
The constraint it expressesCTC needs at least one column per character, and in practice
more, since a character usually occupies several columns and blanks need somewhere to go. The
network produces one column per four input pixels, after two halvings, so the number of columns
is xed by the input width and the longest trainable label follows from it.
maxChars =

310

where

INPUT_WIDTH

0.75a safety margin, leaving a quarter of the columns spare for blanks and for characters occu-

What happens without the limitA label longer than the available columns has no valid
alignment at all, so its total probability is zero and the loss is innite. Innity propagates through
the gradient and destroys every weight in the network on that single step. So this is not a quality
lter but a correctness one: a single over-long label in a training set ruins the run, and the
symptom is a loss that becomes not-a-number and never recovers.
9.65 HIDDEN_SIZE
HIDDEN _ SIZE = 256
Output.256, the LSTM width per direction.
What it setsHow many numbers the recurrent layer carries from one column to the next, per
direction. With two directions concatenated, each position is described by 512 numbers after the
rst layer.
The trade-oA wider state can remember more about the line, which the task needs: the
reader must carry enough about preceding letters to resolve an ambiguous one. But the recurrent
weight matrices are four times the hidden size by the hidden size, so the parameter count grows
with the square of this number, as does the work per step, and the cached activations grow with
it linearly. At 256 the two layers hold the large majority of the network's parameters.
256 is the value the architecture this network follows uses [17]. Taking a published gure
rather than searching for one is reasonable for a parameter whose eect is gradual and whose
evaluation would cost a full training run each time.
