Output.the directory checkpoints are written to and read from.
What it is forOne named location for trained weights, taken from the central paths module,
so that the training code and the inference code agree on where checkpoints live without either
hard-coding a path.

9.71 InitConv
def InitConv ( rng , outCh , inCh , kh , kw )
Inputs
rngthe seeded random generator
outCh
the number of lters
inCh
the input channel count
khthe lter height
kwits width
Output.a pair: the randomly initialised lters and a zero bias.
Why initialisation matters at allThe starting weights are random, but not arbitrarily so.
If they are too small, the signal shrinks as it passes through each layer and arrives at the output
as nearly nothing, so there is no gradient to learn from. If too large, it grows and saturates. With
twelve convolutional layers either eect compounds twelve times, so the scale of the initial weights
determines whether the network trains at all.
He initialisation
w∼ N
 
0,
r
2
fanIn
!
,fanIn = inCh×k h ×k w (9.34)
Source:[28]
where
weach weight, drawn independently
N(0, s)
a normal distribution with mean 0 and standard deviation s
fanIn
how many input values each output is computed from
2the factor that accounts for ReLU, explained below
The reasoning runs as follows. An output is a sum of fanIn products of a weight and an input.
For independent terms, variances add, so the output's variance is fanIn times the product of the
weight and input variances. Keeping the output's variance equal to the input's therefore requires
the weight variance to be one over fanIn. That is the Glorot argument [29].
313

=== PDF PAGE 315 ===
The factor of 2 is the correction for ReLU [28]. ReLU sets every negative value to zero, discarding
about half the signal's variance, so without compensation the variance would halve at every layer
and after twelve layers be reduced by a factor of four thousand. Doubling the weight variance
exactly osets it. The dierence between the two initialisations is only that factor, and for a
network this deep it is the dierence between training and not.
Why the bias starts at zeroThere is nothing to break symmetry in a bias, because the
weights are already random, so each unit already computes something dierent. Zero is the
neutral starting point, and it is also harmless here because the batch normalisation that follows
removes any constant oset anyway, as noted under ConvBlockForward.
9.72 InitBatchNorm
def I n i t B a t c h N o r m ( ch )
Inputs
chthe channel count
Output.four arrays: the scale at one, the shift at zero, and the running statistics at zero and
one.
Why the scale starts at one and the shift at zeroThose values make the learned part
of the layer the identity: the output is the standardised input unchanged. So the layer begins
by doing exactly the normalisation it exists for and nothing else, and any departure from that is
something training chose. Starting the scale at a random value would add a per-channel distortion
the network would have to learn to undo before making progress.
Why the running statistics start at zero and oneA mean of zero and a variance of one
describe already-standardised data, so a network evaluated before any training behaves as though
normalisation had been applied correctly. They are also the values the exponential average con-
verges away from fastest: being the neutral assumption, any real data moves them in a meaningful
direction immediately.
These two pairs are treated dierently everywhere else in the le. The scale and shift are
parameters with gradients and are updated by the optimiser; the running statistics have no
gradients and are updated by observation. TrainableParams separates them by name, which is
why the naming convention for them is load-bearing.

The same one-over-fan-in scaling as the convolution, expressed as a uniform range rather than a

framework's linear layer uses exactly this, which keeps checkpoints interchangeable.
Why the bias is random here but zero for a convolutionBecause no normalisation follows
a linear layer. A convolution's bias is removed by the batch normalisation after it, so its initial
value is immaterial; a linear layer's bias survives into the output and a small random value is as
good a starting point as zero.
9.74 InitLstmDirection
def I n i t L s t m D i r e c t i o n ( rng , inputSize , h i d d e n S i z e )
Inputs
rngthe random generator
inputSize
the input width
hiddenSize
the hidden width
Output.four arrays: both weight matrices and both biases for one direction.
The distributionUniform between plus and minus one over the square root of the hidden size,
for every weight and both biases. The code comment records that this matches the reference
framework's own initialisation, which is the reason for the specic form.
315

=== PDF PAGE 317 ===
Why the hidden size rather than the input size sets the scaleThe input weights are
scaled by the hidden size too, although their fan-in is the input size. That is the framework's
convention rather than what the variance argument would give. It is defensible because the two
are comparable in this network, the input to the second layer being twice the hidden size, and
because the gates' sigmoids are forgiving: they saturate gracefully rather than destroying the
signal, so the initialisation scale is less critical than for a deep convolutional stack.
All four gates are initialised identically here. A common renement is to initialise the forget
gate's bias to a positive value, so the gate starts near 1 and the cell retains information from
the rst step, which helps a network learn long dependencies sooner. It is not done here, which
matches the reference framework's default.
9.75 InitConvBlock
def I n i t C o n v B l o c k ( rng , params , prefix , inCh , outCh , nLayers )
Inputs
rngthe random generator
params
the parameter dictionary, lled in place
prefix
the block's name prex
inCh
the input channels
outCh
the output channels
nLayers
how many groups
Output.nothing; the parameters are added to the dictionary.
What it doesCreates one block's worth of parameters under the names the forward and
backward passes expect, with the channel count switching from the block's input to its output
after the rst group.
Why the naming convention is load-bearingThese names are a contract between four
separate pieces of code: this initialiser, the forward pass, the backward pass and the checkpoint
format. They must agree exactly, and a disagreement fails with a missing key rather than silently
training the wrong array, which is the reason for building them from the same arithmetic in every
one of those places rather than listing them.
The rst group takes the block's input channel count and every later group takes its output
count, which is why the channel variable is reassigned inside the loop. Getting that wrong
produces a shape mismatch at the rst forward pass rather than a subtle fault.
316

=== PDF PAGE 318 ===
9.76 InitBackboneParams
def I n i t B a c k b o n e P a r a m s ( rng , params )
Inputs
rngthe random generator
params
the parameter dictionary, lled in place
Output.nothing; the backbone's parameters are added.
The channel progressionOne channel in, then 32, then 64, then 128, with two, four and six
groups in the three stages. The channel count doubles at each stage while the spatial size halves,
which is the standard arrangement in a convolutional backbone.
Why that arrangementTwo reasons, one about capacity and one about cost. Deeper layers
represent larger and more varied structures, strokes giving way to junctions and then to letter
parts, and there are more distinguishable large structures than small ones, so more channels are
needed to hold them. And since each pooling quarters the spatial size, doubling the channels
leaves the work per layer roughly halved rather than growing, so the deeper stages are aordable
despite being wider and having more groups.
Why the backbone is shared between the two networksBoth the reader and the namer
use exactly this backbone, initialised by this one function. The features useful for recognising a
letter and for recognising a hand are largely the same features, as argued under AuthorClassier-
CNNNumpy, so there is no reason for the two to dier, and sharing the initialiser means a change
to the architecture applies to both rather than to one.
9.77 TrainableParams
def T r a i n a b l e P a r a m s ( params )
Inputs
params
the full parameter dictionary
Output.the subset the optimiser may update.
What it excludes and whyThe running mean and variance of every batch normalisation.
They are stored alongside the parameters, because they must be saved in the checkpoint, but they
are not parameters: no gradient is computed for them, and they are updated by observing the
data rather than by descending a loss.
317

=== PDF PAGE 319 ===
What would happen if they were includedThe optimiser would look up a gradient for
them, nd none, and fail with a missing key, which is the benign outcome. Had a zero gradient
been supplied instead, the optimiser would apply weight decay to them and shrink them steadily
toward zero, so the stored variances would fall and inference would divide by a smaller and
smaller number, amplifying every activation. That is a silent corruption which would appear only
at inference, as a network whose validation accuracy during training was ne and whose saved
checkpoint performs badly.
The separation is by name sux, which works but ties correctness to a naming convention. A
parameter accidentally named with one of those suxes would be silently frozen, and one of
these accidentally named otherwise would be silently decayed.
9.78 SaveCheckpoint
def S a v e C h e c k p o i n t ( path , params , extra = None )
Inputs
path
where to write
params
the parameters
extra
additional values such as the epoch number
Output.nothing; the checkpoint le is written.
What goes into a checkpointEvery parameter, including the running statistics, plus meta-
data: which epoch it came from and the best validation loss seen so far. The metadata is what
makes resuming possible.
Why the metadata must be in the leResuming a run requires knowing which epoch to
continue from and what the best loss was, so that the scheduler and the best-checkpoint logic
behave as though the run had never stopped. Keeping those in the checkpoint rather than in a
separate le means they cannot be separated from the weights they describe, which is the failure a
parallel log le invites: resuming from the wrong epoch number silently misschedules the learning
rate.
The metadata keys begin with a double underscore, which is the same convention LoadState-
DictAny uses to tell metadata from weights. The two functions are in dierent les and must
agree on it, which is worth noting as another implicit contract.
9.79 LoadCheckpointMeta
def L o a d C h e c k p o i n t M e t a ( path )
Inputs
318

=== PDF PAGE 320 ===
path
a checkpoint le
Output.the epoch it was saved at and the best loss recorded, where present.
What it is forReads only the metadata, without the weights, so that a resume can establish
where it is continuing from. Reading the metadata alone is cheap, where loading several hundred
megabytes of weights to read two numbers is not.
Both values are optional, since a checkpoint written before the metadata convention existed
has neither. Returning nothing rather than a default lets the caller distinguish "no record"
from "epoch zero", and it starts the run at epoch 1 rather than claiming to resume.
9.80 LoadParamsInto
def L o a d P a r a m s I n t o ( params , c h e c k p o i n t P a t h )
Inputs
params
the parameter dictionary, lled in place
checkpointPath
the checkpoint to read
Output.how many arrays were loaded.
Why it loads into an existing dictionaryThe parameters are created rst by the initialiser,
with the right names and shapes, and the checkpoint's values are then copied into them. The
alternative, building the dictionary from the checkpoint, would take the network's structure from
the le, so a checkpoint from a dierent architecture would load without complaint and fail later
with a shape mismatch deep in a forward pass.
Why partial loading is allowed, and why the count is returnedNames present in both
are copied and the rest are left as initialised. That is what makes awarm startpossible: loading
the backbone from one checkpoint while leaving the head freshly initialised, which is how the
namer is started from the reader's learned features.
But partial loading is also how a wrong checkpoint fails quietly: a le matching nothing loads
nothing and training proceeds from random weights, which looks like training that is simply going
badly. Returning the count, which the callers print as a fraction of the total, is what makes that
visible. A warm start reporting a small fraction is a warning that the two checkpoints do not
correspond.
9.81 AppendLog
def Ap pe nd Lo g ( path , line )
319

=== PDF PAGE 321 ===
Inputs
path
the log le
line
the line to add
Output.nothing; the line is appended.
Why a log le as well as printed outputTraining runs for days, so the console scrollback
is lost or truncated long before the run ends. A log le persists, and because each epoch writes
one line of keyed values, the whole history of a run can be read or plotted afterwards.
Why appending rather than rewritingAppending means a resumed run continues the same
log rather than replacing it, so the record spans the interruptions. Appending is also safe against
a crash: each line is complete when written, so an interrupted run leaves a readable log ending at
the last nished epoch, where a rewritten le could be truncated mid-write.
9.82 BackboneForwardTrain
def B a c k b o n e F o r w a r d T r a i n (x , params )
Inputs
xthe input crops
params
the parameters
Output.a pair: the collapsed feature sequence, and the cache.
What it is forThe shared convolutional backbone with caching: three blocks, two poolings,
and the collapse of the height. Both networks call it, so the part they have in common is written
once.
Why sharing the implementation matters more than sharing the weightsThe two
networks do not share weights; each trains its own copy. What they share is the code, and that is
what guarantees the architectures are identical, which in turn is what makes a warm start from
one to the other possible: the parameter names and shapes agree because they were produced by
the same function.
9.83 BackboneBackwardTrain
def B a c k b o n e B a c k w a r d T r a i n ( dx , cache )
Inputs
320

=== PDF PAGE 322 ===
dxthe gradient with respect to the backbone's output
cache
what the forward pass saved
Output.the gradient with respect to the input and every backbone weight gradient.
What it doesWalks the backbone in reverse: uncollapse the height, then the third block, then
the second pooling, the second block, the rst pooling and the rst block, collecting weight gradi-
ents. It is the mirror of the forward sequence and contains no arithmetic beyond the sequencing.
The input gradient is computed and returned although nothing uses it, since the crops are data
rather than parameters. It is produced because each layer's backward pass produces it as a
matter of course, and discarding it earlier would mean a special case in the rst convolution.
It is also what the numerical gradient check compares against when verifying the backbone.
9.84 EvalBackboneForward
def E v a l B a c k b o n e F o r w a r d ( params , x )
Inputs
params
the parameters
xthe input crops
Output.the backbone's output, with no cache.
Why a third version of the backbone existsValidation needs a forward pass with the
inference behaviour, using the stored batch statistics rather than the batch's own, and with no
caching, since no backward pass follows. Running the training version and discarding the caches
would use the wrong statistics and would allocate the caches anyway.
Why using the stored statistics for validation is the pointValidation estimates how the
saved checkpoint will perform, and the saved checkpoint will be used with the stored statistics.
Validating with batch statistics would measure something slightly dierent and, worse, something
that depends on how the validation set happened to be divided into batches, so the same weights
would score dierently with a dierent batch size.
9.85 IterateBatches
def I t e r a t e B a t c h e s ( dataset , batchSize , rng , shuffle )
Inputs
dataset
the dataset to iterate
321

=== PDF PAGE 323 ===
batchSize
how many items per batch
rngthe random generator
shuffle
whether to shue the order
Output.batches of items, one at a time.
Why the order is shued for trainingThe examples arrive grouped by page and therefore
by writer. Without shuing, consecutive batches would each contain one writer's hand, so the
network would be pulled toward that hand and then away from it, which both slows convergence
and makes the loss oscillate with the data order rather than with learning. Shuing makes each
batch an approximately representative sample, which is the assumption the gradient estimate rests
on.
Why validation is not shuedValidation computes a mean over the whole set, and a mean
does not depend on the order. Keeping the order xed makes the measurement exactly repro-
ducible between runs, which matters when comparing two checkpoints: any dierence is then
attributable to the weights rather than to the batching.
Batches are produced one at a time rather than built as a list, so only the current batch is
in memory. For a dataset of tens of thousands of crops that is the dierence between a few
hundred megabytes and several gigabytes.
9.86 InitTextModelParams
def I n i t T e x t M o d e l P a r a m s ( seed , n u m C l a s s e s )
Inputs
seed
the random seed
numClasses
the output size, the charset plus the blank
Output.the reader's complete parameter dictionary.
What it buildsThe shared backbone, two bidirectional LSTM layers, and a linear text head
producing one score per class.
Why the seed is explicitEvery random draw comes from a generator created from this seed,
so the same seed gives the same initial weights. That is what makes a training run reproducible,
which matters for comparing two congurations: without it, a dierence in the result could be a
dierence in the starting point rather than in the change being tested.
322

=== PDF PAGE 324 ===
Why the class count is one more than the charsetThe extra class is the CTC blank,
which is not a character but needs an output slot of its own, as described under DecodeCtc.
Deriving the count from the charset rather than stating it means the two cannot disagree, and a
disagreement would be severe: the network would be trained with every character's index shifted
by one relative to what inference assumes.
9.87 BuildLstmLayerParams
def B u i l d L s t m L a y e r P a r a m s ( sd )
Inputs
sdthe parameter dictionary
Output.the LSTM parameters gathered into the nested structure the forward pass expects.
What it is forThe parameters are stored at, keyed by name, because that is what a checkpoint
is and what the optimiser iterates over. The recurrent forward pass wants them nested: a list of
layers, each with a forward and a backward direction, each with its four arrays. This converts one
view to the other.
Why the at storage is the primary formBecause three separate things need it at: the
optimiser, which iterates over every parameter without knowing the structure; the checkpoint,
which is a at collection of named arrays; and the gradient dictionary, which must be keyed the
same way. The nested form is needed by one function, so it is the derived one.
The nested structure holds references to the same arrays, not copies, so an update applied by
the optimiser to the at dictionary is visible through the nested view. That is essential and
would break silently if any step copied.
9.88 TextModelForward
def T e x t M o d e l F o r w a r d (x , params )
Inputs
xthe input crops
params
the parameters
Output.a pair: the raw per-column class scores, and the cache.
What it doesThe reader's whole forward pass with caching: the backbone, the transpose that
turns the image into a sequence, the two recurrent layers, and the text head.
323

=== PDF PAGE 325 ===
Why it returns raw scores rather than log probabilitiesThe inference version ends with
a log softmax; this one stops at the raw scores, because CtcLoss performs the softmax itself.
The reason is the cancellation described in that entry: taking the softmax and the loss together
gives a gradient that is a simple dierence, whereas computing the softmax here would require
dierentiating back through it as a separate step, which is both more work and numerically worse.
This is why the gradient the loss returns is named for the raw logits: it is the gradient with
respect to the output of this function, so the backward pass can start here with no intervening
layer.
9.89 TextModelBackward
def T e x t M o d e l B a c k w a r d ( dRawLogits , cache )
Inputs
dRawLogits
the gradient from the loss
cache
what the forward pass saved
Output.the gradient with respect to the input and every weight gradient.
What it doesReverses the forward sequence: the text head, the two recurrent layers, the
transpose, and the backbone, collecting weight gradients keyed by parameter name.
The transpose, reversedThe forward pass moved the width axis to the front to make a
sequence; the backward pass moves it back, so the gradient leaving for the backbone is an image
gradient again. A transpose is its own inverse given the right axis order, so nothing is computed,
but the axes must be put back in exactly the order the backbone expects, and an error there
produces a gradient that is correct in magnitude and scrambled in arrangement.
9.90 EvalTextForward
def E v a l T e x t F o r w a r d ( params , x )
Inputs
params
the parameters
xthe input crops
Output.the log probabilities per column, with no cache.
What it is forThe reader's forward pass in inference mode, for validation: stored batch statis-
tics, no caching, and the log softmax applied, since validation decodes the output rather than
passing it to the loss.
324

=== PDF PAGE 326 ===
9.91 TextNumpyBatch
def T e x t N u m p y B a t c h ( batch )
Inputs
batch
a collated batch
Output.the images, the encoded labels and the original texts.
What it doesPrepares a batch for the network: the images converted to the input convention
and the labels encoded into class indices. The original texts are carried through as well so that
validation can compare the decoded output against them.
The image conversion must match TensorFromResized in recognise.py exactly, including the
inversion and the standardisation. A dierence between the two would produce a network that
trains successfully and reads nothing at inference, which is the hardest class of fault to nd
because both halves appear to work.
9.92 ValidateTextModel
def V a l i d a t e T e x t M o d e l ( valSet , params , batchSize , rng )
Inputs
valSet
the held-out set
params
the parameters
batchSize
the batch size
rngthe random generator
Output.a pair: the mean validation loss and the character accuracy.
Why a held-out set is necessaryTraining loss falls whether or not the network is learning
anything general, because a network with enough capacity can memorise its training examples.
The only way to tell memorisation from learning is to measure on data the network has never been
trained on. That is theheld-outor validation set, and the gap between training and validation
loss is the measure of overtting.
Why two numbers are reportedThe loss and the character accuracy answer dierent ques-
tions, and both are needed. The loss is what training minimises, so it is the quantity the scheduler
and the best-checkpoint logic must use, and it is sensitive to small changes in condence. Charac-
ter accuracy is what anyone actually cares about, but it is coarse: a network can improve its loss
substantially with no change in accuracy, because it has become more condent about answers it
already had right.
325

=== PDF PAGE 327 ===
Augmentation is o here, through the evaluation view, and batch normalisation uses its stored
statistics, through the evaluation forward pass. Both are necessary for the measurement to
describe the checkpoint that will be saved rather than some variant of it.
9.93 BuildPagesSplit
def B u i l d P a g e s S p l i t ( maxPages , dataDir )
Inputs
maxPages
how many pages to use
dataDir
where the pages are
Output.a pair of training and validation datasets.
Why the split is by page and not by lineThis is the important decision. Lines from one
page are written by one person, on one sheet, with one pen, in one session. Splitting by line would
put some of a page's lines in training and others in validation, and the network could then score
well on validation by recognising that particular page's characteristics rather than by reading.
The validation gure would be optimistic, and the amount by which it was optimistic would be
unknowable.
Splitting by page makes the validation lines come from sheets the network has never seen, which
is the situation it will actually face. This isleakageavoidance, and it is the commonest way
a held-out measurement is quietly invalidated: the split has to be made at the level at which
examples are genuinely correlated, which here is the page.
The page limit exists to allow short runs during development. It takes the rst n pages rather
than a random sample, which means a short run always uses the same pages, so two such runs
are comparable with each other even though neither represents the whole database.
9.94 BuildTekliaSplit
def B u i l d T e k l i a S p l i t ( dataDir )
Inputs
dataDir
where the corpus is
Output.a pair of training and validation datasets from the alternative corpus.
What it is forThe same split for the second corpus, which arrives already cut into lines.
Having both behind the same interface is what lets the training function take a source argument
and otherwise be identical, so a change to the training procedure applies to both without being
made twice.
326

=== PDF PAGE 328 ===
9.95 TrainTextModel
def T r a i n T e x t M o d e l ( numEpochs , batchSize , lr , seed , maxPages , dataDir , source = '
pages ' ,
resume = False , wa rm S ta rt = None )
Inputs
numEpochs
how many passes over the data
batchSize
examples per step
lrthe initial learning rate
seed
the random seed
maxPages
how many pages to use
dataDir
where the data is
source
which corpus, default pages
resume
whether to continue a previous run
warmStart
a checkpoint to initialise from
Output.the best validation loss reached.
The training loop
Build the splitand initialise the parameters.
Create the optimiser and scheduler:RMSprop with a small weight decay, and the
plateau scheduler watching validation loss.
Optionally warm start or resume.
F or each epoch:iterate shued batches, and for each run the forward pass, compute the
CTC loss and its gradient, run the backward pass, select the trainable gradients and take
an optimiser step.
After each epoch:validate, step the scheduler, save the checkpoint and append to the
log.
Why two checkpoints are keptA best checkpoint, written only when validation loss im-
proves, and a rolling checkpoint written every epoch. They serve dierent purposes and neither
substitutes for the other. The best one is the model to use, since later epochs may overt and be
worse. The rolling one is for resuming, since resuming must continue from where the run actually
stopped, not from the best epoch, or the epochs between would be repeated.
327

=== PDF PAGE 329 ===
Why the best is chosen on loss rather than accuracyLoss is the ner measure. Character
accuracy on a validation set of a few thousand lines moves in visible steps, so several epochs can
tie, and choosing between them would be arbitrary. Loss distinguishes them, because it responds
to condence as well as to correctness.
What warm starting is forInitialising from an existing checkpoint rather than from random
weights. It is how the joint checkpoint is produced: start from the database-trained weights and
continue on the mixed data, so the network keeps what it learned from the large corpus while
adapting to the photographs. Training that from scratch would need the whole corpus again and
would reach a similar place far more slowly.
The progress line is printed with a carriage return and no newline, so it overwrites itself rather
than lling the console with thousands of lines. The per-epoch summary is printed normally
and also written to the log, so the transient detail stays transient and the durable record is
kept.
9.96 InitAuthorModelParams
def I n i t A u t h o r M o d e l P a r a m s ( seed , n u m A u t h o r s )
Inputs
seed
the random seed
numAuthors
how many writers to distinguish
Output.the namer's complete parameter dictionary.
What it buildsThe same shared backbone, then a two-layer head: a linear layer, a ReLU, a
dropout, and a second linear layer producing one score per writer. No recurrent layers, for the
reasons given under AuthorClassierCNNNumpy.
Why the writer count is a parameterIt sets the nal layer's output size, so a checkpoint
is tied to the number of writers it was trained on. Adding a writer means a new nal layer and
retraining it, and a checkpoint from a dierent count cannot be loaded. That is also why the
writer names are stored inside the checkpoint, as described under LoadStateDictAny: the count
alone is not enough, since the same ten writers in a dierent order would load without complaint
and attribute every page to the wrong person.
9.97 AuthorModelForward
def A u t h o r M o d e l F o r w a r d (x , params , training , rng )
Inputs
xthe input crops
328

=== PDF PAGE 330 ===
params
the parameters
training
whether dropout should be active
rngthe random generator for dropout
Output.a pair: the raw per-writer scores, and the cache.
What it doesThe backbone, the average pooling over position, and the two-layer head with
dropout between the layers.
Why the training ag is an argumentBecause dropout must be active during training
and inactive during validation, and the same function serves both. Passing it explicitly rather
than holding it as state makes it impossible to validate with dropout accidentally left on, which
would add noise to the measurement, or to train with it o, which would silently remove the
regularisation.
The random generator is passed in rather than created here, so that the dropout masks come
from the run 's own seeded generator and a training run is reproducible. A generator created
inside the function would reseed itself on every call and the masks would be unrepeatable.
9.98 AuthorModelBackward
def A u t h o r M o d e l B a c k w a r d ( dLogits , cache )
Inputs
dLogits
the gradient from the loss
cache
what the forward pass saved
Output.the gradient with respect to the input and every weight gradient.
What it doesReverses the namer's forward sequence: the second linear layer, the dropout,
the ReLU, the rst linear layer, the average pooling, and the backbone. The average pooling's
backward pass spreads the gradient evenly back over every position, which is why the namer's
backbone receives gradient everywhere rather than only at the winning rows as the reader's does.

9.101 ValidateAuthorModel
def V a l i d a t e A u t h o r M o d e l ( valSet , params , batchSize , rng )
Inputs
valSet
the held-out set
params
the parameters
batchSize
the batch size
rngthe random generator
Output.a pair: the mean validation loss and the accuracy.
What it measuresThe cross-entropy loss and the fraction of crops attributed to the right
writer, over the held-out set with dropout o and stored statistics in use.
330

=== PDF PAGE 332 ===
Why accuracy means more here than for the readerFor a classier, accuracy is the
quantity of interest directly: the crop is attributed correctly or it is not. For the reader, character
accuracy is an aggregate over a sequence and a line can be mostly right, so the measure is coarser
relative to what matters. The namer's accuracy is therefore the headline gure and the loss is the
ner measure used for choosing the checkpoint.
The split for the namer must also be by page, for the leakage reason given under BuildPagesS-
plit, and it matters more here. The namer's task is to recognise a writer, and lines from
the same sheet share far more than handwriting: the same paper, pen, lighting and scan. A
line-level split would let it score nearly perfectly by recognising the sheet.
9.102 TrainAuthorModel
def T r a i n A u t h o r M o d e l ( numEpochs , batchSize , lr , seed , resume = False ,
w ar mS ta rt = None )
Inputs
numEpochs
how many passes
batchSize
examples per step
lrthe initial learning rate
seed
the random seed
resume
whether to continue a previous run
warmStart
a checkpoint to initialise from
Output.the best validation loss reached.
What diers from the reader's loopThe same structure: batches, forward, loss, backward,
step, validate, save, log. The dierences are the loss, which is cross-entropy rather than CTC, the
dropout, which must be enabled for training and disabled for validation, and the data, which is
labelled by writer.
Why warm starting matters more hereThe namer has far less data: ten writers' pages
against a database of thousands. Training its backbone from scratch on that would overt badly,
because a deep convolutional stack has ample capacity to memorise a few thousand crops. Starting
from the reader's backbone gives it features already learned from a large corpus, so the small
dataset is spent on learning to tell writers apart rather than on learning what a stroke is.
This istransfer learning, and the reason it works here is the argument made earlier: the features
that distinguish letters and the features that distinguish hands are largely the same features. The
reader learned them from data the namer could never have had.
331

=== PDF PAGE 333 ===
The two networks are trained separately rather than together with a shared backbone and two
heads, which would be the obvious alternative. Separate training keeps each network's accuracy
independent of the other's, which matters for this project because both are used as judges of the
synthesised output: a shared backbone would make the two scores correlated, and the harmonic
mean that combines them assumes they are measuring dierent things.
